Chapter 1

The year I started grade 1 was 1977. I had turned six that March, and in September my mother walked me the four blocks to the elementary school in Brule Falls, where she was the secretary, and left me at the door of the grade 1 room before she went down the hall to her own desk. She did not hold my hand on the way. She carried her purse and a bag of file folders, and I carried a lunch tin with a sandwich and an apple in it, and we got there at twenty past eight.

Brule Falls was a pulp-mill town of about four thousand people on the river, a long way north of anywhere most people have heard of. The mill was at the east end of town and everyone called it the mill, never by the company's name, because there was only one and nobody needed to tell it apart from anything. Most of the men in town worked there, or had worked there, or sold something to the men who did. When the wind came from the east the whole town smelled of it, a sour, cooked smell that got into the laundry on the lines. There was a water tower on the hill behind the school, a railway trestle over the river that the freight went across twice a day, an outdoor rink beside the school, and the church of St. Anne on the corner of Main and Second. That was most of it.

My father was Armand Lacasse and he ran Lacasse Hardware on Main Street, three doors down from the corner store and across from the bank. He was a brisk man who moved quickly and talked quickly, and he could talk to anybody who came through the door for as long as they wanted to stand there, about the mill, the river, the price of stovepipe, whose roof had come down under the snow. He also counted everything. He sold nails by the pound out of open kegs and weighed them on a hanging scale, and if the scale said a pound and an ounce he took a few nails back out. He kept the pennies from the till in paper rolls. My mother was Hélène, and she worked at the school from eight until four and ran the house the rest of the time. My brother Denis was ten, four years older than me, and the loudest person in the house. He liked boats, and he had maps of the sea taped up on the wall over his bed, the Atlantic and the Pacific and a chart of the Gulf of St. Lawrence that he had sent away for, though the nearest salt water was a thousand miles from us. My father's mother lived two streets over on Laurier Street, and we called her Mémère. She was a widow, and she went to Mass every morning.

That was the town and the people in it, and that year I started learning them one at a time.

After school I went to the store instead of home, because my mother did not get off until four and my father was there. It was two blocks from the school. I would come in the front door under the bell, and my father would look up from whoever he was talking to and point with his chin at the back, and I would go around the end of the counter and sit on an upturned nail keg behind it until he was done.

The counter was long and worn pale on top where people set things down. Underneath it, on the floor behind, was where things ended up. Screws came in small cardboard boxes, a hundred to a box, and the boxes split. A customer would pick one up by the end and the bottom would let go, or a box would come off the shelf when somebody reached past it, and the screws would go everywhere. My father picked up what he could, and the rest he swept at the end of the day with a push broom into a dustpan and tipped into the scrap pail by the back door, along with the wire ends and bent nails and the bits of chain off the cutter. The scrap pail went out with the garbage on Thursdays.

I started picking them up before he swept. I would get down on my knees behind the counter with my head under the bottom shelf and pull out every screw I could find, wood screws mostly, some flat heads and some round, a few machine screws with no nuts, and put them in my pocket. Some days there were three or four. Some days there were twenty. When my pockets got heavy I asked my father for a box to put them in, and he gave me a shoebox that a pair of work boots had come in, size ten, with a lid. He did not ask me what I wanted with them. He said, "Don't take any off the shelf," and I said I wouldn't, and I didn't.

At home I sat on the kitchen floor by the register where the heat came up and tipped the shoebox out on the linoleum. I had taken an empty egg carton from the cupboard where my mother saved them for Mémère, who used them for something I never found out. It had twelve cups. I sorted the screws by length, the shortest in the first cup and the longest in the last, and when I had more lengths than cups I took a second carton. I did not know the sizes at first. I took one of each length to the store the next day and held them up against the boxes on the shelf until they matched, and my father showed me how the end of the box gave the gauge and the length, number eight by an inch and a quarter, number six by three-quarters. I wrote the lengths in pencil on strips of masking tape from the roll in the kitchen drawer and stuck a strip on the side of each cup. My printing was not good yet. The quarters looked like sevens. But I knew what they said.

I did that most days that fall. I came home with the day's screws, tipped them out, and put each one in its cup. When the cups got full I started keeping the overflow in the shoebox in paper envelopes, one envelope for each length, with the length written on the envelope.

One evening in October I had both cartons out on the floor between the table and the stove, and Denis came in the back door from somewhere and through the kitchen on his way to the stairs. He did not look down. His boot caught the end of the first carton and sent it across the floor, and the screws went under the table and under the stove and against the baseboard, and the carton landed upside down by the fridge. Denis said, "Watch it," and kept going up the stairs.

My mother was at the counter peeling potatoes. She looked at the floor and then at me and said supper was in half an hour. I got the carton and turned it right side up, and I went under the table on my stomach for the screws there, and then I lay flat and got the ones from under the stove with the handle of a wooden spoon. Some had stayed in their cups and some hadn't, and I could not tell anymore which of the ones still in the cups had been knocked in from somewhere else, so I tipped them all out, the second carton too, and started from the beginning. I measured each one against the ones I was sure of. I had them all back in their cups, with the tape strips pressed down where they had lifted, before my father came in the back door and my mother put the potatoes on the table. I put the cartons in the shoebox and the shoebox on the chair by the door, and we ate.

Nobody said anything about it at supper. Denis talked about a ship he had read about that went down in a storm off Newfoundland.

On a Saturday morning in September, before any of that, my father gave me a dollar bill and sent me to the corner store on Main Street for a loaf of bread and a quart of milk. It was the first time he had sent me for anything by myself. He was in the kitchen in his store clothes with his coffee, and he took the dollar out of his wallet and folded it once and put it in my hand and said, "Bread and milk. White bread. Bring back the change."

The corner store had a wooden floor that went up and down, a cooler along the back wall that hummed, and prices on paper tags stuck to the edge of each shelf. I found the bread, fifty-three cents, and the milk in the cooler, a quart in a waxed carton, forty-one cents. I knew how to add. I did it in my head twice on the way to the counter and got ninety-four cents both times, so the change would be six cents.

The woman at the counter rang it in and opened the drawer and put a nickel and a penny in my hand. Before she could close the drawer I put the coins down on the counter between us and set the bread beside them and the milk beside that, and I looked back at the shelf where the bread tag was, and over at the cooler, and I counted it out: fifty-three and forty-one is ninety-four, and six is a dollar. She stood there with her hand on the drawer and waited. When I was done I picked up the nickel and the penny and put them in my pocket and said thank you, and she shut the drawer.

At home my father was still at the table. I put the bread and the milk down in front of him and took the coins out of my pocket and laid them on the oilcloth, and I counted it again out loud for him, fifty-three for the bread, forty-one for the milk, six back. He watched me do it without saying anything. Then he picked up the nickel and the penny and looked at them and put them in his own pocket, and said, "Good," and went to work.

That afternoon, sitting on the nail keg behind the counter, I heard him tell it to a man who had come in for stovepipe elbows. He told it twice, the second time to the man's brother, who came in while the first one was still there. He did not tell it to me.

My father brought the bicycle home in the back of the station wagon one evening in October. Someone had traded it in at the store against a bill, or sold it to him for two dollars; I never knew which. It was small, a red two-wheeler with a white seat that had a tear in it and black tape over the tear, and the chain was rusty. He oiled the chain on the back step with the can from the store and pumped up the tires and lowered the seat as far as it would go with a nine-sixteenths wrench, and the next evening after supper he took me and the bike down to the lane behind the store.

The lane ran behind the Main Street buildings from the store to the bank corner, gravel, with puddles in the low spots, the backs of the buildings on one side and a row of sheds and board fences on the other. It was flat and nobody drove down it after six. My father held the back of the seat and told me to pedal and look ahead, not down, and he ran alongside me in his work shoes with his hand on the seat, and then he let go.

I went about fifteen feet and the front wheel turned in the loose gravel and I went over to the left and down, onto my hip and my hand. The gravel took the skin off the heel of my hand. My father picked the bike up and said that was good, that was far, and held the seat again. The second time I went farther, past the back door of the barbershop, and then I looked back to see where he was and went into the fence. The third time the front wheel dropped into a pothole full of water and stopped, and I went over the handlebars into the puddle.

I got up. My pants were wet down one side and the heel of my hand was bleeding a little where the gravel had been. My father came up the lane and stood the bike up and held it out to me by the handlebars. I took it from him and walked it back up the lane, past the store and around the corner and home, and put it in the shed in the back yard, against the wall behind the lawnmower, and shut the door.

My father came in a few minutes later. He said we could go out again tomorrow. I said I didn't want to. He said it again the next evening, and I said no again, and after that he stopped asking. The bike stayed in the shed behind the lawnmower all that fall and all that winter, with the snow drifted up against the shed door, and it was still there when the next summer came.

The town library was two rooms above the post office, and my mother took me there on Saturday afternoons and let me take out two books. One Saturday in October I took out a picture book about magnets. It had a red cover and drawings inside of a horseshoe magnet picking up nails and paper clips, and a compass, and a boy holding two bar magnets that were pushing each other apart. I could not read most of it. I could read the pictures.

We sold horseshoe magnets at the store, on a card by the till, red with silver ends, thirty-nine cents. I asked my father for one. He looked at me for a while and then took one off the card and wrote it in the book he kept under the counter, and gave it to me.

For a week I tried it on everything in the house. I started with nails, because the book showed nails, and it picked up a two-inch common nail from the shoebox and held it by the head, and then a second nail hanging off the end of the first, and a third off the end of that. It picked up screws. It picked up a spoon from the kitchen drawer, but not every spoon; the good ones in the box in the dining-room sideboard it did not pick up at all. It stuck to the side of the stove so hard I had to pull it off with both hands. It stuck to the fridge door and the radiator in the front hall and the scissors. It picked up a nickel and a dime from my pocket, which my father did not believe until I showed him. It did not stick to the brass doorknob on the front door, or the brass kick plate under it, or the copper bottom of my mother's good pot, or a penny. It did not pick up the gold cross on the chain my mother wore, which I tested while she was sitting down. It did not pick up tinfoil.

I made a list. I had a notebook that year already, a school scribbler with a blue cover, and I ruled two columns down a page and printed YES at the top of one and NO at the top of the other. Nails, screws, stove, fridge, kitchen spoon, radiator, scissors, nickel, dime, Denis's belt buckle. Doorknob, kick plate, pot, penny, cross, tinfoil, good spoons.

What I wanted to know was why. The doorknob was metal and the nails were metal. I asked my mother at supper and she said they were different kinds of metal. I asked what kind, and she said the doorknob was brass. I asked why brass didn't work, and she said that was just how it was. I asked her again the next night, and she said to look in the book. I said I couldn't read that part. I asked her again on Wednesday while she was doing the dishes, and on Thursday while she was folding laundry on the kitchen table, and on Thursday night after Denis had gone up she dried her hands on the tea towel and sat down at the table and said, "Bring me the book."

She found the page. It had a drawing of a piece of iron cut open with little arrows inside it, all pointing different ways, and then a second drawing with the arrows all pointing the same way and a magnet beside it. She read it to me slowly. It said that iron and steel were made of tiny parts that were each a little like a magnet themselves, and in ordinary iron they pointed every which way, and when you brought a magnet near they turned and lined up and the iron was pulled. It said some metals, like brass and copper and gold and aluminum, did not have parts that could line up that way, and so the magnet passed them by. It said nickel was one of the few other metals a magnet would pull, and that was why the nickel and the dime were in the YES column and the penny was not.

When she finished I said, "Read it again." She looked at me. Then she read it again from the top, at the same speed, with her finger under the words this time so I could follow along, and I watched the arrows in the drawing while she read. She closed the book and said it was time for bed. I took the book and the magnet upstairs, and in the scribbler, under the two columns, I printed IRON across the YES side in big letters and NICKEL under it.

Mémère went to the seven o'clock Mass at St. Anne every weekday morning, and had as long as anybody in the family could remember. On Sundays she went to the nine o'clock with the rest of us, but the weekday one was hers. She lived on Laurier Street in a small house with green trim and a kitchen at the back that was always warmer than ours, because she kept the oil stove going from September to May. I stopped there some days on the way home from the store. She would sit me at the table with a glass of milk and a slice of bread with brown sugar on it, and she would tell me in French who had been at Mass that morning and who hadn't, and who had been there yesterday and not today.

In October I asked my mother at supper if I could go with her. My mother looked at my father. She said Mass was at seven, which meant I would have to be up by half past six and dressed by myself and walk over in the dark, and go straight from the church to school without coming home. I said I knew. My father said, "Let him," and that was the end of it. My mother telephoned Mémère after the dishes, and I heard her say my name twice and then say oui, oui, and hang up.

My father gave me a wind-up alarm clock from the store, a Westclox with two bells on top, and I set it for twenty past six. In the morning the house was dark except for the light over the stove. I got dressed in my school clothes, ate a piece of bread standing up, and let myself out the back door. It was two streets. The streetlights were still on and there was frost on the lawns, and over the east end of town the steam from the mill showed white against the sky where the lights from the yard caught it. Mémère's porch light was on. I knocked and she came out in her black coat and the hat with the long pin through it, and pulled the door shut behind her, and we walked the block and a half to St. Anne together without saying much.

Inside it was cold and smelled of floor wax and wet wool. There were maybe twenty people, mostly old women in headscarves, and two or three men in mill jackets who had come straight off the night shift and sat at the back. Mémère's pew was the third from the front on the left, and she went in from the aisle and knelt right away on the kneeler. I knelt where she knelt, beside her on the same board. When she stood, I stood. When she sat, I sat. The Mass was in French and I did not follow most of it, but I did not need to, because I watched her.

The thing I watched most was her sign of the cross. She did it slowly, the same way every time: forehead, chest, left shoulder, right shoulder, and then she brought her thumb to her lips and kissed it. Nobody in my family did it like that. My father did it fast, like brushing something off his shirt, and Denis barely did it at all. I did it the way Mémère did, at the same speed, and kissed my thumb at the end. She saw me do it the first morning and did not say anything about it.

It took twenty-five minutes. Afterward she walked me to the corner by the school and went home, and I was at the school half an hour before the bell, so I sat on the bench in the boot room until my mother came in at quarter past eight and unlocked the office.

I went every weekday after that. In the middle of November it turned cold all at once, the way it does up there, and one afternoon in her kitchen Mémère looked at the thermometer outside her window and then at me and said that tomorrow I was to stay in bed. The radio said it would go to twenty-eight below overnight. She said, "Tu restes couché demain. C'est trop froid pour toi." I said all right.

The alarm went at twenty past six. I put my snow pants on over my school pants, and my parka and my toque and the scarf my mother had knitted, and two pairs of mitts, and let myself out the back door. The snow squeaked under my boots. The inside of my nose froze on the first breath. I was on Mémère's front step at quarter to seven, and I stood there until the porch light came on at ten to and the door opened. She looked at me for a while with her hand on the door. Then she said, "Bon," and went back in for her coat, and we walked to St. Anne, and I knelt where she knelt.

In November my parents rearranged the upstairs. Until then Denis and I had shared the front bedroom, two beds and one dresser between us. The back bedroom, the bigger one, with the window that looked out over the yard and the shed and the lane, had been my mother's sewing room and the place where everything went that didn't go anywhere else. The small room at the top of the stairs had the linen shelves in it and the Christmas boxes. My mother had decided Denis was too old to share. The sewing machine was going down to the basement and the Christmas boxes into the crawlspace, and each of us would get a room of his own.

My father explained this at supper on a Sunday. Before Denis could say anything I said I wanted the back room, the one with the window over the yard. I said it plainly. My mother said we would see. Denis didn't say anything; he was eating.

The next Saturday my father and Denis carried the sewing machine down to the basement and the boxes up into the crawlspace. When the back room was empty Denis went into the front bedroom and took his maps off the wall one at a time, careful with the tape, and carried them across the hall into the back room and started putting them up on the wall beside the window. I stood in the doorway. I said I had asked for it first. Denis said he was older. My father came up the stairs with a hammer and a coffee can of picture hooks, and looked at Denis with the Atlantic half up on the wall, and looked at me, and said, "Denis is older, and he needs the room for his boats. You take the little one."

I didn't say anything. I went into the front bedroom and took the brown blanket off my bed and folded it over my arm, and I picked up the shoebox of screws with the egg cartons inside it, and I carried them across the hall into the small room. The linen shelves had been emptied and the room smelled of mothballs. There was no bed in it yet. I put the blanket on the floor and went back for the rest: the scribbler, the magnet, the library book, my coffee tin, my alarm clock. I lined them up along the wall under the window, which was a small one that looked at the neighbour's siding, the shoebox first and the clock last, each thing an inch from the next.

Whatever I have become, the boy at the bottom of it was someone who watched before he spoke.

That afternoon my father and Denis carried my bed frame and mattress across the hall and set them up along the other wall, which left about two feet between the bed and my row of things. After supper I borrowed my father's tape measure from the drawer and measured the room. It was eight feet by nine feet two inches. I wrote that in the scribbler.

The coffee tin was a one-pound Maxwell House tin with a white plastic lid that snapped on. I kept my allowance in it, which was twenty-five cents a week, paid on Saturday mornings, and the pennies I found under the counter at the store when I was down there after screws. My father let me keep the pennies. He said anything on the floor behind the counter at closing was mine, and anything on the counter was his. Some weeks there were none, and one week there were eleven, because a roll had split. I counted the tin every Sunday after Mass and wrote the total at the back of the scribbler, the date and the amount, one under the other. By the end of November it was two dollars and eighty-one cents.

The drugstore on Main Street had model kits on a shelf at the back, and Denis had been looking at one since October, a balsa kit of the Bluenose with the hull in pieces and the sails printed on paper and a small tube of glue. It was four dollars and ninety-five cents. Denis had been saving for it out of his allowance, which was fifty cents, and what he got for shovelling Mémère's walk, and on a Sunday afternoon at the end of November he came into my room and stood in the door and said he had four dollars and forty-five cents.

"Lend me fifty cents," he said. "I'll pay you back Saturday."

I said no.

He looked at the tin on the floor at the end of my row. "You've got almost three dollars in there."

"Two eighty-one," I said. "No."

He said a word in French that we were not allowed to say in the house and went back across the hall to his room and shut the door. I heard him moving things around in there for a while.

That night after my mother had turned off the hall light I got up and opened the closet in my room. On the top shelf were my winter boots from the year before, which were too small now and which my mother was keeping in case they fit somebody. I pulled the chair over from the wall and stood on it and put the coffee tin up on the shelf behind the boots, pushed all the way to the back, and moved the boots in front of it so it didn't show from the floor.

If there is a lesson in those years, it is that a child who hoards learns to count before he learns to share.

I put the chair back where it had been. The next Sunday I got the tin down, counted it, and wrote the total at the back of the scribbler: two dollars and eighty-one cents. Then I put it back behind the boots.

The outdoor rink by the school went in at the start of December. Two men from the town flooded it at night with a hose off the hydrant, and it was boards on four sides, about four feet high, painted white once and mostly grey now, with a bench on the school side and a light on a pole at each end that came on at four. The older boys played there in the evenings. In the afternoons, from the end of school until it got dark, it belonged to whoever was there first.

The ones who were there first that December were Kyle Brandt, Marcus Oake, and a boy everybody called Pickle. Kyle was a year ahead of me in school. Marcus and Pickle were in grade 1 like me, in the other class. They played shinny every afternoon, the three of them, with one puck and two pairs of boots set on the ice for nets, and Kyle decided the sides. Kyle talked the whole time, calling the score and who had cheated. Marcus didn't say much; he went where Kyle pointed. Pickle was small and fast and laughed at everything, including when he fell down. I never found out why they called him Pickle, and as far as I know nobody else did either.

I stood at the boards and watched them on the first day until the lights came on, and then I went to the store. The second day I did the same. I watched who passed to whom, and which of them shot from where, and how Kyle always took the side with Pickle when the sides were uneven.

The third day I brought my skates. They had been Denis's, brown leather with the stitching coming loose at one toe, and they were two sizes too big, so I wore two pairs of wool socks and stuffed newspaper in the toes. I sat on the bench and laced them up as tight as I could pull, and walked across the snow to the gate in the boards, and stood on the ice holding the top of the boards with one hand. I had my father's old stick from the basement, cut down with the hacksaw.

Kyle skated past and stopped in a spray of snow. I said, "Can I play?"

He looked at me and at the skates. "You're Lacasse," he said. "From the hardware."

I said yes.

"You're with Marcus," he said, and skated off, and that was it.

I was not much good. My ankles bent in at the sides and I could not stop except by running into the boards, and Pickle took the puck off me whenever he wanted it. But nobody told me to go home, and when the lights came on and they took their boots off the ice and went, Kyle said, "Tomorrow," to all of us, me included.

After that I was at the rink every day after school that winter. The first week I got there when they did. The second week I left the school by the side door at the bell instead of going out the front with everybody, and I was on the bench lacing up when they came around the corner. By the third week I had my skates on and was out on the ice with the puck, going up and down along the boards, by the time they got there, and Kyle came through the gate and said, "Lacasse is here already," as if it were a thing that happened every day, which by then it was. I went to the store after, when the lights came on, with my skates tied together by the laces and hung over my stick on my shoulder, and sat on the nail keg until my toes came back.

There was a hill behind the mill that everybody in town went sledding on. It was a long bank of fill that the mill had pushed up years before, grown over with grass in summer, and it had two sides. The near side, toward the road, was long and gentle, and that was where the small children went with their mothers and the pieces of cardboard. The far side, toward the river, was short and steep and ran down to a ditch at the bottom that froze solid in winter, with a lip of packed snow on the near edge of it. Only the older boys went down that side. Denis went down that side.

Denis had a toboggan, a six-foot wooden one with the front curled over and a rope through the curl and a cushion tied on with twine. It had been my father's. On the Saturday after Christmas Denis left it leaning against the wall by the back door and went to the drugstore. I took it. I pulled it by the rope down our street and past the store and along the road to the mill, and up the near side of the hill, which took a while, because it was long and the toboggan was heavier than I was.

At the top I went over to the far side. There were four or five older boys there, none of whom I knew by name, and they were going down two at a time on their own toboggans and shouting at the bottom. I waited until the line in front of me was clear, and I put the toboggan at the top of the run with the curl pointed down, and sat on it near the front with my boots under the curl and the rope in both hands, and pushed off with my mitts.

It went fast. The run was packed hard and icy from a week of the older boys, and the toboggan took it straight down. At the bottom it hit the lip of snow at the edge of the frozen ditch and left the ground. I remember the rope going slack in my hands. It came down on the far side of the ditch flat and hard, and the jolt went up through the boards into my back, and the toboggan slewed sideways and threw me off into the deep snow along the edge of the run. Snow got down my collar and up my sleeves and into my mouth.

One of the older boys at the bottom laughed and said something about Denis's little brother. I got up and brushed off what I could. The toboggan was ten feet away on its side. I went and stood it up and took the rope and started back up the steep side, which was harder going up than it was coming down, kicking toe holds in the side of the run and pulling the toboggan up behind me a few feet at a time.

At the top I put it down in the same place, on the same line, with the curl pointed at the same spot at the bottom. I sat down on it near the front with my boots under the curl and the rope in both hands, and pushed off.

When I got home Denis was in the kitchen. He asked where his toboggan was, and I said by the back door, which was true by then. There was a crack in the second slat from the front that I had not seen before, about three inches long. I didn't mention it, and he didn't find it until the next weekend.

That was 1977. I went to bed on New Year's Eve in the small room at the top of the stairs, eight feet by nine feet two inches, with the alarm clock set for twenty past six because Mémère went to Mass on New Year's Day too, and my things along the wall where I had put them: the shoebox of screws, the egg cartons with their masking tape, the scribbler with the YES and NO columns, the magnet. The coffee tin was on the top shelf of the closet, behind the boots. I had counted it that morning.

Chapter 2

In 1978 I turned seven and was in grade 2 in Brule Falls. My father, Armand Lacasse, ran Lacasse Hardware on Main Street, and my mother, Hélène, worked as the secretary at the elementary school. Denis was eleven. Outside the house there were Kyle Brandt, Marcus Oake and Pickle. I went where they went, provided I could get home when I was supposed to. We had the streets between our houses, the bush behind Pickle’s place and the river below the railway trestle. I also went to Mémère’s, two streets over, where I could generally find something to eat or something that had been put aside to go out with the garbage.

The alarm clock was beside her garbage, standing upright as though it still had a job there. It had a round metal case, two bells on top and a handle between them. There were separate keys on the back for the clock and the alarm. The glass was still in it. I picked it up and turned it over. One key would move a little and the other would not. I held it against my ear, then carried it home with both hands, with the face turned towards my shirt.

It strikes me now that I never once asked my father for anything I could take apart myself.

My mother made me put newspaper on the kitchen table. I spread it beyond the clock on all sides because I did not know how much was inside. The screws in the back were small, with straight slots. I had to try the screwdriver in each slot and push down hard enough to keep it from climbing out. Once I had the back off, I set the screws along a fold in the paper. They rolled into the crease and stayed there. The case came away from a frame holding the works between two metal plates.

I turned the winding key and watched. I could see wheels moving, but most of them went too slowly to show anything unless I waited. Near the top, a small toothed wheel rocked back and forth. Something caught and released with it. That was where the tick came from. I put a finger against it and the other wheels stopped. When I lifted my finger, they started again. The spring had enough pull to move everything, but that little part only let it move a bit at a time.

I removed another screw. A plate shifted, and the ends of several little shafts came out of their holes. I had been expecting the wheels to remain standing in their places. Instead they lay against one another. I separated them on the newspaper, putting the larger ones nearest the clock case. There were more pieces than I had expected from looking through the back. One had a shaft so fine that I could hardly pick it up between my finger and thumb.

I went down to the store and waited at the counter. My father was talking to a customer, with one hand beside the scale and the other moving loose pennies into a little stack. When he finished, I asked why the small toothed wheel rocking back and forth made the tick and kept the spring from letting go all at once. I showed him the motion with my finger. He said it let the spring out a tooth at a time. I asked what made it do that. He moved his hand back and forth, much as I had done, then turned to the counter again.

For the next week I turned the clock over and tried to put it together so it would still tick. The newspaper got folded around it at supper and opened afterwards. I could put some of the shafts into one plate, but fitting the other plate over all their ends was another matter. If I got two into their holes, a third slipped out. I would take the plate off and move a wheel to another place. Sometimes I wound the spring a little and held the pieces together with my fingers. I got a few ticks that way. When the parts would not settle, I left them on the newspaper and went outside.

My mother gave me a school scribbler after I had been using loose paper for the clock screws. It had ruled pages and enough room on the cover for a name, a subject and a grade. I put Ray Lacasse on the name line. I left the subject blank because I had several things to put in it. The first was the shoebox of screws. I brought it to the kitchen table, moved the folded newspaper containing the clock to one side and tipped the screws out in a heap.

I already kept them roughly sorted by size. The shortest ones collected at one end of the box, although some worked their way beneath the others when I carried it. I made separate piles on the table. I measured a screw against the wooden ruler, then found the others that matched it. A few had damaged heads or points. I kept those with the screws of the same length. I was counting what I had, and I had them whether they were any good or not.

With the ruler I drew a vertical line down the page. I pressed too hard at first and left a groove underneath the pencil mark. On one side I wrote the size. On the other I wrote the number. I counted each pile by moving the screws from the table back into the box. When I lost my place, I tipped that pile out and counted it again. The screws made small hard clicks against the bottom of the shoebox. I had to keep the box flat so the finished piles would not mix before I was done.

I wrote everything in pencil. If I found another screw under the newspaper, I could rub out the number and change it. The rubber on the end of the pencil left crumbs across the page. I brushed them towards the edge of the table with the side of my hand. Some of the figures became faint after I had corrected them twice, so I wrote over them more firmly. I did not use a new page just because one number had changed.

The hockey cards came next. Their numbers were already printed on them, which saved measuring anything. I laid them out in order and wrote the numbers in the scribbler. When I had two of a card, I put a small two beside its number. There were gaps where I had no card. I left those gaps on the page as well. It took more room that way, but I could add a number later without squeezing it between two others or drawing an arrow across the margin.

After that I listed what was on my closet shelf. I stood on the floor and brought the things down a few at a time, putting them on the bed before I wrote them in. I counted the shoebox as one thing on that list. The screws were on their own pages already. I did the same with the cards. I put each thing back on the shelf after writing it down, with the things I used most near the front. The scribbler fitted beside the shoebox. I could reach its edge without moving anything else, and the pencil lay inside the cover with its point towards the fold.

The Cubs met in the church hall on Tuesday nights. At the first meeting I stood with the other boys and followed them when we were told where to go. The hall had a hard floor and folding tables stacked against a wall. With everybody in it, the air warmed up, although the floor stayed cold through the bottoms of my shoes. There was plenty of standing in one place while the leader explained what would happen next. I looked at the badges on the boys who had been there longer.

I went back the following Tuesday. We were to learn the reef knot and the bowline for our first badge. Each of us had a piece of rope. Mine had a stiff end and a softer end where the strands had spread. I ran the rope through my hands while the leader showed us the first knot. He held his own rope up so we could see which end crossed the other. I copied it on my lap, pulled the two ends and got a reef knot that stayed flat.

I untied it and did it again. There were two movements, one after the other, and I could keep those in order. If I turned the rope around, the knot still looked the same. I pulled it tight enough that I had to work at the middle with a fingernail to loosen it. When the leader came past, I held it up. He looked at it and went on. I undid it once more while he finished with the others.

The bowline needed a loop left in the rope before the free end went through. I made the loop, put the end through and brought it around the standing part. When I pulled, the loop disappeared. The leader showed me again, holding the rope closer this time. I followed until he took his hands away. Then I had two pieces crossing where I thought there should have been one. I pulled one and the whole thing came straight.

I tried a dozen times. Sometimes I got a knot, but it was a lump with a loop sticking out of it. Sometimes the loose end stayed in my hand and the rest fell open. I laid the rope on the floor to copy the shape without having to hold it. That worked until I picked it up. I could still make the reef knot, so I made that instead while the boys beside me continued with the bowline.

At home I had no rope laid out and no plan to practise. The following Tuesday I stayed there. When the time came to leave, I had other things spread on the kitchen table and did not clear them away. I quit the Cubs after two meetings, luckily for the Cub leader. The next Tuesday passed the same way. I never went back to the church hall for another meeting, and there was no badge to put with the things on my shelf.

We decided to build a fort when the ground had dried enough that scrap boards would not sink into it. I had found a hollow near the river with a flat rock floor. The rock was bare over most of it, with earth around the edge where boards could have stood. I had gone down into it and checked the space by taking long steps from one side to the other. It was dry when I found it. There would have been no need to clear much before we started.

I brought it up when Kyle Brandt, Marcus Oake, Pickle and I were together. Before I got far with the description, Kyle said the fort would be in the bush behind Pickle’s house. He pointed that way and started talking about where the entrance would go. Pickle went with him. Marcus followed. I opened my mouth, shut it and went after them. Kyle’s spot had dirt underfoot, roots running through it and trees close enough together to hold boards between them.

We stood there deciding how large it would be. Kyle used his foot to mark a place in the earth. A wall would run from one tree to another, then turn towards the back. The opening would face Pickle’s house. I could see the roof would have to go around a trunk if we made it as wide as Kyle wanted. We had no boards there yet. We stepped over the place where the wall was supposed to be and used it as an entrance anyway.

On Saturday I went behind the hardware store for scrap lumber. There were short ends and longer boards that had been put aside. I chose pieces I could move. Some had nails still in them, bent flat against the wood or standing out where I could see them. I turned those sides away from my legs. A long board was manageable if I held one end under my arm and dragged the other. Carrying two at once made them cross and knock against my ankle.

The ends rasped along the ground behind me. Where the route narrowed, I had to stop and swing the board around before going on. I took the pieces to the bush behind Pickle’s house and laid them near the trees Kyle had chosen. Then I went back for more. The shorter pieces were easier to carry, but they did not cover much space when we leaned them up. A board that looked substantial behind the store could leave a wide gap on either side once it was standing in the bush.

Kyle directed where the longer ones should go. Marcus held an end while a board was shifted, and Pickle moved around inside the marked space. I brought the scrap to the pile and tried a piece against the trees. It slid down when I let go. Another caught on a branch and stayed at an angle. We leaned more beside it. By the time I had finished dragging boards that Saturday, the ground at Kyle’s spot was scuffed bare in places. My hollow by the river still had its flat rock floor, but I did not take any of the lumber there.

Denis spread his maps of the sea on the living-room floor. He opened them carefully enough to keep the folds from turning underneath, then set his hands down on either side of the part he wanted to look at. I had to go around him to cross the room. He liked boats and the lines showing where they could go. He traced routes with a finger, moving it over water that occupied most of the paper. The names were set far apart in some places and crowded together near the land.

He told me the ocean was ten miles deep in the middle. He said it while looking down at the map, as if the number was printed under his hand. I asked which ocean. He said the ocean. I stayed beside the map and looked for a depth marked in miles. There were figures on it, but I could not make them into ten miles. Denis moved his hand to another part and went on with what he had been saying about the sea.

I got my scribbler from the closet shelf. I found a clean space beyond the lists and wrote down the number he had given me. Ten miles was more distance than I could set out conveniently on the floor or the street. I could count ten one-mile lengths, but I had no way to stack them downwards. I left room underneath the figure. The page had the same blue rules as the pages of screws and cards, and I kept the writing between them.

At the library I asked the librarian for the encyclopaedia volume on oceans. I needed help getting the right volume, but once it was open I followed the headings and figures myself. The book was thick and would not stay open at the place I wanted until I pressed down near the middle. I looked through the parts about depth. There were measurements for different places, so I had to keep checking which ocean each figure belonged to before deciding what to copy.

I found the deepest figure for the Atlantic. It came to a little over five miles, about half of what Denis had said. I copied it into the notebook beneath his number and checked it against the book. Then I drew a line under it. I did not have my wooden ruler with me, so the line went up slightly at one end. I made it darker on a second pass, which thickened it without making it any straighter.

When I brought the scribbler home, Denis’s maps were still his to open and fold. I had the depth written down where I could find it. I turned back through the other pages before putting the notebook away. There were counts that had been rubbed out and replaced, and spaces still waiting for hockey card numbers. On the ocean page I left both figures visible. The one from the encyclopaedia had the line under it. I closed the cover and returned the scribbler to the shelf, with the pencil inside it.

In grade 3 I passed St. Anne on my way home from school. Mémère’s hip was bad that week. I had seen how she moved when she got up, setting a hand down first and taking her time before the first step. She still went to early Mass. One afternoon I went into the church by myself instead of continuing along the street. The outer door was heavy enough that I had to put my weight against it, and it closed behind me before I had gone much farther.

Inside, the air was cooler than it had been outside my coat. I could hear my shoes on the floor. I went to the candles and stood close enough to see which ones had been lit. Some were low in their holders and some had most of their length left. There was a box for the money. I had a penny ready in my hand and pushed it through the slot. It struck something below with a small click.

I lit a candle for Mémère’s bad hip. I held the light to the wick until it caught, then waited to see that it stayed burning. A little wax softened around it. I kept my school things close to my legs so they would not touch the stand. Once the flame was steady, I moved away and went to a pew. There was room to kneel without having to move past anybody. I put my things down beside me before lowering the kneeler.

I said the prayer Mémère had taught me. I knew the words in their order and went through them from beginning to end. My hands rested together in front of me. The edge of the pew was smooth where my fingers lay. When I had finished, I stayed on my knees long enough to put my school things together, then stood up. I went back along the aisle and pushed through the doors to the street.

I did the same thing the next afternoon. I stopped on the way home, put a penny in the box and lit a candle. I used the same place to kneel. On that visit there was more wax beneath one of the candles, hardened in a ridge along its holder. I looked at it while making sure my candle had caught. Then I said the prayer and went home. The stop fitted between leaving school and coming through our own door.

I went every afternoon that week. Each time I took a penny, and each time I lit a candle for her hip. I did not bring Mémère with me. I went in with my school things, knelt by myself and walked the remaining streets afterwards. At home I put my things away as usual. The pennies came from the coffee tin. I could hear the other coins move when I tilted it to find one, then set the tin back with its lid pressed down all the way around.

On hot-dog day at school I brought three quarters from that tin. They were loose in my pocket, separate from anything else. I had taken them out before leaving home and checked the amount in my hand. One quarter bought a hot dog. I put all three back in my pocket and carried them to school. When I sat down, I could feel them against my leg through the cloth. They had weight enough that I knew they were still there without looking.

Pickle had forgotten his quarter. He told me at the table and asked if I had one. I had my hand near my pocket, but I did not put it in. I said I had none. He was close enough that I could hear him without leaning towards him. There was no need for him to repeat the question. I left my hand on the table and looked towards the place where the hot dogs were being handed out.

When it was my turn, I took one quarter from my pocket. I kept the other two behind my fingers and dropped them back before bringing my hand out. I paid and got my hot dog. The bun was soft where it had been warmed, and the sausage was hotter than the bread. I carried it level so it would not roll out. There was room at the other end of the table, away from where Pickle had asked me for the money.

I sat there and began eating. The first bite left the end of the sausage sticking out farther than before. I pushed it back into the bun with a finger. A few crumbs fell on the tabletop. I gathered them with the side of my thumb and ate them with another bite. The two quarters stayed in my pocket. I did not put them on the table or take them out to count them while I was sitting there.

From that end I could see along the table, but I kept my food in front of me. I ate down to the last piece of bun, folded the paper that had been under it and pressed the fold flat. Pickle remained farther along. I did not move back beside him after finishing. I had bought what I had come for, and there was nothing left on the paper except a damp mark and a few crumbs too small to pick up cleanly.

When I stood, the quarters slipped to the bottom corner of my pocket. I put my hand in then and held them together between my thumb and finger. Their ridged edges fitted one against the other. I kept them in my hand inside the pocket as I went away from the table. At home they could go back into the tin. There would be fifty cents left from the seventy-five I had taken out that morning.

In April 1979 the river ice broke below the trestle. It did not all move away at once. Plates came up against the shore and jammed there, some flat and some tilted over the edges of others. Open water showed between them. Along the bank, the snow had gone soft and dirty where the ground was exposed. Kyle, Pickle and I went down to the river. Marcus was not with us. We stood above the place where the plates had collected and looked for a way onto them.

There was a broad piece nearest the bank with one end resting against the shore. I stepped onto it and put my other foot down after the first. Water came up along an edge where it settled. Kyle went onto another piece, and Pickle came out from the bank. The plates were rough underfoot, with broken rims and patches that had a wet surface. We moved around the edges of those patches and stepped where there was room for a whole boot.

Below the trestle the current kept working at the jam. A narrow opening would close, then another would appear beside it. When one plate moved, it pushed against the next with a short grinding sound. We had to look again at the gaps before stepping back over them. I went past the place where Kyle had stopped. Pickle was nearer the shore. I crossed to a plate beyond them, landing with my feet apart and then bringing the trailing foot forward.

The farthest plate lay clear enough for me to stand on it. A gap separated it from the piece behind. I stepped across and put both boots on the ice. It tipped under me. One side went lower and a thin spread of water ran over the edge. I shifted my feet as it moved, keeping them on the part still above the water. The gap behind me changed width. I turned towards the bank and stepped back across it.

The ice I stepped onto moved as well, but its edge was closer to shore. I went over it and onto the ground. My boot pressed into the soft bank and left a mark filled with water. I took another step up where the dirt held. Kyle and Pickle were still below me, coming back over the nearer pieces. I scraped the wet edge of one sole against the ground, then stood clear of the place where they were getting off.

From the bank I could see the plate I had stood on riding at a different angle. Water showed where its rim had been. A piece nearer the trestle knocked against the jam and shifted the line of ice along the shore. We stayed there a little longer, with our boots on the dirt. Then we went up from the river. There was mud on my soles and on the lower edge of one trouser leg where it had touched the bank.

At the mill’s family picnic in July, I was eight and signed up for every event the organizers called out. The grounds had tables set out and a field with enough room to mark the races. My parents had a place at a table. I went between them and the field, watching for the next group to be called. The grass was dry on top, though it left damp marks on the knees of boys who knelt in it. By then the sun had warmed the sacks lying ready for the race.

I got into one and pulled it up around my legs. There was enough material for my hands to grip on both sides. I had to keep my feet together inside it, which made moving to the start a matter of short hops. We lined up, some with the sacks held at the waist and some higher. When we were sent off, I pulled upward and jumped. The bottom dragged at my shoes whenever I let my hands drop. I kept going to the end of the marked distance and stepped out when the race was over.

For the three-legged race I went with Marcus. We stood side by side while our inside legs were tied together. The first step turned us towards each other. We put our feet back in line and tried again before the start. Marcus did not say much. I watched his free foot and moved mine when he moved his. Once we started down the field, the tied legs had to come forward together. We lost the spacing, stopped with our shoulders pressed together and got moving again.

After that I stayed near enough to hear what was next. There was no need to go back to the table except when I wanted something. When the tug-of-war was called, I joined the whole grade 3 side. We spread along the rope with spaces between our hands. The rope was thick and rough, and I turned it until I had a place I could hold. I put one foot behind the other. When the pull began, the line straightened between us and I leaned back with the rest.

The grass flattened under our shoes. My hands shifted a little on the rope, and I closed them again farther along. There were boys ahead of me and boys behind, all pulling in the same direction. When it ended, the rope dropped lower between our hands. I let go and rubbed my palms down the sides of my trousers. Then I stayed on the field to find out whether another event was being called. My parents were still at the grounds, and I could see the tables beyond the space used for the races.

When they were ready to leave, I begged to stay. I kept asking while the remaining children gathered on the field. There were fewer of us now, and more empty places at the tables. I went back out with the last bunch of kids. The trampled grass showed where the starts had been, and the sacks were no longer laid in their row. I was still there when the men began folding the tables, turning each one onto its side and bringing the legs flat against the underside.

Chapter 3

1980 was the year I turned nine, went into Mrs. Halvorsen's grade 4 and lost my bike for a week. The mill ran three shifts all that year, the same as every year, and when the wind came from the west the smell of it settled on Main Street. Nobody in town mentioned it except people from away.

My birthday fell on a Thursday in March. The snowbanks along Main Street had gone grey and pitted, and the river was still locked solid under the trestle. My mother made a white cake from a box and put nine candles on it, and Denis, who was thirteen and had opinions about everything, said nine was the last birthday that counted for anything until sixteen. Mémère gave me a card with a five-dollar bill folded inside, and it went into the coffee tin before I went to bed. My father gave me nothing that night. He said mine would come on Saturday, after the store closed.

Lacasse Hardware closed at six on Saturdays. My father came home at twenty past, washed his hands at the kitchen sink and took me down to the basement. Our basement was poured concrete, with the oil furnace in the middle, the tank against the north wall, my mother's wringer washer by the drain and a workbench my father almost never used, because he had a better one at the store. Along the east wall there were three pine shelves on steel brackets, and for as long as I could remember they had held paint cans, Mason jars and a box of Denis's old skates. That Saturday the bottom shelf was empty. My father had moved the paint cans to the floor under the stairs and wiped the board down with a rag.

"That one's yours," he said. "Keep it in order or I'll put the paint back."

On the shelf there was a label-maker, the embossing kind with a plastic dial of letters and a trigger you squeezed to punch each one into the tape. It was black and scuffed on the handle. A customer had brought it back in the fall, saying it jammed, and my father had taken it apart on the counter between customers, found a bent spring, straightened it with a pair of needle-nose pliers and put it under the counter to sell at half price. Nobody had bought it. Beside it were two rolls of red tape and a carton from the back room of the store holding about thirty baby-food jars, washed, with the lids in a paper bag. He had been saving them since summer for selling small lots of tacks and brads, and he had decided I could have them.

I spent the Sunday afternoon on it. I had a lot of loose hardware by then. Some came from the floor of the store, where things rolled under the bins, and my father let me keep anything I swept up as long as I swept up all of it. Some came from Mémère's kitchen drawer, and some from the side of the road, where I picked up anything bright. I sorted it on newspaper on the basement floor: common nails by length, one inch, inch and a half, two, two and a half, three; finishing nails; roofing nails with the big flat heads; flat washers; lock washers; hex nuts in quarter-inch, five-sixteenths and three-eighths; wing nuts; a few cotter pins. Each pile went into a jar and each jar got a label.

The label-maker took practice. You turned the dial to the letter, squeezed hard, turned it to the next one and squeezed again, and if you did not squeeze all the way the letter came out half white and half red. My first label said WASHRS. I peeled it off and threw it in the furnace box and made another. By supper I had sixteen jars labelled, with the tape trimmed square at the ends with my mother's sewing scissors, which I was not supposed to use. I lined them along the shelf in order of size, smallest at the left, with my shoebox of screws at the right end, the way a man finishes a sentence with a period. I had been keeping screws in that shoebox since I was six, in rolled-up paper tubes, each tube marked in pencil.

My father came down after supper and looked at it with his hands in his back pockets. He picked up the jar of inch-and-a-half nails, shook it, held it to the bulb and put it back exactly where it had been.

"You'd make a better job of my back room than your brother," he said, which was not saying much, and went back up the stairs.

The encyclopaedia lived on the bottom two shelves of the bookcase in the front room. My parents had bought it from a salesman the year Denis started school, at so much a month, and my father had kept the payment book in the drawer of the hall table until it was paid off. There were twenty-two volumes in dark red, with the letters in gold on the spines. Denis used the S volume for ships and the O volume for oceans, and he had cracked the spine on both. Nobody had opened the E volume much. I took it down at the end of March because Mrs. Halvorsen's grade 3 teacher, the year before, had told us electricity was the movement of tiny particles nobody could see, and I had thought about that on and off all winter.

The article on electricity ran to eleven pages, with diagrams. There was a drawing of a dry cell cut in half, showing the carbon rod down the middle and the zinc can around it, and there were drawings of circuits made of straight lines with little zigzags for resistors and little circles with crosses in them for lamps. I read it at the kitchen table after school until my mother needed the table for supper, and then I took it down to the basement.

On the Saturday I asked my father for a flashlight battery, two flashlight bulbs and some wire. He gave me a D cell from the open box on the counter, two bulbs from the card by the till and about six feet of bell wire off the spool, and he wrote it all on the tab he kept for the house, which was how he kept track of anything that left the store without money changing hands. I did not have bulb holders. I stripped the ends of the wire with my thumbnail and a paring knife, wrapped them tight around the threads of each bulb and held the other ends to the battery with electrician's tape.

One bulb by itself lit, orange and not very bright, but lit. Then I wired the two bulbs one after the other, battery to bulb to bulb and back to the battery, and both of them went dimmer than the one had been. You could barely see them glow with the basement light on. I turned off the light at the string and lay on my stomach on the concrete and looked at them. Then I took them apart and wired them side by side, each bulb with its own two wires going back to the battery, and both came up as bright as one had been by itself.

I wanted to know why. The battery was the same and the bulbs were the same. The article had the answer in it somewhere, in the part about resistance and voltage, which I read over and over with my finger under the lines. I drew the two circuits in my notebook of lists, on a clean page, then drew them again on the back of a feed-store calendar page from the store, larger, with arrows for which way I thought the current went. My mother called down the stairs every night at half past eight. Denis came down once, looked at the bulbs and said it was obvious. When I asked him why it was obvious he said he had homework, and he went back up.

I kept at it for most of a week, every night after supper, with the encyclopaedia open on the floor beside me and the drawings spread out under the bulb on the drop cord. By the Friday I could draw both circuits from memory and tell you which one would be dim. I could not have told you why to save my life. On the Saturday the snow started going off the roofs and Kyle came by to see if I wanted to go down and look at the ice on the river. I took the wire off the bulbs, wound it around two fingers, put the bulbs and the battery in a baby-food jar, punched a label that said BULBS and put the jar on the shelf next to the wing nuts. The E volume went back in the bookcase.

The library in Brule Falls was a brick building one storey high next to the post office, with six concrete steps up to the door and a bicycle rack beside the steps that held eight bikes if nobody parked crooked. In May I went there on a Tuesday after school to take back two books and get two more. I rode the bike I had then, a red one that had been Denis's until he grew out of it, with a chrome fender over the back wheel and a bell on the handlebar that did not work. I did not have a lock. Nobody I knew had one.

When I came out, two boys from the grade 7 class were standing at the rack. I knew them by sight from the schoolyard. They were the kind who stood by the fence at recess and did not play anything. One of them had my bike by the handlebars and was looking at the chain. He looked up at me on the steps, swung his leg over and sat on the seat, and the other one got up on the handlebars facing forward, and they rode off down toward Main Street, slow, wobbling for the first few yards and then steady. The one on the handlebars had his arms out. They turned left at the corner by the post office and I could not see them anymore.

I stood on the steps with my books under my arm. I did not call out to them and I did not go after them. When they were gone I walked home with the books, the long way, by the church.

We ate supper at half past five, after my father had come home and washed. That night it was pork chops and boiled potatoes. My father always asked each of us something at supper, Denis first and then me, the way he went down an invoice. He asked Denis about a math test. Then he asked me where the bike was, because it was not on the side of the house where I kept it.

"I must have left it somewhere," I said.

"Left it where?"

"I don't know. Somewhere."

My father put his fork down. He said a bike was money, that it had cost forty-two dollars new when he bought it for Denis, and that a person did not leave forty-two dollars somewhere. He told me to go and look for it after school the next day, everywhere I had been, and to look properly. My mother said he could look on the weekend. Denis looked at me across the table the whole time and did not say anything, which was unusual for him.

I said nothing when the older boys took my bike, and that silence became the habit of a lifetime.

I did go out looking the next day, because I had been told to. I went to the school, the rink and the library, and I walked past the rack and did not stop. I knew where it was not. I did not know where it was.

It turned up a week later in the ditch by the mill road, about half a mile past the gate, lying in the long grass with the back wheel in the water. My father saw it from the store truck on the way to a delivery at the mill and stopped and threw it in the back. The back fender was bent in against the tire so the wheel would not turn. He took it to the store, clamped the fender in the big vise on his bench, straightened it with a rubber mallet and a block of wood, and brought it home on the Saturday. It never sat quite true after that and it rubbed on the tire on one side if you went over a bump.

He did not ask how it had got to the mill road. He sold me a chain and a padlock at cost, two dollars and forty cents, which came out of my coffee tin, and he showed me how to put the chain through the frame and the back wheel both, not just the wheel. I used it every time after that.

In July the mill siding had a string of boxcars on it most days. The siding came off the main line west of the trestle and ran along behind the mill on the river side, and the boxcars sat there waiting to be loaded with bales of pulp off the dock, sometimes ten of them, sometimes twenty. There was a chain-link fence along the mill property, but on the road side of the siding there was only a ditch and a line of alders, and anybody could walk up to the cars from there. Everybody knew you were not supposed to.

Kyle, Pickle and I went there on a hot Wednesday in the middle of the month. Marcus was supposed to come and his mother kept him home to cut the grass. Kyle wanted to see if there was anything in the cars. There was nothing in them, because the doors were shut and sealed with wire and lead tags, and we walked along the string on the road side reading the names of railways stencilled on the sides and the numbers under them. There were eleven cars that day, brown and dark red, two of them from the States.

At the end of the string Kyle put his hand on the ladder on the end of the last car and looked up at the roof. The ladder was iron rungs bolted to the end wall, and at the top there was a board walk that ran the length of the roof, on the older cars, a strip of planks with gaps between them. Kyle went up four rungs and came back down and said the rungs were too hot.

I went up the ladder. The rungs were hot, from the sun, and I went up quickly to keep my hands off them. At the top I stepped onto the roof walk. From there I could see over the alders to the road, and over the mill dock on the other side, and the river past it, low and brown that summer. The planks of the roof walk were grey and dry and some of the bolts had worked loose. I walked down the middle of them to the far end of the car, about forty feet.

The next car was coupled on, with its own roof walk starting at its end, and the gap between the end of my car's walk and the start of the next was about four feet, with the couplers and the air hose down below. I stepped back two steps and jumped it and landed on the next walk with both feet and kept going. I walked that car and jumped to the next, and the next, eleven cars, ten gaps. Pickle walked along on the ground below, keeping level with me and calling up the number of each car as I got on it. Kyle stayed by the first ladder and then came along behind Pickle.

At the far end of the string I climbed down the ladder on the end of the last car. My hands were black from the rungs and my running shoes had tar on them from the roof of one of the American cars, which had a patch. Kyle said I was crazy. Pickle said he was going to tell everybody at school in September, and he did, and by then it had grown to fifteen cars and a train moving. I wiped my hands on the grass and we walked back along the mill road to town. I scrubbed the tar off my shoes with lard at the back step before I went in, and my mother did not ask about it.

Grade 4 started the Tuesday after Labour Day. Mrs. Halvorsen's room was on the second floor at the end of the hall, with three tall windows on the schoolyard side and radiators under them that knocked in the mornings once the heat came on in October. My mother worked in the office downstairs at the front, typing and answering the telephone and keeping the late book, and if you were late it was your mother who wrote your name down, which for me meant I was never late. Mrs. Halvorsen was tall, with grey hair cut short and glasses on a cord. She had taught Denis two years running, grade 4 and grade 5, and on the first day she looked at my name on her list and then at me and said she hoped I was quieter than my brother. I was.

She had a pull-down map of Ontario on a roller above the blackboard and another of Canada behind it. In the second week of school she pulled down the Ontario one and put the end of her pointer on the dot for Brule Falls and ran it along the blue line of our river, north and east, all the way to where it came out on the bay.

"From here to salt water," she said, "is three hundred miles."

She wrote 300 MILES on the board under the map and we copied it into our notebooks.

That night after supper I took the atlas off the shelf in the front room. It was a big flat one from the same salesman as the encyclopaedia. The page for northern Ontario had our river on it, drawn with every bend, and a scale bar in the bottom corner in miles and kilometres. I got a ball of string out of the kitchen drawer where my mother kept the string and the elastics, cut a length of about two feet and laid the end on the dot for Brule Falls. Then I laid the string along the river, bend by bend, holding each bend down with a fingertip and moving the next finger along, pressing it into every curve the map showed. Denis came and watched for a while and said I was doing it wrong, that you should use a ruler and go straight. I said the river did not go straight.

When I got to the bay I pinched the string at the mouth of the river, lifted it off and laid it straight along the scale bar, over and over, marking each length with a pencil dot on the edge of an envelope. It came to three hundred and forty miles, near enough. I did it again to be sure and got three hundred and thirty-five. I wrote both numbers in my notebook of lists, on the page where I had copied hers:

Mrs. H. 300. Me 335 to 340 (string).

I did not tell Mrs. Halvorsen. I did not see what it would get me, and she had said three hundred out loud in front of the class, which is a hard thing for anybody to take back. The notebook went back in my drawer.

I asked Father Doucet about serving at the end of September. Nobody had suggested it to me. My mother had not, my father had not, and Mémère, who would have liked it, had never said a word. I had been watching the altar boys at Sunday Mass for most of a year from our pew, which was on the right side about halfway back, and I had decided I could do what they did, because most of it was standing in the right place and handing the right thing to the priest at the right time.

After the ten-thirty Mass on a Sunday I waited at the side door of St. Anne while Father Doucet shook hands on the front steps. When he came back through to the sacristy I asked him whether I could be an altar server. He was a heavy man with a red face and black-rimmed glasses, and he was taking off his vestments as he walked, folding them over his arm. He looked at me for a second and asked how old I was. I told him nine. He said nine was fine, that he had a card of the responses, and that if I could say them all to him without looking by next Sunday he would put me on.

The card was a piece of stiff paper about the size of a recipe card, typed on both sides, with the priest's parts on the left and the server's on the right, in French on one side and English on the other, because St. Anne said the early Masses in French and the ten-thirty in English. I learned the French side first, because that was what I would need for the weekday Mass. I learned it at the kitchen table after school, covering the right-hand column with a ruler and sliding the ruler down one line at a time. My mother heard me through it twice. By the Thursday I could do it without the card. On Sunday I said it all to Father Doucet in the sacristy, both sides, and he nodded and showed me where the cassocks hung, black, in sizes, with the white surplices on hooks above them.

He put me on for weekday Mass at seven, with an older boy to show me the first week. I picked the mornings myself. Mémère went to the seven o'clock most days of the week but not all of them, because on Tuesdays she went over to help at the parish hall and on Fridays she did her shopping early, and I told Father Doucet I could do Monday, Wednesday and Thursday. He wrote it in a book.

Those mornings my mother woke me at twenty past six. It was still dark by October. I walked over to St. Anne in the cold with my hands in my jacket and went in by the side door, put on the cassock and the surplice and lit the two candles on the altar with the long brass taper, the way the older boy had shown me. Mémère was always already there when I came out, in the third pew on the left, with her black coat buttoned to the neck and her beads in her hand. There were never more than a dozen people at the seven o'clock, most of them old women, and two or three men on their way to the day shift at the mill.

I knelt where I was supposed to kneel, said the responses, moved the book from one side of the altar to the other, poured water over Father Doucet's fingers at the lavabo and held the towel. Mass took twenty-five minutes. Father Doucet did not waste time on a weekday. After it, I hung up the cassock and Mémère was waiting on the front steps, and she walked with me as far as the corner of Main Street, where I went one way to school and she went the other way home. Some mornings she gave me a quarter, and the quarters went into the tin.

The paper route came up at about the same time. A boy who had done it two years was moving with his family to Timmins, and the man who dropped the bundles for the paper put a card up in my father's store window. My father said I should go after it. I did, and I got it, because nobody else had asked yet.

It was forty houses around Main Street and the streets either side of it, a tight route, maybe a mile and a half if you walked it all. The paper came out afternoons, Monday to Saturday. The bundle was dropped at the corner by the store at a quarter to four, tied with wire, and I cut the wire with a pair of side cutters from my shelf, loaded the papers into a canvas bag with a strap that went across my chest and walked the route. On weekdays the bag was light, twenty minutes' carrying. On Saturdays the paper had the weekend section and the flyers from the grocery stores and the car dealer in it, and the bag weighed close to twenty pounds, and the strap cut into my shoulder.

I collected on Friday evenings, door to door, with a ring of paper tickets and a change purse. The paper cost a certain amount a week and I kept a certain part of it, and after the man from the paper took his share I cleared about three dollars and sixty cents a week, more if somebody tipped at collection, which a few of them did. I wrote every Friday's take in my notebook of lists and put the money in the tin, except for one dollar, which my father said I could spend.

In September and October the route was fine. The leaves came down and I kicked through them, and on dry days I could do it in under twenty minutes and be home before Denis.

On Halloween my mother wanted Denis to take me out. She said so at supper the night before, in the way she said things that were already decided. Denis was thirteen and said he was going out with his own friends and did not want to be dragging his little brother around. My mother said he would. I said I would not go with him.

"You're not going out on your own," my mother said.

"I'm not going on my own," I said.

The next night, as soon as it was dark enough, I put on my costume, which was my father's old green work coat, rolled up at the sleeves, with a pillowcase and a charcoal beard my mother drew on me with a burnt cork, and I went out the front door ahead of Denis. It was cold, a few degrees below freezing, with a wind off the river and flurries that did not stick. I went to Kyle's house first, on our street, and knocked, and Kyle came out with a pillowcase and a rubber mask. Then I went to Marcus's, on the next street over, and his mother let him come because I was standing on the step. Then Pickle's, behind the rink. Pickle had two younger cousins staying and they came too. Then I went to two more houses on Pickle's street where I knew there were boys about our age, and knocked, and asked if they were going out, and they came.

By the time we got back to Main Street there were eleven of us, counting the cousins, all between eight and ten. We were the biggest pack out in town that night. Other kids crossed the street to look at us. We went down Main Street and up every street off it, working one side and then back down the other, the way I did the paper route, because it was the fastest way to cover the houses. Kyle wanted to lead and I let him. He walked at the front with the mask on and knocked on the doors. I walked in the middle of the crowd all evening, with boys on every side, and when the doors opened I held out my pillowcase with everybody else's.

We passed Denis and his friends on the corner by the post office. Denis looked at the size of us and looked at me in the middle of it and said nothing. When I got home at half past eight my pillowcase was close to a third full.
