I sorted it on the kitchen floor into chocolate bars, hard candy, apples and things I did not want, and I traded the things I did not want to Denis the next day for two of his chocolate bars.

The first real snow came in the second week of November, and after that it did not let up. By the start of December the route was a different job. The Saturday bundles were the heaviest of the year, with the Christmas flyers in them, thick as a catalogue, and I could not lift the full bag to my shoulder from the ground. I had to set it on the bench outside the store and get under the strap. Half the houses on the route were old people or men on shift, and their walks were not shovelled. I waded up to their doors in snow over the tops of my boots and came back out with snow packed down inside my socks. Two of the porches had no rail and the steps were glazed with ice under the snow.

The first week of December the cold came down hard. It was thirty below on the Tuesday morning by the thermometer outside our kitchen window, and not much better by four o'clock. The bundle was frozen to the sidewalk where the man had dropped it, and the wire was too cold to hold with my bare hand while I cut it. I did the route that Tuesday and every day that week, because the papers were paid for. On the Saturday the bag was so heavy it pulled me sideways on the ice and I fell on the step of the fourth house and the papers went into the snow, and I picked them up and shook them off and delivered them wet.

That Saturday night I told my father I was giving up the route. He asked why. I said it was too heavy and the walks were not shovelled. He said that was what a paper route was in winter. I said I knew that now. He did not argue, which was not like him, and he only said I had to tell the man from the paper myself and not leave him short.

On the Monday I waited at the corner for the bundle and told the man from the paper when he dropped it. I said I would do the rest of the week. He said a boy who lived behind the rink had asked about it in the fall and he would give it to him, and he did. The other boy started on the Monday after, and I gave him my canvas bag, because it belonged to the paper, and my list of houses with the ones that tipped marked on it in pencil, because he asked for it. Over the whole route I had cleared a little over forty dollars, and that was in the tin.

The second week of December there was a big snowfall, about a foot overnight, the kind that brought the town plough out twice before morning. The Oakes asked me on the Saturday if I would shovel their walk, because Marcus was down with a cold and his father was on days. It was a short walk, from the front step to the sidewalk and around the side to the back door, and it took me about forty minutes with their big aluminum shovel. When I finished I knocked and they gave me two dollar bills, which was the going rate for a walk that size and maybe a little more.

That was the same week the note came home about the class gift for Mrs. Halvorsen. It was a sheet run off on the spirit duplicator in the office, which meant my mother had run it off herself, in purple ink that smelled of the fluid. It said the class was collecting for a Christmas present for Mrs. Halvorsen, that there would be a jar on the windowsill in the classroom until the Friday before the holidays, and that each child should bring what he or she could. There was no amount on it. My mother read it at the kitchen table and put it on the fridge under a magnet and said to me that I had money of my own, and that was all she said about it.

The jar was a big pickle jar with the label soaked off and a slot cut in the lid with a knife, sitting on the windowsill behind Mrs. Halvorsen's desk where she could not see it if she was sitting down. Two of the girls in the front row had made a sign for it out of construction paper. Every morning that week people went up to it before the bell and put money in. Most of it was dimes and quarters. Kyle put in a dollar on the Wednesday, folded small, and made sure people saw it go in. Marcus put in fifty cents when he came back from his cold. One of the girls put in a two-dollar bill and somebody said so out loud. By the Thursday the bottom of the jar was covered.

On the Thursday I had the two dollars from the Oakes in my jacket pocket all day. I had put them there that morning. I carried them up the stairs to Mrs. Halvorsen's room at a quarter to nine and hung up my jacket in the cloakroom with the money in it, and at recess and at lunch and at three o'clock it was still in the pocket. I did not go up to the windowsill. After school I walked home the usual way, past the library and the church, with my hand in the pocket. At home I went straight down to the basement and took the coffee tin down off the shelf where it sat beside the shoebox of screws. I took the lid off and unfolded the two bills and flattened them on my knee. I put the whole two dollars in the coffee tin rather than chip in for the class gift, which tells you most of what you need to know about me at nine. Then I put the lid back on, pressed it down all the way round with the heel of my hand, and set the tin back in its place on the shelf.

I brought nothing to the jar on the Friday. Nobody asked me. Nobody was keeping a list.

On the last afternoon before the holidays the two girls from the front row went up and gave Mrs. Halvorsen the present, which was a wool scarf in a box from the dry-goods store on Main Street and a card signed by the class. I had signed the card on the Wednesday when it went round, the same as everybody, in pencil, between Kyle and one of the cousins' names. Mrs. Halvorsen unwrapped the box at her desk, put the scarf on over her cardigan and thanked us all, and read out every name on the card, mine with the rest. Then she let us go twenty minutes early.

That night I counted the tin on the basement floor, the way I did at the end of every month, coins in stacks by kind and bills flattened under the jar of three-inch nails. It came to sixty-one dollars and thirty-five cents. I wrote the number in my notebook of lists under the November total, put the money back in the tin and put the tin back on the shelf at the end of the row, beside the shoebox of screws.

Chapter 4

In 1981 I turned ten, finished grade 4, and went back into Mrs. Halvorsen's room for grade 5. We lived in Brule Falls, where the mill stood by the river and my father ran Lacasse Hardware on Main Street. My mother, Hélène, worked in the elementary school office. Denis was fourteen that year. He took up more room at the table than he needed, with his elbows out and a map of the sea spread beside his plate. I kept my things downstairs, on a shelf above the concrete floor, where nobody needed to move them to set out supper.

My notebook of lists filled a second scribbler that year. The first had gone soft along the spine. On some pages the pencil had rubbed onto the facing page, so a total appeared twice, once properly and once backwards and faint. I kept it underneath the new one. There were figures in it that I no longer used, but I did not throw it out. I had numbered the pages, and the first page of the second scribbler carried the next number. I wrote that number small in the upper corner before putting anything else on the sheet.

Every jar on the basement shelf had an entry. I counted the screws by size, with separate lines for flat heads and round heads, and kept the smallest ones in the shoebox until there were enough to need a jar. A screw that had lost its point still counted, though I put a pencil mark beside that total. I counted washers, nuts, bolts, and bent nails worth straightening. The shelf had jars of different widths, so the rows in the notebook were more regular than the things they described. I could find a count without having to lift anything down.

The coffee tin had its own pages. I wrote the date, the amount put in or taken out, and what remained. Pennies made the most work. I spread them on the basement floor in stacks of ten, counted the stacks, and counted the loose ones last. A nickel went into a separate row until the pennies were finished. I did not like adding coins while some were still hidden in the bottom of the tin. The lid fitted tightly enough that I used the end of a flat screwdriver under its rim. There was a bright patch where the printed surface had worn away.

Farther back were the things I meant to buy. Beside each item I put the price at the store, copying the dollars and cents separately so I could add down the page. A price without tax was no use for deciding whether I had enough. If I changed my mind about an item, I drew one line through it. I could still read what had been there. Model kits took up several lines. So did parts I had seen in a catalogue, though those needed postage added and could not be carried home the same day.

Every Sunday night I took a ruler and ruled a fresh page. I drew the date column narrow, the description column wide, and left enough space for dollars and cents on the right. Then I copied forward the totals from the week before. I can see now that the lists were how a small boy held the world still. After that I checked the addition with the coins beside me. If the two amounts differed, I counted the tin again before rubbing out a figure. By bedtime there were pale scraps of eraser across the table and a clean total at the foot of the page.

The railway trestle stood over the river beyond the streets, with the rails continuing straight across while the banks fell away beneath them. Kyle Brandt proposed that we walk it. Marcus Oake came along quietly, and Pickle went down the bank far enough to look underneath before climbing back to us. From the approach we could see the gaps between the ties. The river showed in short strips, each one shut off from the next by a timber. There was no board walkway beside the rails, just the ties projecting out past them.

We started by daring one another. Kyle said that walking as far as the first support would not count. Pickle said across meant across, and Marcus stood with his shoes against the ends of two ties. We could make rules for it while all four of us remained on the bank. I looked along the track to the middle of the span. The ties were close enough for a step, though their upper faces were not all level. Some had cracks along the grain. The rail was fastened down with plates and spikes, with loose stones ending where the trestle began.

Kyle hung back at the bank when I put my foot on the first exposed tie. I went ahead of the others. Looking back, I think I was trying to earn a place in that gang by being the one who would go first. I stepped from timber to timber inside the rails, putting a whole sole down where there was room. The ties had grit on them, and I kept off the rounded edges. Behind me Kyle was still talking. His words reached me clearly at first, then mixed with the sound of the river as I moved farther out.

Near the middle I looked down between my feet. The water passed below in a separate strip through each opening. A piece of pale foam would appear in one gap and be gone before I had moved to the next. I stopped where I judged the middle to be. There was no mark for it, and I did not count the ties to establish it. I put one foot across the timber, turned, and brought the other foot round. Then I went back over the same length, using the broad parts of the ties and stepping over the metal plates.

Kyle asked whether I had reached the middle. I said I had. He went out next, and Pickle followed him. From the bank their feet seemed to move in short jerks, one shoe lifted while the other stayed planted. I stood beside Marcus and watched them turn. The river remained visible between the timbers behind them. When Kyle came off the trestle, he scraped the sole of one shoe against a stone. Pickle stepped down beside him. We had used up the dare, and there was nothing to carry away from it. We walked back along the track until the ballast widened towards town.

I kept my hockey cards in an order that matched a list in the scribbler. Doubles had a mark beside them. The best card had a line of its own, without a mark, and I knew where it sat in the stack even when the cards were face down. I handled that one by the edges. The corners were square enough to fit neatly against the others, and there was no crease through the middle. Some of the doubles had softened at the corners from being pushed into pockets. I put those together at the back.

Kyle offered me two of his doubles for that card. He laid them out so both faces showed and tapped each one as he counted. Two cards made a wider display than one. I knew both of them. I also knew, from the list I had made and gone over, that the exchange did not come out in my favour. My best card would leave a space in a line I had nearly filled. His doubles would add cards without filling it. I had enough information to give him an answer before I picked either one up.

I started to tell him. I said that I already had one of them, and my hand stayed on top of my own stack. Kyle kept his two cards laid out. He said I would still be getting two. I looked at the card under my thumb, slid it free, and said, "okay". I handed it over with its face towards him. He lifted his two off the surface and put them in front of me. I squared them against the rest of my cards. The trade was finished as soon as he put mine into his stack.

At home I changed the list. I drew a line through the entry for the card I had given Kyle, then adjusted the marks beside the two I had received. The total number of cards had risen by one. I checked that twice because the figures made the exchange look better than it had been. The cards themselves still fitted in the same place. I pushed the edges against the table to straighten them and put them away. There was no need to change the price list or the coffee-tin total. Only the card page had altered.

For a while I could find the missing card by reaching to the place where it had been. My fingers would lift the card now ahead of that position before I looked at its face. I did not ask Kyle to trade back. When we had the cards out again, his stack included mine, and he handled it as one of his own. I kept the two doubles. Their corners did not improve from being counted, and neither acquired a place in the unfinished line. In the notebook the crossed-out entry remained legible, with the new total written underneath.

The crystal set began with a library book laid open on the basement table. The drawing showed a board, a coil, a detector, an aerial, and an earphone. There was no battery drawn anywhere. I went through the list of parts before starting and wrote down what I lacked. The crystal detector came from a mail-order catalogue. It was a small thing to send away for, with more room given to its description on the catalogue page than it occupied on the board. I kept the wrapper until I had checked it against the drawing.

I used a toilet-paper tube for the coil. The tube was light enough to roll away whenever I set it down, so I held it against the board while working out where the wire would begin. Winding the turns side by side took longer than winding them anyhow. I pushed several together with my thumbnail, then started pulling them close as I went. At one place two turns crossed. I backed the wire off to that point and wound it on again. The book gave a number of turns, and I counted aloud in tens so I would know where I had reached.

The board had room for the tube and the connections, though I had to turn it round several times to compare it with the picture. I scraped the coating off the wire ends where the connections went. The copper underneath looked different from the winding. I bent the bare ends under the screw heads and tightened them with a small screwdriver. A loose strand could sit beside a terminal and appear to touch it. I checked each one with the point of the screwdriver, then ran my finger along the wire to see whether it moved.

The aerial was a long wire strung to the clothesline. I had to bring its end back to the set without pulling the board towards the window. The book used the word aerial, and I wrote that on my parts list instead of long wire. Downstairs the lead made an awkward turn before reaching the terminal. I left some slack there. With the earphone connected, the set occupied more room than the board itself, because the wire kept its own shape and the earphone lead would catch underneath if I turned the board carelessly.

I put the earphone to my ear and worked at the detector as the book showed. There were small scraping sounds and stretches when I heard nothing. I moved the contact by less than the width of its point. Then a man's voice came through, reading the hockey scores. It was thin in the earphone, but the words were separate and ordinary. I held the detector where it was. The man went on to another score while I looked at the board. The coil sat on its cardboard tube. The screws stayed in their holes. There was still no battery anywhere.

Afterwards I read the chapter three times to understand where the power came from. I went through the explanation with my finger under the lines, then went back to the drawing. The aerial picked up energy from the radio signal. The coil was part of the tuning circuit, and the detector let the earphone respond to the part carrying the voice. The power at the set was small, which was why I needed the earphone held against my ear. I looked underneath the board as well, although I had put every piece there myself.

I explained it to Denis whether he wanted to hear it or not. He had a map out, and I put the board at the edge of it, leaving the aerial lead running away behind me. I told him the man at the other end did not have to know I was listening. Denis said he understood that much. I started again with the wire outside and the energy it picked up. He moved the map clear of a screw head underneath the board. When I offered him the earphone, he took it for a moment, then handed it back before I had finished explaining the detector.

At Lacasse Hardware I could hear most of what happened at the counter from farther back in the store. My father spoke briskly, with time for a few extra sentences if the subject suited him. Nails went onto the scale and pennies came out of the till. He could carry on talking while counting change, stopping only to say the amount. The back room held stock that had not reached the shelves and tools too large to leave where they would be in the way. The big bolt cutters were there, with long handles and jaws that closed squarely when both handles came together.

A travelling salesman brought in a new padlock. He set the sample on the counter and talked to my father about it. The body of the lock sat flat, with the bow standing up out of it. I was close enough to hear him say that it could not be cut with bolt cutters. He did not say small cutters or worn cutters. He said bolt cutters. My father turned the sample in his hand, looked at it, and put it down. The salesman continued talking about the lock while my father stood on the other side of the counter.

I took the sample into the back room. The big cutters were heavy at the joint, and I laid them down before opening the handles far enough to get the metal between the jaws. I fitted the bow of the lock into the cutting edges. With the tool lying flat, one handle could bear against the floor while I pressed the other down. The lock shifted the first time, so I opened the jaws and set it deeper. Then I pushed the handle again. The jaws closed through it. The cut ends were bright, and the sample no longer held together as it had at the counter.

I gathered the two halves and put the cutters back. From the front I could still hear the salesman. I did not carry the pieces out while he was there. I stayed in the back room, turning one half over to look at the place where the jaws had gone through. The cut was not smooth like a saw cut. The metal had been squeezed on either side before parting. There was a small difference between the two ends, depending on how the piece had rested in the jaws. I matched them together and took them apart again.

After the salesman had gone, I put the two halves on my father's desk. He looked down at them and then at me. I said I had used the big cutters. He picked up the part with the bow and turned it so the cut faced the light. I told him what the salesman had said. My father had heard it too. He set the piece beside the other half, leaving a space between them, and asked whether I had put the cutters away. I said I had. The lock stayed on his desk while he went back to the counter, and I returned to the back room.

Once grade 5 started, Friday night meant the public skate. The whole of grade 5 was there, which was enough reason to go. Mrs. Halvorsen's room emptied at the end of the school day, and several hours later the same people occupied the rink in coats and skates. I carried mine by the laces. At the bench I loosened them well down before pushing my feet in, pulled the tongues straight, and worked the laces tight from the bottom. If I rushed that part, I had to sit down again after a few circuits and do it properly.

The rubber beside the ice held bits of dirty snow brought in on boots. I stepped over those and went through the opening. The first strokes made a rasping sound until I got moving with the traffic. People spread out along the boards, then bunched near the entrance where someone would stop to look behind. I passed that patch and went round again. Marcus and Pickle were there, and Kyle was there. Coats that had looked bulky in the school hall looked bulkier with arms held out for balance. Breath showed above the collars when we stopped.

I joined every crack-the-whip line. A hand would come out as the line went past, and I took it. The pull changed according to where I fitted in. Near the inside I had to make short steps while the outer end covered more ice. Farther out I could feel the turn coming through the joined arms before my own skates changed direction. Gloves slipped against gloves. When a grip broke, the loose part of the line carried on across the ice, with everyone spreading out to find room. I circled back to join the next one.

By the later part of the evening the ice had a white covering of shavings near the turns. My blades made a different sound crossing it. I stayed until the lights went off. There was always time spent getting out after that: finding the right place on the bench, undoing tight knots with cold fingers, and pulling boots over socks that had flattened inside the skates. I knocked snow off the blades before carrying them. The rink air followed us through the doors, then the outside cold reached through the damp places in my clothes.

I walked home in a bunch with Marcus and Pickle and anyone else heading my way. Our skates hung beside our legs, and the blades knocked together if we let the laces swing. The bunch narrowed where the cleared part of the pavement narrowed. At each turning there were fewer of us. We talked about who had let go in a line and who had gone furthest when it broke. By the time I reached the house, the heat from skating had gone out of my coat. I left the skates where their blades could dry and went upstairs.

Every night I knelt beside my bed with the rosary Mémère had given me. She was my father's mother and lived two streets over. The beads fitted into one hand with some left hanging below it. I started at the cross and moved along them in order, using my thumb to hold my place. The carpet pressed a pattern into my knees. If I shifted closer to the bed, I could rest my forearms against the mattress, though that put the hanging part of the rosary against the blanket where it was harder to separate the beads by touch.

I said the prayers each night. The rosary had a definite order, and I followed it from the beginning. Denis could be moving about elsewhere in the house while I knelt there. Downstairs a cupboard would shut, or my father would put something on the table. I did not need the house quiet to keep my place. When I reached the end, I gathered the beads before standing up so the cross would not catch against the bed frame. I put the rosary where I could reach it the following night and got into bed.

On Sundays after late stock-taking, my father slept in. The work at the store had kept him there past the usual time, counting what was on the shelves and checking it against what ought to be there. In the morning I got myself up. I put on my clothes, fastened the buttons, and found both shoes before going downstairs. My mother did not have to take me to St. Anne. I went out by myself and walked the streets between our house and the church. Mémère went to early Mass. Her house was close enough that I knew the route she took.

In cold weather the church door was stiff to pull, and I used my weight on it. Inside, I removed my hat and went to a pew. The floor carried the damp brought in from outside. I kept my shoes under the bench when I knelt, with the toes against the floor and my knees on the padded board. Father Doucet stood at the front. I followed the service, sitting, standing, and kneeling with the others. When I lost the place on the page, I found it from the words being spoken and held it with a finger.

After Mass I went out through the same doors and walked home. Snow that had been packed hard on the way there could have loose grit worked into it by then. I stepped round the deeper patches by the kerb. At the house I took off my shoes and put them together. My father's stock-taking papers could still be out, with figures carried into columns and a pencil laid across them. I had my own totals to copy that evening. The rosary would be beside my bed when I went upstairs, its cross lying against the gathered beads.

A family on the river road lost their house to a fire. At school a box appeared in the front hall for money, and clothes were collected as well. Mrs. Halvorsen told us what the collection was for. The house and what had been inside it were gone. My mother worked in the office near the hall, and I passed the collection on my way in and out. Folded clothes occupied more space than the money box. I could see sleeves tucked into the folds and trouser legs doubled over so they would fit in the pile.

I had my tin money with me for a model kit. I had counted out the amount at home, checked the price in my notebook, and taken enough for the purchase. The coins made a lump in my pocket. Sitting at my desk, I could feel them when my leg pressed against the edge of the seat. The coffee tin itself was back on the basement shelf. Its total would need changing that night, after I had the kit and knew exactly what I had spent. I had already left room for the entry.

At the end of school I passed the box in the front hall. I put my hand in my pocket and kept the coins there. The opening in the box was wide enough for them. Nothing prevented me from taking some out. I went past the clothes and through the doors with the money still against my palm. Outside, I let the coins fall back into the bottom of my pocket. They struck one another as I walked. I took the route towards Main Street, where I could buy the kit before going home.

I paid for the model kit on the way home. The box was light compared with the coins I had brought to pay for it. I carried it flat so the contents would stay where they were packed, though I could hear a loose piece shift when I changed hands. At home I took off the lid and checked what was inside against the sheet. Some pieces were joined to runners and others had their own place in the box. I spread the sheet beside them and read the first steps without separating anything yet.

That evening I entered the purchase in the notebook. I wrote the date, the amount spent, and the balance left in the tin. Then I counted the remaining coins to check it. The arithmetic worked. I put the kit box on the shelf and laid the sheet inside it before replacing the lid. At school the collection box remained in the front hall. I passed it again with no model-kit money in my pocket. Clothes were still being collected, and the space around the box had to be left clear for people coming through the doors.

Pickle and I started a soapbox cart for the winter carnival race. We worked in his garage, with the box on the floor and the axle laid across two supports while we measured where it should sit. The floor was cold enough to come through my trouser knees. We kept standing up to move things and kneeling again to look underneath. Pickle held one end while I moved the other. With the box upright, the axle looked close to square. Turned over, it showed a difference between the distances from the corners.

I measured from one edge of the box, marked the position, and tried again from the other edge. The marks did not give me what I wanted. A little movement at one end changed the distance at the other, and tightening it pulled it off the line I had drawn. I loosened it and put a thin piece underneath. That corrected one part and left the wheel at a different angle. Pickle asked me to hold it where it was while he looked. I held it, then let it down when he came back round.

The tools lay within reach on the concrete, though the one I needed always seemed to be underneath something we had moved. I used the tape again and read the figures from the end nearest me. The axle still did not sit square under the box. We could roll the cart a short way, but that did not settle the measurements. I turned it over once more. The pencil lines had become a band of grey marks on the wood. I rubbed at one with my thumb and could still see several possible places where the axle might go.

I left the garage with it unfinished. The next time there was a chance to work on it, I did not go to Pickle's. I stayed home, where the kit box and the jars were on my shelf. Another day passed without my going back. I had not taken any tools there that needed collecting, and nothing from the cart was at our house. Pickle continued with it. When I saw him, there was no new measurement for me to make and no end of the axle put into my hands. He had the cart in his garage and carried on from where we had stopped.

Pickle finished it and raced it alone at the winter carnival. By then the axle was fixed beneath the box, and the cart could be brought out as a complete thing. I watched him with it. There was no place beside him that I had kept by starting the work. The box, wheels, and axle went to the race together, with the fastening I had left unresolved dealt with somewhere in his garage after I stopped coming. Pickle took it to the start himself. When it was his turn, he got into the cart and went down the course.

Chapter 5

The summer of 1982 I was eleven, with grade 6 ahead of me in September, and I was out of the house by nine every morning from the first week of July to the end of August. My father opened Lacasse Hardware at eight. My mother went in to the elementary school two mornings a week through the summer to do the registrations and the supply order, and on the other mornings she sat at the kitchen table with the household bills and a pencil. Denis slept until ten if nobody stopped him, and nobody did. I ate my cereal, rinsed the bowl because my mother counted bowls in the sink, put a towel and two peanut butter sandwiches in a bread bag in the carrier on the back of my bike, and went out the side door.

I had a route, the same every morning, and I did it in the same order. Kyle Brandt first, because Kyle decided things and there was no point arriving anywhere without him. Then Pickle, who lived on the way back down the hill and was usually still eating when I knocked. Then Marcus Oake, who was always ready and always sitting on his back step when I came round the corner of the lane, as if he had been out there since eight. After Marcus I did the rest of it: two streets of houses where there were boys between nine and thirteen, knocking at the side doors, because nobody in Brule Falls used a front door, and asking whoever answered whether their boy was coming to the beach.

What I wanted was seven or eight. Six would do. If I came out of the last house with only two of us, me and Marcus, say, because Kyle had been taken to Sudbury for school shoes and Pickle had been kept home to cut the grass, then we did not go. We rode round again. I went back to the houses where nobody had been up yet, and then to houses I did not usually try, and stood on the step while somebody's mother went to see. Kyle said once that I was worse than the truant officer. Marcus never said anything about it. He rode along beside me and waited at the end of each driveway with one foot on the ground.

I could not have told anyone why two was not enough. Two boys at the river was two boys at the river. Seven or eight was the beach: a heap of bikes in the ditch grass, towels in a row on the sand, enough for a game of anything, enough that somebody always had a quarter for the canteen at the campground on the way home. We rode out the Concession road in a string, two miles of gravel, with the mill trucks going by and lifting dust over us, and we dropped the bikes at the top of the path and went down through the alders.

The beach was a strip of sand on the inside of a bend above the town, upstream of the mill, so the water was clean enough, though it was brown from the bush and so cold until the end of July that your feet went numb before your knees were wet. There was a rock forty yards out that you could stand on, and we swam out to it and back. We skipped stones and kept count, and argued about whether a skip that hit a wave counted. We ate the sandwiches at eleven because nobody could wait for noon, and by two everyone was hungry again and talking about the canteen. At four I started telling people it was time. At half past four we went, because the ride back was mostly uphill and my mother put supper on the table at half past five and did not hold it.

It was on the beach, in the middle of July, lying on the sand with the sun on our backs, that Kyle said his cousin in Kapuskasing made fifty dollars a week on a paper route.

Everybody took that in. Fifty dollars a week was a new bike in two weeks, or close to it. Pickle said he was going to get a route. One of the others said his brother had a route and made way more than that, which nobody believed and nobody bothered to argue with. Kyle said his cousin was saving for a car. I said nothing. I had had the route on our side of town myself, from the fall I was nine until that spring, and I had a fair idea what a route paid.

That night after supper I sat at the kitchen table with my notebook. My route had been fifty-two houses. The paper came six days a week, and the carrier kept four cents a paper; the rest went back to the paper. Fifty-two houses was three hundred and twelve papers a week, and three hundred and twelve papers at four cents was twelve dollars and forty-eight cents. At Christmas you got tips. Some weeks you lost a customer who moved away or stopped taking it, and some weeks a customer would not open the door on collection night and you were out the money anyway, because the paper charged you for every copy you took off the bundle whether you got paid for it or not. Twelve dollars was about right. I had made about twelve dollars.

Then I did it the other way round. To make fifty dollars at four cents a paper, six days a week, you needed two hundred and eight houses. I wrote that underneath. Two hundred and eight houses was four routes, more or less; it was the whole of the upper town. In case the paper in Kapuskasing paid five cents, I did a second line, and that came to a hundred and sixty-seven houses, rounded up, since you cannot deliver to two-thirds of a house. I drew a line under the lot.

My mother looked over my shoulder on her way to the sink and asked what it was. I said it was Kyle's cousin. She read it and said, "Well," and ran the water. The next day at the beach Kyle's cousin came up again, and the car, and I let it go by. I did not show the page to Kyle. I did not tear it out either. I kept the page.

The flashlight was the good one. I had bought it in May from the store with money out of the coffee tin. My father let me have it at cost, four dollars and ten cents, and wrote it in the book under my name the way he wrote down anything that left the shelves, whoever took it. It had a chrome body with ridges for grip, a black rubber ring around the head, and it took two D cells. You turned the head to bring the beam in from wide to narrow, and at narrow it would put a spot on the trestle from our back step. On the side there was a slide switch with off and on and a little button in the middle of it for signalling. It lived on the shelf beside my bed with the batteries out, because batteries left in a flashlight leaked and ate the contacts.

In the middle of August Denis asked to borrow it. He was going night fishing below the trestle for pickerel, and his own flashlight was a plastic one from the grocery that gave about as much light as a match. He asked at supper, with my father there, and I said he could. He took it up to his room and put the batteries in himself.

He came in at two in the morning; I heard the side door and the stairs. When I woke up the flashlight was on my bed, at the foot, on top of the blanket where I would see it. I picked it up and turned it over. The lens had a crack across it from one side of the rim to the other, with a shorter crack running off it at an angle, as if it had come down on a rock. The switch was stuck halfway between off and on. I pushed it with my thumb, and then with the edge of a dime, and it would not go either way. I unscrewed the head and looked at the bulb, which was fine. I took the batteries out and put them in the kitchen drawer where batteries went, and I put the flashlight in the top drawer of my dresser, at the back, under my winter socks.

Denis came down at eleven and ate cereal out of the mixing bowl and did not say anything about it, and I did not say anything about it, then or after. I did not tell my father, who would have made Denis pay for it, at cost or not. It stayed in the drawer under the socks.

The water tower stood on the hill at the top of the town behind the rink, a round tank on four steel legs with a ladder up one of them. The tank was pale green with BRULE FALLS around it in black letters you could read from the highway, and it had a pointed roof with a vent on top. There was a chain-link fence around the bottom with a gate and a padlock on the gate. Everybody in grade 6 knew somebody's brother who had been up it. Denis said he had been up it, which may have been true.

Kyle decided on it on a Thursday near the end of August, the week before school went back. Marcus had to be in by nine, so it was Kyle, Pickle and me. We met behind the rink at half past eight, with the sun just down and the sky still light over the mill, and we sat in the long grass by the fence slapping mosquitoes until it was dark enough that a car on the hill road would not pick out three boys on a ladder.

The fence was about eight feet. It had no barbed wire along the top, only the twisted ends of the chain-link. Kyle went over at the corner post, where you could get a foot on the gate hinge. Pickle went next and caught his shirt on the top and tore it down the back. I went last. On the inside, the ladder came down one leg of the tower and stopped well above the ground, so that you could not just walk up to it. Kyle climbed onto the cross-brace between the legs, stood up on it holding the leg, reached the bottom rung and pulled himself up. Then he reached down for Pickle, and once Pickle was on, Pickle reached down for me.

From there it was the cage. A caged ladder is a ladder inside a tube made of hoops of flat steel every few feet, with straps running up between the hoops to hold them together, so that your back is never more than a couple of feet from steel. We went up in single file, Kyle, then Pickle, then me, with three or four rungs between the bottoms of Pickle's running shoes and my hands. The rungs were round bar, painted, and the paint had come off in flakes where hands had gone, so they were rough under the palm and left rust on it. I counted rungs. I lost the count at about ninety when Pickle stopped and Kyle called something down that I could not make out, and when we started again I went on from ninety, so the number I had at the top, a hundred and thirty-one, was not one I would have put in a notebook. The mosquitoes came up with us for a while and then dropped off.

At the top the ladder came up through a square hole in the catwalk, a grating floor about three feet wide that went right around the tank, with a railing at the outside edge about the height of my chest and a second rail lower down. We reached the top of the water tower just after dark, foolishly, with no rope between us. Kyle was already sitting down when I came up through the hole. Pickle was standing at the rail looking down at the roof of the rink.

We went partway round to the mill side and sat in a row with our backs against the tank and our legs out under the bottom rail and over the edge. The steel of the tank was still warm from the day. The grating pressed squares into the backs of my legs through my shorts, and I could see the ground through it, or where the ground was.

From there you could see the whole of the mill. It was lit all night, every night; the mill did not stop for dark or for Sundays. There were the big lamps on poles over the woodyard and the log piles, and the windows of the machine room in a long lit row, and the two stacks with their red lights at the top, and steam coming off the roofs with the lamps under it, so that it looked solid enough to stand on. A switcher was moving cars in the yard with its headlamp on, and you could hear the couplers bang a second or two after you saw the cars jerk. The smell came up the hill the way it came everywhere in Brule Falls, like cabbage boiled too long, and none of us said anything about it, since none of us had ever lived anywhere it was not.

Kyle tried to find his house and said it was the one with the porch light, which could have been any of forty. Pickle counted the cars going over the highway bridge and got to eleven. I worked out which dark strip was the river by where the lights stopped, and followed it down to the trestle, which you could not see, but you knew the line of it from the gap it made in the lights on the far bank. Kyle spat over the edge. Then Pickle did, and we listened and heard nothing.

We stayed up there about twenty minutes by Kyle's watch, which lit up if you pressed the button on the side. Then Kyle said, "Okay," and we went down. I went first going down, since I had been last coming up, then Pickle, then Kyle. Going down a caged ladder in the dark you feel for each rung with your foot before you let go of the one above. At the bottom I hung from the last rung, found the cross-brace with my feet, and got down the leg to the grass. We went back over the fence at the corner post and down the hill road, and Pickle went off home holding his shirt together at the back, and I came in our side door at a quarter to eleven. My father was at the kitchen table with the store's receipts in piles and a rubber band around each pile, and he asked where I had been, and I said behind the rink, which was so.

In September, when school started, I took over the workbench in the basement.

The bench had been there when my parents bought the house, a heavy thing built of two-by-sixes with a top of two-inch planks, set against the foundation wall under the window well. My father had used it before the store took all his time, and since then it had become a place for putting things down: paint cans, a box of mason jar lids, a carton of extension cords, Denis's hockey bag in the off-season. I asked my father if I could have it and he said, "Clear it off first," which in our house meant yes.

It took a Saturday. I moved the paint cans to the shelf under the stairs, lids up, oldest at the back. I took the hockey bag up and left it outside Denis's door. I scraped the top of the bench with a putty knife and swept it and wiped it down. There was a four-inch vice bolted on the right-hand end with the jaws worn smooth, and I took it apart, cleaned the screw with a rag and kerosene from the can in the garage, put a drop of oil on it and put it back together. It closed easier after that, though the jaws still did not meet square.

The pegboard I bought at the store: a sheet of quarter-inch pegboard that my father cut to two feet by four on the panel saw at the back, two dollars and sixty cents, and a card of hooks for a dollar fifteen. He wrote both in the book. I screwed two strips of one-by-two to the wall with concrete plugs so the board would stand out from the foundation and the hooks would have room behind, and screwed the pegboard to the strips. Then I put up the tools. A sixteen-ounce claw hammer. A flat screwdriver and a Robertson, the square-head kind, a number two. Needle-nose pliers and slip-joint pliers. A hacksaw. Tin snips. A twelve-foot tape. A small square. A tack hammer that had been my grandfather's, which Mémère said I could have since she had no use for it.

When they were all on their hooks I took a pencil and traced around each one on the board, close to the edge, so that when the tool was off its hooks its outline was still there on the board. My father had a board like that in the back of the store over the key-cutting machine, and he said he had seen it done at the mill. The point was that you could stand at the bottom of the basement stairs and see from there what was missing. The first time there was an empty outline it was the claw hammer, and Denis had it. He had used it to knock a nail into the blade of his hockey stick and left it on the garage floor. I got it back and hung it up, and after that I looked at the board every time I came down the stairs.

Above the pegboard I put up a shelf, a length of one-by-eight on two steel brackets, and on it I put the jars. The screws in the shoebox had outgrown the shoebox. I had been sorting them by size since I was eight or so, with dividers I made out of cereal box cardboard, and the dividers had given out. My mother saved jars for me through the fall: peanut butter jars, jam jars, and the small square ones the mustard came in. I washed them and let them dry upside down on the bench.

I labelled each lid with the label-maker from the store, the kind where you turn a wheel to the letter you want and squeeze the handle and it presses the letter white into a strip of red plastic tape with a sticky back. #6 x 1/2 WOOD. #8 x 1 WOOD. #8 x 1-1/2 WOOD. 6-32 MACH. 8-32 MACH. NUTS. WASHERS. FINISH NAILS. SPADE CONN. My father reminded me that the tape was stock and cost money, so I kept the labels short and did not make any mistakes I could help.

I put the jars on the shelf in the order I used them, left to right, the ones I reached for most at the left, nearest the vice. It was not size order and it was not alphabetical. Denis came down, looked at it, and said it made no sense. My father came down that night to change the furnace filter and looked at the shelf for a while. He took one jar down, turned it to read the lid, and put it back where I had it. He said, "That's not how I'd have done it," and then, "It's not wrong, though." The bolt bins at the store were in size order and stayed that way.

In October, in science, our grade 6 teacher said that a battery runs out of electricity. She said it while she was holding up a flashlight battery, and she wrote it on the board under a drawing of a flashlight with arrows in it, and we copied it into our science notebooks.

We had a set of encyclopaedias in the living room, twenty volumes in brown covers with gold on the spines, that my parents had bought on time the year Denis started school. I had read the article on electricity the year before, and that afternoon I went back to it, and to the articles on batteries and on electric current, and read them at the dining room table with the volumes open on top of each other. On the Saturday I went to the town library and took out a book on electric motors, written for boys, with drawings of every part and a chapter on building a motor out of a nail, a cork and a magnet.

In a box in the furnace room there was a toy boat of Denis's with a battery motor that had stopped working two summers before. I took the motor out of it. It was a small can about the size of a thimble with two tabs bent over at one end. I pried the tabs up with the smallest screwdriver on the pegboard, and the end cap came off with the brushes in it, two thin strips of copper that rode on the commutator. The armature slid out of the can. It had three poles of thin iron plates stacked up, and each pole was wound with copper wire finer than a hair, varnished a dark brown. There were two curved magnets glued to the inside of the can. I started counting the turns on one pole and stopped at about forty, since you could not see where one ended and the next began. Nothing was burned and nothing was broken that I could find, except that one brush was bent away from the commutator, so that it would not touch.

What the books said, when you put them together, was that the electricity went round. It came out of one end of the battery, went through the wire and the motor or the bulb, and came back into the other end. The battery did not hold electricity the way a bucket holds water. It pushed. There was something in the chemicals that did the pushing, and when the chemicals were spent the push was gone, and the electricity that had been going round stopped going round. But it had not gone anywhere.

I brought this to supper on the Monday. Denis, who was fifteen and in grade 10 and had done electricity himself, said that was stupid. "It's dead," he said. "The electricity's gone. That's what dead means."

"It went round," I said. "It came back in the other end."

"Then put it back in and it'll work."

"It doesn't get used up. It's the push behind it that gets used up."

My father said to eat. My mother told Denis he did not need to say it so loud for it to be right. Denis said it louder.

On the Tuesday I brought the library book to the table and opened it to the drawing with the arrows going round the circuit, and my mother made me put it on the counter, and I read from it from where I sat, which was worse. On the Wednesday Denis said it was like a garden hose, and the water comes out the end and it's gone. I said the hose was full of water whether the tap was on or not, and when you turned off the tap the water was still in the hose, it just was not going anywhere, and it was the tap that was the battery. Denis said that proved his point. My mother asked whether either of us could pass the beans without making a speech about them. On the Thursday my father, who sold batteries, said all he knew was that people came back to the store wanting new ones, and that suited him fine, whichever of us was right. That was the closest he came to taking a side.

On the Friday Denis said, "Fine, it's the push. Pass the butter." I said that saying fine was not the same as saying it was the push. He said it was the same for him, and he took the butter himself, and that was the end of it at supper. I did not take it to the teacher. The motor sat in a mustard jar on the shelf over the bench with its parts beside it, and I did not put it back together.

In December Marcus found a book at the library on codes and signals, with the Morse alphabet in a chart in the back and a chapter on making your own telegraph out of a buzzer and a key. Marcus lived across the back lane and two houses down, and his bedroom window looked out on the lane. He came over with the book on a Saturday and showed me the chapter, and we decided to do it, one at each end.

The parts came from the store. For each of us a doorbell buzzer, the plain kind in a metal box that you screwed to the wall by a back door, and a six-volt lantern battery. The keys we made on my bench: a strip of springy brass about four inches long, screwed down at one end to a block of pine, bent up so it stood off the block, with a round-head screw set in the block underneath to touch when you pressed it. I made both and Marcus paid me for his block. The wire was bell wire, eighteen-gauge, two conductors in a brown jacket, and to go from my bench to his bedroom and back was about a hundred and forty feet, and we needed two runs, one for each direction, so it was two hundred and eighty feet. My father sold it to us at cost, three cents a foot, eight dollars and forty cents, and Marcus and I split it four-twenty each. My half came out of the tin, and I wrote it in the notebook.

We ran it on a Sunday in the snow. Out through the window well, along the top of our back fence on fence staples, up the clothesline pole, across the lane at a height a truck would clear, to the eave of Marcus's garage, along his fence, and up the outside wall of his house to his window, where his father let him drill a hole through the frame. My key sounded his buzzer and his key sounded mine. At half past seven, which was when we had agreed, I pressed the key three times and his buzzer went three times, and a second later mine went three times back.

The chart went A, dot dash; B, dash dot dot dot; C, dash dot dash dot; and on. Marcus said you were not supposed to learn it off the chart, you were supposed to learn it by the sound, so that when you heard dash dot dash dot you heard C and not two dashes and two dots. I learned it off the chart, a few letters a night, with the chart propped against the vice. I could send. I sent slowly, but what I sent was right, and Marcus wrote it down at his end and sent back R for received.

The receiving was the trouble. Marcus sent, and I sat at the bench with a pencil and a pad, and the letters came faster than I could find them. I started writing down the dots and dashes as they came and working them out after, but by the time I had one letter down there were three more gone by. Marcus got faster every week. By the second week of January he was sending whole words, HI RAY and SCHOOL TMRW and once a sentence about Kyle that I got half of. He would send a word, and I would send back the first letter of it, or nothing, and he would send it again slower, and I would get two letters of it.

I got as far as K on the chart, dash dot dash, the eleventh letter. After that I did not learn any more. One night at the end of January I unhooked my key from the battery and put the key and the chart in the drawer of the bench, under a box of fuses. The wire stayed up on the fence and over the lane. Some nights after that the buzzer went in the basement at half past seven, a few letters and then a gap and a few more, and I could hear it from the kitchen, and I left it.

Lent in 1983 began on the sixteenth of February. On the Sunday before, I wrote in my notebook, at the kitchen table, LENT 1983, and under it two lines: No candy. Stations, Fridays, 7 pm. Nobody told me to. My mother read it upside down from across the table and asked whether it was something from school, or from the church, and I said it was not from anywhere, and she said, "All right then," and did not ask any more. My father said that candy was cheaper not bought than bought, which was true, and went back to the paper.

The candy was the corner store on the way home from school. I had been spending about a quarter a week there, on licorice mostly, and sponge toffee when they had it. Over Lent that money went into the coffee tin instead, and I wrote it in the notebook each Saturday with the rest of the count. By Easter the tin had a dollar seventy-five in it that would have gone to the corner store.

The Stations of the Cross were at St. Anne at seven on Friday evenings through Lent. The first Friday was the eighteenth of February, and it was twenty-four below with a wind off the river. I walked to Mémère's first, two streets over, and she was ready in her coat and boots with her cane and her beads in her pocket, and we walked to the church together. Mémère walked slowly on account of her hip. It took us twenty minutes to do what took me eight on my own. She went up the church steps on the railing one step at a time, and I went up beside her and held the door.

The church was never warm on weekday evenings. The radiators along the walls ticked and you could see your breath for the first while. There were thirty or so people, most of them older women, a few men from the mill still in their work jackets, and nobody else from my class. The Stations were fourteen plaster plaques on the walls between the windows, seven down each side, with a Roman numeral and a small wooden cross over each one. The priest went from one to the next with two altar boys, one carrying a cross on a pole and one with a candle, and at each station he read out which it was, Jesus is condemned to death, Jesus takes up his cross, Jesus falls the first time, and we all said, We adore you, O Christ, and we praise you, because by your holy cross you have redeemed the world, and went down on one knee, and got up. Then he read a page, and we said an Our Father and a Hail Mary and a Glory Be, and he moved on to the next one, and we turned in the pews to face it. Mémère said the responses in French under her breath while everyone else said them in English, and nobody minded. It took about forty minutes for the fourteen. By the end my knee was red from the floor of the pew, since I did not use the kneeler for the genuflections and nobody had said you could.

By the door at the back there was a wooden box on a post for the poor, with a slot in the lid. I had a dime in my coat pocket every Friday, the corner store dime, and I walked past the box with it each week and put it in the coffee tin when I got home.

The third Friday Mémère's hip was bad. I went to her house first and she called to me from her chair in the front room that she was not going, to go on by myself, and I went. The church was the same without her, the same thirty or so, and I knew the responses by then. The fourth Friday her hip was bad again, and I went on my own again. The fifth Friday, the eighteenth of March, she came, and it was mild for once, three above, with water running in the gutters, and she stopped twice on the way to stand and lean on the cane. The sixth Friday she did not come. The seventh was Good Friday, the first of April, raining, and she came, in a plastic rain bonnet, and the church was full that night, people standing at the back by the poor box.

I went seven Fridays out of seven. I wrote a tick in the notebook beside the line each week. On Easter Sunday after Mass Mémère gave Denis and me each a chocolate egg in coloured foil, and I ate mine on the walk home, and on the Monday I went back to the corner store with my quarter.

That spring Denis was sixteen and working Saturdays at the store for my father, three dollars an hour, sweeping out and stocking shelves and carrying paint and bags of lime out to people's cars. He was saving for an outboard motor. There was one advertised on the board at the grocery, a used six-horsepower Johnson, a 1972, for a hundred and twenty-five dollars, and Denis had been to see it twice and had pulled the cord on it and said it started on the third pull. He wanted it by the first of July. He did not have a boat to put it on yet, either, but he said the motor was the expensive part and boats turned up.

By the middle of June he had a hundred and fifteen. He had been through every coat pocket in the house and sold his old hockey skates. He was ten dollars short.

Everybody in the house knew about the coffee tin. It was a Maxwell House tin with a plastic lid, and it lived on the shelf in my closet, and the count of what was in it lived in the notebook, updated every Saturday. That Saturday the count was thirty-four dollars and eighty-five cents.

Denis came into the kitchen on the Sunday night while I was at the table with the notebook. He sat down across from me, which he did not usually do, and said, "Lend me ten."

"No," I said.

"I'll give you twelve back. By August. I'm working every Saturday."

"No."

"You've got thirty-four dollars in there," he said. He knew the number. "It's not doing anything. It's sitting in a can."

"It's doing what it's for."

"What's it for, then?"

"For being in there," I said. "It's for keeping."

He looked at me for a while across the table. Then he said, loud enough for the whole house, that I was the cheapest kid in Brule Falls and maybe in Ontario, and he went out the side door and let it bang. My mother came to the kitchen door and looked at me and then at the side door, and went back to the living room.

Later I heard him in the front room asking my father. My father said that it was my tin, and that it was my money, and that if Denis wanted ten dollars he knew where the broom was. He offered Denis an extra Saturday afternoon in July when the summer stock came in, and Denis took it.

The first of July went by without the motor. Denis worked his Saturdays and the extra afternoon, and he did not ask me again, and he did not talk to me much that month either, except to say pass the salt in a way that made it plain what he thought. He had the ten dollars by the last week of August.

The Saturday after he asked, I counted the tin again, the way I did every Saturday. I tipped it out on my bed, made piles of quarters, dimes, nickels and pennies, counted each pile twice, and put it all back in. Thirty-five dollars and ten cents. I wrote it in the notebook under the week before.

Chapter 6

In 1984 I turned thirteen, and Denis turned seventeen. I was in my last year before high school in Brule Falls. Denis wanted to leave school and get work on the Great Lakes freighters. He kept a map folded on the kitchen table until my mother made him move it, and then he kept it on his knee under the table. The fold showed above his trousers when he sat back. My father could see it from the other end. Through that winter we had potatoes most nights, and the argument usually started before I had finished mine.

My father said Denis would finish school. Denis said there were men on the boats who had never finished and were making proper wages. He did not have a wage to name every time, but when he had one he repeated it. My father asked whether that was before deductions. Denis said it was more than he got for being in a classroom. My mother told Denis to stop answering everything as if it were a trick question. Then she told my father that saying the same thing louder was not getting them anywhere.

I sat on the side nearest the kitchen doorway. From there I could get up without making either of them move his chair. I cut my meat into pieces before I started on it. If I left the potato until last it went cold, so I ate some of each and kept the gravy over the cut edges. Denis put his fork down when he wanted the floor. My father carried on with his fork in his hand. My mother set hers across the plate, with the handle pointing right. I watched those things and knew when to finish what was in my mouth.

One night Denis brought the map out and spread part of it beside his plate. My father told him to put it away. Denis tapped a place on it and said there was work beyond Main Street. My father said Main Street had paid for the food he was eating. My mother took the butter dish off the map before the paper caught beneath it. She told Denis he could keep a map in his room and told my father nobody had said the store did not pay for the food. I scraped the last potato out from under my knife.

When the voices got louder I began clearing. I took my own plate first, then whatever was empty. I stacked them beside the sink rather than trying to carry a pile with forks sliding between them. There was always something left on Denis's plate. I waited until he pushed it away. Sometimes I stood with one hand on the back of my chair while he made another point. If my mother told me to leave the plates, I sat down again. I did not put anything into the argument. The plates gave me a reason to stand and a place to put my hands.

My father turned to me one evening while I was reaching for the bread plate. He asked what I thought. Denis looked at me too, and my mother stopped moving the serving spoon. I said I didn't know. I understand now that my silence in that house was a kind of bargaining. My father told me I must have some opinion. I slid the bread plate towards the edge of the table and said nothing else. Denis pulled his map together along the folds. My mother asked whether anybody wanted the last potato. I took the plate to the counter when she was finished with it.

The argument came back on other nights with a different starting point. School, wages, how long a man stayed aboard, whether Denis knew anything about the work. I heard the same questions while I ate cabbage, while I broke a slice of bread, while I worked a bit of gristle to the side of my plate. My mother argued with both of them and then told them the food was getting cold. By the time the snowbanks were high beside our walk, I could clear the table in three trips. I kept my glass until the end and drank the water standing at the sink.

On Saturdays I got myself up for the eight o'clock Mass at St. Anne. In winter the bedroom window was still dark when I put my feet down. I laid my socks on top of my trousers the night before so I would not have to open drawers. The house had its usual early noises: the furnace stopping, water moving in a pipe, a floorboard under my heel. I washed, dressed and pulled my boots on at the door. The boots had stiffened where the road salt dried along the leather. I worked each heel down before I did the laces.

Mémère lived two streets over. I went there first, keeping to the packed part of the pavement. After a thaw the snow beside the kerb had a grey crust, and the ridges made by tyres could turn an ankle if I stepped across them badly. She was ready in her coat. We walked to St. Anne at her pace. She kept her handbag on the side away from me. At the steps I held her arm, with my other hand on the rail when there was room. The ice lay thin over the stone, particularly near the edges where boots had carried wet snow up from the street.

Inside, I stamped the loose snow off before we went to the pew. My coat made a thick fold between my back and the wood. I put my gloves together rather than setting one on either side of me. Mémère knelt, and I knelt beside her. I knew when to stand and when to sit without looking around. At that hour the heating had not taken the chill out of everything. The edge of the pew felt cold through my hands. When I got up from the kneeler, the knees of my trousers held two shallow creases.

After Mass Mémère went to the back to talk with the other women. I stayed kneeling. I could hear the low talking and the doors opening, followed by the scrape of boots on the mat. I looked at the grain in the wood ahead of me and kept my hands together. I did not have a long prayer worked out. Sometimes I repeated the part I knew and then stopped while I was still on my knees. By then one foot would be pressed against the other at the ankle, and I shifted it enough to flatten the sole.

I stayed there a while, until the talking at the back thinned out. Then I stood, put on my gloves and went down the aisle. Mémère was near the doors, her handbag held in both hands. Outside, the light had come up over the street. I took her arm on the steps again. The sand scattered there had gone into the ice, leaving brown grains below a clear surface. We went down one step at a time. On the walk back I watched where my boots went and shortened my stride to hers. At her house I waited until she had the door open before I turned for home.

Before the Christmas break I cleared my bedroom closet. There were clothes on the shelf and a shoebox of screws on the floor. I put the clothes elsewhere in the room and wiped the shelves with a damp cloth. The back corners held grey lint that rolled under my finger. I left the door open until the boards were dry. The closet was narrow enough that I could reach both sides without moving my feet. With the clothes out, I could see the shelf brackets and the pale strips where the wood had been covered.

I brought cigar boxes home from the store. They were light wood and cardboard, with lids that stayed attached, and they fitted on the shelves two deep if I used the shorter ones at the front. I tried them several ways before I put anything in them. A box that had to come out sideways was no good. I wanted to be able to read the label and lift the lid without taking down everything beside it. The shoebox went on the floor while I divided the screws. I used the bedspread to keep the different lengths apart.

I labelled the boxes in marker: resistors, wire ends, switches, bulbs. The screws got separate boxes by size. I measured the ones I could not place by sight and kept the odd ones together until I had enough to make another division. Some had straight slots with the edges turned up. Others still had sawdust in the threads. I did not throw those away. I rubbed off what came loose and put them with their size. The marker bled into the soft cardboard, so I wrote slowly and left space between the letters.

The resistors had coloured bands and wire legs bent at different angles. I laid them beside one another before putting them away. The switches were less tidy. Their backs and terminals caught together, and one had a nut that would come off if I turned it too far. I screwed the nut down by hand and put the switch in its box. I coiled the longer wire ends around two fingers. Short pieces went straight in. The bulbs needed a shallow box so I would not have to dig through them. I put their glass ends in the same direction.

I taped a list inside the closet door. Each line named a box and what was in it. I used my notebook first, crossed out the arrangements that did not fit, and copied the result onto a clean sheet. The tape had to be pressed hard along the painted wood. One corner lifted the first night and I added another piece. When I took something out, I changed the list. If I removed two screws, the number went down by two before I shut the door. I kept the pencil on the shelf where I could reach it without leaving the closet.

For a while I spent more time opening the boxes than using what was in them. I could shut the bedroom door, open the closet and have a counter of my own. I moved the bulbs up one shelf and the wire down, then changed the list to match. A wire end with a cracked covering went into the same box as the sound ones, though I put it along the side. I did not start a repair that required it. Most evenings I checked a lid, counted something and closed the closet again. The labels faced out in a straight row.

I borrowed a library book about how radios worked for the Christmas break. It had drawings of circuits and a cutaway drawing of a tube. I read it on my bed with the book propped against my knees. When a drawing was on the facing page, I held that page down with my thumb and went back and forth between the words and the lines. I knew a bulb used electricity to make its filament hot. The tube had a heater too, but the book gave its other parts separate names. I copied those names into my notebook and joined them with little lines.

I read the whole book once and then started again. The second time I went more slowly over the part about a weak signal becoming stronger. There was a grid between other parts, and a small change there was meant to control a larger current. I could repeat the words. I could point to the grid in the drawing. When I tried to put the book down and say how it happened, I got as far as the small change and had to open it again. I drew the tube larger, taking most of a page. A larger drawing did not supply the missing part.

I asked the librarian whether there was anything else on the subject. I showed the page with the tube and said that was the part I wanted. I went back over what I already had during the rest of the break. The book lay open beside my boxes, but none of the loose parts in the closet made the drawing any plainer. I took out a resistor, held it by its wire ends and put it back. I corrected nothing on the list because nothing had stayed out. By the end of the break I had read the book twice, and the tube still worked on the page better than it did in my account of it.

At supper I tried another part on Denis. I said radio waves could go through the walls of the house but a hill could block them. He asked why the house did not block them if the hill did. I said there was more in a hill, and that it depended on the waves. Denis asked how much more. I did not have a measurement. I put my knife down and used my hands to mark the house, then the hill farther away. My father told me to keep my sleeve out of the butter. I moved the butter dish and started the explanation again.

I said the hill was thick all the way through, while a wall was only a wall. Denis said the wall had wood in it and the hill had trees on it. I told him the trees were not what I meant. My mother asked him to let me finish. Once he did, I had little left beyond what I had already said. I mentioned the signal and then the tube, although the tube did not explain the hill. Denis went back to eating. I took my hands off the table and finished too. Later I looked up the passage again, read it once and shut the book. I did not make another drawing.

At the outdoor rink I signed Kyle Brandt, Marcus Oake, Pickle and myself up for the grade 8 broomball team. Kyle wanted to know when we played. Pickle asked whether his name was already down. I said it was. Marcus stood beside the list and read it. I had written all four names in the spaces available, with mine first because that was the one I started with. The pencil had a blunt point and left a broad grey mark on the paper. I pressed hard enough that the names could be read from a little way back.

The rink boards had snow packed against the outside. Inside, the ice had white patches where it had been scraped and grey patches where it had been used. We wore boots for broomball. A quick turn could leave one foot going while the other stayed under me. Kyle called for the ball whether he was in front or behind. Marcus followed the play. Pickle struck at it with more swing than he needed and had to turn his whole body to get back into position. I stayed where I could see the others and took my turn when the ball came near enough.

Then the winter carnival setup list went up. Everyone from my class was on it, so I put our names down for that too. It was another sheet with lines and a space beside each name. I found where the grade 8 names were grouped and wrote ours there. The evenings were already dark by the time I got to the rink. My breath left a damp patch at the top of my scarf. I pulled the scarf down when I went inside the boards, then pushed it up again when the wind came across the open end.

I was at the rink every evening the carnival committee met. I knew the evenings from the list. There were things laid out and moved, places kept clear, and the same marks on the boards to tell where everything belonged. I stayed near Kyle, Marcus and Pickle when they were there. We stood with our hands in our pockets and shifted from one boot to the other. If somebody read from the list, I listened for our names. I had signed us up, and I checked the sheet when there was a chance, following the line with my finger through my glove.

When the rest were leaving, I stayed to sweep the ice. The broom was wide, with a wooden back and bristles worn shorter at the ends. I pushed in straight runs and turned it where the handle would clear the boards. Loose ice gathered ahead of it in a low white ridge. Close to the gate there was grit brought in on boots. The broom moved that along with the scrapings. I kept my mitts around the handle and went over the easy lengths. Where a patch was frozen down, the bristles passed over it. I left the patch and carried on to the loose stuff.

Through the open gate I could see the others getting farther away. I made another run, tapped the broom against the bottom of the boards and put it away. The sweepings stayed in a pile near the edge. I did not fetch a scraper for the frozen patches. Outside, the snow along the road was high enough to hide the bottom of the fence in places. I went home with the cold worked into the toes of my boots. At the door I knocked the snow off each sole separately. My mother asked if I had been at the rink, and I said I had.

The returned drill came into Lacasse Hardware on a day when wet snow was turning to water just inside the entrance. A man set it on the counter and said it had quit the first time he plugged it in. The cord was wrapped around the handle with the plug caught beneath a turn. My father unwrapped enough to look at it, then reached for a refund slip. I stood at the end of the counter where the loose nails were weighed. The drill had a scuffed housing, but there was nothing about a scuff that said whether a motor would run.

While my father wrote, I took the drill to the back room. I laid it on the bench with the cord hanging away from the screws. I used a screwdriver that fitted the slots and put each screw where it would not roll. The housing needed to come apart evenly. I lifted one side a little, worked around it and lifted again. Sawdust lay inside the vents in a packed brown layer. It was finer than shavings and held together where it had been pressed into the corners. Some fell onto the bench when I set the cover down.

The brushes were worn down to stubs. I had to look closely to see how little was left against the turning part of the motor. I did not need to plug it in to compare the man's account with what was on the bench. I tipped the open half enough to see the other side, keeping the loose parts in place with my fingers. There was sawdust there too. I set the cover beside it and looked at the screw holes, then at the vents again. I left the screwdriver on the bench. There was no reason to close it before my father saw it.

I carried the drill back with the open side up. My father still had the refund slip in front of him. I put the drill on the counter and set the cover beside it. A little sawdust came out near the base. I said the brushes were worn down. My father looked into the housing. The man looked too. I did not add anything about the first time he had plugged it in. The inside was there between us, with the short brushes and the packed vents. My father stopped writing long enough to move the drill away from the edge of the paper.

I went back for the screws and brought them out in my palm. I laid them beside the cover, all pointing roughly the same way. The man had his hands on the counter. My father put the pen down and turned the drill slightly towards himself. I stayed at the end until he picked up one of the worn brushes to look at it. Then I returned to the back room and wiped the patch of bench where the sawdust had fallen. I had opened the housing and shown what was inside.
