I left the drill on the counter for my father to deal with.

Pickle came to the store after his bike chain snapped. He brought the broken chain with him, folded into a small greasy length. A link had opened, and the two ends did not sit together properly when he laid them on the counter. He said a new one was three dollars. I knew the price, but I checked it anyway. The replacement was in its packet. I set it beside the broken one without opening it. Pickle put a dollar on the counter and said that was all he had. The note was soft at the folds and had a black mark near one corner.

He asked me to lend him the other two dollars. I said no. Then he asked whether I could get the family discount. I said the price was three dollars. He put a finger on the packet and slid it a short distance towards himself. I kept my hand on the other end. Behind me the shelves held the small boxes and packets I could find without turning all the way around. Pickle said he could pay the rest later. I said I could take the dollar as a deposit. He looked at the chain on the counter and then at the replacement still under my hand.

I rang it up at full price. The keys needed a firm push, and I watched the amount come up before I took his dollar. I marked the deposit and put the new chain aside. Pickle asked once more about the two dollars. I told him I wasn't lending it. He picked up the broken chain, got a bit of grease on the counter and wiped at it with the side of his hand. That spread it into a wider mark. I moved the packet farther back, clear of the grease. He put the old chain in his pocket. The new one stayed at the store.

When he had gone, I wiped the counter. I folded the cloth over the grease and used a clean part for the last pass. The deposit was one dollar against three, with two still owing. I checked that the packet and the record of it were together. I did not take money out of my coffee tin at home. That evening I counted what was in the tin because I had a loose coin to add, and put the lid back on. Pickle's dollar was in the till at Lacasse Hardware. My coins were where I had left them, apart from the one I had just put in.

When the roads were clear enough for bikes, Kyle dared us to go full speed down the mill road hill and cross the single plank over the culvert at the bottom. The plank lay along our line of travel. On either side was the opening of the culvert and the broken ground beside it. From the top, the board looked narrower than it did when we walked down to look. It was ordinary rough wood, with dirt pressed into the grain. I put a boot on it and then took the boot off. Kyle said the point was to ride it, not stand there measuring it.

Marcus had his bike turned across the road. Pickle stood with one foot on a pedal. Denis was there watching. He looked from the hill to the plank and said it couldn't be done. Kyle said it could if a person kept going. Denis said we would brake before we reached it, and once we did that we would be finished. I wheeled my bike uphill past the others. The road had dried along the centre, with damp gravel still gathered at the sides. I turned the front wheel downhill and put my weight on the pedal.

I went first while Denis watched. In hindsight, the dares were the only place I felt larger than my brother. I pushed hard until the slope did most of the work. The pedals came around faster under my shoes. I kept the front wheel pointed at the near end of the plank. The culvert opened on either side as I got closer, and the board stopped looking like a line and became a piece of wood wide enough for the tyres. I did not use the brakes. The front tyre struck the plank, the back followed, and both wheels were on the road beyond it.

I kept going until the road levelled enough to turn. Then I came back towards the hill, out of the saddle for the steeper part. The others were still above me. Kyle had moved his bike to the edge. Marcus watched the front wheel as I passed. Pickle said something about the board, but I was breathing through my mouth and kept pedalling. Denis was walking away. He did not have a bike beside him. I saw his back between the others and the road home, then lost sight of him when I put my head down for the last part of the climb.

At the top I turned around to do it again. I put one foot down while I straightened the handlebars, then set off. The second run carried me down the same dry strip. I lined the tyre up with the plank and let the speed take me across. There was a short change in the sound under the wheels, wood instead of road, and then gravel again. I rode far enough to make the turn without dropping a foot. When I looked uphill, Denis had gone. Kyle, Marcus and Pickle were still there with their bikes. I started back towards them, pedalling slowly at first.

Denis let me work with him on an old outboard motor in the garage. We had it where we could get at the lower part without lying on the floor. The garage was cooler than the house even when the door stood open. Oil had darkened the wood beneath the motor, and old grit stuck to anything we put down carelessly. Denis had the tools spread out within reach. I put the sockets in order and found the sizes he asked for. Some bolts came free with a short pull. Others needed the wrench moved back and fitted again because there was not enough room for a full turn.

I liked taking off a part when the fasteners were all visible. I could count them, loosen them and lay them with the piece they belonged to. I wiped the faces we uncovered and kept the washers from slipping into the dirt. Denis told me where to hold while he turned. I held there until he moved his hand away. We set the pieces out in a row, with the heavier ones at the back of the bench. The old oil came off on the rag in black streaks. I folded the rag to find another clean patch instead of fetching a second one.

The crankshaft seal stopped us. Denis worked at it, changed position and tried again. The edge was hard to get behind, and what little purchase there was did not last. I held the light and moved it when his hand blocked it. He asked for a different tool, then gave it back after trying it. We looked through what we had. The job needed a puller, and there was no puller in the garage. Denis said so and went back to the seal. I set the tools I had been holding on the bench, clear of the oily parts.

I stayed a little longer that evening, standing beside the motor while Denis tried to get behind the seal again. Nothing came free. I put the light down where it shone across the work and went into the house. The next time he went out, I stayed in my room. I opened the closet, took down a box and checked the screws against the list. Through the house I could hear the garage door when it moved. I did not go to see what he was doing. After that I stopped coming out to the garage. The motor stayed with Denis, laid open among the tools.

Chapter 7

In 1985 my brother Denis went to work on the lake freighters, and in the fall of the same year Marcus Oake's family moved away. I turned fourteen that March. Denis was eighteen and had signed on as a deckhand on a bulk carrier that loaded grain at Thunder Bay, and he was to report at the end of the month, when the ice was off the lakes and the season opened.

My mother made his farewell supper on the Saturday before he left. She made tourtière, though it was nowhere near Christmas, because Denis asked for it, with mashed potatoes and canned peas, and she bought a lemon meringue pie from the bakery on Main Street and set it on the good plate on the counter. Mémère walked over from two streets away and sat at the end of the table nearest the stove, where she always sat. My father had closed the store at six and come home with the day's cash in the green zippered bag, which he put on top of the fridge, where it stayed until the bank on Monday.

Denis did most of the talking. He had sent away for a navigation chart of the Great Lakes, a real one and not a road map, and he had it folded beside his plate and put his finger on it between mouthfuls: Thunder Bay, the locks at the Soo, down Lake Huron and the rivers to Lake Erie and the Welland Canal. He said a deckhand made more in a month than he had made in a year of Saturdays at the store, and the company fed you three meals a day and a lunch at night. He talked loud, the way he always did, so Mémère never had to ask him to repeat anything.

My father let him go on. He asked whether the union dues came off before or after, and whether overtime was time and a half or straight, and Denis did not know, and my father said he should find out before he signed anything. Then, when my mother got up for the pie, my father laid his fork across his plate and said that starting next Saturday Ray would take Denis's shifts at the store. Eight to six, same as Denis, three dollars an hour, lunch unpaid. He said it the way he would tell a customer the price of a box of screws, and picked his fork up again.

I had other plans for those Saturdays. The arena was keeping its ice in until the third week of April that year for an old-timers' tournament, and on Saturday afternoons there was shinny from one to four for anybody with skates and a stick. Kyle Brandt and Pickle and I had agreed to be there every Saturday until the ice came out. I had told my mother. I had not told my father, because it had not occurred to me that it was any of his business.

I didn't say anything. My mother set the pie down and looked at my father and then at me, and then she cut it. Denis grinned at me across the table and said the four-inch spikes went in the second bin from the end, not the third, whatever anybody told me. Mémère asked whether the boat had a priest on it. Denis said no. She said she would light a candle, then. I ate my pie, and nobody said anything more about Saturdays.

Denis left on the Thursday. My father drove him to the bus at the Esso station before the store opened, while I was at school, and when I came home his bed was stripped and his chart was gone from the wall over his desk and there were four thumbtack holes in the plaster.

The next Saturday I was behind the counter at eight. My father had me there at a quarter to, so he could show me the till before he unlocked the door. It was an NCR register with a crank on the side for when the power failed, and he showed me how to ring a sale and a return, how to void a mistake and initial the slip, and how to make change by counting up from the price instead of subtracting in my head. He had me do it five or six times with money from the drawer until he was satisfied. Then he turned the sign.

I had been in and out of the store all my life, and sorted screws in the back since I was eight, but I had never stood behind the counter with my father down the aisle and the till at my back. The first man in wanted a pound of two-and-a-half-inch common nails, and I scooped them into a paper bag and set it on the scale and took some out and put some back until the needle sat on the line. Sixty-nine cents. He gave me a dollar and I counted up into his hand, seventy, seventy-five, a dollar. My father watched from the paint counter and said nothing, which meant I had done it right.

By noon I had rung up thirty-one sales and voided one, a box of number eight wood screws I had rung in as number tens, and initialled the slip. The keys I sent down to my father, because I had not been shown the key machine yet. At twelve I ate the sandwich my mother had packed on the stool in the back room among the cartons, and at one I was back at the till.

At about ten past one Kyle and Pickle went by on the other side of Main Street with their skates tied together and slung over their sticks. Kyle saw me through the window and crossed over and put his face to the glass. I held up a hand. He shook his head and went back across, and the two of them went on up the hill to the arena. A woman came in for a furnace filter and I went to find it. At six my father counted the drawer and wrote the total in the book and gave me twenty-seven dollars in an envelope with my name on it, and I took it home and put it in the coffee tin.

Every Sunday that first season Denis was on the boats, I walked to St. Anne by myself after supper and lit a candle for him.

The first time was the Sunday after he left. I finished supper and put on my jacket and said I was going for a walk, and my mother said all right, and I went down Champlain Street to Main and along Main to the church. The front doors were locked by then but the side door by the sacristy stayed open until eight. Inside it was cold and smelled of wax and floor polish. The only light was the sanctuary lamp and the votive candles at the side altar of St. Anne, in red glass holders on a stepped iron stand, three or four of them going from people who had been in during the day. I took a wooden taper from the box beside the stand and lit it from one of those and lit a new candle in the bottom row, and shook out the taper and stuck the burnt end in the tray of sand.

Then I knelt on the kneeler in front of the stand for a few minutes. I said Denis's name, and the name of his boat, which I had copied into my notebook off his first postcard, and I said the Our Father once through. Then I got up and genuflected in the aisle and went out the side door and walked home.

I did it every Sunday after that, and nobody in the family knew. My mother thought I went to Kyle's or for a walk, and I let her think it. My father did the store accounts at the kitchen table on Sunday evenings and did not look up. Mémère went to the seven o'clock Mass in the morning and I went at seven at night, so we never crossed. I didn't write it in the notebook, though I wrote down most things. By September it was dark when I walked down, and the candles were most of the light in the church, and some nights later in the fall there was frost on the side steps when I came out.

That September I started grade 9 at Brule Falls High School, a long brick building on the hill behind the arena, with a newer wing on the back for the shops and the gym. Kyle and Pickle started the same year, and so did Marcus, for the few weeks he had left.

I had shop with Mr. Szabo every second day, a double period after lunch. The shop room was at the end of the new wing, down a half-flight of stairs, with a roll-up door onto the parking lot where the lumber came in. The floor was concrete painted grey, with yellow lines around the machines: a drill press, a band saw, two wood lathes, a metal lathe, a bench grinder, and a table saw only Mr. Szabo used. The shop's hand tools hung on a pegboard on the back wall, each traced around in black marker so you could see from the door if anything was missing. It smelled of cutting oil and pine sawdust.

Mr. Szabo was a short, thick man with a grey brush cut, a pencil behind his ear and a brown shop coat. He had been a machinist before he taught. On the first day he told us he had three rules: you wore your glasses, you did not touch a machine he had not shown you, and at the end of the period every tool went back on its outline, and nobody left until it did, bus or no bus. It was the only room in the school where I knew what was wanted and could see at the end of the period whether I had done it, and by October it was my favourite place in the building.

By the second week I wanted my own tools at school. The shop screwdrivers had been through a hundred kids and the tips were rounded, and the pliers at my bench didn't close straight. So at the end of a Saturday, after the drawer was counted, I asked my father what the steel toolbox in the window cost him. It was a red box about eighteen inches long with a lift-out tray, latches at both ends and a folding handle, and the tag said $18.95. He went to the filing cabinet and found the invoice and ran his finger down it and said eleven forty. I said I'd take it at eleven forty. I counted it out of the coffee tin that night, mostly in quarters, and brought it in a paper bag on Monday after school, and he counted it on the counter and rang it in as a cost sale and took the box out of the window and wiped the dust off it with a rag.

I already had a few tools of my own, from the ones that came back to the store damaged and couldn't be sold: needle-nose pliers, a Robertson with a chipped handle, a six-inch adjustable wrench. I filled out the rest over the next week, paying out of the tin at cost each time: three Robertsons, red, green and black, a flat screwdriver and a stubby, slip-joint pliers, a small ball-peen hammer, a ten-foot tape. For small parts I took a handful of each size of machine screw, nut and washer from the shoebox at home.

The tray was bare tin and I didn't like things sliding around in it. I took a heavy carton from the back of the store that a shipment of caulking guns had come in, and at the kitchen table I measured the tray and drew a plan in my notebook, then cut strips with a box knife against a steel straightedge. I notched them halfway where they crossed so they locked together like the inside of an egg carton, and glued their bottom edges down with white glue. Each screwdriver got its own slot, largest to smallest from left to right, and the pliers lay in a long slot along the back. At the front I made nine small squares for the parts and labelled each with masking tape. The hammer, the tape and the wrench each had a place in the bottom of the box. When I closed the lid and shook it nothing moved.

I kept the box in my shop locker by the roll-up door, with a combination lock I bought at cost. At the end of each period, while everybody else hung the shop tools back on their outlines, I checked every piece of mine back into its place, screwdrivers left to right, pliers, the nine squares, and if a slot was empty I found what went in it before I shut the lid. Mr. Szabo watched me do it once or twice from the front of the room and didn't say anything.

The Oakes moved away in the second week of October. Marcus's father had found work a long way south, and they were driving down behind the moving truck. Marcus had told me in August, at the river, in the same voice he used for anything, and after that there wasn't much more to say about it.

On the Friday before they left, they had a going-away evening for the neighbours. The truck had come that afternoon and taken everything but the kitchen table, so the living room was just carpet with dents in it where the couch and the television had been, and along one wall the boxes that were going in the car. Marcus's mother had a card table in the middle with egg salad and ham sandwiches cut in triangles, a plate of date squares, bottles of pop and paper cups, and there were folding chairs from the church hall around the walls. The room echoed when anybody talked.

It was to start at seven. I knocked at five to. Marcus's father opened the door and said come in, Ray, and I was the first one there. Marcus was sitting on a box along the wall and I sat on the one beside it, marked KITCHEN in his mother's printing. We had orange pop and sandwiches and didn't say much, because we never did. The neighbours started coming at twenty past and stood around the card table in their coats talking to his parents about the highway.

Kyle and Pickle came at eight. Kyle ate four date squares and told Marcus the place he was going would be flat and he'd hate it, and Pickle asked if they could have the boxes after, for a fort, and Marcus said the boxes would be a long way off by then. They stayed an hour and then Kyle said there was something on television, and they left, and some of the neighbours went at the same time. By half past nine it was the family and two couples in the kitchen, and Marcus and me in the living room.

We stayed on the boxes. His parents stacked the folding chairs against the wall to go back to the church in the morning and folded up the card table. The overhead light was off by then and the only light came in from the kitchen. Every so often one of us said something, about the trestle, or the dam at the mill, or whether there would be a river where he was going, or Mr. Szabo and the dustpans we were making. Marcus said the new house had a workbench in the basement that the last people had left. Mostly we sat.

At a quarter past eleven his father came to the doorway and said they were leaving at six and he expected my mother would want me home. Marcus walked me to the front door and said see you, and I said see you, and walked home. It was cold enough that the puddles had a skin of ice on them. In the morning on my way to the store I went past the house. The car was gone and the curtains were down, and you could see straight through the front window into the empty room.

That month I went out for the junior hockey team. The ice didn't go in at the arena until November, but the coach, who taught gym, ran dryland training before school for anybody who wanted to be considered, at six in the morning, starting the third week of October. It was posted on the board outside the gym, and Kyle and I both put our names on the sheet.

On the Monday I set the alarm for twenty past five and walked up the hill in the dark in a sweatsuit. There were about twenty of us at the gym door at six. The coach came out in a ski jacket with a whistle and took us around to the concrete stairs that went up from the back parking lot to the playing field, about forty steps with a steel railing. The parking lot lights were off at that hour and there was only what came from the school windows. We ran the stairs up and down until he blew the whistle, did push-ups on the asphalt at the bottom, and ran them again, for forty-five minutes. Then we showered and sat in homeroom with our legs shaking under the desks.

I went every morning that week. On the Wednesday it rained and the steps were slick and we ran them anyway. On the Thursday a boy from grade 11 was sick in the bushes at the side of the lot, and the coach waited for him to finish and then blew the whistle again. On the next Monday the alarm went at twenty past five and I turned it off and went back to sleep, and got up at seven as I always had. I didn't go on the Tuesday either, or after that. I never went back to tell the coach. I saw him in the halls most days and in gym twice a week, and he never asked and I never said.

In November Mr. Szabo took apart an electric motor at the front of the shop. It was out of an old bench grinder, a cast-iron housing about the size of a coffee can, and he had it in the big vise on the demonstration bench. He took the end bells off with a socket wrench, tapped the shaft out with a rubber mallet and held up the armature: the laminated iron core, the copper windings in the slots, the commutator at one end with the ends of the windings soldered to its bars. Then he held up the two carbon brushes.

He explained it in a few sentences. The field windings in the housing made a magnetic field that stayed put. Current came in through the brushes to the windings on the armature and made it a magnet too, and the two pushed against each other and the armature turned. The commutator switched the current over every half turn so the push always went the same way. He said that was all we needed for now, and put it back together, and we went back to our dustpans.

I went to the library the next day at lunch and signed out a book on electric motors, a thick blue hardcover from the sixties full of diagrams. Most of it was more than I could follow, but some of it I followed, and it didn't sit right with what Mr. Szabo had said. There was a chapter on the kind of motor in a furnace fan or a washing machine, and it said that in those the magnetic field itself turned around inside the housing and the rotor chased it. There was a drawing with arrows going around in a circle. I wrote three questions in my notebook. The first was why the field turned when nothing that made it was moving.

On the Thursday I waited until the end of the period, after I had checked my toolbox in and everybody else had gone, and took the book up to the bench where he was sharpening a chisel and opened it to the drawing. He read the page holding it at arm's length and said that was a different motor, an induction motor, and what he'd taken apart was a universal motor with brushes. I said the book said the field turned. He said in that motor it did, in the sense the book meant. I said how could a field turn if nothing moved. He said that was what alternating current did. I said that didn't explain it. He said it explained it fine if I knew what alternating current did, and did I. I said it went back and forth. He said think about two of them going back and forth out of step. I said the book didn't say it like that. He said the book was written for people who already knew.

Then I said he'd told us the two magnets pushed on each other, and the book said it was the force on the wire, that a wire carrying current in a field got pushed sideways, and it gave a rule with three fingers. He said that was the same thing said two ways. I said it didn't look like the same thing. He said it was, and that he had rewound more motors than the man who wrote the book. The bell went. He handed the book back and went on with the chisel. I didn't read any further in it, and I took it back to the library the next week when it was due.

In the spring of 1986 I got a ten-speed, a blue Raleigh with drop bars and the shift levers on the down tube. That left the old bike, a CCM with a banana seat and a coaster brake, leaning against the garage.

There was a boy three houses down and across who was maybe ten and had been riding his sister's bike, and I had seen him looking at the CCM. One Saturday morning in May before work I wheeled it down to his house. He asked how much. A new one like it was sixty dollars in the Eaton's catalogue, and a used one with rust on the fenders and the seat cover split the way mine was would have gone for ten in the classifieds. I told him twenty. He went into the house and was gone ten minutes and came out with a jam jar and a cigar box.

We sat on his front step and counted it out. Quarters and dimes and nickels, a lot of them, some pennies, two one-dollar bills and a two. I stacked the quarters in fours and the dimes in tens and the nickels in twenties along the edge of the step and put the bills under a stack so they wouldn't blow off. It came to nineteen sixty-five. He went back in and came out with thirty-five cents more in pennies and a nickel, and I counted that too, and it came to twenty even.

I sold my old bike to a younger boy for twice what it was worth, an early sign of how little I cared to be fair with money.

I filled my jacket pockets with the coins and put the bills in my wallet and let go of the handlebars. He rode it up and down the street while I walked home with my pockets dragging, and I put it all in the coffee tin before I went to the store.

In July Kyle said we were jumping off the trestle. The railway trestle crossed the river half a mile below the mill, a steel span on concrete piers with a timber deck and the rails on top. Kyle said the older boys had done it in June. He said it was forty feet above the river and the pool under the middle was deep enough for anything, you could drop a car in it. Pickle said he'd heard thirty. Kyle said forty.

I didn't believe either figure. The week before, on a Tuesday evening after supper, I went out alone with a four-ounce bank sinker from the fishing drawer at the store and a hundred feet of nylon mason's line wound on a stick, and a roll of electrician's tape in my pocket. I walked out along the tracks from the crossing on the river road and onto the trestle, stepping on the ties, until I was at what I judged was the middle, over the dark water of the pool. You could see the river through the gaps between the ties.

I tied on the sinker and let the line down until it just touched the water and made a ring, then pinched the line at the top of the rail and wrapped a piece of tape around it there. Then I let out more, feeling for bottom, while the current pulled it a little downstream, until it went slack. I drew it up tight with the sinker resting on the bottom and marked the line again where I judged the water surface was, paying out and taking in two or three times until I got the same place twice. Then I wound it back on the stick and walked home.

In the driveway I hooked my father's twenty-five-foot carpenter's tape on a nail in the garage door frame and stretched it along the concrete, laid the line beside it and chalked off the lengths. From the rail to the water was thirty-one feet four inches. From the surface to the bottom was twelve feet nine inches, at that spot, at the July level of the river. I measured the whole thing twice and wrote both figures in my notebook with the date.

The next day I told Kyle. He said I must have done it wrong. I said I'd done it twice. He said thirty-one was close enough to forty to call it forty. Pickle asked how deep twelve feet was, and I said about two of his father, and he said that was plenty.

The day we went was the hottest of the month, thirty-one degrees on the thermometer outside the store at noon, when my father let me off for the afternoon because it was slow. Kyle and Pickle were waiting at the corner of Main and the river road with towels round their necks. We walked out to the crossing and along the tracks with the smell of creosote coming up off the ties and the rails too hot to touch, and left our towels and shoes on the bank by the abutment.

We went out onto the trestle in our cutoffs, single file on the ties, Kyle, then Pickle, then me, until we were about where I had dropped the sinker. The water was dark green and slow on top, and you could see the current working under it where it came around the piers. You could hear the mill from there.

Kyle stepped outside the rail with his toes on the ends of the ties, looked down at the water, and went, feet first with his arms over his head, and came up downstream shouting. Pickle went next, holding his nose, and tipped forward on the way down and came in with a slap, and came up and started swimming for the bank. Then I went. We jumped from the railway trestle into the river that July, stupidly, three of us in a row. I stepped off the end of the tie with my arms at my sides. It took longer to get there than I had expected. The water was cold below the top foot or two and went up my nose, and I went down until the light went brown, and didn't touch bottom, and then I came up.

I came up thirty feet or so downstream of where I'd gone in, because the current had taken me while I was under. Kyle was already sitting on the gravel bar below the abutment and Pickle was crawling out beside him. I swam across the current at an angle and came out on the gravel with the water running off me. Kyle said that was forty feet. I said it was thirty-one. Pickle said it didn't matter.

I sat on the gravel with my elbows on my knees until I had my breath back. Then I got up and climbed the embankment by the abutment, holding onto the brush, and walked back out along the hot ties in my bare feet, stepping over the gaps, to the middle. Kyle and Pickle were still on the gravel bar. I stood outside the rail on the end of a tie and stepped off again, and went down feet first, and came up downstream, and swam back to the bank.

Chapter 8

In 1986 I was fifteen and in grade 10 at Brule Falls High School. Kyle Brandt and Pickle were still around. That fall, before the river had begun to freeze along the banks, the three of us went down to the log boom above the mill. There was a strip of bare ground between the trees and the water, worn down to roots where people went to look at the logs. Bark lay in the mud. We left our jackets on the bank, where the sleeves would stay out of the water, and stepped onto the timber.

The logs nearest shore were packed fairly close. Their wet sides came together and separated with a knocking sound that carried underneath us. A boot placed down the middle of a log would hold it for a moment. A boot placed on the side would turn it. Kyle went first, taking short jumps with both arms out. Pickle followed him. I waited for a space between them and stepped onto the first log, which sank enough to put water over the edge of my sole.

We ran once we were clear of the bank. Stopping meant standing on something that was already turning, so there was less to do if I kept moving. I landed with one foot and used the other to reach the next log. Bits of bark came away under my boots. The ends were easier to see than the sides, pale circles just above the water, but some of the gaps between them were wider than they looked from shore.

Kyle turned back before I did. Pickle was closer to the bank, moving sideways across two logs that had drifted apart. I kept going towards the point where the boom chains met. The river pulled through there, carrying loose pieces of bark into a brown strip of current. The chains were wet and dark except where one link rubbed another. They held the outer timber together while the smaller logs shifted inside it.

Out there the logs moved lengthwise as well as rolling. I put my foot on one end and felt the whole piece slide away underneath me. I took the next step before it had gone far. The water showed in long narrow openings, then disappeared as the timber closed. I reached the meeting of the chains, turned on a log broad enough for both boots, and started back along the outer row.

That row gave me a line to follow. I could see the bank without looking between every step. Several logs rolled towards the open river as I crossed them, bringing their wet undersides up behind my heels. Kyle and Pickle were ashore by then. They stood near our jackets, watching where I put my feet. I ran the last few logs, jumped onto the mud and took two more steps before stopping.

There was river water inside one boot. I pulled it off, emptied it and wrung the top of my sock between my hands. Pickle scraped a strip of bark from his sole with a stick. Kyle put his jacket on without putting his arms through the sleeves. We went back up the worn path from the river. My sock bunched under my toes when I pulled the boot on, and I stopped at the top to straighten it.

I started at Dube Radio and TV that fall. On my first Saturday, Lionel Dube put me behind the repair counter, where there was a clear place for taking in sets and very little clear space anywhere else. The parts drawers stood against the wall behind me. They were small enough to pull out with two fingers. Some had paper labels left from whatever had been in them before. Others had no labels, and several would not close because a lead or a screw was caught across the front.

I opened one looking for a part Lionel wanted and found resistors, two washers, a fuse and the broken end of a pencil. The next contained capacitors mixed with screws. A drawer marked with one resistance held several other values. I took it out and tipped the contents onto the counter, using an old envelope to keep the pieces away from the edge. Lionel found what he wanted elsewhere while I was still separating the washers.

There was no single large job to it. I could empty a drawer, sort what was there and put it back in the time between one thing at the counter and the next. I started with screws because I could do those without looking anything up. Machine screws went apart from wood screws. Nuts went with nuts, washers with washers. I put the odd pieces in a separate drawer until I knew what they were.

Over the next month I stayed after my paid hours to work through the rest. I did not put that time in with my hours for Lionel. Resistors needed their colour bands read. Capacitors had values printed in small type, sometimes on the side that had lain against the bottom of the drawer. I turned each one over before putting it in a group. When a marking was too worn to read, I left the part out of the labelled stock.

I used masking tape across the drawer fronts. It would take pencil, but pencil rubbed off on my fingers, so I went over the labels in ink. Each label gave the type and value, with the writing large enough to read from where I stood at the counter. I cut the tape square with scissors. A loose corner collected dust and lifted farther each time a hand went past it, so I pressed the ends down with my thumbnail.

The last drawer I labelled was no fuller than the first. There was simply less in it that did not belong there. I could reach behind me for a fuse without opening three drawers. Parts with long leads lay lengthwise, and the little bags of screws stood with their folds upwards. I kept the spare roll of tape in the same drawer as the pen.

I also started a notebook at the counter. Each set got its own entry, with room for the fault as it had been described and for what I replaced. I wrote the model number carefully because one figure could send me to the wrong service sheet. I have often wondered since whether I chose the repair counter or it chose me. The notebook stayed beside the place where I wrote the intake slips, far enough back that a television put on the counter would not land on it.

For Mrs. Okafor's science fair, I decided to build a small electric motor and measure its efficiency at different voltages. It sounded like something I could put on a table and explain with figures. There would be a motor, a supply, meters and a sheet showing how the results changed. I wrote the heading before I had made anything turn. Under it I left columns for voltage, current and output, with enough space to make several trials at each setting.

I wound the coil with the turns pushed close together. The wire had to stay where I put it while I dealt with the ends, and the ends had to be scraped clean where they made contact. I used a small file for that. A patch that looked bare under the room light could still have coating on it, so I turned the wire and filed again. The scrapings made a fine dust on the paper underneath.

The brushes were the part I kept moving. Too much pressure held the motor back. Too little gave me a connection for part of a turn and no connection for the rest. I bent them in, then out again, working with pliers near the bend so I would not pull the whole arrangement loose. When the motor stopped, I could start it with a push. That left me with the question of where to begin a reading.

Mrs. Okafor's science room had a hard work surface that showed every clipped end of wire. I kept the pieces in a shallow box while I worked so they would not roll off. My results sheet lay beside it. At one voltage the motor would run long enough for me to write down the meter readings. At the same setting on the next trial, the current would be different or the motor would slow before I had finished.

I changed the brush pressure and tried the readings again. The brushes burned at the contact points, leaving black places I had to clean before the next run. Increasing the voltage made the trouble arrive sooner. Reducing it sometimes left the coil sitting where it was. I had figures in the columns, but the figures did not make a set I could use. There were crossed-out entries and arrows to numbers written in the margin.

In the second week I gathered the loose parts and put them in the box. The coil went in first, then the brushes and the short lengths of wire. I folded the results sheet so it would fit over them without catching on the leads. I gave up on the science fair project in the second week, mercifully before anyone had seen it. I closed the lid instead of making another set of columns.

At home I pushed the box under my bed. There was enough clearance for it to go flat, and I put it far enough in that my heel would not strike it when I sat down. I did not leave any of the parts on the desk. At school I told Mrs. Okafor I was not entering. I had the words ready and said them while I was at her desk. She had no motor to inspect and no figures to check. I went back to my place with the entry left at that.

At lunch in the back of Dube's I read the service manuals. There was room to set my food down if I moved the manual towards the rear edge of the table. I kept my hands off the pages while I ate, then wiped them before turning to a foldout. The sheets had been opened along the same creases often enough that they tended to close themselves unless I held a corner down.

A service manual gave me a fault in one place and the circuit for it somewhere else. I followed the references back and forth. The print was small, and several lines could travel together across the sheet before separating towards different parts. I laid a clean scrap of paper beneath the line I was following. That kept me from coming back from lunch with the right component number from the wrong section.

Lionel kept a television theory textbook on the shelf. It was heavier than a service manual and would stay open if I pressed down near the binding. I read the sections that matched what was on the bench. I could get through a page and recognise every part named on it, then reach the end without being able to say exactly what the current was doing. I would go back to the drawing and start there.

The horizontal output transformer was one part I could identify without tracing a line. In a set that had been running, it was hot. The heat stayed after the set was switched off, while the cabinet and the pieces farther from it had already begun to cool. I could see the transformer in the drawing and find its connections in the set. That did not tell me why it ran as hot as it did.

I asked Lionel at lunch. The textbook was open beside me, and I put my finger on the transformer in the circuit so there would be no question about which part I meant. He said it had a lot of work to do. He gave me something about the output stage and the voltage, then went back to what was on the bench. I could repeat his answer, but I could not use it to account for the heat.

I looked at the circuit again before putting the book away. There were connections I had passed over because they went out to another part of the drawing. I followed those until I came back to a point I recognised. My lunch paper had left a faint square on the table, and I wiped it up before I returned to the counter. The book went on the shelf with its spine facing out.

On the walk home I kept going through the circuit. I started at the supply, reached the output stage and went back when I lost a connection. Main Street had wet patches along the kerb and grit where the pavement had dried. I stepped around the deeper water while putting the parts in order. At home I drew the circuit from memory on a sheet of paper. I left room between the symbols, added the connections I had missed on the first pass and went over the whole route with the point of my pencil.

Lionel paid me in an envelope on Friday. I had my hours in my own notebook, separate from the book for repairs, and I checked the envelope before putting it in my pocket. I did it at the counter, with the notebook open. There was room beside the intake slips to lay the notes flat and put the coins in a line where they would not roll.

I entered my starting and finishing times on the day I worked. At the end of the week I added the hours down the page and checked the addition from the bottom up. Time spent on the parts drawers did not go in that column. If I had finished the paid part of a Saturday and then stayed to sort, the line ended at the earlier time. The number I compared with the envelope was the number of hours I had logged for pay.

The notes sometimes came folded together. I separated them before counting because two folded notes could pass through my fingers as one. Coins went by denomination. I counted the larger ones first and left the pennies until the end. Then I compared the amount with the figure beside my hours. There was no shortage on the first Friday, and there was none on the Fridays after it.

I still counted it. Lionel could be within arm's reach, putting something away or looking at a repair slip, while I had the money laid out. I did not wait for him to turn his back. I did not put the envelope away and check it at home. The counter had a level surface, my hours were there, and that was where I did the counting.

Once I had finished, I put the coins in the bottom of the envelope and slid the notes in above them. That kept a penny from being caught between two notes and falling out later. I folded the flap down without sealing it. The envelope went into an inside pocket, folded only as much as the pocket required. I closed the notebook and put it away before going back to the counter work.

The following week had its own line of hours and its own envelope. I did not use the previous week's amount as the check, even when the hours looked much the same. I added the new column. If a figure was badly written, I went over it before making the total. My pencil left a grey mark along the side of my finger where it rested against the page.

As the weather got colder, I arrived with stiff fingers and let them warm before handling small change. Wet cuffs stayed back from the notebook. I set the envelope on a dry part of the counter, out of reach of the damp marks left by whatever had just been brought through the door. Every Friday the amount was right. Every Friday I counted it, put it back together and carried it out in my pocket.

One television came back after it had already been through the shop. The customer set it on the counter with the old repair still part of the business to be settled. I found its entry in the notebook. The model number matched. There was the fault I had written down and the work that had been done before the set went out. The cabinet had the same marks around the back screws that it had carried the first time.

Lionel came to the counter while the customer was still there. He looked at the set and told me my repair had been sloppy. He said it in the space between us, with the television sitting at the edge of the counter. I had the notebook open under my hand. I took my hand off it, moved the intake slips aside and got a grip on the cabinet.

I said nothing. I carried the set to the bench, keeping it close enough that its weight did not pull at my wrists, and set it down with the back facing me. The customer stayed at the counter with Lionel. I removed the screws and put them together where none could roll beneath the chassis. The back came off with dust caught around the ventilation slots.

The cold solder joint was one Lionel had made himself. I knew the place from the earlier work. I had seen his iron there, and the joint had the solder he had left on it. It was dull around the lead, with a line where the solder had not taken properly. I moved the lead enough to see that it was loose in the joint. There was no need to search farther for that fault.

I let the iron heat while I set out the solder and cleared enough space to work. The old solder softened unevenly at first. I held the tip so the lead and the connection both took heat, then fed in fresh solder. It ran around the joint instead of sitting on top. I took the iron away and left the connection alone until the surface had set.

The set stayed on the bench while I checked the work. I looked around the joint for anything I might have disturbed getting at it. The screws were still together beside the back. I put the cabinet together, seating the back before tightening the screws, so I would not pull it crooked with the first one. Dust came off on the bench where the lower edge had rested.

At the counter, the notebook was still open to the same entry. I added the work in the space below it. My first description of the fault remained where I had put it. Lionel's statement to the customer did not go into the book. I had redone the joint, and that was what I wrote down. I returned the pen to its place, brushed the loose dust from the counter and moved the next slip into the clear space.

On Friday nights I walked straight from Dube's to the restaurant on Main Street. My pay envelope was in my pocket, and my hands smelled of the shop even after I had washed them. In the cold weather the walk was long enough for the cuffs of my trousers to stiffen where they were wet. I stamped the loose snow from my boots before opening the restaurant door.

The grade 10 crowd filled the back booths. Kyle and Pickle were usually far enough in that I had to turn sideways between a table and a coat to reach them. Someone would move a knee, and I would squeeze onto the seat with my jacket still on. There was never as much room as it had looked like from the aisle. Once I sat down, I had to arrange my feet among the boots underneath.

The table had plates and cups on it, with paper napkins pushed into the spaces between. I moved what was in front of me just far enough to put my elbows down. Kyle took up room when he talked. Pickle could get a comment in from the end of the seat while someone farther along was still speaking. With the tables close together, a conversation from the next booth could become part of ours without anyone getting up.

I kept the envelope in my pocket. Taking off my jacket meant finding somewhere to put it, and a coat laid across the seat was likely to be sat on. I worked one arm out at a time and folded it against my side when the restaurant got warm. The lining caught on the envelope flap. I pushed the flap flat through the cloth and left the money where it was.

Those were the Friday nights of grade 10, the last year Kyle and Pickle were around. We could occupy most of a booth without having much on the table. A cup would be moved to make room for someone's hands, then moved back when a plate arrived. I stayed squeezed in, listening and talking when there was an opening. If I went out to let someone pass, I had to work my way back to the same few inches of seat.

Outside, Main Street thinned out while we were still there. The windows held our reflections over the dark pavement. Meltwater spread beneath our boots and dried at the edges. When the door opened, cold air reached along the floor before the door closed again. I put my jacket back on a sleeve at a time, with my shoulders pressed against the seat.

We stayed until the owner began turning chairs onto the tables. The first chairs went up nearer the front, their legs pointing towards the ceiling. Then the clear part of the floor came closer to our booth. We gathered coats, moved the cups out of the way and slid along the seat to get out. I checked that the pay envelope was still in my pocket. On the pavement I pulled my cuffs straight over my wrists before starting home.

One Saturday that winter I went to confession at St. Anne. No one had told me to go. It had been two years since the last time. Snow had been carried through the entrance and ground into the mat, leaving a wet place beyond it where people had stepped onto the floor. I cleaned my boots as well as I could and went in wearing my winter coat.

I knew where to go. The church had the same divisions of aisle and pew, and I went past them to the confessional. My coat made it awkward to kneel. I pulled the bottom out from under my knees and settled it behind me. There was a worn place where hands had rested, and the wood close to my face had the dry smell of a heated room in winter.

Father Doucet was on the other side of the screen. I gave him the length of time since my last confession and began. I did not make the two years shorter. The words belonged to a procedure I still knew, although I had not used it in that time. I spoke towards the screen, stopped when I was finished and listened to what he said.

When it was done, I got up with one hand against the wood because the coat had caught beneath my leg again. I straightened it before stepping out. I stayed to finish the prayers I had been given. The floor under the pew was cold through the soles of my boots. When I went outside, the damp around the entrance had begun to freeze at its outer edge.

The next morning I was at early Mass with Mémère. She lived two streets over, and early meant getting dressed while the windows still gave little light. I wore a clean shirt under my coat. At St. Anne I stood beside her in the pew and put my gloves where I could reach them without searching through both pockets. Her purse sat close against her, with its fastening turned upwards.

I followed the standing, kneeling and sitting. Mémère moved at the proper times without looking to see what I was doing. Her coat brushed mine when we knelt. We left with the others after Mass, passing from the warm air by the doors into the cold outside. She closed her purse before putting on her gloves, using both hands to make sure the fastening had caught.

After that I went to confession every month on my own. It was not put on the household list or left for Hélène Lacasse to remind me. I went back to St. Anne and gave Father Doucet the time since the previous confession. In 1987 the interval was a month instead of two years. I wore the coat the weather required, wiped my boots at the entrance and pulled the hem clear before I knelt.

Mémère's television stopped working that winter, and she asked me to look at it. It stood in its usual place at her house, with the cord running behind it. I tried it and checked what I could there. I had no clear bench and none of the drawers of parts. I told her I would take it in to Dube's, where I could open it properly and get at what needed replacing.

The cabinet was awkward to carry through a doorway. I held it underneath and kept the cord gathered so it would not hang around my feet. At Dube's I put it on the bench and made an entry in the counter notebook. I wrote down the fault as I did for the other sets. Being Mémère's television did not give me a model number I could leave out or a cabinet I could put anywhere.

I took the back off and kept the screws together. The capacitor I replaced had its value marked on the body. I checked the marking before going to the drawers, then checked the replacement against it before fitting it. The drawer label was still clean enough to read at arm's length. I put the other parts back before returning to the bench, leaving only the replacement and the tools I needed beside the set.

The old leads came free when I heated the connections. I fitted the new capacitor in the same place, with enough clearance that its body did not rest against another part. After soldering, I clipped the excess from the leads and took the clipped ends off the bench. I checked the joints before putting the back on. The set worked, and I left it running long enough to check the repair before carrying it out.

I charged her the full counter rate for the capacitor and the labour. I used the same calculation I would have used on the slip for another set. The part had a price, and the work had a charge. I wrote both down and added them. There was no family rate on the paper. The total went at the bottom, with enough space around it to read it without following my pencil down the column.

At her kitchen table Mémère set out her purse. The television was back in its place. I put the charge where she could see it and opened the purse to take the money. The notes were folded in one section, with the coins in another. I separated what was needed, counted it on the table and checked it against the total. She sat opposite me while I did it.

I took the full amount. The coins had left small round damp marks where my fingers put them down, and I picked them up one at a time so none remained beside the paper. I put the rest of her money back in the purse and closed the fastening. Then I folded the charge slip along its existing crease. The capacitor and the labour were paid for, and I put the payment in my pocket.

Chapter 9

In the fall of 1987 I was sixteen and in grade 11 at Brule Falls High School, and I was working Saturdays and two evenings a week at Dube Radio and TV. The evenings were Tuesday and Thursday, six to nine. Saturday was nine to five with half an hour for lunch, which I ate at the bench. Lionel Dube paid me four dollars and twenty-five cents an hour, in cash, every second Saturday, in a small brown envelope with my name and the hours pencilled on the front. A full fortnight was twenty-seven hours, which came to a hundred and fourteen dollars and seventy-five cents, and Lionel paid it to the cent. If the shop was slow on a Tuesday and he sent me home at eight, the envelope said so.

Denis had been on the lake freighters since 1985. He was a deckhand on a bulk carrier running ore and grain between the Lakehead and the lower lakes, and he was gone from the end of March until the locks closed in January. He sent my mother a postcard every month or so, usually of a lock or a grain elevator, with three or four lines on the back about the weather and the cargo. She kept them on the fridge under a magnet from the hardware. His room was still his room. My father had not moved anything in it except to take the storm windows out from under the bed in October.

My father was still running Lacasse Hardware on Main Street, and my mother was still the secretary at the elementary school. The mill was running three shifts. My week was school, Dube's, home, and Dube's again.

I had kept my money in a coffee tin since I was seven. By the end of that summer the tin held four hundred and twelve dollars and sixty cents, most of it Dube's twenties folded once and the rest in quarters and dimes I had rolled at the kitchen table. On a Tuesday in September I carried it down to the bank across from the post office in a plastic bag from the hardware and opened a savings account. The teller counted the bills twice and the rolls once and wrote 412.60 in a blue bank book with my name typed inside the cover. I took the tin home empty and put screws in it.

The same week I bought a ledger at the drugstore for three dollars and forty-nine cents, a bound book with a green cloth cover and pages ruled in red and blue, the kind my father had used at the store before he went to carbon receipt books. I ruled it the way I wanted. On the left-hand pages went what came in: the date, the hours, the rate, the amount, and a running balance. On the right-hand pages went what went out. The first entry on the right was the ledger itself, 3.49.

After that every Dube pay went into the left column the Saturday I got it, and everything I bought went into the right. A pack of solder, sixty-forty rosin core, which Lionel sold me at cost, two dollars and ten cents. A chocolate bar, forty-five cents. A can of pop, sixty. A pair of work gloves, a haircut, a set of jeweller's screwdrivers. On Sunday nights after supper I sat at the kitchen table with the ledger, the bank book and my wallet and balanced them. What was in the bank plus what was in the wallet had to equal the last figure in the balance column. If it was out, it was usually out by sixty cents, and it was usually a pop I had not written down, and I wrote it down.

My father came through the kitchen one of those first Sundays for the coffee pot and stopped behind my chair. He read the page over my shoulder the way he read an invoice, with one finger going down the right-hand column.

"Solder," he said.

"Two-ten. Lionel gives it to me at cost."

"What's his cost?"

"Two-ten."

"Then he's not giving you anything." He poured his coffee. "You want a third column. Money owed."

"Nobody owes me anything."

"Not yet," he said, and took his coffee into the front room for the hockey.

My mother asked once what all the columns were for, and I showed her. She looked at it for a while and said it was very neat. I did not add a third column.

On Saturday mornings I left the house at twenty past eight and walked down to Main Street, and the way went past St. Anne. Saturday Mass was at seven-thirty and over by quarter past eight, so when I came by the doors were unlocked and the church was empty, except sometimes for the caretaker running a dust mop down the centre aisle.

The first time I went in was in September, the week a postcard came from Denis saying they had been held two days below the Soo for weather. I went up the steps, took off my cap, and went down the left side aisle to the second pew from the front, beside the rack of votive candles at the side altar. I knelt. The kneeler was cold. The church was only heated for Sunday, and the furnace had not come on yet that fall.

The candles were a quarter each. There was a brass slot in the rack for the money and a box of long wooden tapers. I put my quarter in, lit a taper off one of the candles already going, lit a new one in the second row, shook the taper out and stood it in the sand tray. Then I knelt again and said Denis's name and the name of his boat, and an Our Father, and got up. I was there perhaps five minutes.

After that I did it every Saturday until the shipping season closed in January, and again from the end of March. I did not tell my mother or my father. I did not tell Lionel why I sometimes came in smelling of taper smoke, and he did not ask. In the ledger the candles went in the right-hand column as candle, .25, the same as the solder. I was at the counter at Dube's by five to nine every one of those Saturdays. Lionel liked to look up at the clock over the door when I came in, and the clock was always on my side.

There was a boy in my shop class whose uncle had given him a dirt bike, a used 125 with a cracked rear fender and a kick start that took three or four tries. On Sunday afternoons that fall he took it out to the gravel pit two miles outside town on the old highway and let whoever came along ride it. In October I walked out there with three of the others after Mass. He rode ahead and waited for us at the township gate.

The township took gravel from the pit for the roads, and the loaders had cut faces into the sides of it anywhere from ten to forty feet high. The floor was flat and packed hard, with puddles in the low spots. The others rode loops around the floor and up the shallow ramp at the east end where the trucks came in. I did two loops. Then I rode up the truck ramp to the top and along the rim to the west face, the steepest in the pit, thirty feet or so of loose sand and gravel at the pitch of a roof. I stopped short of the lip with the engine idling and looked down. The others had stopped on the floor and were looking up.

I rolled over the lip in first gear, standing on the pegs, with the back brake on. The back wheel locked and slid and the front went straight down, and halfway I let off the brake because the bike was coming round sideways, and it straightened and ran the rest of the face with gravel coming down behind it. At the bottom the front wheel dug into the soft stuff where the face met the floor and the bars turned in my hands. I got a foot down and kept it up. The engine stalled.

The boy whose bike it was came over and looked at the front wheel and the forks and said it was fine.

I got off and pushed it back up the face. There was no riding up that sand. It took most of ten minutes: I walked it up beside me with the front brake on between pushes, digging my boots in, the sand going out from under me and the bike every few feet. At the top my shirt was soaked under my jacket. I walked the bike back from the lip along its own tracks, forty or fifty feet, to where the rim flattened out, so as to come at the face with some speed and land further out on the floor. I turned it around to face the lip, put it in first, and started kicking it over.

Mrs. Okafor taught grade 11 physics in the room at the end of the science wing, the one with the black lab benches and the gas taps nobody was allowed to turn on. She was strict about units and about writing out every step, and she gave a unit test every four weeks, forty marks, on legal-size paper run off on the ditto machine so the questions came out purple and smelled of the fluid.

The test at the end of November was on current electricity. Question seven was worth three marks: a four-ohm resistor and a six-ohm resistor in parallel across a twelve-volt battery; find the equivalent resistance and the total current. I had been doing that sum at Dube's for a year. I wrote out the reciprocal formula, put the numbers in, got two point four ohms, and divided it into twelve volts for five amps. I showed every step and put the units after every line.

The tests came back the next Monday. I had thirty-four out of forty. Question seven had a red X through it and a zero in the margin, and beside the X Mrs. Okafor had written 10 Ω, which is what you get if you add the two resistors as though they were in series. Either the answer key had been made for the wrong drawing, or she had marked from memory, or she had read it too fast.

I took out the textbook and turned to the section on parallel circuits. The worked example on page 312 was a three-ohm and a six-ohm resistor in parallel, with the same formula I had used set out step by step and the answer, two ohms, in a box at the bottom. I put my test beside the book and looked from one to the other. My working was the book's working.

Around me people were going up to Mrs. Okafor's desk with their tests to argue for half marks on questions they had got wrong. There was a line of four or five. She went through each one with her pen, gave back a mark here and there, and said no more often than yes.

I looked at the red X for a while. Then I closed the textbook, opened my binder to the divider marked Unit 3, punched the test on the three-hole punch on the windowsill, and put it on the rings with the zero showing. I let the teacher keep the wrong mark rather than argue it, and I have been letting people keep their wrong marks ever since.

At supper my mother asked how the physics test had gone, because she had the date on the calendar on the fridge. I said thirty-four out of forty. She said that was good. My father said that was eighty-five percent, and eighty-five percent of a hardware order was not a hardware order. I said it was a physics test. He said he knew that.

At Dube's I worked the repair counter and, more and more that fall, the second bench at the back. Lionel had the first bench, under the window, with his soldering station, a tube tester he still used for the old sets, and a Simpson meter older than I was. The second bench was mine on Tuesday and Thursday nights and on Saturday afternoons once the counter slowed. It had a soldering iron with a chisel tip, a meter of my own that I had bought used from Lionel for forty dollars and entered in the ledger, a box of clip leads, a set of nut drivers, and an isolation transformer to plug the sets into.

The filing cabinet beside the parts drawers held the service manuals Lionel had collected over twenty-five years. Each had a schematic that unfolded across the bench, a parts list, and a chart of the voltages you should read at each pin with the set working. Lionel mostly did not use them. He had seen every set that came through the door before, or one like it, and he knew what was wrong with it from the way the customer described it at the counter.

On a Thursday night in December a man carried in a twenty-inch table model from his truck and set it on the counter. He said the sound was fine but there was no picture; the screen just stayed black. He had had it four years. Lionel came out from the back, turned the set around, read the model number off the label, and nodded.

"Flyback," he said. "That chassis, it's always the flyback. I'll have to order one from the distributor in Sudbury. Sixty-eight dollars for the part, plus labour. Week, ten days."

The man said all right. Lionel wrote him a ticket, and the man went out. Lionel put the set on my bench.

"Pull the back and get the old flyback out for me," he said. "I'll call the order in tomorrow morning." He went back to his own bench, where he had a VCR open with the tape mechanism out on a towel.

I took the back off with a nut driver, plugged the set into the isolation transformer and turned it on. The sound came up at once. The screen stayed dark, with no raster, not even a line across the middle. There was no whine from the horizontal section, the high thin sound a working set makes.

I turned it off. Before I touched anything I got the manual for that chassis out of the filing cabinet. It was a stapled booklet of about forty pages with the schematic on a fold-out sheet in the middle. I unfolded it and held the corners down with a roll of solder and two nut drivers. The horizontal section ran left to right across the bottom of the sheet: the oscillator, then the driver, then the output transistor, then the flyback transformer, which made the high voltage for the picture tube. If any stage ahead of the flyback quit, the flyback would get nothing to work with, and there would be no picture whether the flyback was good or not.

I turned the set back on and started at the left. The chart gave the voltage at each pin to one decimal place. I clipped the black lead of my meter to chassis ground and went pin by pin with the red, and wrote each reading on a pad beside its number on the chart. The oscillator transistor read within a tenth of a volt of the chart on all three pins. The driver transistor did not. Its base was sitting at almost nothing, where the chart called for a small negative bias from the drive signal coming through, and its collector was sitting at full supply, which meant it was not switching. The output transistor after it read the same way: nothing coming in, nothing going on.

So the oscillator was making its signal and the driver was not getting it. Between the two on the schematic there was a coupling capacitor, ten microfarads, electrolytic. I turned the set off, waited, and found the capacitor on the board by its reference number. It was a small blue can standing near the edge of the board with a brown crust at its base where it had leaked. I took it out with the iron and a strip of braid and put the meter across it. It read open.

The parts drawers had ten-microfarad capacitors at the right voltage in the third row. They cost Lionel thirty-nine cents. I soldered the new one in, clipped the leads, looked over the joints with a magnifier, and turned the set on. The sound came up, then the whine, then the screen went grey from the centre out and the picture came up on the channel the man had left it on, the Sudbury news.

I let it run while I wrote up the ticket. I did not take the flyback out.

When Lionel came over at quarter to nine to tell me to sweep up, the set was still going on my bench. He looked at it, and at the pad with my readings, and at the old capacitor lying on the schematic.

"Coupling cap," I said. "Ahead of the driver. The flyback's good."

He picked up the old capacitor and turned it over and looked at the crust on it. He did not say anything for a bit.

"Huh," he said. "Well. Don't order the flyback, then." He put the capacitor down. "Charge him an hour."

"It was forty minutes."

"An hour's the rate for a bench repair."

I wrote one hour on the ticket, thirty-nine cents for the part, and no flyback. The man came in on Saturday, paid, and carried the set back out to his truck.

That was the year, I think, that I started to trust a manual more than a man. Lionel had been right about that chassis, he told me, eleven times running. The manual did not know about the eleven times and did not need to. It said what the voltages should be, the meter said what they were, and where the two did not agree was where the fault was.

Mrs. Okafor's unit on electromagnetism started after the Christmas holidays. It was magnets and compasses the first week, then the field around a straight wire, then coils, then induction, with a demonstration where she pushed a bar magnet in and out of a coil wired to a galvanometer and the needle swung one way and then the other. Most of it I knew from Dube's. What I did not know was in the last paragraph of the chapter, which said that light was an electromagnetic wave of the same kind as a radio wave, differing only in frequency, and then went on to the review questions. It did not say how anyone knew that.

I asked Mrs. Okafor after class. She said it came out of Maxwell's equations, which were university physics and needed calculus. She wrote MAXWELL on a slip of paper and gave it to me.

The town library was on the second floor of the town hall, over the clerk's office. The science section was a shelf and a half: an encyclopedia set from 1972, a book about the space program, a book of electrical experiments with dry cells, and a book about Einstein for general readers. The encyclopedia had a column on Maxwell with two equations I could not read. In a week of lunch hours I read everything on the shelf that touched the subject and came out with the same paragraph my textbook had given me, in different words.

I asked the librarian, and she told me about interlibrary loan, which I had not heard of. You filled out a card for what you wanted, she sent it to the regional library, and they found a library somewhere in the province that had it and would lend it. I did not know a title. She wrote on the card: university physics textbook, first year, electricity and magnetism, Maxwell's equations. There was no charge. She said three or four weeks.

It took twenty-six days. The book came from a university library in the south in a padded bag, with a slip in the front cover giving three weeks and no renewals. It ran to eleven hundred pages. I weighed it on the parcel scale at the hardware and it was four pounds twelve ounces.

I worked through the chapter on electromagnetic waves at the kitchen table, on Dube nights after I got home and on Sunday nights after the ledger. The derivations were in calculus, which I did not have, so I read over them and followed the argument in the words between. A changing magnetic field made an electric field, which was what Mrs. Okafor's coil and needle had shown. A changing electric field made a magnetic field in turn. So each could keep the other going across empty space with no wire at all. When Maxwell worked out how fast such a thing would travel, the speed came out of two constants that had been measured on a laboratory bench with capacitors and coils and had nothing to do with light. The answer was three hundred thousand kilometres a second, which was the speed of light as measured the other way, with mirrors.

The book had the two constants in a table at the back. I did the arithmetic myself on the back of a Dube's repair ticket, square root and all, on my calculator, and it came out to three point zero zero times ten to the eighth metres per second. So what came in on the rabbit ears of every set on my bench and what came in the window over Lionel's bench were the same thing at different frequencies. The book had a chart of it running from the long radio waves at one end to gamma rays at the other, with visible light a narrow band in the middle.

Mr. Szabo ate his lunch in the shop room at the desk by the tool crib, and anyone who wanted could come in and use the grinder or the drill press as long as he could see them. I went in on a Wednesday with the book under my arm. He had a thermos of soup and a sandwich in wax paper and was reading the Sudbury paper. Mr. Szabo had been an electrician before he was a teacher.

I told him radio waves and light were the same thing.

He put the paper down. "Radio is electricity," he said. "Light is light. You put a wire up, you get radio. Hold a wire up to a light bulb, you get nothing."

I said you got nothing because the wire was the wrong length. An antenna had to be about half as long as the wave it was catching. A radio wave was metres long. A light wave was less than a thousandth of a millimetre. Hold up a wire that size and it would work.

"Nobody can make a wire that size."

I said that was a problem with wire, not with light, and opened the book to the chart and showed him the radio band and the light band on the same line.

He looked at it a long time, chewing. He said if they were the same, why could you see one and not the other. I said because the eye only worked in that one narrow band. He said a dog could hear a whistle he couldn't, and that didn't make the whistle a radio. I said it wasn't about what you could see, it was about what the thing was, and you could measure the speed of both and it was the same speed. I showed him my arithmetic on the repair ticket. He took a calculator out of his desk drawer and did the square root himself. It came out the same.

"That's two numbers in a book," he said. "Somebody measured them."

"Somebody measured everything in the book."

He said in his trade you trusted what you could put a meter on. I said you could put a meter on these; they were measured with capacitors and coils, the same as anything at Dube's. He said Maxwell had never been to Dube's. We went back and forth with the book open between his soup and the drill press until the bell went at twelve-forty, and neither of us had moved an inch. He folded his paper and said I would be late for French.

The book went back to the town library on the day it was due. I had copied the table of constants and the chart into the back of my notebook, with the arithmetic.

In February a girl in my homeroom had her father hurt at the mill. He was on nights in the finishing room and a roll came off a hoist; it broke his leg in two places and his hip, and he was sent to the hospital in Sudbury. On the Monday the principal announced on the PA that the school was taking up a collection for the family, and every homeroom got a brown envelope with the family's name written on the front.

I had my Dube pay in my shirt pocket. Lionel paid at five on Saturday, after the bank had closed, and I always took it in on Monday after school. It was a hundred and fourteen dollars and seventy-five cents, still in his envelope.

The collection envelope started at the front desk by the window and came back row by row. People put in a dollar or two, or change; one boy put in a five. The girl had not come to school that day. When the envelope got to me I held it a moment and passed it over my shoulder to the desk behind without putting anything in. Nobody said anything. It went on around the room and up to the teacher's desk, and she sealed it and sent it to the office with a boy from the front row.

After school I walked down to the bank across from the post office and deposited the whole pay. The teller stamped the bank book and wrote in 114.75 and the new balance, and I checked the balance against the one I had in my head. That night I wrote the deposit in the ledger beside the pay.

At supper my mother said the elementary school had taken up a collection too, because the family's two younger ones were in grade 2 and grade 4, and the staff had put in twenty dollars each. My father said the store would send a gift certificate over. My mother asked if the high school had done anything. I said they had passed an envelope around homeroom. She said that was good.

The ski trip was the first Friday in March. It cost thirty-one dollars for the bus, the lift ticket and rental skis, boots and poles, paid at the office in cash with a permission form my mother signed, and it went in the right-hand column. I had never been skiing. I signed up mostly for the bus. Most of grade 11 was going, and it was three hours there and three back.
