The buses left the school lot at quarter to six in the morning. It was twenty-four below, and the drivers had had them running half an hour before anybody got on. I sat halfway back with two boys from shop class and slept part of the way with my head against the window, which was frosted on the inside.

The hill had a T-bar on the main run and a rope tow on the beginners' slope beside the lodge. The rentals were in the lodge basement. The man at the counter looked me up and down, handed me boots with four clamp buckles, and set the bindings with a screwdriver without asking my weight.

I spent the day on the rope tow. It ran off a gas engine in a shed at the bottom, over pulleys on posts up the side of the slope, and it was about four hundred feet long. You took hold of it with both mitts and it pulled you up, twisting in your hands. By ten o'clock my leather mitts were wet through and the palms were black. I rode it up and came down in a snowplough and rode it up again.

In the morning I went with the two shop boys until they moved over to the T-bar. Then I went with three girls from my English class who were also on the rope tow, until they went in to warm up. At lunch I had a bowl of chili in the lodge for two dollars and twenty-five cents and a hot chocolate for seventy-five, at a long table with a group from my math class, and in the afternoon I went up and down with them. The last hour I went with whoever was in the line ahead of me. I did not go on the T-bar.

The buses left at five. I got on near the last, and the only seat on ours was the middle of the back bench, between two boys from the other grade 11 homeroom. The heater at the back ran hot and the floor was wet with slush from the boots, and the bus smelled of wet wool. It was dark by half past five. The boy on my right went to sleep against the window and the boy on my left talked across me to someone in the next seat up for most of the first hour. I sat with my knees against the seat in front and looked up the aisle at the road coming into the headlights through the windshield. We got to the school at quarter past nine, and my father was in the lot with the truck running. On Sunday the chili and the hot chocolate went in the ledger.

I had got my learner's permit the spring before, the week after I turned sixteen, and my father had taken me out in the store's half-ton on summer evenings on the mill road. It was a three-speed on the column. I booked the road test for a Tuesday in May 1988 at the town test centre, a one-storey building beside the arena with a gravel lot. The fee was fifteen dollars, and it went in the right-hand column.

My father drove me there in the half-ton and sat inside on the bench by the counter. The examiner was a man with a clipboard who had me work the signals, the brake lights and the horn before we left the lot. We went out onto Main, left at the lights, down past the hardware, and up to a side street by the water tower, where he had me do a three-point turn. Then he had me park behind a parked car. I did all of that the way my father had shown me.

Coming back, at the stop sign at the bottom of the hill below the water tower, I slowed, looked left and right, saw nothing coming either way, and rolled through at a walking pace without the wheels coming to a full stop. The examiner wrote on his clipboard. He did not say anything until we were back in the lot with the engine off.

"You've failed," he said. "Rolling stop at the water tower. You can rebook at the counter. Same fee."

I said all right. I went inside past the counter. My father stood up off the bench.

"Well?"

"Rolling stop."

He looked at the counter and back at me. "You'll book again."

"Sometime," I said. "I'm on at Dube's at six."

He drove me to Dube's.

I did not rebook. I went back to walking to school and walking to Dube's, and getting rides from my father on wet nights, and from Lionel in the van when we ran late on a Thursday. In June my mother asked whether I had booked the test again. I said no. The learner's permit stayed in my wallet behind the bank book receipt, and the licence sat for most of a year. In the ledger the fifteen dollars is on the right-hand page for the third week of May, between a pack of solder and a candle, and there is no second one.

Chapter 10

In 1988 I started grade twelve at Brule Falls High School. I was seventeen, and I still worked at Dube Radio and TV after school and on Saturdays. My school papers went into the upper drawer of my desk at home. The slips with my hours and pay went into the drawer below. I kept better track of the lower drawer. Each time I put money in the bank, I entered the amount in my notebook and drew a line under the new balance. At school, I folded notices and put them in my coat pocket until the corners wore through.

Calculus had seemed a reasonable course to put on the timetable. I could rearrange an equation and substitute numbers, and I had usually done enough in mathematics to get through without much work at home. At first I copied the examples from the board. I ruled the margins and put the date at the top. The first pages looked like the work of somebody who intended to use them. I had a sharp pencil, an eraser without a black edge, and all the headings in the right places.

The problem sets grew longer. A page that had held six questions began to hold twelve, with parts underneath each number. I did the first few and left the others. Then I copied the questions without working them. I brought the book home in my bag and put it beside the desk. After supper I sometimes opened it, looked at where I had stopped, and shut it again. Nothing prevented me from doing the work. The desk was there, the lamp worked, and nobody needed the space.

On the first test, I knew the opening questions because they resembled the examples I had copied. Farther down the page, I wrote out the information given and could not turn it into the next line. I left spaces so I could return to them. By the end, the spaces were still there. When the test came back, the mark was a failure. I folded it once and put it inside the textbook. The red marks showed through the thin paper where I had pressed the fold.

I went to the guidance office and changed to the easier mathematics course. There was a form, a place for the course I was leaving, and another for the course I wanted. I printed the names carefully. I did not take the failed test out and work through it. I did not ask for extra questions or arrange to make up the homework. Once the change had been made, I put a different book in my bag. The truth is that I was lazy, and no amount of later polish changes it.

English and history went much the same way, without a change of course. I read far enough to answer what was likely to be asked. If a written answer required a page, I stopped near the bottom of the page. I checked due dates more often than I checked what I had written. Papers came back with comments that would have required another attempt, and I filed them under the next assignment. My marks were enough for me to continue. I left them there and spent the available part of the evening on whatever I had already chosen to do.

The college brochure fitted into the same desk drawer, but it did not stay there. It described the instrumentation program in Sudbury, the admission requirements, and the work the courses were supposed to lead to. I read it at the kitchen table with a sheet of paper beside me. I copied the subjects and put a mark beside the ones whose names I understood. There were measurements of temperature, pressure, level and flow. Those were quantities I could picture on a dial or write down with a unit after the number.

The word instrumentation covered more than I had first taken it to cover. At Dube Radio and TV I had used a meter to find whether voltage was present and whether a connection was open. The brochure described using an electrical signal to stand for something happening elsewhere. I went to the library to find out what sat between the pressure in a pipe and the number somebody read. I found explanations of thermocouples and pressure transmitters and copied the parts I could follow into my notebook.

A thermocouple began with two different metals joined together. That was simple enough to draw. The explanation after it took more room. I drew the joined end, brought the two lines back across the page, and labelled the other ends. A voltage appeared in the circuit according to the temperatures at the junctions. The voltage was small. I wrote millivolts in full the first time, then used the abbreviation. Beside the drawing I put a box around the fact that the temperature at the measuring end was not the only temperature involved.

For several evenings I read about why the two metals made that voltage. I could repeat the description sooner than I could explain it. When the account went beyond the diagrams and into a derivation, I turned back to the diagrams. I made another sketch with a hot junction and a reference junction and followed the explanation that used those names. I wanted enough to understand what a reading represented. The longer mathematical treatment stayed where it was. I did not work it out on another sheet.

The pressure transmitter was easier for me to picture. Pressure acted on an element that moved or changed under the load, and the transmitter converted that change into a signal. I drew a diaphragm as a bowed line, then drew it again without the bow. The account described a current signal that could travel to another instrument. I copied the range of four to twenty milliamps and wrote down what the two ends represented. I had no transmitter in front of me to open. The drawing and the book were as far as I went.

After shop one day, I stayed by Mr. Szabo's desk and asked whether a thermocouple measured heat or measured difference. He said temperature, and I said the book had made a point of the two junctions. He asked what I meant by difference. I opened the notebook and showed him my drawing. We went over which end was being measured and what had to be known about the reference end. I kept returning to the voltage depending on both. He kept separating heat from temperature. The other desks had emptied while we were talking. I put the notebook away with the drawing still showing through the cover and went to work at Lionel Dube's.

The college recruiter came to the guidance office with printed material and a figure that was easier to remember than the course descriptions. Ninety-five percent of graduates had jobs within six months. I wrote ninety-five, put a percent sign after it, and underlined six months. The office had chairs set close enough that I kept my knees together to leave room for the papers. The recruiter talked about graduates and employers. I looked again at the figure I had written and at the instrumentation heading in the brochure.

There were several things the sentence did not settle. It did not tell me whether the jobs were connected to the training. It did not tell me whether the figure covered every program together or the one I was considering. I asked about instrumentation. The answer brought me back to the college's graduates more generally. I kept the sheet. There was room in the margin for my questions, and I wrote them there rather than across the information I would need for the application.

At the repair counter, I asked Lionel Dube what he had heard about the program and about work for people who had finished it. I gave him the ninety-five percent figure. He asked whether it meant work in the trade. I said that was what I wanted to find out. We had the brochure on the counter between us. He read the part I indicated, then gave it back. What he had heard did not supply a set of placement figures for that program. I folded the brochure on its existing crease and took it home.

I wrote to the college asking for the actual figures by program. I used a clean sheet, put my address at the top, and named instrumentation in the first paragraph. I asked how many had graduated, how many had work within six months, and how many of those jobs were related to the course. I also asked which graduating group the figures described. The letter was short enough that I could copy it without crossing anything out. I kept the rough version in the desk drawer so I would know exactly what I had asked.

When the reply came, I opened the envelope along the top and set both sheets flat. I read it once, then read it again with my letter beside it. I put a pencil mark against each part that answered a question and went back over the wording where it did not match what I had asked. I paid attention to the headings above the figures and the words beneath them. I did not turn the reply into a different promise. I folded it and kept it with the brochure and my copy of the letter.

I applied to the instrumentation program in Sudbury. The application required information I could get from the school and details I could copy from my own papers. I filled the spaces, checked the address, and put the pages in order. Armand asked what the course was called, and I told him. Hélène looked over the completed form for an empty box. Once it had gone, I continued with the easier mathematics course and the rest of my timetable. The brochure stayed on the desk, with the corners beginning to turn up from being opened at the same pages.

The 1989 winter carnival took up the whole weekend by the river. It was the large event of our grade twelve year, and I spent it on the grounds with Tina Marchand's crowd and the rest of the grade. The snow where people walked had packed into a hard, uneven surface. Near the edges, loose snow worked over the tops of boots. I wore the same winter coat through the weekend and brought my mitts inside at night so they would be dry enough to put on again in the morning.

Tina decided when we were going down and where we were to meet. I went at the time she gave me. Once we were there, the group shifted from one part of the grounds to another, and I shifted with it. People from school stood in bunches with their shoulders raised inside their coats. The river was beyond the marked areas, and the wind had a clear run across the grounds. When we stopped moving, I worked my fingers inside my mitts and stamped the snow that had lodged under my heels.

I played in the broomball game. The broom handle was awkward with mitts on, and the surface had been scraped and scuffed by the time I went out. I ran after the ball, stopped badly, and had to turn back when it went behind me. There was no useful place to stand still. I followed the play from one end to the other and took my turn coming off. Snow had got between my boot tongue and my sock. I pulled the tongue straight before going back out and finished the game with the same damp patch against my shin.

For the tug-of-war, we lined up on the rope with room between us to lean back. I wrapped my mitts around it and set one boot behind the other. The first pull took the slack out and brought everybody closer to the same angle. My heels cut short tracks. I moved a hand, got hold again, and pulled when the others pulled. The rope dragged across the rough surface of my mitts. Afterwards I opened both hands and worked the fingers, then followed Tina and the others to the next part of the grounds.

There were stretches when we stood waiting for something to start, and stretches when half the grade seemed to be moving through the same narrow gap. I stayed through both. We went home at the end of each day and came back again. By Sunday, the packed paths had a grey surface, and the snow pushed up beside them contained everything boots had brought from the road. I knew where the bad footing was near the boards. I still put a foot into it once and came out with a crust of wet snow on my trouser cuff.

On Sunday night I was still there when the boards came down. I helped lift them clear and carry them to the stack. The lower edges had frozen into the packed snow, and we rocked them loose before lifting. I took an end when it was handed to me and set it down where the others were putting theirs. Without the boards, the area looked wider and less definite. I brushed the snow off my coat sleeves. Tina was still on the grounds with the crowd, and I stayed until the part I was carrying had been put away.

I went out with Tina for six weeks. That included the carnival, though most of our time together did not require winter games or a whole grade around us. She chose where we went and when we met. She would tell me after a class or when we were leaving the school, and I would say that the time suited me. If she changed it, I used the new time. I did not put forward somewhere else to go. My part was to arrive with my coat done up and whatever money I expected to spend in my pocket.

Sometimes the arrangement was only to walk after school. Tina chose the direction, and I walked beside her. If she wanted to stop, we stopped. If she wanted to meet the others, I went with her to meet them. I knew when I had to be at Dube Radio and TV, and I told her those hours. Outside them, I let her put the outings in place. I did not keep a separate list of things I wanted us to do. There was no list to compare with hers.

She talked more than I did. I answered what she asked, but I could let a question sit until she supplied another one. She would say what day she meant, and I would check it against work. She would name the place, and I would repeat the time. At the end of an outing, I went home by the ordinary route. Hélène sometimes asked whether I had been with Tina. I said yes and hung up my coat. I gave the answer as though it accounted for the evening, which was generally all I had to report.

After six weeks, Tina stopped with me on the school steps. Other people were leaving around us, so we stood to one side of the way down. The steps were damp where the snow had been carried onto them. I had my bag over one shoulder. Tina said it was over. She did not leave the statement unfinished or ask me what I wanted to do about it. I looked at her and said, "okay". Then I went down the steps and started home.

I did not ask why. I did not ask whether she meant for that day or altogether. There was no second conversation on the steps. I walked past the places I passed on other school days, with the bag striking against the back of my coat. At home I took out the books I needed and left the others in the bag. I did not write anything about Tina in the notebook where I kept my money. The six weeks had not required an entry when they began, and I made none when they ended.

At school afterwards, Tina still had the same corridors and doors to use. I did not change my route to produce a meeting, and I did not follow her to continue the conversation. If the grade was gathered somewhere, I stood with it. The carnival weekend remained a weekend I had spent with her crowd. There was no property to return and no arrangement I had to cancel. I went to my classes, then to the repair counter when I was due there. I had let her set the beginning, the meetings, and the end.

One night, two boys from shop class said nobody would climb the outside of the mill's chip conveyor. The bet concerned the steel gantry that rose towards the chip pile. From below, the conveyor cut a long slant through the dark. The boys kept the terms simple: up the outside to the top, where it met the pile. I had a flashlight. We agreed that they would stay below and watch for it. There was no need to bring anybody else into it, and we did not.

Up close, the steel had less room around it than it appeared to have from the ground. I put the flashlight in my coat pocket so I could use both hands. I started where I could get a boot onto a lower member and reached for the next upright. The surface left dirt on my hands. I moved along the outside, using the points where the members joined rather than trying to keep my soles on the broad sloping faces. After each move I brought the other foot up before reaching again.

The conveyor housing ran beside me. Bolts and flanges interrupted the straight edges. At some places I could stand with most of a sole supported, and at others I had only the front of the boot on the steel. I kept my coat clear where a corner would catch it. When I wanted to see the next hold, I took out the flashlight, looked, and put it away again. Below, the two boys were shapes near the place where I had started. I did not call down to them.

Farther up, the chip pile occupied more of what I could see beyond the structure. The smell of the wood was stronger there. Bits of material had lodged where horizontal pieces met the uprights, and I brushed a place clear before putting my hand on it. The route did not require a calculation. It required the next handhold and somewhere to put a boot. I went on until I reached the upper part where the gantry met the pile, then worked myself into a sitting position on the steel.

I took the flashlight out and held it where the boys below could see it. I kept it there long enough to make the signal plain. From that position I could see the lower length I had used and the ground beyond it. The light showed my sleeve and the edge of the member under me when I lowered it. I put it back in the pocket before standing. There was nothing else in the bet to do at the top, and I did not stay to find something.

Coming down, I used the same joints in the steel, looking below my boots before I lowered them. I turned where I had turned on the way up. Near the bottom I could hear the boys properly again. I stepped off onto the ground and rubbed my hands together to get rid of the loose dirt. They had seen the flashlight. That settled what they had said nobody would do. I checked that I still had it in my pocket, and we left the conveyor where it was.

Near the end of school, a classmate asked to borrow forty dollars. His family's car had died, and he needed the bus to Sudbury for a job interview. He told me the amount and what it was for without making a long account of it. We were in the school, with enough space between us and the next group for an ordinary conversation. I knew the fare was money he needed before he could get on the bus. I also knew what I had in the bank.

By the entries in my ledger, the balance was more than six hundred dollars. The latest deposit had been entered, and I had checked the addition. My college money was not all in cash divided into envelopes. Most of it was simply that balance, with figures in the notebook to show how it had accumulated. Forty dollars was an amount I could have taken out. There was no uncertainty in the arithmetic and no need to wait until I got home to discover whether I had it.

He asked for a loan, not a gift. I said no. I did not open my bag or reach into a pocket while I considered it. I left my hands where they were and gave him the answer. He had explained about the car and the interview already. I did not ask him to repeat either part. There was no smaller amount that I offered in place of the forty, and I did not suggest that he come back after my next pay.

The conversation ended there. I did not go to Lionel Dube with the problem or ask Armand whether there was another way for the classmate to reach Sudbury. I did not look at the bus times for him. He had asked me for a particular thing, and I had refused it. When I went to work, the hours I put in were my own hours at the repair counter. When I came home, the money in the bank was still there, apart from anything already recorded in the book.

That evening I opened the ledger to the last used page. I followed the column down with a finger and checked the balance again. There was no forty-dollar subtraction to enter and no loan to note beside somebody's name. I had ruled the page to leave room for deposits and withdrawals, and the empty line below the total remained empty. I shut the book and put it in the drawer with the other pay records. I did not learn anything further about the interview, and I did not go looking for an account of it.

As the time for Sudbury came nearer, I took my tools and parts out of the bedroom closet and laid them on the floor. I had three tackle boxes to divide them between. I opened all three so I could see the trays at once. The hinges and catches worked, but the compartments were different sizes. A screwdriver that fitted along the bottom would not fit across a divided tray. I began with the long tools, put them where the lids would close, and arranged the smaller things around those decisions.

I sorted screwdrivers, pliers, cutters and the rest of the hand tools before dealing with the loose parts. Some parts still had their values or sizes marked on packets. Others needed to be compared before I put them together. I separated screws by size and kept small nuts where they would not pass underneath a divider. Anything with wire ends went into a compartment where the ends would not be bent each time I took out something beside it. The floor slowly cleared around the open boxes.

Each tray received a label. I printed the words rather than writing them in the hand I used for school notes. I wanted to be able to open a box and read the label from above. Where one tray contained several kinds of parts, I put more than one line on the label. I moved things twice when the wording made the arrangement sound more definite than it was. Then I shut the trays, closed the lids, and tried the catches with the boxes upright.

I made an inventory for each box on a separate sheet. The tools were listed by type and size. For the small parts, I wrote what the compartment contained and counted what was worth counting separately. I left enough space at the top to identify the box without unfolding the whole sheet. When each list matched its contents, I taped it inside the lid. I pressed the tape down along its full length. The paper lay clear of the hinge and did not fold into the tray when I shut it.

After that I weighed what I could carry on the bus. I weighed a box, wrote the figure down, and did the same for the next one. I set them beside the other things I was taking and tried the handles together. Weight on a sheet was one part of it; getting a hand around the handles was another. I took out things I had duplicates of and weighed the boxes again. The removed pieces went back on the floor in a separate group, away from anything already entered on an inventory.

I made a second list for what would stay in the closet at home. That list included the pieces removed for weight and the stock I had not intended to carry. I checked the three inventories against it so that an item did not appear in both places. Then I put the things that were staying onto the closet shelf and left the list with them. The boxes for Sudbury stood closed in my bedroom. If I opened one to check something, I put it back against the same edge of the wall.

During the last week of August, before I left for Sudbury, I went to seven o'clock Mass at St. Anne every morning with Mémère. It was my choice. She had not set the week as something I owed her, and Hélène had not put it among the things to do before leaving. I arranged to walk with Mémère and was ready each morning. The tackle boxes remained in the bedroom while I put on the clothes I had set out and went to meet her.

The mornings were cool enough for a jacket. The pavement was darker where it held damp, and there was little traffic on the streets we used. Mémère kept her usual walking pace. I walked beside her without taking her arm or carrying anything for her. At the church, we went through the door and along the aisle to a pew. I followed her into it and left enough room between us to kneel. My watch showed how much time remained before seven. I pushed my sleeve back down over it.

I knew the order of the Mass. I stood when it was time to stand and knelt when it was time to kneel. The wooden edge ahead of me had been worn smooth where hands rested. I put mine there and later put them back in my lap. Father Doucet was at the front. Mémère followed what he was doing without turning to check whether I followed it too. When the collection came along, I passed it on. I kept my own money in my pocket.

We came out by the same door and walked back through town. Mémère had her morning arranged beyond Mass. I had packing and the ordinary preparations for leaving. At the place where our routes separated, I said I would see her the next morning. Then I went home and took off the jacket. The day had warmed enough by then that I did not need it indoors. Before working on anything else, I hung it where I would find it when I got up again.

I went each morning of that last week. There was no extra arrangement after the first one. I got ready, met Mémère, and walked to St. Anne. The repeated part took very little deciding. By the last morning, the boxes in my room had their inventories taped in place and the things staying home were in the closet. I did not take a list to church. I had nothing to check there against the weight I could manage on the bus.

On that last morning, Father Doucet was at the door as we were leaving. I stopped and asked him for a blessing. I used those words and stayed where I was while he gave it. Mémère waited beside me. When he had finished, I thanked him, and we stepped outside. I did not add an account of the college or tell him what I expected from it. Mémère and I went down the steps together, and I walked with her until we reached the place where I turned for home.

Chapter 11

In September 1989 I was eighteen, and on the Saturday of the Labour Day weekend I took the bus down to Sudbury to start the two-year instrumentation technician program at the college. The bus left at seven in the morning and got in a little after one. It was hot for September, close to thirty degrees, and the bus had no air conditioning that worked, so I rode most of the way with the window cranked down two inches and my duffel bag on the seat beside me so nobody would take it. I had two hundred and eleven dollars in my wallet, the rest of my money in the bank, a letter from the residence office with my room number on it, and three tackle boxes in a cardboard carton under the bus that I had taped shut on four sides.

The residence was a brick block of four storeys at the back of the campus, facing a parking lot and a stand of birch. My room was on the third floor, at the end of the east corridor. It was about eleven feet by fourteen, with cinderblock walls painted the colour of weak tea, a linoleum floor, two steel-framed beds, two desks with three drawers each, two wooden chairs, a shelf unit bolted to the wall over each desk, and one window with an aluminum frame that slid sideways. There was a radiator under the window that clanked when I touched it, though it was not on. The room smelled of floor wax and of whoever had been in it the year before. The other half had not been claimed. The office had told me my roommate would arrive Sunday.

I set up my half that first night. I took the bed by the window because it was there and the other one was nearer the door, where the corridor light came under. I made it with the sheets I had brought, a grey wool blanket on top, and folded the corners under the way I had been shown, tight enough that the blanket did not lift when I pressed it in the middle. I put the three tackle boxes under the desk, stacked largest at the bottom. They were green Plano boxes, two of them the cantilever kind with trays that swung out, and in the lid of each one I had taped an inventory list on a file card: in the first, screwdrivers by size, a set of jeweller's screwdrivers, needle-nose and side-cutting pliers, a wire stripper, a soldering iron and stand, a spool of sixty-forty rosin-core solder, desoldering braid and a roll of electrical tape; in the second, resistors in labelled film canisters, capacitors, a bag of diodes, fuses, terminal lugs, and a small multimeter with its leads wrapped around it; in the third, nuts, bolts, machine screws, washers, and a few odds that did not belong in either of the others. I checked each box against its card before I slid it in. Nothing had shifted on the bus.

I put the course binders on the shelf in the order they appeared on my timetable: Process Control, Electrical Circuits, Instrumentation Fundamentals, Technical Mathematics, Communications. Each binder had its own colour of dividers. Then I opened a ledger I had bought for two dollars and forty-nine cents at a drugstore on the way from the bus station. It was a green clothbound book with red and blue ruling, the kind used for small business accounts. On the first page I wrote the date and the opening balance of my bank account, and below that I entered tuition, seven hundred and thirty-five dollars, paid by cheque in August, and the residence deposit, one hundred dollars, paid in July. I left space for the residence bill, which had not come, and for books, which I had not bought. I entered the bus ticket and the ledger itself. Then I took a roll of masking tape and a black marker and labelled the three desk drawers, top to bottom: TOOLS, PARTS, PAPER. In the top drawer I put the things I used every day: a pencil case, a ruler, a scientific calculator, a flashlight. In the middle drawer I put a coffee can of spare batteries and some wire. In the bottom drawer I put the letter from the residence office, my timetable, and a pad of graph paper. I went to bed at a quarter to eleven. The corridor was quiet because most people were not in yet.

Sanjay Mehta arrived the next afternoon, a little after three. I heard him before I saw him. He was talking to someone in the stairwell, and then talking to someone else on the landing, and then he came along the corridor with a hockey bag over one shoulder and a cardboard box in both arms and kicked the door open with his foot because it had swung half shut. He was about my height, thinner, with thick black hair and a moustache he was trying to grow. He was wearing a university sweatshirt from somewhere else and jeans with the knees gone. He put the box down on the floor, said his name, shook my hand, and said he was in engineering, first year. Then he looked around the room.

He walked to the window, looked out at the parking lot, and dropped the hockey bag on my bed. The blanket went down under it. He sat on the end of the bed, bounced once, and said this was the one for him; he liked to see outside when he woke up. Then he got up and went to the desks. My desk was against the wall on the window side, his on the door side. He pulled his desk out, swung it around, and pushed it up under the window so that it sat crosswise to the bed, where he could see the parking lot from his chair. That left no room for my desk where it was, so he pulled mine away from the wall, with the tackle boxes still under it scraping on the linoleum, and pushed it over to the inside wall by the door, where his had been. He did it all in about two minutes, talking the whole time about the drive up, the traffic coming out of the city, a girl he had met at a gas station in Parry Sound.

I stood by the closet and watched him do it. I said nothing. When he was finished I went to the window bed, lifted his hockey bag off it and set it on the floor, and stripped the bed: blanket, top sheet, bottom sheet, pillowcase. I folded them over my arm, carried them across to the bed by the door, and made that one up the same way I had made the first, with the corners tucked and the blanket tight. I moved the tackle boxes back square under my desk where he had left them crooked, and checked that the drawers still opened. Sanjay watched me for a while and then said I was very neat. I said I was. He unpacked a hot plate, a box of instant noodles, a clock radio which he set on his desk and did not turn on, and four pairs of running shoes. By suppertime there were three people from down the hall in the room, sitting on his bed and on his desk, and I went to the cafeteria.

Classes started on the Tuesday. Process control was taught by Dr. Ruth Abernethy, who was in her fifties, short, with grey hair cut straight across at the level of her jaw and a pair of half-glasses on a cord. She had worked, we were told, at a refinery in Sarnia and then at one of the smelters before she came to teach. She did not take attendance. She wrote her name on the board, then under it, in capital letters, FEEDBACK, and under that she drew a box, an arrow going into it, an arrow coming out, and a line from the output arrow curving back around underneath to a circle at the input with a minus sign in it. She said that was the whole course, and the rest was detail.

She used a level tank to explain it. Water comes in at the top through a valve, goes out at the bottom through a pipe. You want the level held at a mark on the side of the tank. That mark is the setpoint. A float or a pressure cell measures where the level actually is; that is the measured variable. The difference between the two is the error. The controller looks at the error and moves the valve. If the level is low it opens the valve; if the level is high it closes it. The measurement goes back around to the controller, and the loop is closed. She said a man standing at the tank with his hand on the valve wheel and his eye on the gauge glass was a feedback controller, and a poor one, because he got tired and went for coffee. She said the pneumatic controllers in the lab worked on three to fifteen pounds per square inch, and the electronic ones on four to twenty milliamps, and we would learn why the bottom of the range was not zero. Then she said that every loop could be made to behave badly, and the most common way was too much gain, and she put down the chalk and that was the end of the hour.

The textbook was a hardcover the size of a phone book, and it cost forty-two dollars and fifty cents at the college bookstore. I entered it in the ledger that afternoon, with the other books: the circuits text, the mathematics text, a lab manual, and a pack of lab sheets, two hundred and twelve dollars and forty cents altogether. The chapter on loop stability was chapter seven. The lecture schedule had us reaching it in November. I read it that week, before the second lecture, sitting at my desk with the lamp on and the door shut. A good deal of it was over my head on the first reading: transfer functions, Bode plots, phase margin. I read it a second time and then a third and copied the diagrams onto graph paper, and I kept coming back to one question, which was why a loop with too much gain did not simply get to the setpoint faster, but went past it, and came back past it the other way, and kept on swinging.

The answer was in the delay. Nothing in a real process happens at once. The valve takes time to stroke. The water takes time to reach the tank. The level takes time to rise, and the transmitter takes time to see it. By the time the controller sees that the level has reached the mark, there is already water in the pipe that has not arrived, and the valve is still open, and the level carries on up past the mark. Then the controller sees the error the other way and closes the valve hard, and by the time the level stops falling it has gone below the mark. With low gain the controller makes small corrections and the swings die out. With more gain each correction is bigger, and if the gain is high enough the swings come back as large as they went out, or larger, and the loop oscillates at a steady period that depends on the delay. The book called it the ultimate gain and the ultimate period. I worked through the example in the text with a pencil, half a degree at a time, building a table of where the level would be every few seconds, until I could see the swings growing on the paper.

Sanjay came in that Thursday at about midnight. I was still at my desk. He had been at the pub on campus and he smelled of it, though he was walking straight. He said hello and sat down on his bed to take his shoes off, and I turned my chair around and said I wanted to show him something. I took a lab sheet out of the bottom drawer, turned it over to the blank side, and drew the tank on it, and the valve, and the controller, and the line going back around. Then I drew a time axis along the bottom and the setpoint as a dashed line across, and I drew the level for low gain, a slow curve coming up and settling in under the line, and the level for medium gain, coming up quicker and going over once and back and settling, and the level for high gain, going up past the line and down below it and up again, each swing as tall as the last. I told him about the water in the pipe that had not arrived yet. I told him the period of the swing came from the delay and not from the controller. He said he had to be up for an eight-thirty. I said it would take another five minutes, and I went on to the phase lag, and how if the delay around the loop added up to half a cycle the correction arrived exactly backwards, pushing the way the error was already going. He said it was like a shower with a long pipe, where you turn it hotter and nothing happens and you turn it more and then you get scalded and turn it all the way cold. I said that was it, and wrote SHOWER at the top of the sheet. He lay back on his bed with his clothes on and said he was going to sleep now, and he did. I pinned the lab sheet to the cork strip over my desk with two thumbtacks and went to bed at one.

In the second lecture Dr. Abernethy drew the same three curves on the board, in the same order, though hers were neater. She asked if anyone could say why the third one kept going. Nobody answered at first, and then I said it was the delay in the loop, that the correction arrived late. She looked at me over her half-glasses and asked how late. I said if it was half a cycle late it would be pushing the wrong way. She said that was chapter seven, and asked whether I had read ahead. I said I had. She said good, and went on with chapter two.

Sanjay's party was on the third Saturday of September. He had been planning it all week; he had told everyone on the floor and, as far as I could tell, most of the second floor and a good part of the engineering building. People started arriving at about nine. By ten the corridor was full from the stairwell door to the fire exit at our end, people sitting on the floor with their backs against the cinderblock, people standing in the doorways of the rooms, cases of beer stacked against the wall by the washroom. Doors along the corridor were propped open with shoes. The air was thick with smoke and the overhead lights were too bright for it, and somebody had taken the cover off the fire alarm in the stairwell and taped a paper bag over the head of it. Sanjay was everywhere at once, carrying two bottles by the necks, introducing people to people.

I had been at my desk with the door shut. At a little after ten Sanjay knocked on our own door and opened it and said I had to come out, everybody was out, I was missing it. I got up and went and stood in the doorway. I stood there for ten minutes, by my watch. A girl from the second floor asked what program I was in and I told her, and she said she did not know what that was, and I told her it was measurement and control of industrial processes, and she said oh and moved on along the wall. Someone handed me a bottle of beer and I held it without opening it and then set it down on the floor by the door frame. A man I did not know was trying to get the corridor to sing something and mostly failing. I looked at my watch again. Then I stepped back into the room, closed the door, and turned the lock button on the knob. I walked out of the residence party after ten minutes and went back to my schematics, which is the clearest picture of who I was turning into. They were the circuit diagrams for the power supply on the trainer board we were building in Electrical Circuits, three sheets of them, and I spread them across the desk, weighted the corners with the stapler and a roll of tape and two film canisters of resistors, and traced the rectifier section with a pencil until I could have drawn it from memory. The noise in the corridor went on until about two-thirty. I put in a pair of foam earplugs from the parts drawer at midnight and went to bed. Sanjay came in at some point after that. In the morning there was a cigarette burn on the linoleum near his bed and a man asleep on his floor in a parka, whom I stepped over on the way to breakfast.

The residence bill came at the end of September, in the mailboxes in the lobby, in a long white envelope with a window. It was one sheet, typed on a computer printer with the sprocket holes still on the sides. It listed room rate for the fall term, meal plan for the fall term, a damage deposit carried forward from the hundred I had paid in July, a key deposit, and a student activity levy of eighteen dollars which I had not been told about. It came to one thousand six hundred and forty-three dollars and fifty cents, less the hundred, payable by the fifteenth of October.

I took it to the residence office the next morning, between nine and ten when I had no class. On the wall beside the counter there was a notice board under a sheet of plexiglass with the rates for the year posted on it, single and double rooms, the three meal plans, the deposits and the levies, all typed and signed at the bottom. I stood at the board with the bill in one hand and a pencil in the other and went down it line by line. The double room rate matched. The key deposit matched. The activity levy was there on the board, in smaller type at the bottom, with a note that it went to the residence council. The meal plan I had signed up for was the middle one, nineteen meals a week, priced by the term. The board gave the term price, but under it there was also a weekly figure, for people who moved in or out partway through. I wrote the weekly figure on the back of the bill and went back to my room and found the college calendar, which gave the first and last days of residence for the fall term. I counted the weeks on a calendar page, Sunday to Sunday, from the day I had moved in to the day the residence closed in December, and got fifteen weeks and two days, and the meal plan covered fifteen weeks with the first weekend free. I multiplied the weekly figure by fifteen with the calculator and then again by hand in the margin. It came out to the term price on the board, to the cent. The total on the bill was correct.

I wrote a cheque for the balance and took it back to the office that afternoon. The woman at the counter stamped the bill PAID and gave me a receipt from a carbon book. I entered the amount in the ledger, under the date, and drew a line under it. Then I took a plain envelope from the paper drawer, wrote RESIDENCE 1989–90 on the front of it, put the stamped bill and the carbon receipt and the July deposit receipt inside, and slid the envelope into the back of the ledger, inside the cover. I did the same afterwards with an envelope for the bookstore receipts and one for tuition.

The first calculus test was on the first Friday of October. Technical Mathematics was mostly algebra and trigonometry for the first few weeks, which I had no trouble with, and then it went into derivatives, which I had not done. I had dropped calculus in grade 12 after the first month and taken a second accounting credit instead, and I had not thought about it again until I saw it on the course outline. The test was six questions in fifty minutes: limits, the derivative from first principles, the power rule, the product and quotient rules, a related-rates problem about a ladder sliding down a wall, and a question about finding the maximum of a box made from a sheet of cardboard. I did the first three, made a start on the fourth and got the quotient rule backwards, and wrote down what I knew about the ladder, which was not enough. I did not get to the box.

The tests came back the following Wednesday. Mine had a thirty-eight in red at the top, out of a hundred, circled. Below it the instructor had written see me. I folded it in half, put it in the binder behind the Technical Mathematics divider, and after class walked over to the bookstore and asked whether there was a solutions manual for the textbook. There was, on a shelf behind the counter: a paperback with the worked solutions to the odd-numbered exercises at the end of each chapter. It cost eighteen dollars and ninety-five cents plus tax. I bought it and entered it in the ledger under books.

After that I worked calculus every night after supper. I ate at the cafeteria at five-thirty, came back to the room by six-fifteen, shut the door, and sat at the desk with the textbook open on the left, a pad of graph paper in the middle, and the solutions manual face down on the right. I started again from the first section of the chapter on limits and did every exercise at the back of each section, odd and even, all of them, in pencil, numbered down the left margin. When I finished an odd one I turned the manual over and checked it, and if I had it wrong I worked out where I had gone off, wrote the correct line under mine, and did the problem again from the start on a fresh line without looking. The even ones had no worked solutions, only answers in the back of the textbook, and those I checked against the answer and nothing more. Some nights it was thirty problems and some nights it was twelve, depending on how long they were. I stopped at ten or when I had finished a section, whichever came later. I kept the finished sheets in order in a separate binder, which by December was two inches thick.

The instructor had office hours on Tuesday afternoons from two to four in a small room in the main building with a window onto an air shaft. I went the first Tuesday after the test and every Tuesday after that, at two o'clock, with a list of the problems I had not been able to finish, written out on a single sheet with the page and number of each one. For the first few weeks there were six or eight on the list. He was a heavy man in a cardigan who taught in a monotone and was patient at the desk. He would take my list and go down it, and on each one he would ask me to show him where I had stopped, and then ask me what the next step had to be, and wait until I said it. He did not do the problems for me. Some weeks the line outside his door was four or five students long and I waited in the corridor with my list until it was my turn. By the middle of November my list was down to two or three problems a week, and once there was only one, and he looked at it and said I could have worked that out myself, and I said I had, but I wanted to be sure of the method.

The second test, in early November, I got sixty-one. The third, at the end of November, I got seventy-three. The final exam was on the twelfth of December, a three-hour paper in the gymnasium, at rows of folding tables, with snow coming down past the high windows and the radiators knocking. There was a related-rates problem about a conical tank draining, and a maximum problem about the cheapest way to fence a field against a river, and I did both. I got seventy-four on the final and seventy-one for the course. I entered the marks for all my courses on a page at the back of the ledger, after the money.

Sanjay had a bottle of rye in his closet, a forty-ouncer of Canadian Club that he had brought from home and kept behind his running shoes, though drinking in the rooms was against the residence rules. One Friday night in early October, at about nine, he took it out and two water glasses from the shelf over his desk and poured about two inches into each, no ice, and sat down in his chair and pushed one of the glasses across the desk toward me, his desk being the nearer one to where I was standing at the closet. He said it was Friday and I had been at my desk all week and I had to have one drink with him at least.

I picked the glass up, walked over, and set it back down in front of him, next to his own. I said I had a lab write-up due Monday. It was the write-up for our first lab in Instrumentation Fundamentals, on differential-pressure flow measurement: we had run water through an orifice plate on the lab bench at five different flow rates, read the differential on a mercury manometer and on a pneumatic transmitter, timed the flow into a weigh tank with a stopwatch, and were to plot flow against the square root of the differential and calculate the discharge coefficient. I sat down at my desk with my back to him, got out the data sheet and the graph paper, and started on the calculations.

Sanjay sat there for a while talking: about a professor in his statics course, about the girl from Parry Sound, who had written him a letter, about a car he wanted to buy in the spring. I answered him now and then with a word or two and kept working. I took square roots on the calculator and wrote them in a column to four places. I plotted the five points and drew the best straight line through them with a clear plastic ruler, and they lay close to it, except the lowest one, which was off, I worked out, because the manometer reading at that flow was too small to read to better than a millimetre. He drank his glass, and after a while he picked up mine and drank that too, more slowly. At about ten-thirty he put the bottle back behind his shoes, rinsed the two glasses in the washroom down the hall, put on his jacket, and went out. I heard him knock on a door down the corridor and voices, and then the stairwell door. I finished the calculations at about midnight and wrote out the discussion section on Saturday morning.

Thanksgiving that year was the ninth of October. On the Wednesday before it, Sanjay came back to the room at about four in the afternoon from the bus station downtown, where he had gone to buy his ticket home for the long weekend. The fare had gone up since the summer, or he had counted wrong, and he was twenty dollars short. He had not bought the ticket. His bank was not one that had a branch in Sudbury and his card did not work at the machines at the college. He stood in the middle of the room with his wallet open in his hand and told me all this and then asked whether I could lend him twenty dollars until the Monday after Thanksgiving, when he would be back and could get money wired to him.

I was at my desk with the ledger open, entering my week's cafeteria snacks and a pack of graph paper. I had the term's money in the bank, more than enough to get me to Christmas by my own figures, and I had forty dollars in my wallet, two twenties, which I had taken out at the bank machine on Monday. Sanjay knew I had money; he had seen me at the ledger every night. I closed the ledger and said no. I refused to lend my roommate twenty dollars for his bus fare home, a meanness I still cannot account for. I did not give a reason and he did not ask for one. He stood there a moment longer, then closed his wallet and put it in his back pocket and said all right, and went out into the corridor. I heard him knocking at doors. About ten minutes later he came back with a twenty-dollar bill folded in his hand that he had got from someone down the hall, and he put on his jacket again and went back downtown to the bus station before it closed. He went home on the Friday, came back on the Monday night, and said nothing more about it, then or after. As far as I know he paid the twenty back on the Tuesday.

The residence emptied out over that weekend. I had the room to myself from Friday until Monday night.

On a Sunday in the middle of October I walked out along the highway north of the city. It was cold that morning, a degree or two below freezing when I went out at eight, with frost on the cars in the residence lot and on the grass by the road, and the sky clear. I wore my work boots, jeans, a flannel shirt, a wool sweater, and a canvas jacket, and I had a pair of leather work gloves in the jacket pocket, though I took them off when I climbed. I walked through the city and out past the last of the houses and gas stations and kept on along the shoulder of the highway with the traffic going by. North of the city the country is bare rock and stunted birch and jack pine, a good deal of it black from the old smelter fumes, and the highway has been blasted through the ridges in places. I had seen a big rock cut from the bus coming down in September, and I walked until I came to it. It took about an hour and three-quarters.

The cut was a gap blasted through a ridge, with a rock face on each side of the highway. The face on the east side, the side I was walking on, was close to sixty feet high at its highest point, sloping down at each end to where the ridge met the ground. It was a dark grey rock, almost black where it was wet, rusty orange in streaks where the iron in it had weathered, and it went up nearly straight. All the way up it, from the ditch to the top, there were the half-round grooves left by the drill holes, where the drillers had bored rows of holes down into the rock from above and loaded them and blasted, and the rock had broken away along the rows and left half of each hole standing in the face. They ran up in vertical lines a couple of feet apart, each groove about the width of three fingers, some of them running the full height of a bench before the next bench started, some of them broken off partway where the rock had come away differently. There was a ledge about a third of the way up where the blast benches met, and another one higher.

There was a ditch at the foot of the face full of shattered rock and a little ice. I climbed down into it, crossed it, put my gloves in my pocket, and started up. I used the drill-hole grooves as holds. A groove is too narrow to put a hand inside, but you can get the edge of your fingers hooked over the lip of it where the rock bulges out between two holes, and you can get the toe of a boot jammed sideways into the bottom of the groove where it narrows. I went up with my hands in two grooves side by side and my feet in the same two, a few inches at a time, moving one hand or one foot and then the next. Where the groove gave out I reached across to the next row. The rock was cold and rough and dry except in the shade, where it was damp, and I kept to the sunny parts. My fingers went numb after the first twenty feet and I stopped on the first ledge and put them under my armpits until they came back, and then went on.

The second bench was steeper than the first and the drill grooves were broken in a few places, with smooth rock between. In one place I had to traverse sideways about eight feet to get to a row that went on up, with my feet on a crack running across the face and my hands on the lip of one groove and then the next. A transport truck went by below on the highway and the air off it came up the face. I reached the upper ledge, which had some loose rock on it that I did not stand on, and climbed the last fifteen feet, where the face eased back and was broken into steps, and came up over the top onto the ridge on my hands and knees.

The top of the cut was bare rock with a thin soil in the cracks, a few jack pines growing crooked out of them, and blueberry bushes gone red, and a lot of loose blast rock that had never been cleared. There was a wire fence along the rim at one end, sagging, and a survey stake with orange flagging. I walked along the rim, back from the edge, from one end of the cut to the other, about three hundred yards, looking down now and then at the highway and the opposite face. The opposite face had the same drill grooves on it. I could see from up there how the benches had been laid out, three of them, each one set back a little from the one below, and where the drill rows had been offset from one bench to the next. From the north end of the ridge I looked for an easier way down and found that on the far side of the ridge, away from the highway, the rock dropped off in a series of broken steps and slabs into the bush, perhaps thirty feet altogether, with a gully in it full of fallen rock. I climbed down that way, facing in, using the slabs and the edges of the steps, and came out at the bottom in a stand of birch and walked around the end of the ridge through the bush, back to the highway. My hands were scraped across the knuckles and the fingertips, and there was rock dust in the knees of my jeans. I put my gloves back on and walked back to the city along the shoulder. I was back at the residence by two in the afternoon and did calculus problems until supper.

By December the room was arranged the way it had been arranged in September, with Sanjay's desk under the window and mine by the door, and my bed made with the corners tucked. The sheet with the three curves and SHOWER written on it was still pinned over my desk. The ledger was full to the middle of the book. The last entry for the term was the bus ticket home, and below it I drew two lines and carried the balance forward.

Chapter 12

In 1990, I was nineteen and living in Sudbury with Sanjay Mehta, in an apartment where the kitchen table had to serve for meals and schoolwork. My second year in instrumentation at the college began that fall. I had a place for the rent receipts, a place for grocery money, and a separate envelope for bus fare. The table was less orderly. Sanjay left things where he had finished with them, and I moved them to the other end when I needed room. I usually worked with my books against the wall and my paper near the edge, where my elbow could clear the salt shaker.

I found the quarry that summer, before classes started again. It was outside the city, beyond a stretch of gravel road with standing water in the wheel ruts. There was no work going on and no equipment left that I could see. The entrance had grown narrow with brush. I went through on foot, carrying my shirt after the walk had warmed me up. Gravel had worked into one shoe. I stopped to shake it out, then followed the old track until the ground opened into cut rock.

The water was deep at the bottom. The low side had a shelf where I could get in, and I swam out far enough to look back at the wall. Below the surface, the stone fell away until I could no longer make it out. I went back to the shelf and walked around. The highest ledge was reached by a sloping cut, then a short climb where I had to use my hands. Loose pieces lay in the cracks. I pushed them aside with my shoe before putting my weight down.

At the top I took off my shoes and set them together, with the socks tucked inside. The ledge had enough room to stand clear of the wall. I looked at the water directly below, moved to the edge, and jumped. My feet went in first. The cold reached my chest as I went under, and the light above narrowed before I came back up. I wiped the water off my face and swam to the low side, using the same shelf to get out.

Instead of taking the sloping route, I climbed back up the face. Water ran out of my shorts and darkened the rock under my feet. I used the horizontal breaks where the stone had been cut, testing each hold before pulling on it. In one place I had to move sideways to get my foot onto a wider piece. I came over the edge on my knees, stood, and walked back to the ledge. My shoes were where I had left them. I jumped again.

After the second swim I stayed on the low shelf until the water had stopped running off me. There was grit on my palms from the climb and a scraped place on one shin. I put my socks on over damp feet and had to loosen both shoes. On the way out I took a different line through the brush so I would not have to push through it sideways. By the time I reached the road, the front of my shirt was wet from carrying it against my shorts. I went back to the apartment and hung both over the bath.

My first part-time job in Sudbury was at Pilon Electronics. Gord Pilon gave me the back bench on a Saturday. He showed it to me with one hand resting on a carton that occupied most of the working surface. There were circuit boards underneath it, a television chassis with two knobs missing, loose lengths of mains cord, and three containers holding screws mixed with clipped leads. A soldering iron lay under a folded service sheet. The sheet had a brown hole through one corner.

Gord said I could use whatever was there, provided I made room to work. I started by unplugging everything. I put the unfinished jobs to one side and kept any paper with the item it described. Some had names and dates attached, and some had nothing. Those went on a shelf until Gord could tell me what they were. I emptied the screw containers onto newspaper, sorted the usable screws, and put the clipped leads in the rubbish. Under the carton I found a steel rule stuck to the bench with old flux.

I scraped the worst of the dirt off with a blade held nearly flat, then wiped the surface. The bench top had a burn near the back and a shallow hollow where something heavy had sat for a long time. I put the meter away from that hollow. I wanted the repair in front of me, the iron to my right, and enough space on the left for a service manual to lie open. There was an outlet strip screwed underneath. I checked its cord before plugging it back in.

A strip of pegboard fitted the wall behind the bench. I cut it to length, drilled the fixing holes, and mounted it on short spacers so the hooks would sit properly. I hung the side cutters, long-nose pliers, wire strippers, and screwdrivers, then traced around each one with a pencil. After checking that the spacing worked, I went over the outlines with a felt pen. The small screwdriver needed its own hook. If it shared with the larger ones, it dropped through and landed behind the bench.

I set small parts bins in a row beneath the board and labelled them. Resistors had separate places by value range, with capacitors farther along and fuses at the end. I did not empty anything unidentified into them. A part without a legible marking stayed in the container it came in until I could test it. I put a stiff-backed logbook beside the meter and numbered the pages. For each job I left room for the reported fault, what I found, parts fitted, time spent, and the result of the final test.

Gord came back while I was writing the first headings. He moved the pliers off their hook, looked at the outline, and put them back. He asked whether I had found a place for the work. I showed him the clear centre of the bench. He brought over the first item and told me what it had been doing. I entered the date before opening it. By closing time there was solder on the tip of the iron, one job in the book, and no loose screws left on the working surface.

In the fall, Dr. Ruth Abernethy put us on the temperature rigs in the lab. Each one had a heater, a sensor, and a controller. The rig on our bench carried on rising after the output had been reduced, which made it easy to set up a cycle that lasted longer than anyone wanted to watch. The exercise was to bring the temperature to a new setpoint and hold it there. We had to record the response, state our settings, and show that the result could be repeated.

My lab partner wanted to turn several controls at once. I moved the sheet between us and wrote down the starting values. We ran it as it stood, watching the temperature rise, pass the setpoint, and return. I marked the times on the paper. Then I took the integral action out and made one change to the proportional setting. The next run was slower near the top and did not carry as far past it. I let the temperature settle before touching anything else. The heater still held enough heat to spoil a quick comparison.

I worked out a sequence that let us approach the setting without chasing each rise and fall. I used small changes, recorded each one, and allowed the rig to settle between runs. Once the proportional response was usable, I brought the integral action back in and lengthened its time until it removed the offset without starting the same cycle again. I wrote the final values in a box. Underneath I put the order in which I had changed them and the reason for waiting between tests.

When Dr. Abernethy came to the bench, my partner took the sheet. He pointed at the boxed figures and explained how he had arrived at them. He used the first person. I stood beside him, close enough to see the paper bending under his thumb. She asked about the settling time, and he read the figure I had written in the margin. The rig was holding at the setpoint by then. She watched it, checked the sheet, and gave him the mark. I said nothing. When she moved on, I switched the heater output off and waited until the rig was ready to leave.

The first instrumentation exam was that fall. I arrived with two pens, pencils, an eraser, and the calculator I used in the lab. The hall had narrow desks set in rows, each with enough space for the paper and little else. I put my coat over the chair and kept the calculator case under it. Before starting, I read through the questions and put a small mark beside the ones I could do without turning to the formula sheet. There were fewer of those than I had expected.

I could wire a transmitter and work out the current for a given point in its range. On the exam paper, several questions put the same material into terms I had not used at the bench. I spent too long trying to get one calculation to agree with the answer I had decided it ought to produce. I erased it, worked it again, and got the same result. By the time I moved on, I had used a large part of the sitting. I left working on the remaining pages, but some answers stopped before the required result.

I failed. I wrote the result on the inside cover of the notebook I had used for the course and put the notice behind it. I checked which sections had cost me marks and looked through my notes for those sections. Several pages had the final formula without the steps that led to it. Others had examples copied from the board with a number changed halfway through. I could remember what had happened in the room, but the page itself did not contain enough to reproduce the work.

That evening Sanjay asked me to go out. He was standing at the kitchen counter, putting on his coat while I had the exam notice and notebook open on the table. I said I had work to do. He looked at the papers and said the work would still be there afterwards. I moved the notice under the notebook so I could use the edge of the table. He went, and I stayed with the pages until the kitchen window had turned black. When I stopped, I stacked everything in the order I meant to use it next.

At Pilon's, I was using resistors from a fresh bag when the question of their tolerance came up. A parts supplier's rep had told Gord that the new batch was all within one percent. Gord passed that along while setting the bag on my bench. The colour bands were clear, and the parts looked the same as the ones already in the bin. I had a job open that required several of that value. Before taking any out for it, I moved the job back and made room beside the meter.

I shorted the probes together and checked the reading. Then I took twenty resistors from different parts of the bag and laid them on a clean sheet of paper. I measured them one at a time without holding the bare leads between my fingers. For each one I waited for the display to settle, wrote the reading in the logbook, and moved it to the other side of the sheet. The twenty readings took more space than the note about the job they were intended for. I wrote the marked value and the meter range above them.

Gord asked what I was doing. I told him I was checking the bag before I used it. He said the rep had already told him the tolerance. I showed him the column of figures. He looked at it for a moment and went back to the front. I calculated the limits on paper and compared the readings with them. Then I selected the parts for the repair, clipped the leads to length, and fitted them. I entered the parts used on the job's page, keeping the sample readings on a separate page I could find again.

After that I measured a sample from every bag before filling the bins. I wrote the date, marked value, and readings together. Some bags arrived while I had a unit apart, and I left them closed until there was room to lay out the sample. I did not mix the new parts into the old stock first. The meter lived at the back left of the bench, with its leads loosely coiled beside it. When I finished checking a bag, the paper went away and the job came back to the middle.

I found the control-theory textbook in the college library while looking for another worked example. It was a university text, thicker than our course book, with smaller type and exercises that started several steps beyond the ones we were being given. I opened it at a chapter on response and recognised enough of the notation to keep reading. The next page used mathematics I had to look up. I borrowed it anyway. At the apartment I put it on the kitchen table and began with the earlier chapters instead of the page that had caught my attention.

I worked through it in the evenings on lined paper, leaving a wide margin for corrections. A single example could take most of the time between supper and bed. I copied every step, including the ones the book left out. Where I could not follow a change from one line to the next, I worked backwards from the printed result. Sanjay sometimes asked whether I wanted to come along when he went out. I said no, or said I was in the middle of something. He stopped waiting by the door once I had answered.

One section brought me back to derivative action. The course had given us what it did and when it might be useful. The university book went further into the terms behind it, but I still wanted to know why a noisy measurement became worse. After class I waited until the room had cleared and asked Dr. Abernethy. She said derivative action responded to the rate of change. A small change in the signal could have a large rate if it happened quickly. She drew a short, steep line beside a longer, gentler one and put the chalk down.

I took that answer onto the bus to Pilon's for the next week. There was not always room to open the book, so I kept a folded sheet inside my notebook. I drew a slow change in temperature and then put short upward and downward variations along it. On another part of the page I wrote changes in value divided by changes in time. The numbers were small until I shortened the interval. Then the calculated rate became large even though the difference between the readings remained small.

At the apartment I made two columns of sample readings. One rose evenly. The other rose by about the same amount overall but went up and down between points. I calculated the rate between each pair. The second column produced large alternating figures. I drew those below the original readings and could show on paper what her short answer had meant. I kept the sheet in the textbook. By then it had been folded enough times that one corner had come off, leaving a white triangle loose in the bottom of my bag.

In January I sat the resit. Snow was banked along the college walks, with a grey crust on the side facing the road. I stamped my boots before going into the hall and put my wet coat over the back of the chair. This time I divided the available time by the marks and wrote the resulting allowances at the top of my rough paper. I moved on when an allowance ran out. I completed more of the paper, checked the units in the calculations, and went back to the questions I had left.

I failed by two marks. I read the result twice, then counted the marks available in the sections I had mishandled. There had been enough on a question I had partly answered. There had also been enough in several smaller errors spread across the paper. I put the notice in my bag and went to the library. The past papers were kept on file there. I checked how far back they went, wrote down what was available, and made a list of the papers I had not worked through.

At home I took my notes out of their binder and began again from the first lecture. I did not start with the subjects I liked or with the questions I had nearly got right. The first page covered definitions I had used for more than a year. I wrote them out in full, then gave each one a numerical example where that was possible. If a formula used a particular set of units, I wrote those beside it. I kept the original notes in a stack on the left and the rebuilt version on the right.

I made a separate sheet for errors. It had no headings by subject, only the date, the question, and what I had done wrong. Wrong signs, omitted conversions, assumptions not stated in the question: they all went onto it. When the sheet filled, I started another. I did every past paper the college library had on file, working to the time allowed. Then I did them all again. On the second pass I covered the earlier answers and used fresh paper. I checked both the result and the steps that produced it.

The kitchen table acquired a pale patch where my forearm rubbed against it. I sharpened pencils over a folded piece of newspaper and emptied the shavings before Sanjay used the table. He still asked me out. Sometimes he asked while filling a glass at the sink, sometimes as he was leaving. I kept giving him the same answer. My meals were short enough that I could move the plate aside and bring the paper back without losing the line I had been working on. I kept bus fare separate so I did not spend it on copying.

One winter weekend Sanjay and his engineering friends hired a van to go skiing. He told me about it while checking what he would need to take. There would be room for me, and I could pay my share. He named the costs he knew and said the rest could be divided afterwards. I had my notebook open and added the amounts on a scrap of paper. I said no. He asked once more before the weekend, with the time they planned to leave. I said I was going into the lab.

After he had gone, I washed my breakfast dishes and took the bus to the college. The lab was empty. I left my coat away from the bench, checked the connections on the loop rig, and started with the settings I had recorded during class. Without another pair of hands at the controls, each change was mine to enter and write down. I drew columns for the time, setpoint, measured temperature, and output. I used the clock to keep the intervals even. The heater took its usual time to bring the rig up.

I ran it first with proportional action alone. I waited for the temperature to stop changing, recorded the remaining difference, and changed the setpoint. Then I brought in integral action and repeated the run. I could leave the rig working long enough to see what happened after the part of the response we usually recorded. A setting that looked useful at first could produce a slow swing later. I kept that run going until there was enough on the sheet to show the pattern, then changed one setting and started again.

I ate what I had brought at a separate table and kept the food away from the rig. Outside, snow slid down part of the window where the glass was warm. I went back to the bench and tried the same sequence from a different starting temperature. By the end of the afternoon I had several sheets covered in figures and a few plots drawn with a ruler.
