I turned the heater off, watched the temperature come down, and left the controls set for the next use. I returned on the second day and repeated the runs that needed checking.

The third exam sitting was in the spring. I had reached the end of the past papers twice and put the error sheets at the front of the rebuilt notes. On the morning of the exam I read those sheets, then closed the binder. I took the same calculator, spare pens, pencils, and eraser. The hall windows had been opened before we went in. There was a cool draught across the desks nearest the wall, and the corner of my rough paper kept lifting until I held it under the calculator case.

I read the entire paper first. I wrote the time allowances as I had in January and started with the first question. Where a question asked for an explanation, I gave the explanation as well as the calculation. I put units on the intermediate figures. On one answer I caught a conversion error by checking the size of the result against the range given in the question. I crossed out the line, left the working legible, and did it again below. I had time at the end to check that every page had my identifying details.

I passed the instrumentation exam on my third attempt, thankfully with a mark to spare. When I got the result, I entered it beside the other two inside the notebook cover. I put the rebuilt notes back into their binder and closed the rings carefully so none of the punched holes caught outside them. At the apartment Sanjay asked how it had gone. I told him I had passed. He asked whether I would come out that evening. I said I had a shift at Pilon's and left the binder on the shelf above the table.

The rush job for a mine filled the bench at Pilon's with units, loose covers, and sheets kept under whatever weight was close to hand. Gord wanted the lot finished quickly. I cleared other work into labelled spaces and put the mine job in order so I could move through it without losing track of what had been tested. Each unit got its own entry in the logbook. I kept removed parts beside the unit they came from until I had completed the repair and checked it. The bench light stayed on after the light at the front had been switched off.

The work took longer than the first estimate. There were repeated operations, but the faults were not identical, so I checked before replacing anything. I measured supplies, examined joints under the lamp, and followed the signal through where I needed to. When I found a fault, I wrote it down before moving on. Gord came to the back to ask how many remained. I gave him the count from the book. We kept the finished units apart from the ones still waiting, with their covers fitted and screws checked.

On the last one I ran the test again after putting the cover on. I left it operating while I cleared the clipped leads and put the tools back on the pegboard. Then I took the final readings and entered them. Gord looked through the completed work and said that was the lot. My fingertips had grey marks where I had handled the cases. I washed them at the sink, dried them, and came back to close the logbook. There were still a few minutes left on the final run.

Gord opened his desk drawer and brought out a bottle of rye. He put two glasses on the bench and poured into both. One stood near my closed logbook, with a ring of light at the bottom from the bench lamp. I asked for coffee instead. He looked towards the front, then went to get it. I moved the glass far enough from the book that I could open it if I needed to. Gord came back with the coffee, and I drank it while the last unit finished running.

He drank his rye and talked about getting the job out. I checked the unit once more, switched it off, and marked the final test complete. The coffee had left a dark line inside the cup. I rinsed the cup and put it back, then checked that the iron was unplugged. My glass of rye was still on the bench, with the same amount in it. I left it there when I went home. Outside, I checked the time and walked to the bus stop with my work hours written on a folded slip in my pocket.

At the end of the year I took my first-year textbooks off the apartment shelf to sell to incoming students. I checked them for loose papers and erased the few pencil marks I had made in the margins. The bindings were sound. I put the purchase receipts beside them and copied the new prices onto separate slips. Those were the prices I asked. I stacked the books by size so the smaller ones would not bend under the others, then took them to the college in a bag with a piece of cardboard at the bottom.

I arranged to deal with buyers separately. I did not stand among the groups comparing schedules and carrying new binders through the corridor. I waited at a table with the books in front of me and opened each one when someone wanted to inspect it. The first-year students checked editions and turned the pages to see how much had been written inside. I pointed out the condition and showed them the price. When a sale was agreed, I counted the cash before putting the book into the buyer's hands.

One first-year wanted a book but could not afford what I asked. He showed me the money he had and asked whether I would come down. The difference was small enough to count in a few notes and coins. I said the price was the amount on the slip. He looked through the book again, stopped at the contents, and asked a second time. I kept my hand on the cover of the book underneath it and said no. He put it back on the stack. I straightened the slip after he had gone.

Back at the apartment I entered the sales in my ledger. Each title had a line for the original cost and another for the amount received. I counted the cash again, matched it to the entries, and put it away. The unsold books went back on the shelf with their price slips inside the front covers. Sanjay came in while I was finishing and moved his things off the end of the table. I closed the ledger, put the receipts into an envelope, and checked the hours I was due to work at Pilon's.

Chapter 13

In the spring of 1991 I finished the two-year instrumentation technician program at the college in Sudbury, and in September of that year I went back for a third year of electronics. I had turned twenty in March. Tuition for the extra year came to a little over nine hundred dollars, and I had it in the bank before I signed the registration form. I kept my job at Pilon Electronics, full days through the summer and then three evenings a week and Saturdays once classes started. I also kept my half of the apartment with Sanjay Mehta: two bedrooms, a kitchen, and a bathroom with a tub and no shower, on the second floor of a frame house off Notre Dame Avenue. The rent was four hundred and ten dollars a month. I paid my two hundred and five on the first, by cheque, and Sanjay paid his when he remembered, which was usually by the fifth.

I told my mother about the third year on the telephone on a Sunday evening, after six, when the long-distance rates went down. She asked whether it would lead to more money. I said it would lead to better work, which would lead to more money, and that a year cost less now than it would later. She said that sounded like me. I kept the call to eleven minutes and wrote the charge in the ledger when the bill came.

The ledger was a green clothbound account book with ruled columns, the third of its kind since I had come down to the college. Every dollar in and out went into it on the day it happened: wages, rent, groceries, a pair of work boots, a bag of transistors. At the end of each month I ruled a line, added the columns, and checked the balance against the bankbook. If they disagreed by a dime I went back through until I found the dime.

The project for the third year was a temperature controller built from discrete parts. It took me from September to April, and I will come to it.

Pilon Electronics was a narrow store on Larch Street with a counter at the front, shelves of televisions waiting for pickup along one wall, and a workroom behind a curtain with four benches. Gord Pilon owned it. He ran the counter, wrote the tickets, quoted the prices, and came into the back only when a job had gone past what he had quoted. That summer he gave me the corner bench under the back window, which nobody else wanted because the afternoon sun came straight onto it. The bench came with a pegboard, a Weller soldering station with a cracked sponge tray, a Hitachi oscilloscope that needed five minutes to warm up before the trace stopped drifting, and a row of margarine tubs full of mixed parts.

Every bench in the shop worked the same way. When you needed a 2.2K resistor you tipped a tub out and stirred it with a screwdriver until you found one, or until you found a 2.7K and decided it would do. I timed myself once by the clock over the door. Finding a single 2N3904 in the tubs took four minutes and ten seconds.

In July the shoe store two doors down tore out its stockroom shelving and stacked the plywood in the lane for the garbage. I asked the manager and he said to take what I wanted. I carried eleven pieces back, half-inch fir, some of it painted grey on one face, and added a sheet of hardboard from the back of a console television we had scrapped. Gord lent me his circular saw, a combination square and two sawhorses from his garage, and I built the cabinet on Saturday afternoons in the lane behind the shop, after the counter closed at four.

It was twenty-four inches wide, thirty high and ten deep, to fit the back of the bench under the pegboard. Inside were four columns of eight drawers, thirty-two in all, each about five inches wide and three high. I cut the drawer sides from the thinner offcuts, glued them with carpenter's glue, pinned them with brads, and used the hardboard for bottoms. Instead of pulls I drilled a three-quarter-inch hole in each drawer front for a finger. The runners were strips of the grey plywood glued to the dividers and rubbed with a candle end so the drawers slid. It took four Saturdays, and I spent the fifth sanding every edge, because I had put a splinter into my thumb on the first drawer I tried.

I numbered the drawers one to thirty-two, left to right and top to bottom, and labelled each one by part and value. I typed the labels on Gord's typewriter on strips cut from an index card and covered them with clear tape: 1N4148 SIGNAL DIODE, 1N4004 RECTIFIER, 2N3904 NPN, 2N3906 PNP, 10K 1/4W, 100 MFD 25V, and so on down to drawer thirty-two, which was fuses. On the left side panel I screwed a small wooden rack that held a stack of three-by-five cards, one card per part, in alphabetical order, each one saying which drawer the part was in and roughly how many I had. If I added a part I wrote its card the same day.

I also bought a hardbound record book with numbered pages from the stationery store on Durham Street, a dollar ninety-five, and started a repair log. One line per job: the date, Gord's ticket number, the make and model, the complaint, what I found, the parts I used and the time it took. By the end of August I could look up a Panasonic VCR that had come in chewing tape and see that I had replaced the pinch roller on the same model in June.

The last thing was the pegboard. I took every tool down, laid it on the board where it belonged, and traced around it with a black marker: side cutters, needle-nose pliers, the solder sucker, the nut drivers by size, five screwdrivers, the plastic alignment tools, the tweezers. An empty outline meant a tool was somewhere it should not be. I never left at night without wiping and tinning the iron tip, switching off the station and the scope, and putting every tool back in its outline. Every habit I rely on today was built at that bench in those years.

Gord came back on the Monday after I finished, pulled out drawer fourteen, read the label, read the card on the side, and pushed the drawer shut. He said I was going to make the rest of them look bad. Then he went back to the counter. Nobody else in the shop built one, and nobody took parts from mine, because I asked them not to.

I bought my first car in the third week of August with money I had been putting aside under its own heading in the ledger since the previous Christmas. I wanted something I could keep running myself, which meant something old and American with room to work under the hood. The ad in the paper said 1977 Chevrolet Nova, six cylinder, automatic, eighty thousand miles, fifteen hundred or best offer, with a number in New Sudbury. The odometer read in miles because the car had been bought new in Michigan and brought across.

The man who owned it met me in his driveway on a Thursday evening. He was heavy and friendly and talked a great deal. He said it had eighty thousand miles and had never been in an accident, and that his wife had driven it to work and back and not much else. The car was dark green with a white vinyl roof that had started to lift at one corner. He offered to take me around the block. I said I would look first.

I opened the driver's door and looked at the pedals. The rubber pad on the brake pedal was worn smooth and through to the steel along its left edge, and the ribs on the accelerator pedal were gone where a heel would rest. The driver's seat had broken down on the door side so that the bolster sat two inches lower than the passenger's, and the vinyl had split along the seam and been mended with black tape. Neither was what eighty thousand miles did to a car. The odometer had five digits. I took it that the car had gone around once already and was at a hundred and eighty.

Then I opened the trunk, took out the spare and the jack, and lifted the carpet. I was looking for welds, a bead across the floor pan where the back end of one car had been joined to the front of another, or fresh seam sealer where the factory would not have put it. There were none. The floor had surface rust over original metal, and the factory sealer was dry and cracked the way it should have been. I put the carpet down and the spare and jack back in.

Last, I walked around the car with the low sun behind me and read the paint along the door seams. On the passenger side the rear door was a shade bluer than the quarter panel beside it, and there was a fine grey overspray on the rubber weatherstrip along its edge, the kind you get when a door is painted in place without masking. I ran a thumbnail down the seam and felt the ridge. That door had been hit, or someone had painted out rust on it. Either way it was not what he had told me.

I had him start it so I could listen to it idle, then checked the oil and the transmission fluid, which was red and did not smell burnt. I drove it around the block. Then I stood by the passenger door and offered him nine hundred and fifty dollars. He said the ad said fifteen. I said the car had done more than eighty thousand miles and the rear door had been painted, and I put my thumbnail back on the seam. He looked at it for a while and said twelve. We settled at eleven hundred.

I paid him on the Saturday with eleven hundred-dollar bills from the bank and got a signed bill of sale and the ownership. That night I wrote the amount in the ledger together with the safety certificate, the plates and the first quarter of insurance, which came to three hundred and sixty dollars more. The following Sunday I changed the oil, the filter, the plugs and the PCV valve myself in the lane behind Pilon's, and started a logbook for the car on the same pattern as the repair log: date, odometer, what was done, what it cost.

On a Sunday in the middle of October I drove out of the city on the highway north and turned onto a gravel road that climbed a hill with a red-and-white radio mast on top of it. I had seen the mast from the college parking lot all through the two-year program and had driven past the turnoff twice since buying the car. It was about fifteen kilometres out. The road ended at a cinder-block transmitter building with a steel door and an air conditioner set into the wall. A chain-link fence ran around the base of the mast, with a sign wired to it saying DANGER, HIGH RF, AUTHORIZED PERSONNEL ONLY. The gate was held with a chain and a padlock, but the chain was long enough that the gate opened a foot and a half.

The mast was a guyed steel lattice, three-sided, about a metre across each face, and by my count of the sections a little over sixty metres tall. A ladder ran up the inside of one face. The guy wires went out to concrete anchors in the bush on three sides. Halfway up, a set of panel antennas was clamped to the legs, and near the top there was a small platform with a railing, below the beacon and the whip.

It was about six degrees and overcast, with a wind out of the northwest. I had brought work gloves. I went through the gate, past the fenced-off section at the base, and started up the ladder. I counted rungs, thirty to a section, and kept three points on the steel. The ladder had no cage. At the panel antennas I kept to the back of the ladder, away from their faces, in case they were transmitting. The coaxial feed lines ran down the leg beside me, clamped every metre, and I noticed that the clamps were stainless and the bolts holding them were not, and that the bolts had rusted. The wind picked up as I went higher and the mast moved, a slow sway I could feel through the rungs.

The top platform was a steel grating about a metre square with a pipe railing on three sides. I climbed through the gap onto it and sat down with my back against a leg and my boots braced on the toe board. From there I could see the city to the south, the big stack at Copper Cliff with its plume going east, the black slag heaps, the highway, and lakes I did not know the names of. The beacon above me was off in the daylight. The wind made a steady noise in the guy wires.

I sat there about forty minutes. I ate the two cheese sandwiches I had brought in my jacket and drank the coffee from the small thermos. Once a light plane went over, lower than I expected, and I followed it until it dropped toward the airport. When my hands had gone stiff I put the gloves back on, climbed through the railing onto the ladder, and came down the way I had gone up, counting the rungs backwards, three points on the steel. At the bottom I went out through the gate, pulled it to, and set the chain the way it had been. I drove home and wrote the kilometres in the car log. I did not mention it to Sanjay.

Sanjay's father came up from Toronto in November on business, two days of meetings with one of the mining companies, and on the Wednesday evening he took the two of us to dinner. Sanjay had told me a week ahead and told me to wear something with a collar. I wore the grey shirt I kept for interviews and the one pair of trousers I owned that were not jeans. I washed the Nova that afternoon, picked his father up at his hotel at seven, and Sanjay sat in the back.

His father was a tall man with grey at the temples who shook my hand hard and asked about the car before we were out of the hotel lot. He had driven a Nova in Toronto in the seventies and knew the engine. He asked what I had paid, and when I told him he asked how I had got it down, and I told him about the door.

The restaurant was downtown, up a flight of stairs, with white tablecloths, cloth napkins folded on the plates and a waiter in a black vest. Before we had opened the menus, Sanjay's father told the waiter to bring whisky for the table, Scotch, and named the brand. The waiter set three heavy tumblers on the cloth and went for the bottle. Before he came back I turned mine upside down on the tablecloth.

The waiter poured two and looked at mine. Sanjay's father looked at it too, and then at me.

"I'm driving," I said.

"Good," he said, and told the waiter to bring me water. The waiter left my glass where it was, upside down, for the rest of the meal.

I drank water through the dinner, four or five glasses of it, the waiter filling it each time it was half down. I had the sirloin, medium, with a baked potato. Sanjay's father had a second whisky with the soup and a third with the main course, and Sanjay had a second. His father asked about my project, and I told him about the thermistor bridge and the transistor stages and what I meant it to hold. He asked what the parts cost. I said thirty-one dollars and forty cents. He asked what a controller like it sold for, and I said a commercial one would be three or four hundred, but it would not be built from discrete parts. He asked why mine was, and I said because I wanted to know what every part in it was doing. He nodded and asked Sanjay the same kind of questions about his courses, and Sanjay talked for most of the dessert. His father paid. I did not see the bill.

It had started to snow when we came down the stairs, the first that stayed. I brought the car around, let it warm up with the defroster on high, and drove Sanjay's father to his hotel first. He shook my hand again in the car and thanked me for driving. Then I drove Sanjay home. He fell asleep in the back seat before we were off Notre Dame. I parked, woke him, and wrote the kilometres in the car log before I went up.

After Christmas, while I was rebuilding the controller on the lab bench, I started reading about digital electronics and microprocessors. The college library had a shelf of it, mostly from the late seventies: a Motorola data book for the 6800, two textbooks on the 8080 and the Z80, a book on digital filters with a cracked spine, and back issues of trade magazines with articles about microcomputers in process control. Most of the plants I expected to work in still ran on pneumatic and analog instruments, and everything Dr. Ruth Abernethy had taught us in the two-year program was analog from one end to the other. But every magazine had another article about a computer doing the work of a cabinet full of them.

The thing I got stuck on was sampling. A computer cannot watch a signal. It reads it, takes a number, does something with the number, and reads it again. One of the books said, in one paragraph, that if you sample a signal at less than twice its highest frequency you get a false reading, a slower signal that is not there at all, and that this was called aliasing. It gave a formula and went on. I could repeat the formula, but I could not see why it was true, and I did not want to use a thing I could not see.

So I drew it. I bought a pad of graph paper and plotted a sine wave at nine cycles a second across a page, then marked where it would be read at ten samples a second, and joined the marks. The marks made a smooth wave at one cycle a second. I did it again at eleven samples, nineteen, twenty and twenty-one. I did it with a square wave and with two sine waves added together. For about three weeks I drew waves in the evenings, and on the bench at Pilon's when it was slow. Then I set it up properly on the Hitachi with two signal generators, one as the signal and one triggering the sweep as the sampling clock, and watched a false wave crawl across the screen at the difference between the two frequencies. After that I could see it. It was the same thing that made hubcaps look as if they were standing still when a car went by under the streetlights at night.

Sanjay came in late one night near the end of January and found me at the kitchen table with the graph paper spread out. He was in engineering at the university and he had taken a course in programming. He looked at the pages for a while and said that in ten years nobody would build a controller like mine, that every loop would be run by a computer, and you would change the tuning from a keyboard instead of with a soldering iron.

I said a computer running a loop was blind between samples. If the process moved faster than half the sample rate, the computer would act on a reading that was not there. And it added delay: it had to read, then calculate, then write the output, and all the while the process went on moving. Delay in a loop was phase lag. Phase lag was what had made my controller oscillate in October. Dr. Abernethy had a rule she wrote on the board every term, that every lag in the loop takes away gain you could otherwise use. A transistor stage answers in microseconds and never stops looking.

"So sample faster," Sanjay said.

"Then you pay for it," I said.

He said the chips were getting cheaper every year. He said a computer could do things my circuit never would: change its own gains, keep a record of every reading, run fifty loops at once off one board. I said the record was a fair point. I said the fifty loops all failed together when the one board failed. He said you put in two boards. I said then you had two boards to fail. We went on like that until twenty to three. Neither of us changed anything. Sanjay went to bed, and I finished the page I was on, a sine wave at seven cycles sampled at eight, and then went to bed myself.

In February Sanjay's girlfriend moved into the apartment for a month. Her lease had run out and the room she had found was not free until March. Sanjay told me on the Thursday that she would be staying a few weeks, and she arrived on the Saturday with two suitcases and a box of textbooks. She had exams coming and studied every evening at the kitchen table, with her books and binders open across the whole of it, a set of highlighters in a mug, and the lamp from Sanjay's room plugged in beside the toaster.

The kitchen table was where I worked in the evenings when I was not at Pilon's or the lab. I kept a sheet of hardboard on it to protect the arborite, with the iron in its stand at one corner and my parts in a muffin tin. The first evening I came home and found her books on it, my hardboard was leaning against the wall with the muffin tin on the floor beside it.

I did not say anything to her, or to Sanjay. I carried the hardboard, the iron and stand, the muffin tin, a desk lamp and the roll of solder into my bedroom and set up on the bed. I sat with my back against the wall and the hardboard across my knees, put the iron's stand on the seat of a kitchen chair beside the bed, and ran an extension cord under the door from the hall outlet, because my room had only the one and the lamp was on it. The flux smoke stayed in the room, so I opened the window two inches, though it was twenty below most of that month and I worked in a sweater.

I built the calibration fixture for the controller on that bed, a small board that held the thermistor beside the reference thermometer in the ice bath, along with the linearizing network and a set of test leads. Once a drop of solder fell off the tip and burnt a hole the size of a pea in the blanket. I ate supper at the counter standing up, or in my room. Sanjay knocked once in the second week and asked if everything was all right, and I said it was.

She left on the second Saturday of March. Sanjay drove her things over in a borrowed van. That evening I carried the hardboard, the iron, the lamp and the muffin tin back out to the kitchen table and set them up where they had been.

In March one of the full-time technicians at Pilon's came over to my bench on a Wednesday afternoon while I had a VCR open. He was a man of about thirty who had the bench nearest the curtain, did televisions mostly, and smoked at the back door. He said his rent was due on Friday and he was fifty dollars short, and payday was not until the Friday after. He asked whether I could lend him fifty until then. He said he would pay it back on payday and that he knew I had it, because I never spent anything.

I had it. The ledger at home showed one thousand seven hundred and forty-two dollars and eighteen cents in the bank as of the end of February, and the envelope in my desk drawer had sixty-five dollars in it for groceries and gas. I knew both numbers without looking them up.

I said no. He asked whether I was sure. I said I was. I was, I can admit at this distance, a cold young man about money.

He stood there a moment longer and then went out through the curtain to the front. I heard him talking to Gord at the counter, low. When he came back he had two twenties and a ten folded in his shirt pocket, and he sat down at his bench and picked up his iron. Gord did not say anything to me about it, that day or afterwards. I finished the VCR, a pinch roller and an idler tire, and wrote it in the log at an hour and a half.

The project was the main business of the third year. Each of us had to design, build and document a working instrument, and Dr. Ruth Abernethy, who had taught me process control in the two-year program, agreed to supervise mine because it was a control loop. I proposed a controller that would hold an aluminum block at any setpoint between forty and ninety degrees Celsius to within half a degree, using only discrete parts: transistors, resistors, capacitors and diodes, and no integrated circuits. Nobody required that. Dr. Abernethy read the proposal, wrote "Ambitious. Fine." in the margin, and initialled it.

The design was simple on paper. A thermistor sat in one arm of a Wheatstone bridge, with the setpoint on a ten-turn potentiometer in the opposite arm. The bridge fed a long-tailed pair of 2N3904s as the error amplifier, then a second gain stage, then an output stage driving a 2N3055 power transistor, which pushed current through a fifty-watt resistor bolted to the block as a heater. The block was a two-inch cube I cut from bar stock in the college machine shop, with one hole drilled for the thermistor and another for a mercury reference thermometer.

I ran it for the first time in the second week of October, with the setpoint at sixty. The block came up, went past to sixty-six, fell back to fifty-four, came up to sixty-seven, and went on swinging, about ninety seconds a cycle, without settling. I watched it for an hour and wrote down each peak. Dr. Abernethy watched two swings and asked what my phase margin was. I did not know. Over the next week I measured the block's time constant with a stopwatch from a step test and drew the loop on semi-log paper. The gain was too high for the lag between the heater and the thermistor: by the time the thermistor felt the heat, the heater had already put in too much. I cut the gain by a third and added a resistor and capacitor to give the loop some derivative action. It settled.

The second failure came in the last week of November. The loop held sixty to within a degree, and I turned the setpoint up to ninety to try the top of the range. With the error that large, the output drove flat out, and the 2N3055 sat at full current with half the supply across it, putting out more heat than the small heat sink could get rid of. After about four minutes there was a smell. The transistor failed shorted, the heater ran full on until I pulled the plug, the driver transistor went with it, and a one-ohm resistor cracked down the middle. The block reached a hundred and thirty degrees by the reference thermometer before it began to cool. I had no current limit, and I had sized the heat sink for the average load, not the worst one. I replaced both transistors, bolted on a heat sink twice the size, and wrote in my notebook that it was a patch and not a fix.

The third failure was the calibration check with Dr. Abernethy in the last week before the Christmas break. We did the ice bath first and then setpoints of fifty, seventy and ninety against the lab's reference thermometer. At fifty the block read fifty-one and a half. At seventy it read seventy point eight. At ninety it read eighty-seven point one. At seventy it also drifted, creeping up half a degree over twenty minutes as the board warmed. She failed it on calibration and gave me until the end of term in April.

There were two causes. The thermistor was not linear, which I had known and had meant to correct with a trimmer, and the trimmer could only be right at one point. The other was the output stage. The bigger heat sink sat two inches from the bridge resistors and warmed them, and the heater current came back through the same ground trace as the bridge, so the harder the block called for heat, the further the bridge's ground moved. The output stage was making the sensor wrong.

Over the Christmas break I redesigned the output stage. I worked my shifts at Pilon's and spent the rest of the time at the kitchen table with graph paper, a calculator and the data books. The new stage was a Darlington pair with emitter resistors, a half-ohm sense resistor, and a small transistor across it that limited the heater current to two amps no matter what the error amplifier asked for. I worked out the heat sink for the worst case with a margin and put it on its own bracket at the far end of the chassis, upwind of nothing. The heater current went back to the supply on its own heavy wire, and the bridge got its own ground to a single point. For the thermistor I calculated a fixed resistor to go in parallel with it, worked out from three points on the data sheet, to straighten its curve across the range. It came to fourteen pages, and I had it finished by New Year's.

In January I rebuilt the controller on the lab bench from a bare board. I did not patch the old one; I laid the new board out from scratch. The lab was open until ten, and I was there every evening I was not at Pilon's, and on Saturdays after my shift. It was finished at the end of the month. February and March went to testing, with the fixture I had built on my bed: six-hour runs at each setpoint, readings taken by hand every fifteen minutes and entered in a table.

In April I took it to Dr. Abernethy. She ran her own test without me touching it: the ice bath, then fifty, seventy and ninety, then a step from forty to ninety and back. It held within three-tenths of a degree at each point, overshot less than a degree on the step, and ran four hours on her bench without drifting more than two-tenths. She said, "It works," and signed the sheet. I handed in the report the same afternoon, sixty-two typed pages, with the three failures written up in full and the old boards in a bag stapled to the back. Those college years are where, I now believe, I learned to finish things.

The third year ended in the spring of 1992. Convocation was on a Friday afternoon at the end of May, and the class dinner was the next evening at a hotel, twenty-eight dollars a ticket. A notice about both went up on the board outside the lab and a form came in the mail asking for my gown size. I did not fill in the form and I did not buy a ticket. On the Friday afternoon I put new front brake pads on the Nova in the lane behind Pilon's, and on the Saturday I worked my shift and then went home.

The following Tuesday I went to the registrar's counter at the college. There was nobody else in line. I gave the woman my student number and signed a sheet, and she handed me the diploma in a stiff cardboard folder. I checked that my name was spelled right. Then I took it to the library and made a photocopy on the coin machine for ten cents.

I mailed the photocopy home to my mother in Brule Falls with a sheet of paper folded around it, on which I wrote three lines: what it was, that the original was in a folder in my desk, and that I was keeping it there. The stamp was forty-two cents. I put the original in the folder in my desk drawer, under the ledger, and wrote the ten cents and the forty-two cents in the ledger that night.

Chapter 14

In 1992 I finished at the college and began taking contract jobs at the mines around Sudbury. I had done the two years of instrumentation and then the third year of electronics. My books went into boxes, except for the ones I still used, and I rented a room alone in a boarding house on Elm Street. I needed an address for invoices, a bed I could use during the day, and somewhere to keep tools without carrying them back and forth in the car. The room had a window, a narrow bed and enough floor left over for a bench. I measured the space beneath the window before I unpacked.

I built the bench from lumber and a sheet of plywood. I cut the legs so the top would clear the sill and screwed a brace across the back to keep the thing from shifting sideways. The floor was out enough that one leg needed a packing piece. With that in place I could put pressure on the front edge without making the opposite corner lift. I countersunk the screw heads in the plywood and went over it with my palm before setting anything on it. A screw head could catch a drawing or score the bottom of a meter case. I put the tool case underneath, where I could reach it without getting out of the chair.

Above the bench I put up a shelf for bins. Resistors, terminals, small relays, panel lamps and fuse holders each got their own space. I separated the fuses by rating and type, with the markings turned forward where possible. A fuse that fitted the holder was not necessarily the right fuse. I wrote the labels in block letters on strips of paper and covered them with clear tape. The bins were shallow enough that I did not have to empty one to find what was at the back. Loose screws went into smaller containers. Used parts stayed apart from new ones until I had checked them.

The ledger occupied the right-hand end of the bench. I ruled columns for the mine, the dates, the hours, the rate, the invoice number and the day the money came in. There was room beside each entry for a correction or an unpaid amount. I kept my time on a separate sheet while a job was running, then copied the total into the ledger when I made out the invoice. I checked the multiplication twice. A mistake in their favour cost money, and a mistake in mine meant another letter and another wait. I filed the copy beneath the time sheet, both folded the same way.

On the first of each month I reconciled the ledger against my bank book. I put a ruler beneath the line I was checking and marked the payment only when I could account for it in both places. An invoice sent was still just paper. I kept track of tool purchases and room costs as well, so that a good run of hours did not appear to be more money than it was. The workbench held the papers flat, the shelf kept parts out of them, and the window gave me enough light to read the small figures during the day. Once it was arranged, I unpacked the rest of the room around it.

The boarding house served supper at one long table. During the first week I sat there with my plate, my knees beneath the edge and my elbows kept in. Chairs stood close enough together that getting up required somebody to move. Dishes passed along the table and stopped where there was room to set them down. I ate what was served and waited when a dish had to come back from the other end. After a shift, or before one, I had little to add to what was being said. I followed enough of it to know when a remark had been directed at me.

The table had its own pace. Somebody would still be eating when my plate was empty, or a space would open beside me while I was halfway through. Cups and cutlery made small interruptions in the talk. I watched the time if I was due at a site that night. My boots and work clothes were upstairs, my lunch still to be packed, and the drive had to be allowed for. There was no reason to hurry the rest of the table. I finished, set my knife and fork together and took my plate where the other used dishes went.

After the first week I took the plate upstairs. I set it on the workbench, moving the ledger and any open parts bins clear of the edge. There was enough room for the plate between the front of the bench and the tools along the wall. I ate sitting in the same chair I used for drawings and invoices. What I took for independence was mostly a liking for my own company. I kept the salt off the papers and put the cup on a folded scrap so it would not leave a ring in the plywood. When I needed the whole bench again, there was little to clear.

I took my supper up every night after that. If a drawing was spread out, I rolled it back and laid a clean piece of paper beneath the plate. I did not eat over an open relay or leave bread beside a tray of terminals. The bench had to serve both purposes, and that required moving things in a fixed order. I brought up what I needed in one trip. Once I had sat down, I stayed there until I had finished. The room was small enough that I could reach the shelf without standing, but I left the parts alone while I ate.

Then I carried the plate back down. I went downstairs only to return it, put it with the dishes and went up again. On nights when I was working, I changed and checked the contents of the tool case. On evenings in the room, I wiped the place where the plate had been and brought the papers forward. The chair fitted beneath the bench when I went to bed. There was no separate table and no need for one. By the end of the first month, the only sign of supper upstairs was the clean patch I had wiped across the plywood.

At the start-up meeting for my first contract, the shift boss went through the work and the shifts. I had a notebook open on my knee. He gave the day shifts to men he knew, using their names as he went down the list. When he reached me, he put me on nights for the whole job. I wrote down the starting time and the hours. He continued with the arrangements for getting on site and the places where the work was to be done. I said nothing about the shift assignment. When the meeting was over, I closed the notebook and left.

I worked the nights. Before leaving Elm Street I packed the meter, leads, hand tools and the items specified for the job. I checked the leads for damaged insulation and made sure the spare fuses belonged to the meter. At the site I signed in and went to the work assigned. The first part of a shift went into finding the current state of the job: what had been opened, what had been put back, what was waiting for a part, and what could be worked on. I kept those notes separate from the hours I would eventually bill.

A night shift changed the use of the room. I came back while there was daylight at the window, put the tools beneath the bench and drew the shade. Boots stayed near the door so I would not carry grit across the floor. I set out the clothes I would need before going to bed. When I got up, there was time to wash, eat supper and check whatever paperwork had come back with me. The ledger was easy enough to fill in with the hours repeated down the page. I still entered each shift separately rather than writing one figure across several dates.

The assignment did not change halfway through. The men on days stayed on days, and I kept reporting at night. I learned which lights gave a useful view inside a cabinet and where a portable lamp could be set without its lead crossing a passage. Small jobs took longer when the right fitting or drawing had to be found before anything else could proceed. I wrote down part numbers as I found them and checked them against what was installed. By the time the contract ended, I had a full run of night hours to total and a notebook with the work recorded beside the dates.

On a contract that took me underground, a shift boss told me the portable gas detectors had all been calibrated the week before. They were laid out ready to be taken. I picked one up and read the tag, then went through the others. The dates were small and some of the tags had been rubbed by use. I turned each detector so I could read the whole entry. Two of them were out by months. I checked those dates a second time against the current date. They had not become current because the detectors were sitting with the rest.

I set the two aside from the ones with current tags. The cases looked much the same. A clip, a display and a few buttons did not tell me when the sensors had last been checked against a known concentration. I looked at the detector I was to carry, read its tag again and checked that the openings were clear. Mine dust lodged in small recesses and around clips. I did not scrape at the sensor openings or blow into them. I checked the case and the battery indication, then found the test gas bottle and the fitting used to apply the gas.

The bottle had its own label to read. I checked the contents and date, fitted the regulator and attached the tubing and test cap. I turned the detector on and let it complete its start-up before applying the gas. A current calibration tag and a response that morning were two separate checks. The bump test was for the response. I watched the display move and checked the audible and visual alarms. The detector made its noise on the bench with the test gas feeding it. I shut the bottle off, removed the cap and let the reading return in clean air.

I put the test equipment back and clipped the detector where I could read it. Before taking it underground I made sure the tubing and cap had been removed and the openings were exposed. The two outdated tags remained the two outdated tags. I did not alter them or put a fresh date over the old one. My own detector had a current calibration date and had responded to the bottle. I took that one. The check went into my notes with the rest of the preparations, and I carried the notebook in a pocket where the pages would stay dry.

The hoist control panel underground had old relay drawings behind its present arrangement of wires and labels. I asked for those drawings. The panel could be followed contact by contact with a meter, but that did not explain why a condition was required at one point in the sequence and repeated at another. I wanted the sheets that showed the original circuit. When I got them, I checked the drawing numbers and the references between pages. Some lines continued through several sheets before coming back to the relay I had started with. I took the drawings back to Elm Street.

After supper I cleared the bench and spread them beneath the window. The folds had to be held down at the corners. I worked with a pencil, keeping my marks light enough to erase, and followed the supply through the first set of contacts. A relay name appeared beside its coil on one sheet and beside its contacts elsewhere. I wrote the page references on a separate sheet rather than trusting that I would remember them. Where a contact was shown open, I checked whether that was its state with the coil de-energized. Starting with the wrong assumption made the rest of the circuit meaningless.

I spent my evenings working out the order of the interlocks. It was not enough to list the conditions that had to be satisfied. I traced what happened if one condition arrived before another, and what remained picked up after a condition disappeared. I followed the holding circuits and the reset path. A relay could have the right contacts and still do the wrong thing if it picked up at the wrong stage. I went back through the sequence with the coils marked in pencil, then rubbed the marks out and tried it from the stopped condition again. Each pass used the same sheets.

There was one electrician on site who would listen to it over coffee. I brought the drawings out and put a finger on the contact I meant. He followed the circuit from there and put his finger somewhere else. We argued about why the original designers had required that relay to drop before another could pick up. I said the order was there to prevent a condition being carried into the next operation. He took it back through the holding circuit. The coffee stood beside the drawings while we checked the cross-references. Neither of us could settle it by pointing at the coil alone.

Back in the room, I traced that part again. I drew a short sequence on scrap paper, with the relay states beneath each step, and compared it with the circuit. Then I included an interrupted sequence instead of allowing every operation to finish. That was where the arrangement began to make sense. The interlocks did not merely permit the next movement; they required the circuit to have come through the preceding states in the proper order. I went through it once more without my pencil notes. Before returning the drawings, I folded them on their existing creases and kept my own pages with the contract papers.

The flotation plant fault came to me after two contractors had given up on it. A level control on the cells dropped out a few times a night. By the time anyone got to the cabinet, the circuit would often be back in a state that gave little away. A level reading on a display did not show what had happened a minute earlier. The contract gave me three weeks, and I worked nights through it. I put a fresh sheet in the notebook and ruled columns for the time, the indication, the controller state and whatever equipment had started or stopped nearby.

For the first nights I recorded each trip by the minute. I used the same watch and checked the time before writing it down. If I had to copy an indication and inspect a terminal, I recorded which came first. The cells, the transmitter, the cabinet and the pumps were separated by enough distance that walking between them used time. I did not make an observation at one place stand for all of them. When a trip had cleared before I reached the panel, that went into the book as well. Empty spaces were left empty rather than filled from what usually happened.

The transmitter was the first thing to rule out properly. I checked its supply, connections and output, and compared the signal with the level it was meant to represent. I used a loop calibrator to check the receiving side at fixed values. A steady test signal made it possible to see whether the indication followed it through the range. I let it sit at points instead of winding straight from one end to the other. There was no use proving that it could display a mid-range value for two seconds. I wrote the readings down and returned the connections to their working arrangement.

The fault still occurred. I repeated the checks where the first result needed confirming, then put the transmitter lower on the list. That took much of the first week. Between trips I followed the wiring route and checked the terminations against the drawing. I looked for damaged insulation, loose strands and places where a screen or drain had been taken somewhere it did not belong. A terminal could look sound from above while the wire beneath it was poorly held. Where the circuit had to be disconnected for a check, I kept track of each lead and restored it before returning the equipment to service.

The second week went into ruling out the wiring and then the controller. I checked continuity and insulation with the relevant equipment disconnected, and checked the signal at the cabinet as well as at the transmitter. I compared the controller's supply and input with its output state when the level control was operating normally. A controller could be blamed for dropping an output when it had actually been given an input that called for it. I checked the settings against the intended operation, then tested the response with a known signal. Each night added a few more timed trips to the pages already ruled.

The notebook became more useful when several nights could be laid beside one another. I copied the trip times onto a single sheet and marked the equipment changes recorded near them. There were runs that appeared related and others that did not. I kept the ones that did not fit instead of crossing them out. The transmitter had checked out, the wiring had checked out through the tests I had made, and the controller had responded properly to a known input. Yet the working circuit still dropped. I went back to the difference between testing a part by itself and having the whole plant connected and running.

The pump motor gave me the connection I had been missing. I checked the potential between the signal reference and earth under the operating conditions I had been recording. Then I traced the return paths instead of following only the signal conductor from one terminal to the next. There was a second path through the pump motor arrangement. The signal circuit was part of a ground loop, and what reached the controller was being disturbed through that loop. A continuity check with the equipment stopped had not reproduced it. I followed it far enough to account for the path on paper before changing the circuit.

I fixed it on the last night of the contract. I removed the unintended signal-earth path and arranged the signal grounding at the proper single point, leaving the motor's protective earth intact. Then I checked the circuit again with the pump operating. I watched the level indication and the controller state, made the same measurements I had made before, and wrote down the times. The remaining hours gave me no further dropout. At the end of the shift I recorded exactly what I had found and changed. Three weeks of night hours went on the invoice. The last entry in the fault notes was the ground loop through the pump motor.

A man doing the same contract work asked me to lend him the damage deposit for a room. He put the request plainly. It was money for the deposit, to get the room, and he would pay it back. We were between the work and the next thing that had to be done, with no papers spread out and no reason for him to know what was in my bank book. I had been paid on enough of the contracts by then that the balance in my ledger was healthy. The rent on Elm Street was covered, and there was money beyond the amounts I had set aside.

I knew the balance without opening the ledger. I had reconciled it on the first, bringing the bank book and the payment column into agreement. There were still invoices separate from that balance, but the money already received was enough. The deposit he needed would not have emptied the account. I did not work out a repayment schedule or ask which day he expected to be paid. I told him I did not lend money. That was the answer I gave him, and I left it at that. I did not take out my wallet.

He had asked for a loan, so there was no need to mistake the request for anything else. I did not offer part of the deposit or another way of paying it. There was a pause after I answered. I kept my hands where they were and waited until the exchange was finished. Then I returned to the work in front of me. The tools had to be put in their case and the hours entered. Neither required changing because of the request. When I next opened the ledger, the balance was the same apart from the ordinary entries I had to make.

The ledger had no column for loans. There were the mines, dates, hours, rates, invoice numbers and payments, with expenses recorded separately. I checked a total and carried it forward. The money in the bank was money from hours already worked, less what had gone out. I could account for it without adding another person's promised payment to the page. I put the ruler back along the ledger's spine and closed it. The man had asked once in that conversation. I had answered once. There was no deposit amount to enter among my expenses.

In the spring of 1993, a contract electrician bet me ten dollars that I would not climb the headframe at an abandoned mine near the job site. It was raining. The steelwork rose above the unused shaft, with the sheave deck at the top and ladders running up through it. From the ground I could see the large wheel and parts of the landings. Water came off the steel in separate drips where the members met. I looked at the ladder and the route from one landing to the next. The electrician repeated the amount. Ten dollars was what he had said.

I climbed the headframe at the old mine on a bet that spring, idiotically, in the rain. The first rungs left rust on my hands. I put each boot far enough onto the steel that the sole had something beneath it, then moved a hand and went up another rung. Rain ran down the ladder rails and collected where a rung joined them. At the first landing I brought both feet onto the platform before turning towards the next ladder. My clothes were already wet across the shoulders. I kept the centre of my boot over each rung and worked my way up.

The ladder sections did not form one straight climb. At each landing I had to step clear, turn and take hold again. Water lay on the flat steel and ran away through openings in the deck. I wiped one palm against my trousers, which did little, and took the rail again. From inside the structure, the braces crossed the view in pieces. The ground appeared between them when I changed position. I looked where my hands and feet had to go. The wheel at the top grew larger a section at a time, until the underside of the sheave deck was above me.

I came through onto the deck and stood with both boots on it. The big wheel was close enough to reach from there. Its rim and spokes held water, and the groove around it carried the dirt left by disuse. I put my hand against the wheel. That was the top of the climb and the thing the bet had been about. I left my hand there long enough to have touched it properly, then turned back to the ladder opening. There was no other work to do on the deck. I took the rails and lowered one foot until the sole found the rung.

Going down required finding the next rung below each boot before moving the other. The rain was on the back of my neck now. I reversed the turns at the landings, stepped over the same joints in the steel and continued down the sections I had climbed. Near the bottom, the ground filled the spaces between the braces again. I stepped off the last rung and wiped my hands down my wet trousers. The contract electrician gave me the ten dollars. I folded it into my wallet. My cuffs were wet, and rust had worked into the creases of my palms.

At the end of one contract, the crew stood around in the dry while the shift boss handed out cold beers from a cooler. I had finished changing and was putting the last of my work clothes away. The benches had boots beneath them and damp patches on the floor beside them. Doors stood open along the lockers. The cooler sat where the shift boss could reach into it without moving from the group. Each time the lid opened, he brought out another beer. I closed my bag and checked that the small tools I had brought in with me were inside.

He held one out to me. I took it because it was already in front of my hand, and felt the cold through the container. Water had collected on it from the cooler. Around us, the crew stayed where they were, talking with their beers held at their sides or in front of them. I had my bag ready and the car keys in my pocket. I looked at the beer, then handed it back unopened. The shift boss took it. I shifted the bag so that my right hand was free and shook his hand.

There was no work left for me to check on that contract. The hours were written down, and whatever still had to be copied could be done at the bench. I went out of the dry with the bag in one hand. At the car I put it where it would not fall against the pedals or roll when I turned. I checked my pockets for the keys before shutting the door on the bag. The drive back was the same route I used after a shift, with the site behind me and Elm Street at the other end.

At the boarding house I carried the bag upstairs. The workbench was beneath the window, with the bins on the shelf and the ledger at the right-hand end. I took the tools out before putting the clothes away. The meter went in its place, leads loosely coiled rather than wound tight around it. I checked the hours against the dates and copied them onto the invoice sheet. Then I entered the mine, the rate and the invoice number in the ledger. I left the payment date blank. When the figures agreed, I put the papers together on the cleared end of the bench.

Chapter 15

In 1994 I was twenty-three and in my third year of contract work at the mines around Sudbury. I still had the room on Elm Street, second floor at the back, with one window over the lane and the backs of three garages. The rent was two hundred and sixty dollars a month, paid in cash on the first, and for that I got a single bed, a bar fridge, a two-burner hot plate, a dresser with one sticking drawer, and a kitchen table that I had raised on four blocks of two-by-four so I could stand at it. The table was my bench. I had a small machinist's vise bolted to one end, a Weller soldering station, a magnifier lamp on a swing arm, and a row of baby-food jars screwed by their lids to the underside of the shelf above it, so the screws and nuts and spade lugs hung at eye level where I could see what was in them. Work came through the telephone in the downstairs hall. The landlady took messages on a pad she kept by it and pushed them under my door, and most weeks there was at least one, a maintenance superintendent or a contractor who needed a technician for a few days or a few weeks to calibrate transmitters, chase a fault in a control loop, or wire a panel somebody else had designed.

That January I rebuilt my kit. For two years I had carried my instruments in two plastic milk crates and an old tackle box, and they had knocked against each other on every gravel road between Levack and Coniston. The leads tangled. The analog meter had a cracked window I had patched with clear tape. I had driven out to a job in November and found I had left the clamp-on ammeter on the bench at home, and spent an hour of the mine's time borrowing one from their electrical shop. I decided over Christmas that the kit would be set up so that could not happen again.

I went to the army surplus store on Elgin Street and bought six metal ammunition boxes at four dollars each and two larger fibreglass cases with latches that had been made for something else, I never found out what. I bought a sheet of dense grey foam from an upholstery shop and cut it with a bread knife heated on the hot plate, one cut-out for each instrument and one for its leads and one for its spare battery. The Fluke 87 got its own box. The old Simpson 260 got its own box. The megger, the clamp-on ammeter, the decade resistance box, the frequency counter and the pneumatic test gauge each got one. I punched a label for every box on a Dymo label maker I had bought for nine dollars, the kind where you turn the wheel to each letter and squeeze, and stuck the label on the lid and a second one on the end, so I could read it with the boxes stacked in the trunk.

Then I started the binder. It was a black three-ring binder with tabbed dividers, one tab for each instrument. On each page I wrote the make, the model, the serial number, the date I had last checked it, what I had checked it against, and the readings. For the Fluke I had taken it to the electronics lab at the college the month before and checked it against their bench standard, and I wrote down the readings at each range: the volts, the milliamps, the ohms, out to the last digit the display showed. For the others I checked them against the Fluke and wrote that down too, with the date. If an instrument had no known reference reading it did not go on a job. Every page had room for twelve more entries below the first one.

The last thing was the checklist. I typed it on the landlady's typewriter, which she let me use for a dollar, on one sheet of paper, and I covered it both sides with clear packing tape and taped it inside the lid of the main toolbox, a red Waterloo with a cantilever tray. It said: meters, each box, open and look. Batteries, spare, each meter. Leads, both colours, each meter. Binder. Drawings for the job, if sent. Screwdrivers, flat and Robertson, insulated. Wire strippers. Crimpers. Lugs and ferrules. Heat-shrink. Electrical tape, two rolls. Wire markers. Flashlight and spare cells. Hard hat, lamp, belt, battery charged. Safety glasses. Steel-toes. Gloves. Notebook. Pencils, two. Lunch. I went through it before every job, standing in the lane at six in the morning with the trunk open and the dome light on, touching each item as I read it off. It took about four minutes. I did not leave anything at home that year.

In April I bought a signal calibrator. I had been borrowing the mines' calibrators when they had one to lend, and doing without when they did not, and doing loop checks with the decade box and the Fluke, which worked but was slow. A man who sold used test equipment out of a garage behind his house on the Kingsway had one, a handheld unit that could source and read four to twenty milliamps and zero to ten volts, in a leather case with the original leads. He wanted four hundred and twenty dollars. I asked him on the phone whether it had been checked, and he said it had been calibrated last month, it had a sticker on it, it was good.

I drove over on a Saturday. It was raining, the cold sideways rain Sudbury gets in April when the snow is half gone and the slag heaps are black with the wet. The garage had a wood stove going and shelves of oscilloscopes and power supplies with paper tags on strings. He took the calibrator off a shelf and showed me the sticker. It had a date on it from March and a set of initials. I said I would like to check it before I paid, and he said go ahead, and I went back out to the car and brought in the box with the Fluke.

I cleared a space on his bench and set the calibrator to source current and put the Fluke in series on its milliamp range. I checked it at five points: four milliamps, eight, twelve, sixteen and twenty, which is zero, a quarter, half, three quarters and full scale on a standard loop. At each one I let the reading settle and wrote it down. Four point zero zero one. Eight point zero zero zero. Eleven point nine nine eight. Fifteen point nine nine nine. Twenty point zero zero two. Then I did the same on the voltage side, zero, two and a half, five, seven and a half and ten, and wrote those down too. All of it was well inside the tolerance printed in the manual, which he also had, in a plastic sleeve. He stood beside me with his arms folded while I did it, and when I was done he said he had told me it was calibrated. I said that he had. I paid him four hundred dollars in twenties, which he took after I had written the numbers, and I drove home and gave the calibrator its own page in the binder, with the date, the Fluke as the reference, and the ten readings in two columns. Then I cut it a space in the foam of the second fibreglass case.

The crews I worked with on contract jobs were mostly the same thirty or forty men, electricians and millwrights and instrument people who moved from mine to mine the way I did. Some of them had been doing it since the sixties. In the summer a group of them had a Saturday ball team in a slo-pitch league that played at a diamond out past the arena in New Sudbury, and in the winter the same group, more or less, had a team in a curling league at a club on the other side of town. They wore matching shirts in the summer with the name of a bar on the back, which bought the shirts.

In June, on a job putting new level transmitters on the sumps at a mine near Copper Cliff, one of the electricians said at lunch that they were short an outfielder. He asked if I played. I said I had played a bit as a kid. He said they played Saturdays at ten, and afterwards everybody went over to the bar on the shirts. I said no thanks, I had things to do on Saturdays. He said suit yourself, and asked again two weeks later on a different job, and I said the same thing.

What I did on Saturdays was work at the bench. By then I had a small trade on the side repairing things for some of the mines' instrument shops when they were backed up: transmitter boards, chart recorders, a controller with a dead display. I charged forty-five dollars an hour, which was the shop rate the electronics shops in town charged, and I kept a ledger of the hours in the back of my notebook. On a Saturday in July I would have the window open and a fan in it, blowing out, and the parts laid on a towel on the bench in the order they had come off, and I would work from eight in the morning until the light went, with a break at noon for a sandwich. The garage across the lane had a man who worked on his own car on Saturdays, a Pontiac with the hood up, and that was most of what I saw outside.

In November, on a job at a mill, the same electrician who had asked me in June came over to the truck where I was packing up and said the curling league was starting and they needed a fourth for a second rink. I said I had never curled. He said nobody had, you learned, it was Tuesday nights, the bar was upstairs. I said no, but thanks for asking. It seems to me now that I left the crowd behind the way you leave a coat you have outgrown. He shook his head and laughed and went back inside, and I latched the cases and went through the checklist backwards, which is how I did it at the end of a job, and drove home. The next summer someone asked again, a millwright this time, and I said no again, and after that people mostly stopped asking, though now and then one of the older men would say at lunch on a Monday that I had missed a good game.

That same year the mines started taking out relay panels and putting in programmable controllers. The relay panels were the old kind, rows of relays and timers and contactors on a steel backplate, the logic built out of wire. A conveyor system might have two hundred relays in a panel the size of a wardrobe, and when it failed you traced it with a meter and the drawings. The programmable controllers did the same job with a processor and racks of input and output cards and a program written in ladder logic, which was drawn to look like the relay drawings, rungs between two rails, so that electricians could read it. On two jobs that spring I worked beside people installing them and could follow the wiring to the cards but not past that, and I did not like stopping at the edge of the card.

I found a used manual for one of the common processors at the back of a stall at the flea market in the old arena. It was a thick binder, slightly water-stained on the bottom edge, and the man wanted forty dollars. I gave him thirty-five. I read it on weekends at the bench, a few chapters at a time, with my notebook beside it, and copied out the parts I needed in my own words, which is how I had always learned things.

The part I spent the most time on was the scan. The processor does not watch its inputs all the time the way a relay panel does, where a contact closes and current flows and the next relay pulls in. It works in a cycle. First it reads the state of every input card and writes it into a table in memory, the input image. Then it solves the program, rung by rung, from the top to the bottom, using the values in that table, not the real inputs. As it solves each rung it writes the results to an output table. When it gets to the bottom it sends the output table out to the output cards all at once, does some housekeeping, and starts again at the top. The manual said a typical scan took ten to thirty milliseconds, depending on the size of the program.

I drew this out in the notebook four or five times, a box for the inputs, a box for the program, a box for the outputs, arrows going around. The question I kept coming back to was what happens to an input that changes while the program is being solved. The answer, from the way it worked, was that the program did not see it. The input image was fixed for that scan. If a limit switch closed when the processor was halfway down the program, the rungs below that point still saw it open, and it would not be seen as closed until the next read at the top of the next scan. If a switch closed and opened again inside one scan, a short pulse from a proximity sensor on a fast shaft, say, the processor might never see it at all. There were special instructions to read an input immediately, in the middle of the program, and I read that chapter twice. I also worked out that the order of the rungs mattered. If a rung near the top used a bit that was set by a rung near the bottom, it was using last scan's value, and you could build a program that did something different depending on which way round you wrote two rungs that looked the same.

In August I rode out to a mine north of Capreol with a contract electrician who had been putting these controllers in for a year. It was his truck, a three-quarter-ton with a cap on the back, and we left at five-thirty in the morning. It was already warm and there was smoke haze to the north from a fire up past Timmins. Somewhere past Hanmer I asked him how the processor handled an input that changed partway through the scan. He said it saw it right away, it was a computer, it was fast. I said it did not, that it read all the inputs at the start and worked off the table, and the change would not show until the next scan. He said that was the same thing, the scan was twenty milliseconds, nothing in a mine happened in twenty milliseconds. I said a proximity switch on a tail pulley could, at speed, and that a pulse shorter than the scan could be missed entirely, and if you were counting pulses you would count short. He said then you used a high-speed counter card, which was true, and I said that was my point, the program itself would not see it. He said I had been reading too many manuals. I took the notebook out of my shirt pocket and drew the three boxes and the arrow on the dashboard and he looked at it at sixty miles an hour on a two-lane highway and said that was how it was drawn but that was not how it worked. We went on like this for about forty minutes, until the turnoff to the mine, and neither of us changed his mind on the drive. At the gatehouse he said I should come and see one of his installs, and I said I would if it was on a job I was on.

In September I was hired for three weeks at a mine west of the city to wire a new panel for a dewatering pump station on one of the lower levels. The pumps were two big centrifugals with six-hundred-volt motors, and the panel had the starters, the level controls and the alarms, with four to twenty milliamp signals coming in from two level transmitters in the sump and a flow transmitter on the discharge line. I had done the wiring plan the week before from the mechanical drawings, and I had put the signal cables in their own conduit, on the far side of the drift from the cable tray that carried the motor leads, which is how I had been taught and how I had always done it. A low-level signal pair running for any distance beside a heavy power cable picks up current from it, and the readings drift and jump.

The engineer on the project was a young man out of the mine's head office. He went through my plan on the Monday, and on the Wednesday the drawing came back revised. He had taken out my conduit run and put the signal cables in the same tray as the power, alongside the motor leads, for about forty metres from the panel to the sump. He had initialled the change and written a note that it saved a conduit run and two days of labour. I looked at the drawing at the lunch table in the dry with my coffee, and I could see what it would do. Forty metres of shielded pair beside six hundred volt motor leads, with two motors starting and stopping on the levels in the sump, would put noise on the level signals every time a pump came on.

I did not say anything. The engineer had stamped the drawing, and I was a contractor hired to wire the panel. I wired it the way the drawing said. I pulled the signal cables into the tray beside the power cables and laid them flat and tied them every metre with nylon ties, and I grounded the shields at the panel end only, the way the drawing showed and the way it would be done in any case, and I landed every conductor and marked every wire. I did the loop checks with the new calibrator at five points on each transmitter and wrote the readings on the commissioning sheets and in my own notebook. The motors were not running yet when I did the loop checks, because the mechanical people had not finished the pump bases. I signed off my part of the job on the Friday of the third week and handed in the sheets and the marked-up drawing and drove home.

In October, on a Sunday, I drove north of the city on a gravel road I had seen on a topographic map at the library. The map showed a symbol for an old mine working in the bush about two kilometres off the road, near a small lake. It was a cold clear day, a few degrees above freezing, with the leaves mostly down and a skin of ice on the puddles in the ruts. I parked where the road widened at a culvert and walked in on an old track that had grown in with poplar and alder, carrying my lamp in a canvas bag, the big six-volt lantern from the kit, with a small flashlight in my coat pocket and two spare cells for it.

The adit was in a low rock face at the end of the track. There had been a timber collar on the portal once, and most of it had fallen in, and the opening was about a metre and a half high and a little wider than that, with a fan of broken rock in front of it and water running out over the rock. Two lengths of narrow-gauge rail came out of the opening and stopped. I put on my hard hat, switched on the lantern, and went in.

The drift was cut straight into the rock, about two metres high once I was past the portal, with the rails on the floor in an inch or two of water. The water was clear and very cold, and it came in over the tops of my boots in places. The walls were dark green rock with rusty streaks, and every so often there was a drill hole in the back where a round had been fired and the hole had not broken out all the way. At about sixty paces the back had come down. The fallen rock made a slope that went nearly to the roof, with a gap at the top about the height of a man lying down. I looked at the back above the gap with the lantern and it was solid rock, no loose slabs hanging, and the fall was old, the rocks on top of it with a coat of fine mud on them. I pushed the lantern ahead of me and went over it on my stomach, slowly, and came down the other side on the seat of my pants.

Past the fall the drift went on another forty or fifty metres and ended at a face with drill holes in it. About ten metres before the face there was an opening in the floor on the left, a raise going down. It was timbered, a square box of logs on all four sides, and down one side was a ladder made of two rough timbers for stringers with rungs spiked across them. The top of it had a short platform, half rotted. I shone the lantern down. It went about eight metres and opened at the bottom into another level, and I could see water shining on the floor down there.

I tested the top rungs with my hand, then with one boot, then with my weight, one at a time. The first three were sound. I hung the lantern from my belt by its bail, so it swung against my hip and lit the timber beside me, and I went down facing the ladder. I put my weight on each rung near the stringer, not the middle, and kept one hand on a stringer and the other on the cribbing. The sixth rung from the top was soft under my boot and I stepped past it. Two more lower down I stepped past the same way. At the bottom I stood in water to my ankles in a drift that ran off both ways. There was a pile of rusted drill steel against one wall, a wooden powder box with the lid off and nothing in it, and an ore car on its side half under some fallen rock. I walked about thirty metres each way until the drift was blocked, and looked at the rock and the old steel with the lantern, and then I went back to the raise and climbed up, stepping past the same three rungs, and went back over the fall on my stomach, and out the portal.
