It was about three in the afternoon. My feet were wet to the ankle and I drove home with the heater on them.

One afternoon in December I drove downtown to the electrical wholesaler for a box of terminal blocks and some DIN rail for a panel I was building at home on a side job. The lot behind the wholesaler was full, so I had parked two streets over, and on the way back I was walking with the box of terminal blocks under one arm. It was about three-thirty and already starting to get dark, minus eighteen, with a wind coming up the street and snow blowing off the roofs in fine sheets. I passed a church, red brick with a set of wide stone steps that had been shovelled and salted, and the sign out front had the times of the Masses on it, and the date for a Christmas concert, and today's date at the top in plastic letters. I looked at the date and remembered it was Mémère's birthday.

I went up the steps and in. It was warm inside and smelled of floor wax and candle smoke, and there was nobody in it but a woman down at the front, dusting the altar rail. Along the wall on the right there was an iron stand with three rows of votive candles in red glass, most of them unlit, and a tray of long wooden tapers in sand. Mémère went to early Mass every morning at St. Anne's in Brule Falls, and had for as long as I could remember. I set the box of terminal blocks on the end of a pew. I took a taper, lit it from one of the candles that was already burning, and lit one candle in the second row, and put the taper back in the sand. Then I stood there for about a minute, with my hands in my coat pockets, and looked at it. Then I picked up the box and went out and down the steps and walked the two streets to the car, and scraped the windshield, and drove home.

In January 1995 I got a call from the maintenance superintendent at one of the older mines east of the city. He had a hoist control panel that had been half rebuilt. The hoist was a double-drum hoist on a production shaft, put in in the 1960s, with a motor-generator set and the old relay control: a steel panel about four metres long, full of relays, timers, contactors, the overwind and overspeed protection, the slowdown circuits, the brake controls, the signal interlocks from the shaft. The mine had hired a contractor the previous fall to replace the old relays and the rotten wiring and bring it up to the new code. The contractor had pulled about half the old wiring and put in about a third of the new, and then had walked off the job. The superintendent said the man had told him the drawings were wrong and he could not finish it from them. The hoist had been down for six weeks and they were hoisting ore on the other shaft.

I drove out the next morning and looked at it. The panel was in the hoist room, a big high room with the drums behind a rail and the motor-generator set at the far end, and the panel stood along the wall facing the hoistman's chair. Half the relays were new, half old. Some of the wires had new markers, some had the old ones, some had none, and some ended in bundles taped off and hanging. The drawings were a set of blueprints from 1964 with red pencil changes on them from several different people over thirty years, and a newer set the contractor had started, that matched neither the old drawings nor the panel. I spent a day going through it with the superintendent's electrician and a meter. The old drawings were wrong in a dozen places I found in that first day, from changes made over the years and never recorded. I told the superintendent I would take it on at my hourly rate, and that the first thing I would do was trace every wire in the panel and draw it from scratch, because I would not work from the drawings they had. He agreed.

I did the tracing on day shift over two weeks. The mine locked out the hoist and gave me the panel and their electrician as a helper to hold the far end of the meter leads. We worked from terminal one on the top left strip, and for every wire we found both ends, buzzed it with the meter, gave it a new marker with a number, and wrote it on a wire list on a clipboard: wire number, from, to, colour, gauge, what it did if we knew. Where a wire went out to the shaft, to a limit switch on the headframe or a signal station down the shaft, we followed it out to the junction box and buzzed it there. We traced about eleven hundred conductors.

Then I redrew it. I had bought a used drafting table at an office auction the year before for thirty dollars, with a parallel bar, and it stood folded against the wall of the room on Elm Street. I set it up next to the bench, and every evening for four weeks I came home from the mine, ate, and drew from seven until midnight, from the wire list, on sheets of vellum with a pencil and a lettering template. I drew the power circuits first, then the control, one function to a sheet: brake, overwind, overspeed, slowdown, signals, hoistman's controls. Where the old circuit was wrong by the current code, I drew it the way it should be and marked the change in a cloud with a note. When I had a set of thirty-one sheets I took them to a blueprint shop and had three copies made, and took one to the superintendent and one to the mine's chief electrician, and they went over them for a week and signed them.

The rebuild and the testing took two months. I worked twelve-hour shifts, six in the morning to six at night, six days a week. I pulled the rest of the old wire and the old relays, landed the new wire to the new drawings, sheet by sheet, and checked every circuit off with a yellow highlighter on my own copy as I finished and tested it. Then came the acceptance tests. The mine's electrician and a mechanical man ran the hoist from the chair and I stood at the panel with the drawings and a meter, and an inspector from the provincial ministry came for the main ones. We ran the empty skips at slow speed, then at full speed, and tested each limit: the overwind at the top and the bottom, set so that the brakes would set if a skip went past the dump; the slowdown switches that cut the speed approaching the ends of the wind; the overspeed device on the drum shaft. We tripped each one on purpose, more than once. We tested the brakes with test weights in the skips, and timed how long it took to stop, and measured the distance. Every result went on a sheet and was signed by the inspector and the chief electrician. Three times a test failed. Once a slowdown switch had been mounted a few inches out on the headframe, which was a mechanical job, and twice it was my wiring, a relay contact I had landed on the normally open terminal instead of the normally closed, and a timer set wrong. I found each one from the drawings, fixed it, marked the change on all three sets, and we ran the test again. At the end of April the inspector signed the last sheet and the hoist went back into production. I handed in the drawings, the wire list, the test sheets and my invoice, which came to a little over fourteen thousand dollars for the three months, and drove home and slept most of a Sunday.

There was an old man in the room at the front of the second floor, down the hall from me. He had lived there longer than I had and kept to himself as much as I did. We said good morning in the hall and that was about all. In May he knocked on my door one evening, which nobody did, holding a kitchen radio, an old AM table radio in brown plastic with a round dial. He said it only played static and asked if I could look at it, he had seen me carrying meters up the stairs. He said he listened to the hockey on it and the news.

I said I could, and that my rate was forty-five dollars an hour, the same as I charged the mines, with a one-hour minimum. He looked at the radio, and then at me, and said all right. I took it in and he went back down the hall.

I did it that evening. I took the back off it and blew out thirty years of dust with a can of air. It was a transistor set, early, with the parts on a single board and a ferrite rod antenna clipped along the back. The batteries were fine. When I turned it on and tuned across the dial there was static everywhere and no stations. I found it in about twenty minutes with the magnifier: one of the fine wires off the antenna coil on the ferrite rod had broken where it went to the board, probably from the radio being moved across a counter for years, and the end had pulled back under the varnish. I scraped the end clean with a knife, tinned it, and soldered it back on, and put a dab of wax on it to hold it. Then I cleaned the tuning capacitor and the volume control with contact cleaner, because they were scratchy, and checked the electrolytic capacitors with the meter, which were all right. When I turned it on again the CBC came in clear at the low end of the dial, a man reading the weather for northern Ontario, and two other stations came in further up. It was about an hour and a half altogether.

I took it down the hall and knocked. He opened the door and I set the radio on his table, by the window, and turned it on so he could hear it, and gave him the bill, which I had written on a sheet from my notebook: one and a half hours at forty-five dollars, sixty-seven dollars and fifty cents, parts nil. He read it, and went to his dresser and took an envelope out of the top drawer and counted out three twenties, a five, two loonies and two quarters onto the table. I picked them up, thanked him, and went back to my room and wrote it in the ledger at the back of the notebook, with the date, and put the money in the coffee tin on the shelf over the bench.

Chapter 16

In 1995 I came home to Brule Falls to take the instrument technician’s job at the mill. I was twenty-four. The mine contracts around Sudbury had left me with a tool chest, several pairs of work trousers and enough money to move without borrowing from Armand. I packed the room on Elm Street and brought everything north in the truck. The job posting said days. I folded it into quarters and kept it with the papers Walt Kerrigan had given me. At the mill I would be working under Walt, beside Annie Duquette, who was an electrician.

I rented a flat over the laundromat on Main Street. It had a kitchen table with one short leg, a bedroom facing the street and a bathroom where the hot tap needed a firm turn to stop dripping. I put a folded piece of cardboard under the table leg. The laundromat’s dryers ran below the kitchen, and their exhaust made the back steps warm when I came in from work. There was room under the bed for the tool chest once I had taken out the tools I needed at the mill.

I paid the rent by the month and wrote the amount in my notebook on the same page as the truck insurance and groceries. After the boarding house, having a separate kitchen meant I could leave a book open without clearing a place for somebody else. I bought a plain ruled pad for expenses and kept my work papers in an envelope in a drawer. The flat had two cups, four plates and more cupboard space than I could use. I brought my own kettle.

Walt showed me the instrument shop on my first morning. The bench had a steel top, a vice at one end and a deadweight tester under a fitted cover at the other. There were meters in cases, coils of tubing and cabinets of small parts. Annie had her own space for electrical work. She pointed out which extension lead had a damaged socket and which cupboard stuck in damp weather. Walt gave me the keys I needed and took me out to the digester floor before I had finished opening the drawers.

In my first week I asked where the calibration records were kept. Walt pointed under the bench. I pulled out a fruit crate with a split in one side and found the history in carbon copies. Some were folded into envelopes. Some had been stapled to work orders, then pulled loose with the corners still attached. There were dates written over dates and tag numbers on the backs of packing slips. The bottom sheets had taken the shape of the crate. I put the whole thing on the bench and started with the papers that lay flat.

I made three piles, then five, and finally cleared enough bench to put them in tag order. A transmitter might have six sheets covering several years, with a different abbreviation on each. Another had one faded copy with no year. I used the tag number as the first check and the stated range as the second. When neither was enough, I went out to the floor and read the plate. I did not enter a date because it seemed likely. I left the space empty and kept the doubtful sheet beside it.

I bought a ledger that would lie open on the bench. Each transmitter got a page, with the tag number at the top, its range below, and columns for the last calibration date, the as-found readings and the as-left readings. I ruled the columns with a straightedge. For an instrument with several old calibrations I entered them in date order and put the original copies together behind a paper band. The carbon often came off on the side of my hand. By the end of an evening I had to wash before touching the clean pages.

For a month I stayed after my shift and worked through the crate. I took a sandwich out of my lunch box if there was one left and made tea in the shop. The mill sounded different from the bench after the day work had thinned out. I could hear a loose panel somewhere beyond the door and the air escaping when the compressor unloaded. I worked until I had finished a tag, not until I had reached a particular time. Then I covered the tester, put the papers away and drove back to Main Street.

The parts drawers needed the same treatment. There were fittings in drawers marked for fuses, fuses in an unmarked drawer and several sorts of seals together. I sorted by size and type, checked what I could against the stock and made labels with the embossed tape machine. I pressed each letter firmly enough to raise it through the plastic, cut the strip square and wiped the drawer front before sticking it down. I left room for a second strip where a drawer held two sizes. The backing accumulated in a little curled heap beside my elbow.

Before I had finished, the mill held its summer picnic at the river park. The whole crew and their families were there. I drove down on Saturday afternoon and parked where I could pull straight out. There were tables, coolers and people carrying paper plates between them. I got one hot dog and took it back to the truck. The bun split along the bottom, so I kept the plate underneath it while I ate. Annie saw me from beside a table. I nodded to her, then to Walt when he looked over.

I stayed by the truck while I finished eating. There was mustard on one finger, and I wiped it with the folded edge of the napkin before opening the door. I had been there less than half an hour when I left. The mill yard had plenty of space to park. Inside, the shop was empty, and I took the tape machine from the drawer where I had put it on Friday. I had the small brass fittings left to sort, along with two drawers of terminal blocks.

I spread the fittings on a clean rag so they would not roll off the steel bench. The nominal sizes on the drawers were no help until the contents matched them. I checked the threads, separated the elbows from the straight connectors and put the damaged pieces aside. Then I made the labels and pressed down their edges with my thumbnail. I worked through the Saturday afternoon with the shop door open. When I left, the loose tape backing fitted into one hand. I put it in the bin and locked up.

The following week I finished the last pages of the ledger. I checked the tag list against the pages and the pages against the bundles of carbon copies. The crate was empty except for scraps and sheets that could not be tied to an instrument. Those went in a separate envelope with the reason written on the outside. I put the ledger on a shelf above the bench, far enough from the sink to keep it dry, with its spine facing out. A pencil lay alongside it.

Walt took it down when I showed him where it was. He flipped from the front to the middle, stopped at a page and ran a finger down the readings. He turned a few more pages, grunted and put it back exactly where I kept it. It is clear to me now that the mill gave me the order I had always been looking for. After he left, I put the next work sheet beside the ledger. There was space on the bench to do the job without moving a heap of old paper first.

A sales rep brought replacement pressure transmitters to the shop that summer. He set the cartons on the bench and said they held a tenth of a percent straight from the box. The packing slip listed the units by serial number. I checked those against the plates before signing for the delivery. The cartons had foam inserts and folded instructions, all clean enough that I moved the rag with the brass fittings before opening them. The rep said there was no need to spend the afternoon checking what had already been checked.

Before installing any, I set three of them up against the shop’s deadweight tester. I checked the tester’s oil level and the test connections, made sure the weights matched the pressure units I was using and connected a meter to read the transmitter output. I took five points across each range: zero, one-quarter, one-half, three-quarters and full scale. At each point I let the reading settle and wrote it down before moving the weights. I kept the serial number at the top of each set of figures.

Two were within the claim. One was four times worse. At its worst point the error came to four-tenths of a percent of span. I brought it back to zero, checked the connections and ran the points again. The figures stayed where they had been. I wrote the test pressures, expected outputs and measured outputs on the packing slip, with the serial number boxed beside them. I made a copy for the shop and put the original with the unit. It went back in its foam with the leads tucked clear of the lid.

The other two stayed for installation. I entered their checks in the ledger and kept the return paperwork with the delivery copy. From then on I tested every unit that rep delivered. I left the cartons on the receiving side of the bench until I had the numbers. A unit only moved to the other end when the check was finished. It took time to set up the tester, so I did the deliveries in a batch when I could, but each serial number had its own readings. The box did not go out to the floor as evidence of calibration.

Walt posted the callout roster beside the shop door. My name was on nights for the digester area. I read the dates, then took the folded job posting out of my shirt pocket and opened it against the bench. Days was still there in the same line. I folded the paper back along its old creases and put it away. The Monday meeting was the place to bring it up. I had the words ready in the plain form I meant to use: the posting had said days.

At the meeting I stood with my coffee. Walt went through the work that had to be done and the jobs that would have to wait. Annie had a question about access to equipment. I shifted the cup to my other hand when it got hot through the paper. The posting sat against my chest behind the pocket seam. There were places where I could have spoken. I drank some coffee, looked at the roster again when it came round and stayed through the end without mentioning it.

I took the pager on my way out. Walt showed me the number and where the spare battery was kept. I clipped it to my belt and wrote my dates in the notebook at the flat. On those nights I put my work clothes on the chair by the bed and left the truck keys beside the kettle. When it went off, I read the message under the kitchen light before getting dressed. At the mill I checked in, got the job details and went to the digester area with my meter and the tools the work called for.

One of the jobs there was a level loop that hunted up and down every few minutes. The reading climbed, the controller corrected, and the movement carried on past where it should have settled. Then it came back the other way. I watched enough cycles to put times beside them. I checked the signal at the points I could reach and compared the indication with the controller. There was no useful answer in watching one swing and leaving. I stood where I could read the display and filled a page with values.

I borrowed a control-theory textbook from the library and brought it to the kitchen table. The chapter on loop tuning had worked examples with response curves. I put my notes alongside it and used a separate pad for the arithmetic. The cardboard under the table leg had compressed, so I folded another piece before laying out the graph paper. Below me the dryers stopped and started. I worked through the examples in pencil, rubbed out the wrong steps and kept the useful equations on one sheet I could take to the mill.

Annie and I argued about it over lunch for a week. I thought the integral setting could account for the repeated correction. She said I should look harder at the valve. I showed her the times I had written down. She asked what the stem had been doing at those times, which my page did not show. The next day I had more readings and the same position. She said a setting could only do so much with a valve that did not move when it was told. We ate from our lunch boxes and went back over the figures.

I checked the information available on the valve and compared it with the controller settings. At the flat I read the section on process lag again. I drew curves until the kitchen eraser had left crumbs down the fold of the book. On Friday Annie asked whether I was ready to change the integral setting. I said I wanted a bump test first. There was a Sunday shutdown coming. We could make a controlled change then and record what actually moved, with the equipment in the proper condition for the test.

On Sunday I wrote down the starting output and put the loop in manual for the test. I made the step change and recorded the response against time. Annie watched the valve. We repeated the work carefully enough to separate a delay in the process from a delay in movement. The command changed before the valve moved. Then the stem shifted farther at once than it should have. I brought the output back through the other direction and got the same sort of hold and jump. I wrote the times beside the stem observations.

I plotted the response by hand on graph paper at the shop bench. I used one scale for time and kept the points spaced according to the readings, even where that left a long flat stretch. The valve’s movement made the trouble plain. It stuck, then broke free, and the level correction arrived in a lump. The integral setting had been my choice at lunch. The test gave it to Annie. I put the plot with the job sheet and recorded the finding as a sticking valve, without changing the settings to cover it.

Denis came home on leave in July 1995. He arrived at the flat carrying a case of beer and a bottle of dark rum. He put the case on the kitchen floor, took the bottle out of its bag and asked where I kept the glasses. I had cups in the cupboard and two ordinary drinking glasses. He brought both glasses to the table. I moved my work papers onto the counter before anything was opened. He had come ready for a night of it, with his coat already off and the first beer in his hand.

I opened a beer at eight. Denis talked about the lakes and the ships, about loading, weather and the hours between one job and the next. He used the length of the table when he needed to show how something had been arranged on deck. I shifted the ashtray-sized dish I used for loose screws out of his way. He drank steadily. The empty bottles went back into the case until he lost track of which row he had started. I turned the case so the empties were at one end.

The kitchen window was open, but the room held the day’s heat. I drank some of my beer while he talked and set it beside my elbow. When he opened the rum, he poured into one glass and reached for the second. I told him I still had beer. He left the glass near the bottle. Later he picked it up again and I shook my head. He went on about the lakes, setting out distances and turns with a finger on the table. I moved my bottle each time his hand came close to it.

At midnight I still had the same warm half-bottle. The label had loosened where my thumb had worked at its edge. Denis reached for the second glass again, and I said no to the rum again. I took the half-bottle to the counter when I got water from the tap. There was a wet ring on the table where it had stood. I wiped it with the dishcloth and left the two glasses where Denis had put them. My work papers stayed on the counter for the rest of his visit that night.

As the weather cooled, the widow who lived below the flat asked me about her furnace. It would run for a short time, stop, then start again. At night the rooms did not stay warm. She showed me the thermostat and described what she had been doing with the setting. I did not turn it farther up to see whether that would satisfy it. I asked her to leave it where she normally kept it and went down to look at the control wiring. I brought a screwdriver, my meter and a pad.

I spent the first Saturday tracing the circuit. I shut off the supply before opening things, identified the terminals and drew what was actually connected. The old wire colours were less useful than the connections. I put small numbered markers on the ends where I needed them and checked continuity with the circuit isolated. Then I restored power for the readings that required it, keeping clear of the live terminals. The furnace started, stopped and started again. I noted what the thermostat contacts were doing each time.

There was no reason to pull the whole run of wire out of the wall. I checked the connections I could reach and tightened only what needed it. The trouble came back to the thermostat. I left everything covered and the supply restored when I stopped for the day, with my drawing folded in my pocket. During the week I checked what replacement would suit the control circuit. I wanted the right switching arrangement and a proper rating, not merely something that fitted the marks on the wall.

On the second Saturday I went to Lacasse Hardware. Armand brought out the thermostats and laid them on the counter. I checked the specifications on the package before choosing one. He asked whether it was for the flat. I told him whose furnace it was and paid with my own money. He counted the change onto the counter beside the receipt. I put both in my pocket and carried the thermostat back in its box. I had the screwdriver and meter waiting upstairs, along with the drawing from the week before.

I isolated the circuit, removed the old thermostat and fixed the new base level on the wall. I made the connections from my numbered wires, checked them against the diagram and fitted the cover. After restoring the supply I watched it call for heat and satisfy. Then I waited through another cycle. The furnace ran properly instead of cutting itself into short starts. I put the old thermostat in the new box and gathered the wire scraps from the floor. The widow tried to put twenty dollars in my hand. I closed her fingers back over it and took my tools upstairs.

That autumn I set out the requirements for my provincial certificate of qualification. The certificate meant thousands of supervised hours and a written exam. I had the schooling behind me, but I still had hours to record and the provincial work to complete. I put the logbook in a cover that would keep its corners from folding in my lunch box. At the end of every shift I entered the date, the hours and the work in terms that would mean something when it was time to have the record checked.

I kept the entries specific. A morning spent checking a transmitter did not become a whole shift of calibration. Time on a fault included the tests and the circuit involved. I left room where a supervised entry needed Walt’s attention and brought it to him while the work was still recent. The ledger belonged to the instruments; this book followed my hours. I kept the two separate. When a shift ended late, I filled the logbook before changing out of my work clothes. That kept a row of blanks from becoming a week reconstructed from scraps.

Through the winter I held myself to two chapters a week in the code book. I marked the chapter boundaries on a sheet at the kitchen table and ticked them off only after I had worked through them. A chapter with tables took longer than one with short provisions. I used scraps of clean paper to cover the answers in the study material and checked my work after writing it out. The table had room for the open book, a pad and my cup, provided I kept the groceries on the counter.

On night callouts I carried the code book in with my lunch box. The fault came first. When the equipment was back in service and there was time before I went home, I read at the shop bench. I kept the book away from the tester oil and wiped my hands before turning pages. Some nights I got through only a few provisions. I put a slip where I stopped and took it back to Main Street. The pager went beside the bed again. The next evening I resumed at the marked line.

There were weeks of double shifts when the two chapters had to fit into smaller pieces. I read before eating, or after, with my work socks drying over the edge of the tub. Snow collected on the outside kitchen sill and stayed there. I copied the references I needed to check into the pad instead of carrying several loose bookmarks. By Sunday night I had the week’s two chapters done. I entered the study work on the sheet and put the code book in the bag for Monday. The exam was still ahead, and the supervised hours were still accumulating.

In 1996 a level switch failed at the top of the chip conveyor. Walt wanted it fixed fast. I got the location and took the tools for checking the switch and its circuit, but the access had to be dealt with before the tools went up. At the gantry, one of the yard hands offered to scramble up without a harness. He expected me to follow him. I kept my tool bag on the ground and said I would go when the lockout was on and a fall-arrest harness had been brought from stores.

The offer came again as if the first answer had been about the climb taking too long. I gave the same answer. The conveyor had to be isolated under the lockout procedure, and I needed the harness. Walt’s job was still waiting at the top. I put the bag where it would stay clear of the access and checked what I had brought. The yard crew had remarks about how much equipment I needed to look at a switch. I stayed on the ground while the request went to stores.

The lockout took its proper steps. I verified the isolation before there was any question of going up. The harness had not arrived. I stood beside my bag, then moved it clear when the space was needed. Looking back, I was still the same daredevil who had climbed every tower in town. The yard crew kept at it, telling me the switch would have been fixed already. I looked up to locate the work position and the route to it, then checked the time. There was still no harness to inspect or put on.

It took the full hour. When the harness came, I checked the webbing, stitching and hardware, adjusted it and made sure the fall-arrest arrangement was right for the access. I secured the tools for the climb. Only then did I go up. At the switch I made the checks the job required and dealt with the failure. I kept the small loose parts contained so they could not drop through the structure. The work did not take as long as the wait. Coming down, I used the same protection I had used going up.

Back in the shop I put the tools away and completed the work record. The level switch had its entry, the parts used had theirs, and my logbook had the hours. I checked the next item on the bench against its tag before opening the ledger. The pencil was where I kept it, beside the spine. I entered the as-found readings first and left the next columns clear until the calibration was finished. When I had the final numbers, I wrote those in, closed the ledger and put it back on the shelf.

Chapter 17

In 1996 the mill decided to tear out the control room I had learned on and put in a distributed control system. The old room was a long horseshoe of panel boards, green steel with rows of controllers set into them, some pneumatic, some electronic, and under every row a bank of chart recorders with red and blue pens scratching on paper. The operators changed the round charts every morning at seven and the strip charts once a week, and the old charts went into cardboard boxes in a storeroom that nobody ever opened. The new system was a set of grey cabinets in a new rack room behind the control room, three operator consoles with colour monitors, and a printer that ran on fanfold paper. The vendor sent two engineers in pressed shirts who stayed at the motel on the highway and ate supper at the same table every night. The switchover was set for the fall shutdown, and before the old boards could be cut loose, every loop in the mill that was moving to the new system had to be checked from the field device to the screen and back.

There were four hundred and twelve loops on the list when I first saw it. Walt Kerrigan put the list on the bench in the instrument shop on the first Monday in June and said the vendor would do the cabinet work and the configuration, and we would do the field end, and he didn't have the people to do it on days because days were for keeping the mill running. He said anybody who wanted overtime could have it, at time and a half, and he would sign the sheets as long as the work got done. Annie Duquette took some of it in June. A couple of the older fellows took a week each and then went to their camps. I took the nights.

My regular shift ran from seven to half past three. I would go home, eat, sleep from five to nine, and be back at the gate by a quarter to ten. The routine for a loop check never changed. I had a Fluke loop calibrator in a padded case, a two-way radio, a clipboard with the loop sheets in numerical order, a six-inch flat screwdriver, a small set of jeweller's screwdrivers for the terminal strips, a roll of electrical tape, and a pencil on a string. At the field end I lifted the leads off the transmitter, clipped the calibrator in, and drove four, eight, twelve, sixteen and twenty milliamps down the line while the night operator on the console read me back the numbers in engineering units. Zero percent, twenty-five, fifty, seventy-five, a hundred. If it was a valve I had him drive it the other way and I watched the stem move and put my hand on the positioner to feel it settle. If the numbers matched within half a percent I initialled the sheet and moved to the next. If they didn't, I went looking, and nine times in ten it was a wire landed on the wrong terminal in the marshalling cabinet or a range typed wrong in the database, and the vendor fixed the database in the morning from my notes.

The marshalling cabinets were in the new rack room, where the air conditioning had not been commissioned yet, and in July the room ran close to thirty-five degrees by midnight. I kept a jug of water on the floor by the door. Inside the door of the first cabinet I taped a tally sheet, four columns on a sheet of graph paper with every loop number down the side, and when a loop was signed off I put a line through it in pencil. The vendor engineers looked at it the first morning and one of them asked whether I wanted a printout from their tracking program instead. I said the sheet was fine. The sheet stayed where it was all summer and I could tell from the doorway, before I put my case down, how far along we were.

The woodroom loops were the worst for blackflies in June and the worst for mosquitoes in July. The digester loops were inside and hot. The tank farm loops were out in the open, and in August there was a week of rain where I did them in a rain suit with the loop sheets in a freezer bag, and the pencil wouldn't write on wet paper so I kept a grease pencil in my top pocket for those. Some nights I did twelve loops and some nights I did three, depending on how far apart they were and how many of them were wrong. The night operators got used to me. One of them kept a pot of coffee going on the console desk and would call out the numbers without my asking for them by the end of July.

The overtime came in on every second cheque. I had been putting money into a savings account at the credit union since I came back to the mill in 1995, and that summer I put the whole of the overtime in and lived on the base pay. By the end of September the account had a little over fourteen thousand dollars in it.

I signed off the last loop on a Thursday night in October, two days before startup. It was a level transmitter on the white liquor storage tank, and the only thing wrong with it was that somebody had painted over the tag. I scraped the paint off with my thumbnail and read the number to the operator and drove the five points and he read them back. I went into the rack room and put the last line through the tally sheet and looked at it for a minute. Then I took it down, folded it in four, and put it in my lunch box. Walt came in the next morning, opened the cabinet door, saw the bare tape marks and said, "Done?" and I said done, and he said good and went off to a meeting about the steam plant.

The startup went the way startups go. The operators complained about the screens for a month, the vendor engineers stayed another three weeks and then went home, and the old panel boards were cut out with a Sawzall and carried out the back door in sections on a forklift. By November the control room looked like an office.

It was in November that I noticed the spikes were gone. On the old boards, the steam header pressure recorder had a pen that drew a thick wavering line most of the day, and every so often, when a big valve somewhere opened or closed fast, the pen would jump and draw a sharp stroke up or down, a quarter inch or half an inch, and come back. Everybody who had worked on the boards knew those strokes. You could look at a week's chart and see where the digester blew or where somebody had slammed a valve on the turbine. On the new trend screen the header pressure was a smooth line. Nothing jumped. The first time I saw it I thought the transmitter had a damping setting turned up, and I went and checked, and the damping was off. I checked the configuration in the system and the filter there was off too. The line was smooth anyway.

The new system scanned every loop once a second. That was in the vendor's brochure and in the training binder, and everybody said it like it was a good thing, because the old controllers didn't scan at all, they just ran. I took the vendor's theory manuals home, which were three blue binders with the company name in silver on the spine, and read them at the kitchen table of the apartment I was renting, a chapter a night. They explained how the input cards worked and how the scan was organized and how the processor went through the loops in order. They did not say anything about what happened to a signal that changed faster than the scan.

The library in town had nothing, but the librarian ordered me an engineering text on sampled-data systems through the interlibrary loan, and it came from Thunder Bay in about ten days. Most of it was mathematics I had last looked at in Dr. Abernethy's course at the college, and some of it was past anything I had been taught. I read the first three chapters twice. I turned it over for a week, on the job and off it. I would be standing at a bench calibrating a pressure gauge and find I had been thinking about it for half an hour.

What I finally did was work it out on paper, which I should have done in the first place. I took a sheet of graph paper and drew a line across it for time, one square for a tenth of a second, and marked a dot every ten squares for the scan. Then I drew a pressure spike like the ones on the old charts, up and back down inside three squares, a third of a second, and put it between two dots. The system read the pressure at the first dot, before the spike, and at the second dot, after it, and both readings were the same. As far as the system was concerned nothing had happened. I drew another one that started just before a dot and caught the top of it, and that one showed up as a single high reading, which the trend would draw as a little point. Then I drew a pressure that swung up and down fast and regular and sampled it once a second, and joined the dots, and got a slow wave that wasn't there at all. That last one was in the textbook, in the mathematics, but I didn't understand it until I drew it. A slow scan doesn't see a fast signal. Either it misses it, or it catches a piece of it at random, or it makes up something slower that looks real. The chart recorder pen had been on the paper all the time, so it saw everything. The system only looked once a second.

I took the sheets in the next day and showed Walt in his office. He looked at them while I explained, and when I finished he said, "Is the header pressure controlling?" I said it was. He said, "Are the operators complaining?" I said not about this. He said then he didn't see the problem, and he had a budget to finish, and if I wanted to write something up for the vendor I could, but don't expect them to change the scan rate on a system that was signed off. He handed the sheets back.

Annie was more interested, or at least she was interested in arguing. I showed her at lunch and then all afternoon in the shop between jobs. She said if a spike was big enough to matter it would last long enough to get caught. I said not necessarily, a pressure wave from a valve slamming could be over in a tenth of a second and still knock something loose. She said the transmitter would smooth it anyway. I said the transmitters we had would follow it fine, I had checked the response times in the data sheets. She said then put a recorder on it and stop drawing pictures. I said that was what I wanted to do, and she said then why was I arguing with her, and I said I wasn't, she was arguing with me. She got out a pad and drew her own dots and her own spike and put the spike on a dot, and I said that was the lucky case, and she said maybe the mill was lucky. It went on until half past three, when she put her coat on and said, "Once a second," in a way that meant she had more to say and would say it tomorrow. I put my sheets in a folder and kept them.

I had been looking at houses since the spring. Brule Falls did not have many for sale at any one time, and most of what came up was either a big old two-storey on the hill that needed everything done to it or a bungalow out past the trestle that backed onto the rail line. In October a house came up on Cedar Street, a storey-and-a-half with white aluminum siding, a gravel drive, a small detached garage, and a full basement. It was four blocks from my father's store and about a ten-minute drive to the mill gate. The asking price was seventy-one thousand five hundred.

The seller's agent met me there on a Saturday in late October. She had a clipboard of her own and a folder with the listing sheet in it and she walked me through quickly, naming rooms. Kitchen, living room, bathroom, two bedrooms up under the slope of the roof, one of them small. In the basement she stopped by the furnace and said it was a forced-air oil furnace, five years old, and the owners had just had it serviced. Going back up the stairs she said the roof had been redone recently too, so there was nothing to worry about there for a long time.

I went back on the Monday after work by myself, with the agent's permission and the owners out, and took a flashlight and a notebook. The furnace had a plate riveted to the side of the cabinet near the burner with the model number and the serial number stamped into it. I copied both down. The plate had a toll-free number for the manufacturer's service department, and I wrote that down as well. Then I went up to the second floor, where there was an attic hatch in the ceiling of the hall closet, and I took a kitchen chair from downstairs and stood on it and pushed the hatch up and climbed into the attic.

It was a low space, cold, with pink insulation between the joists and not enough of it. I crawled along the joists with the flashlight to where the plumbing vent stack came up through the roof deck. Around the stack, where the roofers had cut the hole, you could see the edges of the roofing from underneath, and I lay on my side and counted. There were three layers of shingles on top of the boards. Each time somebody had redone the roof they had laid new shingles over the old instead of stripping them. The top layer might have been recent, but the deck was carrying all three, and the next roofer would have to tear the whole lot off down to the boards.

I phoned the manufacturer the next day from the pay phone in the mill lunchroom on my break. A woman in the service department took the serial number and put me on hold and came back and read me the date it was built. The furnace had been built in 1982, fourteen years earlier. I asked her to say it again and she did, and I wrote it in the notebook under the serial number with the date of the call and her first name.

I went down to the store on Saturday morning and told my father about the house. He was behind the counter, as he always was on Saturdays, measuring rope off the big spool for a customer, and he listened while he measured and cut and rang it in. When the customer was gone he said it sounded like a good house on a good street at a fair price, and in his opinion I should shake hands on the price before somebody else did. He said a five-year-old furnace was nothing to complain about. I told him it was fourteen years old. He asked how I knew and I told him about the plate and the phone call. He said a furnace was a furnace and they lasted thirty years if you looked after them. Then he asked about the roof and I told him about the three layers and he said, "Well," and a customer came in for furnace filters, as it happened, and he went to help him.

I wrote my notes out clean on a pad of lined paper that night. The model and serial number of the furnace, the date of manufacture, the date I called and who I spoke to. The three layers of shingles and where I had seen them, and the price of a full tear-off and new roof, which I got from a roofer in town who gave me a number over the phone, and the price of a new oil furnace installed, which I got from the heating company that serviced half the furnaces in Brule Falls. I took the pad to the agent's office on the Tuesday after work. She read it at her desk and asked if she could photocopy it, and I let her. She said the owners had been told the furnace was newer than that and she was sure they hadn't meant anything by it. I said I was sure they hadn't, but that was what the plate said. I offered sixty-seven thousand five hundred, four thousand under the asking price. She phoned me two days later and said the owners had accepted. The deal closed in the second week of January 1997, and I put most of the overtime money into the down payment.

I moved in on a Saturday with the truck and three trips. I did not own much. A bed, a kitchen table and two chairs, a couch that had come out of the apartment, my tools, and a dozen boxes, most of them books and binders.

My father came over the next Saturday afternoon to see the house. He had not been inside it yet, only past it in the car. He came to the side door with a bottle of rye in a paper bag, a forty-ouncer, and said a house had to be christened. He walked through every room first, knocking on walls and opening the electrical panel and looking at the breakers and telling me which ones were the wrong size, which I already knew. He went into the basement and looked at the furnace and read the plate himself, and said nothing about it. Then he came up to the kitchen and took the bottle out of the bag.

I had no proper glasses yet, only four juice glasses from a box of kitchen things I had bought at a church rummage sale. He poured two fingers into each of two of them at the kitchen counter and handed me one. He said, "To the house," and drank his in about the time it took to tell me what he had paid for his own house in 1964, and what the interest rate had been, and what I should do about the insulation in the attic, which was to buy it from him at cost. Then he poured himself another. He talked about the store, about a fellow from the mill who had brought back a ladder with a broken rung and wanted his money back, and about the price of copper pipe. I stood at the counter with my glass and took a sip now and then. He finished the second one. I had taken about half of mine by the time he looked at his watch, an hour after he came in, and said he had to get back before my mother had supper on the table.

After he left I poured what was in my glass down the sink, washed both glasses, and put the cap back on the bottle. I put it in the cupboard over the fridge, behind a box of matches and a flashlight, where you had to stand on a chair to reach. It stayed there unopened for the rest of the year.

The maintenance crew had a curling team in the mill league that played Saturday nights at the rink from January through March. In the third week of January Annie came over to my bench at lunch and said one of their fellows had put his back out lifting a snowblower and they needed somebody for the rest of the season. She said I didn't have to be any good, nobody on the team was any good, and it was mostly about going to the lounge after. I told her I was busy with the house. She said every Saturday? I said every Saturday, for a while. She said all right, and that if I changed my mind they played at seven, and went off to ask somebody in the pipe shop.

The basement of the Cedar Street house was concrete block, dry, with a poured floor that sloped to a drain in the middle and two small windows at ground level on the south side. The furnace and the oil tank were at the north end, and the rest of it was open. I spent the Saturday nights of that winter fitting it out, starting the week Annie asked.

The first thing was the wall. I bought four sheets of quarter-inch pegboard and a box of pegboard hooks from my father's store, and he gave them to me at cost and wrote it down in the ledger under my name. I screwed one-by-three strapping to the block wall with masonry screws, three rows, and screwed the pegboard to the strapping so there was room behind it for the hooks. Then I took every hand tool I owned out of its box and laid them all on the floor and worked out where they should go, the things I used most at shoulder height and in the middle, the things I used least at the ends. Screwdrivers in a row by size, flat then Robertson then Phillips. Pliers, side cutters, strippers, crimpers. Wrenches in two rows, imperial above metric. Hammers at the left end, the hacksaw and coping saw above them. When everything was hung I took a black marker and traced around each tool on the pegboard, so that when a tool was out you could see from across the room what was missing and where it went back. It took two Saturday nights to lay out and a third to trace.

Under the pegboard I built a bench out of two-by-fours and a sheet of three-quarter plywood, with a shelf underneath, and screwed a vise to the right end. On the wall to the right of the bench I put up rows of small-parts cabinets, the kind with clear plastic drawers in a steel frame, six cabinets of twenty-four drawers each. I bought those at cost too. I labelled every drawer with a label maker I had bought for the purpose: machine screws by thread and length, wood screws by gauge and length, sheet metal screws, bolts, nuts, flat washers, lock washers, cotter pins, wire nuts by colour, compression fittings, pipe fittings, hose clamps, fuses.

When the drawers were labelled I went upstairs and got the shoebox. It was the same one I had kept screws in since I was about nine, a Bata box with the corners taped and taped again, and it had come with me to Sudbury and to the boarding house on Elm Street and to the apartment and now to Cedar Street, still full. I had sorted it by size as a boy and over the years everything had got mixed again. I emptied it onto the bench and sorted it into the drawers, screw by screw, by size. There were a lot of screws in it that I would never use, odd threads from radios and old appliances, and they got a drawer of their own labelled MISC. When it was empty I flattened the box and put it out with the recycling.

The last thing was the binder. I had started keeping papers for the house from the day the offer was signed, and by February I had a pile of them: the purchase agreement, the closing statement from the lawyer, the survey, the insurance policy, the receipts for every piece of pegboard and every box of screws. The owners had left a drawer full of manuals in the kitchen, for the stove and the fridge and the washer and dryer, and I had phoned the furnace manufacturer again and got them to mail me the installation and owner's manual for the model on the plate. I bought a three-inch binder and a set of coloured tabs and a three-hole punch and set it up by room: Kitchen, Bathroom, Bedrooms, Living Room, Basement, Furnace, Electrical, Plumbing, Roof, Exterior, Garage, and a tab at the front for the legal papers. Every receipt and every warranty card went behind the tab for the room it belonged to. The stove manual went under Kitchen, the furnace manual under Furnace, the quote from the roofer under Roof, with a note in the margin of what year I expected to need him. The binder went on the shelf under the bench.

On the first Saturday in February Annie and several of the crew entered the snowmobile race on the lake. It was an annual thing, put on by the snowmobile club, a loop course marked out with orange snow fence and spruce boughs on the ice about half a mile out from the public landing, and there was an entry fee and a trophy and a barbecue on the shore. Annie's brother had a sled he wasn't using that year, and she had asked me on the Thursday in the shop whether I wanted it. She said he'd be glad to have somebody run it and it would be one more for the maintenance crew. I told her no thanks. She said I could just do the slow class if I wanted. I said no thanks again, and she shrugged and said suit yourself.

I drove out to the landing on the Saturday morning anyway, because I said I might, and parked at the far end of the plowed lot away from the trucks and trailers. It was about fifteen below with a light wind from the northwest and a high grey sky. The lot was full and there were people all along the shore and out on the ice near the start, standing around barrels with fires in them. I stayed by the truck with the engine running and the window down a few inches. You could see the course from there well enough. Out past the course, maybe two hundred yards beyond the far turn, was the pressure ridge, the long buckled line where the ice had pushed up on itself that winter, and somebody had put a row of orange stakes in front of it.

I watched the first heat go off at about eleven. The flag came down and a dozen sleds went up the straight together in a cloud of blown snow, and the noise of them came across the ice a second after. I could pick out Annie's red helmet in the second heat. I watched that one and the one after it, and then I put the truck in gear and drove back into town. I turned down the snowmobile race on the lake that February, sensibly as it turned out, since the ice gave way that afternoon. I spent the rest of the day in the basement putting the last of the drawer labels on.

I heard the details on the Monday in the shop. In one of the afternoon heats two riders had gone wide on the far turn, and instead of coming back onto the course they had run out toward the pressure ridge, past the stakes, and the ice beside the ridge had gone out under the first sled and then the second. Both of them went in. People out by the barrels ran out with a rope and a ladder and hauled them out, soaked through, and they were taken to the district hospital and let go that night. One sled was on the bottom of the lake. Annie had finished fourth in her heat and had already loaded her brother's sled on the trailer when it happened. She told it standing by the coffee machine with her coat still on, to whoever was there, and then she went to her bench.

In March I was on a stretch of night coverage, which meant I was the instrument technician on call for the whole mill from eleven at night to seven in the morning, and I spent most of those nights in the shop catching up on bench work unless the radio went. On a Wednesday night a little before three, the recovery boiler control room called and said they were having trouble with the drum level. The drum level on a recovery boiler is not something anybody leaves alone. If the water gets too low the tubes overheat, and if it gets too high you carry water into the steam, and on a recovery boiler the worst thing that can happen is water getting into the smelt bed. There were trips on it that would shut the boiler down long before any of that, but nobody wanted a trip either.

When I got there the operator had the feedwater valve on manual and was holding the level himself by watching the gauge glass on the camera monitor. He said the level on the system had been wandering for an hour, drifting high slowly and then jumping back, and the controller had been chasing it. There were two level transmitters on the drum and the system used one for control and the other for comparison. I pulled up both on the screen. The control transmitter was reading about four inches higher than the other one, and while I watched it drifted up another half inch and then came back down an inch all at once.

A drum level transmitter is a differential pressure cell with two impulse lines, one from the bottom of the drum and one from the top, and the top one, the reference leg, runs down from a condensing pot so it stays full of water. If the reference leg loses water, the transmitter sees less pressure on that side and reads the level higher than it is. That was what it looked like. I took a flashlight, a set of wrenches, my radio and a tubing cutter and went up to the transmitter platform, and the operator kept the feedwater on manual.

I followed the reference leg from the condensing pot down to the transmitter, a run of half-inch stainless tubing about twenty feet long, with a couple of brackets holding it to the steel. At the second bracket from the bottom the tubing had been rubbing on the edge of the bracket clamp where the steel vibrated, and there was a crack about a quarter inch long on the underside of the tube, just above the fitting. It was weeping. Not much, a drop every few seconds that flashed off on the hot steel, which was why nobody had seen a puddle. Every so often the condensing pot would refill the leg enough to catch up, and the reading would jump back.

I radioed the operator and told him what I had and that the other transmitter was good, and he switched the control over to the good transmitter and put the feedwater back on automatic. Then I wrote the permit and locked out the bad transmitter at its root valves and hung my tag. I went down to the shop for a length of half-inch stainless tubing and four compression fittings and a new bracket clamp with a rubber cushion in it. I cut out the cracked section, a little over a foot, and put in the new piece with two unions, and replaced the bracket clamp so the tube couldn't rub again. I opened the root valves slowly, let the condensing pot fill the reference leg, and bled the air off at the transmitter until water came out clear and steady. Then I took my lock off, and the operator and I watched the two transmitters on the screen for twenty minutes. They read within half an inch of each other the whole time. He switched the control back to the original transmitter at twenty to seven and it held.

I went down to the shop and wrote up the report on the standard form, what the trouble was, what I found, the location of the crack, the parts used, the times, and a note that the other drum level transmitters on the boilers should have their tubing brackets checked for the same rubbing. Day shift came in at seven. The shift foreman for the recovery area was in the boiler control room when I went back up to tell the day operator what had been done, and I told him too, and he wrote it down in his book.

The report was supposed to go to Walt, but Walt was in a meeting with the mill manager that morning about the boiler, which the night operator had already logged, and the secretary in the office told me to bring it up. I went up at about nine to drop it off on my way out. The mill manager's office was at the end of the second-floor hallway, with a couple of chairs outside it, and the door was half open. Before I got to it I heard the recovery foreman inside telling the manager about the drum level. He said they had had a problem on his shift with the level, and he had gone up and found the reference leg leaking at a bracket and got the tubing replaced and the transmitter back in service before day shift. He said he had already told the boys to check the other boilers for the same thing. The manager said good work. Walt said something I didn't catch.

I stood in the hallway with the report in my hand for maybe half a minute. Then I turned around and went back down the stairs to the shop and put it in the file drawer under recovery boiler, with the other reports, and went home. I let the foreman take credit for my repair on the recovery boiler, as I always let people take what they wanted from me. I did not mention it to Walt or to Annie or to anybody else. The report is probably still in that drawer.

In April a young labourer on the yard crew had his car repossessed. He was about twenty, from out near Hearst, and had been at the mill since the fall, running a loader and shovelling around the chip piles. He lived in a rooming house down by the tracks on the far side of town, a good forty minutes' walk from the mill gate, and the yard crew started at six. The finance company came for the car on a Friday. I knew him only to nod to. I heard about it on the Monday because he had walked in and been fifteen minutes late, and the yard foreman had told him that if it happened again he was done.

I found him at lunch in the yard shack and asked him how much it would take to get something to drive. He said he had been looking and there was a car at the garage on the highway for twelve hundred, and he had about five hundred saved, and the garage wanted the whole thing up front. I said I could lend him six hundred, and he could get the rest together from his next cheques. He asked how soon I needed it back. I said whenever he had it. I went to the credit union after work and took six hundred dollars out of my savings in cash and gave it to him in an envelope the next morning.

The garage could not let him have the car for a while, because something was wrong with the brakes and they had to order parts, and then he decided against it and kept looking. In the meantime I picked him up. I was on days, seven o'clock start, but I had nothing against getting in early. I left Cedar Street at twenty-five past five every morning and was in front of the rooming house at twenty to six, and he was always on the step. I dropped him at the yard gate at ten to six and went and had coffee in the shop until it was time to start. He got a ride home most afternoons with somebody on the yard crew, and the days he didn't I waited for him in the lot. He didn't talk much in the truck and neither did I. In the first week he tried to give me gas money and I said no.

It went on for five weeks. In the third week of May he bought a 1987 Tempo from a fellow at the mill for a thousand dollars, and on the Monday after he drove himself in. He stopped by my bench that morning to tell me, and said thank you. I said he was welcome. I never brought up the loan.

By the end of May the snow was gone from the yard on Cedar Street, and I measured the attic for insulation and wrote the figures on a sheet of paper and punched it and put it behind the Roof tab in the binder. Annie had moved on from the scan rate, more or less, and Walt had not asked about it again. The folder with my graph paper was on the shelf under the bench in the basement, between the binder and the box for the label maker.

Chapter 18

In January 1998, I took the calibration binder down from the shelf and spread my test equipment along the bench. I was twenty-six, three years into the job at the mill, and the binder had begun to need a wider spine. Each certificate went behind the tab for the serial number stamped on the equipment. I kept the old certificates behind the current one, with the newest facing out. The bench was narrow enough that I had to move the deadweight tester back before I could lay the binder flat without its cover hanging over the edge.

I checked the numbers on the cases against the numbers on the sheets. A model number wasn't enough. Two meters could look the same and have different errors, and a certificate belonging to one was no use for the other. I read each serial number straight from the case instead of copying it from the previous year's list. The small print collected dirt. I cleaned one plate with a corner of rag, held it under the bench light and entered the last three digits again because I had first taken an eight for a three.

Each meter and the deadweight tester went to the lab once a year. I arranged the dates so the equipment didn't all leave at once. Before packing anything, I checked its leads, terminals and battery compartment, and wrote down what went into the case. The deadweight tester had its own fitted pieces, which I counted against the list. Sending an incomplete set away meant getting an incomplete job back. I put the paperwork in an envelope inside the lid and kept a copy in the binder, with the shipping date written across the top.

Every instrument wore a coloured sticker for the year it was due. I put the sticker where it could be read without turning the thing over, but clear of the display and the maker's plate. The colour let me check a case from across the bench. The date still had to be read. A sticker could get rubbed, loosened by oil or transferred with a lid, so I didn't use it in place of the record. That January I pressed down a lifting corner with my thumbnail and replaced another sticker that had become too dirty to read.

I made the year's list on a fresh sheet: serial number, last calibration, next due date, and a space for the date sent. Then I went along the bench once more. The tester's weights were back in their places, the meters were in their cases, and the certificates were filed. I put the binder on the shelf with its spine facing out. It was work I did every January, generally before the month's other paperwork had found its way onto that end of the bench. That year I still had exam questions tucked into the back of it.

Through the winter I studied every night between shifts. On an early shift I worked at the kitchen table after supper. On a late one I used the time before leaving for the mill, then did another set when I got home. I kept the books at one end of the table in the house on Cedar Street and ate at the other. There was room for a plate, a mug and the exercise book without moving much. The furnace came on under the floor, and the bottoms of the pages curled where they lay over the warm patch.

By the spring of 1998, I had two years of logged hours and study behind me for the provincial certificate. I went through the hour sheets before taking them anywhere, checking the additions and making sure the entries could be read. There were figures that needed no studying because I had used them on a job that week. Others stayed on the page unless I worked them out from the beginning. I gave those more space in the exercise book. I wrote the units beside every quantity, including the intermediate answers, so I could see where I had made a wrong conversion.

Any practice question I got wrong went onto a separate list. I didn't cross it off after reading the answer. I covered the worked solution and did it again on clean paper, then returned to it later without the earlier attempt in front of me. Sometimes the second answer was wrong in a different way. I kept both sheets. For a while there was a stack of them held together with a small bolt through a hole in the corner. A paper clip wouldn't hold the thickness, and I had the bolt in a kitchen drawer.

I drove to Sudbury for the written exam in the spring. The road shoulders still held the winter's sand, and the washer fluid left a grey edge wherever the wipers stopped. I had pencils, an eraser, the permitted calculator and my paperwork in a folder. At the desk I set out what I needed and put the rest away. I read the full paper before starting, marked the questions that would take longer and worked through the others first. Where a question asked for a calculation, I left the working visible instead of putting down only the result.

I used the time at the end to go back over the units and the answers I had carried from one part into another. On one sheet I had written a number clearly enough to read but badly enough to question, so I rewrote it. When I was finished, I handed the paper in and drove back. There were no practice questions on the kitchen table that evening. I put the books in a square pile against the wall and wiped the ring left by the mug. When the result came, I had passed on the first sitting. I filed the notice where I could find it.

Later that spring a sheared bolt opened my hand at the mill. I had the load supported and the equipment isolated. The bolt broke while I was working it, and my hand travelled the short distance to the edge beside it. I stopped, put the tool down and looked at the cut. Blood ran into the crease below my fingers before I got a dressing over it. Annie Duquette brought the first-aid box closer. I held the dressing in place while she found tape, then closed my fingers enough to see that they still moved.

Walt Kerrigan looked at the dressing and sent me to the district hospital. I left the job with the broken bolt where it could be seen and told him what remained to be done. There was no point putting a glove over that hand and carrying on. By the time I reached emergency, the blood had come through one corner of the pad. I gave my name, sat where I was directed and kept the hand above my knee. The tape had caught some hair on the back of my wrist. I left it alone.

Claire Bouchard took the dressing off. She asked what had cut me, when it had happened and whether I could feel her touching the ends of my fingers. She washed the wound and had me move each finger separately. There was mill dirt along the edge of one nail that hadn't come off with my first wash. She worked around it, cleaned the skin again and laid out what she needed. I watched her hands. She told me where to put mine and shifted the light until she could see into the cut without my wrist turned awkwardly.

She put in five stitches. Before she had finished, she asked whether I wanted to have supper with her. I had been about to suggest Saturday. I had got as far as opening my mouth when she said Friday, named the diner on Main Street and gave me the time. I agreed to Friday. I agreed to the diner and to six o'clock. She finished the stitch she was working on before looking up. Then she asked whether I had understood the instructions for keeping the dressing dry, and I repeated enough of them to show that I had.

She trimmed the ends, covered the stitches and told me what to watch for. I put my coat on one sleeve at a time, leaving the injured hand until last. There was dried blood near the cuff of my work shirt. Claire wrote down the information I needed and handed it over with the same directness she had used to arrange supper. At the door I said Friday at six, although neither part had been left unsettled. She said yes. Back at the mill, I put the hospital sheet in my locker and gave Walt the account of the bolt.

On Friday I reached the diner a few minutes before six and took a table for two. I had changed the dressing before leaving home. The new one was smaller and fitted under a loose cuff, though it still made handling a knife awkward. Claire came in, took off her coat and sat opposite me. We ordered supper. She asked how the hand was, and I showed her how far I could bend it without pulling at the stitches. She said to leave it there and picked up the menu again. We had pie after the plates were cleared.

Over the pie she asked why the ear thermometers on the ward always read low. I started explaining the thing I used at work: two different metals joined together made a small voltage, and that voltage changed with heat. I used the edge of a paper napkin to show the joined end and the two wires coming back. There was a smear of pie filling near one corner, so I turned it over. I explained that the voltage was small enough that the connections and the kind of wire mattered. Claire listened until I got to the measuring end.

She said there weren't two wires going into an ear. There was a little opening, a cover over the end and a reading that appeared almost at once. She described where the nurse held it and how the angle changed from one attempt to another. I said the sensing part could be inside. She asked how that fitted with the bit I had just drawn touching what it measured. I moved my fork off the napkin and looked at the drawing. It didn't answer her question. She took another bite of pie while I folded the napkin along its clean edge.

I turned the question over during the next week. At the mill I used the technical library and read about infrared sensing. The thermometer was detecting radiation from the ear, not measuring an ear by putting the junction I had drawn against it. There could still be a temperature-sensitive detector inside, but that didn't make my explanation over pie a useful account of the whole device. I read about the field of view, the detector's own temperature and the correction needed to turn its signal into a displayed figure. I made notes on a sheet from my work pad.

The ward's low readings could have come from how the opening was aimed, from the covers, from the condition of the lens or from the equipment itself. I hadn't examined one, so I couldn't choose among those. I wrote that at the bottom, below the things that could be checked. On our next date I brought the page folded in my shirt pocket. Claire opened it between us and read down to the last line. She asked whether I was correcting my first answer. I said I was. She tapped the part about aiming it and asked me to explain that bit without drawing wires.

By the time purchasing changed the supplier of thermocouple wire, I had my certificate filed and the stitches out. The new wire cost less. The rep swore it was made to the standard, and the paperwork that came with the spool said enough to get it onto the shelf. I checked the designation and looked at the insulation and the markings. None of that established what voltage a length of it would produce. I cut a sample, recorded the spool information and made a junction. Then I got the reference equipment and set up a test on the bench.

For the ice bath I used crushed ice with enough water to fill the spaces. I stirred it and gave the reference time to settle. The sensing ends went down together, clear of the sides and bottom. A cup of cold water with ice floating at the top wouldn't have done the job. I checked the meter setting for the type of thermocouple, checked the connections and waited for the readings to stop moving. Then I wrote down the reference and the reading from the sample, along with the equipment numbers and the time allowed for the bath to settle.

I did the second check in boiling water. I kept the junction and reference away from the heated surface and used the reference reading rather than simply writing down a hundred degrees. At both ends the sample was several degrees off. I let it cool, inspected the junction and repeated the checks. I changed what I could check without changing the piece of wire under test. The error stayed with the wire. I had enough blank space on the sheet to put the second set of readings beside the first, which saved anyone having to turn pages to compare them.

My write-up gave the spool identification, the stated type, the reference I had used and both sets of numbers. I added the size and direction of the error and attached the copy of the reference certificate. I didn't write that the wire was cheap and leave it at that. I wrote what it had done at the two test points and why I wouldn't install it. The spool got pulled. I put a tag through its centre so it couldn't be mistaken for material waiting to go out to a job, and kept my sample with the sheet.

Walt was annoyed at the delay. He had work waiting for that wire and asked how long it would take to get something we could use. I could give him the time needed to test another sample. I couldn't give him a delivery date. He looked at the numbers, put the sheet down and went to deal with the missing material. After that I tested every new spool. I left enough lead time to make the checks before the wire was needed, when I could. Each record carried the spool number, because a good length from one delivery said nothing about the next.

Claire took me to her parents' house for Sunday dinner before the August heat arrived. I wore a clean shirt and shoes that hadn't been into the mill. Her father poured me a rye before we sat down. I took the glass to the table and set it to the right of my plate, far enough back that I wouldn't catch it with my sleeve. Her mother brought the food, and Claire made room for the dishes. I waited until there was space to pass one, then took it with both hands and set it down.

Her father asked about the mill. I told him I worked on the equipment that measured and controlled the process. He asked whether that meant electrical work, and I said some of it was electrical, some pressure and some temperature. Claire said I could take a full sheet of paper to correct an answer about a thermometer. I said it had been one sheet on both sides only if she counted the address from the pad showing through. She told her parents about the pie and the napkin. I took a small drink of rye and went back to my dinner.

The glass stayed with me through the meal. When her father reached to give me a second pour, I put my hand beside it and said I was all right with what I had. He set the bottle down. There was still some in the bottom when the plates were moved. I ate what was put in front of me and passed the serving dishes when they came my way. Claire sat close enough that her chair caught mine once when she got up. I shifted over, leaving more room between the legs, and she sat down again.

There was brandy afterward. I turned that down too and kept the same glass until it was empty. Her father left it at that.
