I carried my plate where Claire showed me and helped clear enough of the table to make room for the cups. In the kitchen I rinsed the gravy from a serving spoon before setting it beside the sink. Claire's mother told me where the cloth was. When we left, I put my shoes on by the door and waited while Claire found her coat. The visit had taken the afternoon, and my one glass was still on the table.

On a hot Sunday in August, I drove Annie and two of the mill hands out to the old quarry. They wanted to jump from the high ledge. I had room in the vehicle and brought my swimming things and a towel. The road into the quarry was dry, with loose stone along the ruts and dust hanging behind us. I drove slowly where the ruts deepened. At the place where we stopped, the heat came up through the soles of my shoes. We took our things down and put them clear of the track to the water.

I found a rock in the shade and sat there while they went up. There was a level patch for my feet and enough room beside me to keep the towel out of the dirt. From there I could see the high ledge and the lower place where I intended to get in. Annie stood up above the water with the two mill hands. One went off, surfaced and swam clear. Then another followed. I stayed on the rock. The shade covered my legs but not the toes of my shoes, so I drew them back a little.

When I went swimming, I used the low side. I put my shoes where I could reach them, stepped down and eased into the water. I swam along that edge, came back the same way and climbed out where I had entered. The stone under my feet was slippery near the water, so I kept a hand on the dry part until I had my weight settled. I dried off and returned to the shaded rock. Water ran from my hair onto the back of my shirt until I used the towel again.

They called me up to the ledge. Annie held an arm out to indicate the way, though I could see it from where I sat. I raised my hand and waved them off. Somewhere in those years I stopped needing to prove anything with my body. I put the towel behind my neck and stayed on the rock. They went on jumping and swimming, with time between jumps to climb back up. I moved once as the shade shifted. When they were done, I carried my own things back and drove us into town, with the windows open to let the heat out.

Claire mentioned the ramp at the table in my kitchen. She had a patient, an old man, going home alone with a bad hip. There were four steps up to his door. She described them with her fingers against the edge of the table, then said there was no one at the house to help him up. I asked how high the doorway was from the ground and how much room there was in front of it. She didn't have those measurements. I took out a pad and wrote down what I would need to measure before buying lumber.

At the house I measured the rise, the available run and the clear space at the door. Four steps took more ramp than the description had suggested. I checked which way the door swung and left room for it at the top. The landing had to let him stop and open it without backing onto the slope. I marked the proposed line and measured again, this time from the end back towards the house. I looked at the bathroom too. For grab bars, I needed something solid behind the wall, not screws held by the surface alone.

I paid for the pressure-treated lumber myself. I worked out the lengths on paper before buying it, allowing for the landing, the supports and the rails as well as the sloping deck. There were screws, bolts and washers to add, and the grab bars needed their own proper fixings. I kept the receipt folded in the pad. At the house I stacked the lumber flat and clear of the door. Some pieces were wet enough to leave the pressure treatment on my gloves. I sorted them for their use and left the straighter lengths for the parts where they mattered most.

The work took three weekends. On the first I laid it out, prepared the supports and got the framing started. I checked the levels across the landing and the fall along the ramp, moving the line when the measurement called for it. The four existing steps stayed available while I worked. I kept the cut ends and spare fasteners in a box instead of leaving them beside the route to the door. At the end of the day I stacked what wasn't fixed, put the tools away and checked that nothing could roll or tip into the walking space.

The second weekend went into the landing, the remaining framing and the deck. I used a square, a level and a string line, and checked each section before covering the work underneath. The pressure-treated boards didn't all have the same width, and some wanted to twist when I released them from the stack. I pulled them into place, checked the spacing and fastened them down. A few screw heads needed another turn to sit properly. I went along on my knees and dealt with those before carrying the offcuts away. By then the shape of the ramp was all there.

On the third weekend I finished the rails and the small jobs I had marked in the pad. I checked the exposed ends for splinters and the joints for anything that moved under pressure. In the bathroom I found the backing, drilled the fixing holes and fitted the grab bars where they could be used. I tightened them and tested them with my weight kept over my feet. Then I went back outside and walked the whole ramp, stopping at the landing to open the door. I checked the clearance again and took the tools out to the vehicle.

He wanted to pay me. I told him the lumber and the work were covered, and I wouldn't take money for any of it. I believe now that generosity came to me late and through the back door. I didn't leave him a figure for the materials or the three weekends. I left the route to the door clear and showed him the finished rails and the bars in the bathroom. Before I went, I picked up a washer from beside the lumber pile and put it in my pocket. There were still a few tools to count into the box.

In December, Claire brought me to the hospital staff's Christmas party in the cafeteria. We arrived with our coats on, and I followed her to the coat rack before taking mine off. The tables had been moved to make room for people standing with plates. I took a paper plate and put food on it, leaving space at one edge to hold it without my thumb in anything. Claire went farther into the room. I stayed near the rack, out of the route between the entrance and the tables, with my back close to the wall.

I talked to no one. I ate from the plate, folded the used napkin over the fork and held the whole thing at my waist. When someone needed a coat, I moved enough to let them reach it, then returned to the clear bit of wall. Claire crossed the room a couple of times and stopped beside me. I answered her, and she went back. I could see the clock from where I stood. At ten I put the plate in the waste bin, found her and asked if she'd mind if I went home.

She said she was staying. I said I would see her later and went to get my coat. One sleeve had been pushed inside itself on the rack. I pulled it straight, did up the buttons and put on my gloves before going outside. Snow had collected along the edge of the cleared walk. I took the route home alone, keeping to the part that had been shovelled where there was one. At Cedar Street I brushed the snow from my shoulders, stamped my boots on the step and let myself into the house.

Chapter 19

In January of 1999 the mill took on four apprentices, and three of them had journeymen waiting for them before they came through the gate. The fourth was Jordan Kwan. He had been put down for the instrument shop, but an apprentice needs a tradesman to sign his hours, and the two senior men in the shop had both said no. The first said he had enough on his plate. The second said it to Walt Kerrigan in the lunchroom, loud enough for the whole table to hear: the kid was slow, he barely opened his mouth, he had failed his first block of school and had to write it again, and he wasn't spending his last years at the mill wiping somebody's nose.

Walt came down to the shop the next morning with Jordan's file and put it on the bench. It was eleven degrees in the shop, which was usual before nine in January, and he kept his hands in his parka pockets. He said he wasn't going to order anybody to do it, but if nobody signed, the kid went back to the hall and the mill lost the spot until the next intake. I opened the file. Jordan was twenty. He had a fifty-eight in his first block, which was a fail, and a seventy-one on the rewrite. His shop marks were in the eighties. One instructor had written that he worked carefully and did not ask questions, and it was hard to tell whether that was meant as a compliment. I had been at the mill a little under four years and had never signed for anyone. I closed the file and told Walt I would take him.

Jordan started the following Monday. He was tall and thin and had a new pair of steel-toed boots that he walked stiffly in for two weeks. The two men who had turned him down weren't hard on him after that. They just didn't talk to him much, which suited him, because he didn't talk much either. He would answer a question and then stop. When I gave him a job he did it, slower than I would have, and when it was done it was done right. The first week I had him pull a dozen pressure transmitters off the stock prep floor and bench-check them, and he wrote every as-found and as-left reading in a column on the calibration sheet and initialled each line. Nobody had told him to initial them.

The trouble was drawings. The block he had failed was the one on loop diagrams, and the mill ran on loop diagrams. Every transmitter, valve and switch on the site had a sheet that showed where its wires went: from the device in the field to a junction box, from the junction box on a multi-pair cable to the marshalling cabinet, from the cabinet to a card in the control system rack, and back out again to whatever it was meant to move. If you couldn't read a loop sheet you couldn't troubleshoot, and if you couldn't troubleshoot you carried another man's tool bag. Jordan could read them, but slowly. He traced a pair with his finger and lost his place at the terminal strips, where forty wires came in on one side and went out on the other under different numbers.

So I started using my lunch. At noon the other men went up to the lunchroom by the time office. I stayed down at the long bench under the back windows, and after the third day Jordan stayed too. I would pull a sheet out of the flat file, usually a loop we had been into that morning, and tape it to the bench at the corners, and we ate with one hand. I asked him where a temperature transmitter landed in the marshalling cabinet, and he traced it, and when he got lost I didn't give him the answer. I asked him what the wire number was at the last place he was sure of. He would find it, and then he would go on. When he got to the card I gave him a different loop.

We did that every working day from February on, except during shutdowns, when nobody ate lunch at all. By April we were getting through three sheets an hour. By the summer I was giving him faults instead of drawings, a flow that read zero in the control room or a level that sat high and wouldn't move, and asking him where on the sheet he would put his meter first, and where second. He took longer than I would have, and then he said it, and most of the time he was right. When he was wrong it was nearly always because he wanted to go out to the device first, which is what everybody wants to do, because that's where the trouble seems to be. I told him to start at the card and work outward, because the card was ten steps from the bench and the device might be at the far end of the site. He wrote that down in a small notebook he kept in his shirt pocket. By the end of the year he could take a sheet he had never seen and walk it from the field to the card without using his finger. I signed his hours every two weeks in the logbook the union sent him, in ink, and I never rounded anything up.

That spring I reorganised the shop. Since before I was hired, each man had kept his own tools in his own box, and the shop's tools lived wherever the last man had put them down. The year before we had lost a pressure calibrator worth about nine hundred dollars and never found out where it went, and more than once I had come in on a night call and stood at the bench at two in the morning opening drawers for a terminal screwdriver.

I bought two sheets of half-inch plywood with my own money, painted them white and screwed them to the wall over the bench. I laid every hand tool the shop owned on the plywood, one at a time, and drew around it with a black marker, and put in the hooks, so that from the door you could see what was out by the empty shapes on the wall. Under the board went a sign-out book, a hardcover ledger from stores, with columns ruled for the date, the tool, the man, the work order number and the time it came back. I told the shop that nothing left the room without a line in the book, meters and calibrators included, and that included me. I gave Jordan a drawer of his own at the end of the bench with a label on the front from the label maker: J. KWAN. And I made it a rule that the bench was cleared at the end of every shift, down to bare steel, so the next man didn't start on the tail end of somebody else's job.

The keys took the longest. They had always lived in a coffee can on top of the filing cabinet, ninety-four of them, for every junction box, analyzer shelter, cabinet and padlock on the instrument side of the mill. I spent a weekend walking the mill with the can, matching each key to its lock. Eleven didn't fit anything I could find, and those went into an envelope in the bottom drawer with the date on it. The rest I hung on a board of numbered hooks, each key on its own brass tag stamped with the same number, and I typed a list on the shop computer of what each number opened. The older men complained about the book for about a month. After two months nobody was looking for anything.

Jordan's meters were stolen in June. He drove a 1988 Ford Ranger with a passenger door that didn't lock, and that Monday he had brought his meters back in their case behind the seat, because he had taken them home for the weekend to practise on a loop simulator board I had made him out of scrap. He went into the seven o'clock safety meeting and came out at half past eight, and the case was gone. Whoever took it had gone down the row in the employee lot trying doors. They got a tackle box out of another truck and a pair of boots out of a third. Jordan's case had in it a multimeter, a loop calibrator, a clamp meter and two sets of leads. He had bought them the summer before he started school, with his own money and some of his father's, and it had come to about eleven hundred dollars.

He reported it to mill security and the guard wrote it in his book. Then he came down to the bench and told me, and said he would sign out shop meters until he could buy new ones. The shop had two multimeters and one loop calibrator, and on a busy day they were all out by eight. He was making thirteen dollars and forty cents an hour as a first-year apprentice, paying rent on a room and payments on the truck, and I could do that arithmetic as well as he could. It would be most of a year.

I went to my locker. My own meters were in a canvas bag on the top shelf: the multimeter I had bought in 1995 when I was hired, a loop calibrator I had bought used from a man retiring out of one of the mines, and a clamp meter. I checked that the leads were all there and the batteries were good, carried the bag back to the bench and handed it to him, and told him it was his. He said he couldn't take it. I said the shop meters would do me fine, since I was the one who had made the sign-out book. I gave the young apprentice my own set of meters when his were stolen, the first truly open-handed thing I remember doing. Jordan put them in the drawer with his name on it, and that afternoon I signed out a shop multimeter in the book like anybody else, and brought it back at the end of the shift.

That summer I got interested in the scanner on the paper machine. At the dry end, after the last dryer can and before the reel, the sheet ran at better than six hundred metres a minute through a steel frame shaped like a big letter O. A head travelled back and forth on the frame, crossing the sheet every thirty seconds or so, and weighed the paper as it went without touching it. In the top half of the head was a small sealed source of krypton-85 gas, which gives off beta particles, and in the bottom half was an ionization chamber that measured how many of them got through. The more fibre there was in the way, the fewer got through, and from that the system worked out the basis weight in grams per square metre at every point across the sheet. The operators set the stock flow and the headbox by it.

The source itself was not our business. The mill held a licence for it, the vendor's man came twice a year to do the leak test and check the shutter, and the rule in the shop was that nobody but him opened the housing. The readings were our business. The mill had a technical library in a room behind the engineering offices, two walls of binders and a filing cabinet of old course notes, and that summer I read everything in it on beta gauges. There was the vendor's manual for our scanner, three inches thick. There was a set of training notes from a course somebody had gone on in 1984, with corrections pencilled in the margins. There were photocopied papers from the pulp and paper association on measurement error. I took them home a binder at a time and read them at the kitchen table after supper.

What I wanted to understand was the drift. Krypton-85 has a half-life of a little under eleven years, so the source loses about six percent of its strength every year. The gauge allows for that. Every so often the head runs off the edge of the sheet and takes a reading through plain air to standardize itself, so a weaker source shouldn't matter. But the operators' trend charts showed the basis weight reading creeping a few tenths of a gram over a period of months, and on the other machine the vendor had changed the source early for that reason. I worked nights one week in four that year, and on the nights when nothing was broken I sat in the shop with the manual and the trend printouts and turned it over.

Jordan was on the same rotation that summer, and he didn't agree with me. I said that as the source got weaker there were fewer counts in each reading, and the scatter in the counts didn't shrink in proportion, so the noise grew against the signal and the way the system averaged it pulled the number in one direction. He said that was true but it was too small. He said it was the windows. The head had thin films over the source and the detector to keep dirt out, and pulp dust and size and moisture built up on them, and the air gap between the two halves changed temperature with the dryers. The standardize caught some of that, he said, but not all of it, because the dirt didn't build evenly. We argued it across four or five night shifts, him drawing his on the back of one calibration sheet and me drawing mine on the back of another. Neither of us could prove anything with what we had. It was the most I heard him say at one time that year.

All that summer and fall the mill was getting ready for the year 2000. Head office had sent a directive that every piece of equipment with a date in it was to be listed and checked, which in the instrument shop meant every controller, recorder, analyzer and computer we had. Walt wrote to the vendors and asked them to certify their equipment, and through July and August the letters came back on company letterhead. Each one said the product named was Year 2000 compliant and would continue to operate normally through the date change. Walt punched them and filed them in a green binder with Y2K on the spine, one tab per vendor, and kept it on the shelf over his desk. When the binder was full he told head office the instrument side was done.

I didn't take the letters as the end of it. A letter from a sales office was not the same thing as a controller doing it on the bench, and the people who wrote the letters were not the people who wrote the firmware. On the second Saturday in August I went in and took a spare single-loop controller out of stores, the model we had more of than any other, thirty-one on site, and one of the paperless chart recorders that had replaced the pen recorders in the bleach plant. I wired them up on the bench with a power supply and a signal generator so they had something to work on, and set the controller running a timed program the way the ones in the bleach plant ran. Then I set both clocks to ten minutes to midnight on the thirty-first of December 1999, and watched them.

The controller rolled over and its program went wrong. It counted its elapsed time from the year, decided it was a hundred years behind, dropped out of automatic and held its output at the last value with a fault light on the front. The recorder rolled over to 01/01/00 and kept drawing, but when I went into its menus it had stamped the new records 1900 and sorted them ahead of everything from the day before, so if you went looking for the last hour you couldn't find it. I ran each one three times. I tried the twenty-ninth of February 2000 as well, because 2000 was a leap year and some equipment didn't know it, and both of them passed that. On the Sunday I tested four other models we had spares of, and those came through.

Both of the models that failed had letters in the green binder. I wrote out a list on a sheet of paper: the two model numbers, the firmware version printed on each, how many of each we had and the tag number of every one, where they were in the mill, what each did at the rollover, and the date on the vendor's letter. I didn't put in for the time. On the Monday I gave the list to Walt. He read it standing up, and then read it again sitting down, and asked me if I was sure. I told him I had done it three times. He took the binder down, put the list in the front of it ahead of the first tab, and carried it upstairs.

Lionel Dube died in October. I read it in the Brule Falls paper at the kitchen table on a Thursday: he had died at home at seventy-seven, and the funeral was on the Monday at St. Anne's. He had given me my first job, at the repair counter at Dube Radio and TV in 1986, when I was fifteen, at three dollars and fifty cents an hour to sort his parts drawers, take in sets and write the tickets. He showed me how to read a schematic and how to solder so the joint came out bright, and he was the first man who handed me a broken piece of equipment and expected me to find out what was wrong with it. I hadn't been in to see him since I came back to town, and I hadn't seen him in some time.

I decided to go. I put in for the Monday morning off, and Walt signed it. Sleet was coming sideways off the river. I parked on the side street and walked in about forty minutes before the time in the paper. There was nobody in the church but a woman setting out booklets by the door, and she gave me one. I sat near the back on the left side, at the end of a pew by the side aisle, and I said a prayer for Lionel, by myself, before anyone else came in. Then I read the booklet. It had his picture on the front, standing behind the counter, and the years of the shop under it, 1953 to 1996.

People came in after that, a few at a time and then more, and filled the church from the front. I stayed where I was. When it was over and they carried him out, I left by the side door before the family came down the aisle, and I didn't go to the lunch afterward in the parish hall. I drove to the mill, changed in the locker room, and was at the bench by one o'clock. Jordan had a level transmitter in pieces on the bench. He asked me how it was, and I told him it was fine and asked him what was wrong with the transmitter.

In November the mill held a downtime review on a sheet break. The machine had broken at the press section at a quarter to two on a Tuesday morning and been down nine hours, and on a machine that size nine hours was a lot of paper. There was a new production manager that year. He had come in the summer from a mill in another province, and he ran the reviews himself, at eight on Monday mornings in the conference room upstairs, with the superintendents and shift supervisors and somebody from each of the trades. Walt went for maintenance, and he asked me to come because I had been the one called in that night.

I had pulled the alarm and event log off the control system on the Wednesday. It was a folded printout on green-bar paper, and I had it in a manila folder with the shift log and my own notes on the call. It showed that at 01:38 someone at operations console three had put the headbox pressure interlock into bypass, which the procedure didn't allow without a supervisor's signature, and that it was still in bypass at 01:44 when the headbox pressure dropped and the sheet went. With the interlock in, the stock pump would have tripped, the sheet would have come off at the wet end, and the machine would have been back up in two hours. I had underlined the two lines in pencil.

The production manager put up an overhead of the trend and said the root cause was a drifting headbox pressure transmitter that should have been caught in calibration. He said instrumentation needed to tighten up its preventive maintenance, and he looked at Walt when he said it. The transmitter had been checked six weeks before and found within a quarter percent, and when I pulled it after the break it was still within a quarter percent. The folder was on my knee. I didn't open it, and I didn't say anything. Walt said that it was the shop's equipment and the shop would take it, and that we would go over the calibration records on the headbox loops and report back at the next review. The production manager wrote that down and went on to the next item.

On the stairs going down, Walt didn't ask me what was in the folder, and I didn't offer it. In the shop I wrote the date and the meeting on the front of the folder and filed it in the cabinet under the machine number with the rest of the paper on that break. That week Jordan and I went through the calibration records on every headbox loop, as Walt had promised, and they were all in order, and Walt reported it at the next review.

By December the town had been talking about New Year's Eve for a month. There was to be a party at the rink, and it had been in the paper every week and on posters in every window on Main Street. Claire's ward was going together, the nurses and some of the doctors, and she had bought her ticket in November. I had been seeing Claire since the year before. She asked me if I wanted to come, and I told her I had put my name in to work.

Walt had come round the shop in the middle of November looking for one instrument technician to be on site through the rollover, because head office wanted a tradesman from each department in the mill at midnight in case something went down with the date. He had expected to have to pick somebody. I put my name on the sheet that afternoon. Production had decided to bring both paper machines down at eight in the evening on the thirty-first and start them again on the first, but the powerhouse, the boilers and the effluent plant had to run, and they were all on the control system, so somebody had to be in the room.

On the thirty-first it was minus twenty-eight and clear when I left the house at six. Claire had stopped by on her way to get ready at a friend's and made me a thermos of coffee and two ham sandwiches on brown bread, and put a chocolate bar in the bag. I took the list I had given Walt in August and a clipboard, and relieved the day man at seven. By nine the machines were down, and the powerhouse and effluent operators were in their own rooms at the other end of the site. I had the main control room to myself: eleven screens, the alarm printer and the telephone.

I spent the first hours going round the screens and writing down a value from every loop that mattered, so I would have something to compare against afterward. At eleven I walked out to the bleach plant, where most of the units on the list were, and wrote down what each one was showing. Then I came back, poured a coffee and sat at the console. The rink was across town, and the only sound in the control room was the cabinet fans. At five to midnight I stood up and watched the clock on the main screen.

It went from 23:59:59 to 00:00:00, and the date in the corner changed to 01/01/2000. Nothing alarmed, and the printer didn't move. I went round all eleven screens, then out to the bleach plant with the clipboard to check every unit on the list against what I had written at eleven, then back to the powerhouse and effluent loops. At ten past twelve the phone rang, and it was Walt, at home, asking how it was. I read him what I had. He said good, and that he was going to bed. I wrote it all in the log with the time against each entry, and after that I went round the screens every fifteen minutes and wrote them in the log again.

Walt had arranged for the night man to take over at a quarter to three. I gave him the log and the list and went out to the truck, which had been plugged in since seven and started on the first try. I pulled out of the mill lot at three. The thermometer on the dash said minus thirty-one. Main Street was empty, and there were still cars in the rink lot with their exhaust going straight up in the cold. I drove home to Cedar Street, plugged the truck in at the side of the house, and went to bed.

Claire moved into the Cedar Street house at the end of April 2000. She had been renting the upper half of a duplex near the hospital, and on a Saturday we brought her things over in two trips in my truck and one in her car: a bed, a dresser, a kitchen table she liked better than mine, eleven boxes of books and dishes, and a file box with her nursing papers in it. I had cleared half the closet, two drawers and the shelf over the washing machine. She put a few boxes she didn't need yet in the basement, on the floor along the north wall.

The basement leaked every spring. I had known it when I bought the house, because the man who sold it to me had painted the bottom foot of the north and west walls with a grey sealer, and there was a tide line on the paint. Every April, when the snow went and the ground was still frozen underneath, water came in at the joint between the wall and the floor and spread across to the floor drain, and I went down twice a day with the shop vac. The first week of May, Claire's boxes along the north wall sat in an inch of water by morning. She carried them upstairs and set them on the kitchen floor to dry, and the bottoms of two of them came away in her hands.

The house had never had a proper foundation drain. There was clay tile at the footing from when it was built in the fifties, and from what I could see where it came out at the back corner, it had filled with silt and roots years before. A contractor from out of town had quoted six thousand four hundred dollars to dig it out with a machine. I worked it out the other way. Perforated pipe, filter sock, clear stone and drainage membrane came to about eleven hundred, and the rest was shovelling. I decided to dig it myself.

The trench had to go around three sides of the house, where the ground sloped toward it, and then across the back yard to the ditch along the lane so the water had somewhere to go. I measured it with a tape: thirty-four metres around the house and six out to the ditch, forty metres in all. It had to go down to the footing, a metre and a half on the low side and a metre twenty on the high. I laid it out with stakes and string in May, ordered the pipe through the lumberyard, and they dropped six yards of three-quarter clear stone on the driveway on a Friday for two hundred and ten dollars delivered.

I started digging the first week of June. I got home from the mill at four-thirty, ate something standing up, changed into my old work pants, and dug until I couldn't see the bottom of the trench, which in June was close to ten o'clock. I had a long-handled spade, a pointed shovel, a pick, and a wheelbarrow borrowed from next door, and I piled the dirt on tarps along the trench so it wouldn't go into the garden. The top half-metre was sandy fill and went fast. Below that it was grey clay with stones in it, and some evenings I did three metres and some evenings one. I cut the outside wall of the trench back at a slope as I went down and kept the piles back from the edge, so nothing would come in. Claire was on twelve-hour shifts that summer, days and nights, and on her days off she brought a coffee out at nine and sat on the back step and asked how far I had got.

By the middle of July I had the north side and half the west side open down to the footing, and I could see the old tile, broken in places and packed solid. Then a thunderstorm came through on a Sunday afternoon and put down sixty millimetres in about two hours, by the gauge on the fence post. When it stopped, the north trench had half a metre of brown water in it, and the clay wall at the low end had slumped in for about two metres. I bailed it with two pails, dumping them in the lane ditch, and it took the rest of Sunday and most of Monday evening. Then I dug the slump out again.

In the middle of August it happened a second time, a slow rain over two days, and the water came up in the west trench faster than I could keep up with the pails. I signed a small electric pump out of the shop in the book, ran a hose to the ditch and let it go overnight. In the morning I had a trench full of mud instead of water, and the first eight metres of the west side to dig over again. After that I covered the open length with tarps every night, weighted along the edges with boards, and pulled them off again when I got home.

I started laying pipe in September. I broke out what was left of the old tile, put down a bed of clear stone fifteen centimetres deep, and laid the four-inch perforated pipe in its sock on the stone with the holes down and a fall of a centimetre in every metre toward the corner where it turned out to the lane. I checked the fall on every length with a four-foot level and a block of wood. I scraped the wall clean, hung the membrane on the outside of the foundation down to the footing, and covered the pipe with another thirty centimetres of stone before the backfill. Halfway round I ran short and had to order one more yard.

The last length went in on the second Saturday of October. There had been a frost overnight, and the clay at the bottom of the trench out by the lane was stiff when I started. I laid the pipe, checked the fall twice, covered it with stone, and put a piece of hardware cloth over the outlet at the ditch so nothing would nest in it. Then I ran the garden hose into the trench at the far corner of the house, walked out to the lane, and stood at the ditch until the water came out of the end of the pipe. It took about four minutes. Claire came out and watched it run with me. I shut off the hose, and we went in.

Chapter 20

In 2001, Claire and I planned to get married, and by the time the frost had come out of the yard on Cedar Street there was a date in June written on the kitchen calendar. I had meant to suggest the fall. The mill had work scheduled through the summer, and the house still had places where I had put something down in 1997 and left it there. I also had the idea that a wedding involving the two families could be arranged with a few telephone calls. Claire and her mother had paper spread over the kitchen table when I came in from a shift. There was a list of forty people, a menu and the courthouse telephone number.

I set my lunch box beside the sink and washed my hands. Claire moved a chair out for me with her foot. Her mother had divided the list into families, with small marks beside the names of people who had answered. The civil ceremony would be at the district courthouse. Lunch would follow at the Bouchards' house. The June date had been checked against the availability of both places, though the house did not seem likely to be booked by anyone else. There were questions of chairs, dishes and how much food could be kept cold. I could follow those parts.

I had wanted to say that September would do, and that the two families were enough. Instead I read the menu. There would be cold meat, salads, rolls and a cake. I was asked whether that suited me. I said it did. The forty names were read through, and I said yes to those as well. When Claire pointed to the date, I looked at the shift calendar pinned beside the telephone and said I could arrange it. I had agreed to the date, the guest list and the menu without raising a single point. I see now that I married a woman who would say the things I would not.

Claire put a line through one item and wrote something beside another. Her mother gathered the loose pages into a pile, tapped their edges on the table and left the list of things still to be done. I went out to the garage for a tape measure because they needed to know whether an extra table would fit through the back entrance at the house. That was the first wedding job I took on. I wrote down the width they needed and put the tape beside my lunch box so I would remember it. By supper the date had acquired a box around it on the calendar.

There were smaller decisions over the next weeks, though I did not make many of them. Claire asked whether I had suitable trousers. I tried them on and found that I did. I put the courthouse papers in a large envelope and kept it on top of the refrigerator, where it would stay flat and away from coffee. When something had to be paid, I wrote the amount in my notebook. The figures came separately, which made the wedding seem smaller than it was. There was never a single bill for forty people, a June afternoon and the use of nearly every available chair.

On the day, I checked the envelope before we left Cedar Street. June had brought enough warmth that the car was hot inside, although the shaded side of the house still held the morning cool. At the district courthouse I stood where I was directed and answered when it was my turn. There was a hard edge on the chair beneath my hand. I remembered that more clearly than most of what was said. Claire stood beside me, and we were married in the time allowed. Afterwards the papers went back into the envelope. I carried it to the car with the flap tucked in.

At the Bouchards' house, the lunch had taken over the rooms and the deck space available to it. Chairs stood close enough that getting up required somebody else to move. I found my place beside Claire and put the envelope where it would not be under a plate. Food travelled in both directions along the table. Once I had a plate in front of me, I stayed in my chair. There were forty people to feed, and anyone carrying a dish had more reason to be in the passage than I did.

Claire's uncle poured wine into my glass before the toast. I took one sip when the glasses went up. The wine left a dry patch along the inside of my mouth, and I put it down beside my right elbow. A few minutes later he came past with the bottle and added enough to bring it back near the top. I had not asked for more. There was little room between the glass and my plate, so I shifted the glass farther towards the centre of the table and kept my forearm clear of it.

When he came round again, I put my fingers over the rim. I said I was all right for wine and asked for water. The wine remained full at my elbow for the rest of the meal. I drank the water and ate what was on my plate. Each time a dish arrived, I passed it on before the next one reached me. Claire could speak to people on either side and still know which bowl was needed farther down. I watched for an empty space to set things. Once the plates were being cleared, I carried mine to the kitchen and stayed there long enough to dry a stack of cutlery.

The tea towels grew damp faster than I expected. I folded one over the oven handle and used another. From the kitchen I could hear chairs scraping as people changed places. There were more dishes to come, including the cake plates, so I kept the drying space clear. Claire found me there and put her hand on the middle of my back while she reached past for a clean plate. We still had to get our things from Cedar Street before driving to the cottage. I asked what time she wanted to leave. She gave me a time, and I checked my watch without making an announcement about it.

We drove the long way round to her parents' cottage, happily, with the windows down. The gravel road added distance, but there was no need to make good time. I kept the car out of the loose ridge along the shoulder and slowed where the road narrowed. Dust rose behind us and stayed suspended between the trees. Claire had taken off her shoes. The courthouse envelope lay flat beneath our bags, and the things we needed for the long weekend were in the boot. We could talk without having to leave room for someone carrying a serving dish.

At the cottage, twenty of Claire's relatives were staying for the weekend. Their cars filled the usual parking places and part of the approach. Bags stood near the doors, and there were shoes under the benches. The deck was already full when we arrived. I carried our things inside, put them where Claire showed me and went back out for the last bag. The water pump had been cutting in badly. Its pressure switch was available for attention, which suited an afternoon when most of the available chairs had been taken.

I disconnected the power before removing the switch. The little cover had dust inside it, and there was a crust around the connection where moisture had got in. I took the switch and a few tools down to the end of the dock. I had a screwdriver, a small adjustable wrench, fine abrasive paper and a shallow container for the screws. The boards were dry enough to sit on. I kept the parts in the container rather than setting them loose between my knees, because the gaps in the dock were wider than several pieces I needed to keep.

Most of the afternoon went into that switch. I separated the contacts, cleaned them, checked the movement and worked the stiff parts until they returned properly. It was a small mechanism, with very little travel between doing its job and doing nothing useful. I kept the spring settings where they had been and dealt with the corrosion first. From the end of the dock I could hear the family filling the deck. Sometimes several voices reached me together, without words I could separate. I turned the switch in my hands and cleaned the underside of a terminal screw.

Claire came down once and stood beside the container of parts. She asked whether it would work. I said I would know when I put it back. She stayed for a few minutes, then went up to the cottage. I finished the assembly, checked that nothing had been left on the boards and carried it back. With the switch fitted and the cover secure, I restored the power and ran water while I watched the gauge. The pump started and stopped. I repeated it enough times to see that the contacts were opening cleanly, then put the tools away.

By then the deck chairs had been moved several times. I took a glass of water outside and stood near the steps until I could get through. The end of the dock had left straight marks across the backs of my legs. I rubbed one with my palm while Claire told me where our things had ended up. There were twenty relatives to fit into the cottage for the weekend, and the arrangements had changed since we arrived. I went inside with her and moved our bags. The pump cut in while somebody ran a tap, then stopped at the pressure I had left it.

When Claire's things were moved into Cedar Street for good, the garage became the first place I could put in order without asking where something belonged. There were boxes waiting to be emptied, tools on a shelf behind other tools and household things sharing floor space with things that could leak. I took everything off one section of shelving at a time. That left enough clear floor to work while still allowing the door to close. I swept each section before putting anything back. The concrete held a fine grey dust that returned whenever a box was dragged across it.

I bought bins in a size that fitted the shelves and wrote labels for them. Plumbing went in one, electrical in another, fasteners in several smaller ones. I sorted screws by size and kept washers where I could see the centre holes without emptying a whole container. The wall took a row of hooks and then another. I measured the handles before fixing the lower row so the longer tools would not sit on the floor. The shovel, broom and rake each had a place. I could reach what I wanted without lifting two other things out first.

Claire came to the doorway while I was deciding where to put the extension cords. She watched me coil one and hang it, then asked whether everything in the house would be getting a label. I said only if it needed one. She put a box on the cleared section of floor and told me it could go anywhere dry. I found room for it on a shelf and wrote what was inside on the end facing out. After she went back in, I moved the nearest hook over a little so the cord would hang clear of that box.

The household ledger began at the kitchen table. I used pencil because figures changed and because I disliked a page full of crossed-out amounts. There was a column for the mortgage, one for taxes, one for heat, another for electricity, and separate ones for groceries, the car, insurance, repairs and household purchases. I ruled the lines with a straightedge. The first page had more empty space than figures. Receipts went into an envelope until I entered them. I wrote the date, the amount and enough of a description to distinguish a necessary fitting from something bought with the groceries.

Claire laughed when she saw the columns. She wanted to know where a purchase went if it belonged in two of them. I said I could divide it. That made her laugh again. She brought me a receipt and waited while I found a place for it. I kept the columns. On evenings when I had several receipts to enter, I spread them by date and did the arithmetic once along the rows and again down the page. Claire could find the amount left for a bill without asking me. The pencil stayed in the fold of the book, sharpened over the waste bin when it needed it.

Denis came home from the freighters in August. He still handled keys as though there ought to be a larger piece of machinery attached to them. We went down towards the river, and he brought up jumping from the railway trestle. He wanted the two of us to do it the way we had as boys. The water was lower than it had been in spring. Along the bank, exposed roots held dry mud between them. I kept to the path and then to the level ground beside the river while he looked up at the trestle.

He said there was still plenty of water beneath it. I said I would stay where I was. He pulled his shirt over his head and handed it to me. His keys were in the pocket, so I took them out and held them separately rather than let them fall when I folded the shirt. The metal was warm from his pocket. Denis went up and climbed out. I could see the soles of his feet briefly as he shifted his weight. I remained on the bank with his shirt over one arm and his keys closed in my hand.

He called something down that amounted to one more invitation. I shook my head. He jumped, entered the river and disappeared beneath the surface. The sound reached the bank after the movement. I watched the place where he had gone in, then the patch downstream where his head came up. The man I was at thirty would have puzzled the boy on the water tower. Denis pushed his hair back and turned towards shore. I moved along the bank to where he could get out without climbing over the exposed roots.

He came out dripping and reached for the shirt. I kept the keys until he had pulled it on, then put them into his palm. He asked whether I had forgotten how. I said I remembered well enough. Water ran from the hem of his shirt onto his trousers. We stood there while he wrung out what he could, then took the path back. He had done what he came to do. I had a damp mark on my sleeve from holding his shirt, and a little river mud on the edges of my shoes. Neither required much attention when I got home.

At the mill, the new analyzer house brought a contractor's invoice to my desk because I knew what had been installed and when. It was a wiring bill with labour, material and crew days listed in separate sections. I put it beside my daybook and read it once without a pencil. The total alone did not tell me much. Wiring took time that did not show in the finished job: pulling cable, making supports, terminating, checking and doing the last part again when a drawing did not match what was actually there.

I started with the materials and followed the work through the dates. My daybook had the days when access had been available, the cable pulls I had seen and the checks I had made. I wrote those things down at the time because otherwise one shift ran into another. There was a day when the crew had been waiting for access, and that was properly on the bill. There were other entries that matched the work. I put a small pencil tick beside each one I could support and left the rest until I had the gate log.

The gate log gave me a second set of dates. I laid the pages side by side and used a ruler to keep my place. Four billed days had no crew on site. I checked the preceding and following pages in case the entries had been carried over or the dates had been copied wrongly. Then I went back through my daybook. On those four days there had been no contractor crew working in the analyzer house. The invoice charged for them just the same. I marked the dates, the crew entries and the corresponding amounts, keeping the writing away from the original figures.

I made a copy with the four days clearly identified and sent the marked-up copy to purchasing. My note said which records I had checked and where the differences were. I did not round anything or write that the whole bill was wrong. The materials I could account for stayed as they were, along with the labour that matched the job. I kept my working copy with the daybook reference. It took longer to make the discrepancy easy to follow than it had taken to find it. When the papers were out of the way, I put the ruler back in the drawer and returned to the analyzer work.

Jordan's final school block came up while I was still getting used to having household receipts in the same notebook as my own expenses. He told me about the fees during a shift, after we had finished the immediate job. He had the amount written down and a date by which it had to be paid. He could not pay it. He had worked through the arrangements available to him and was about to drop out. He folded the paper along the same creases while he spoke. I asked whether that was the complete fee or whether more would be due at the start.

It was the fee that would keep him from going. I asked him to bring me the bill. When he did, I checked the name, the school block and the payment instructions. I did not need an account of every dollar he had spent. I needed the right bill so I would pay the right thing. I went to the college office myself. The trip took part of a day I was not on shift. I carried the bill flat in an envelope and had the payment ready, with enough in the account to cover it when it went through.

At the office I waited at the counter and put the bill down when it was my turn. I said I was paying Jordan's fees. The amount was taken against his account, and I waited for the receipt. Before leaving, I checked that the receipt named the school block and showed the payment. There was nothing else to arrange there. I folded the papers together and put them back in the envelope. Outside, I checked my watch and worked out the drive home. I still had time to eat before I needed to sleep for the next shift.

I gave Jordan the receipt at work. He looked at it longer than the information on it required. I told him to pay it back when he could. He started to give me a date, and I said when he could was enough. There was work waiting, so I put the empty envelope in my bag and we went to it. The payment went into the ledger on Cedar Street. I recorded the amount in pencil, with his name beside it, but I did not make a schedule of instalments. Through the rest of that year and the next, I never asked him for it.

Claire's question about the heat from the mill had started before the wedding, on an evening when the kitchen window was open. She asked why the mill could not reuse all the heat it sent up the stack. I began with the equipment I knew: where heat was supplied, where it was recovered and what still went out. She asked why the remainder could not be taken back too. I said something about temperatures and found that I could name the parts more easily than I could explain the limit. She waited while I tried again. I said I would have to read about it.

For weeks I read about heat engines after supper and on the portions of my days off that were not already spoken for. I worked through examples with a pencil beside the book. Heat supplied at one temperature could do work only while some of it passed towards a lower temperature. Even an ideal arrangement left heat to be rejected. The equations used absolute temperature, so I put the conversion at the top of each page until I stopped forgetting it. Numbers that had looked reasonable in Celsius became plainly wrong when I checked what I had divided by what.

That did not mean the stack heat was all beyond use. I separated recovering heat for another job from turning it into work. Water could be warmed with heat that was too low in temperature to make a useful power cycle under the conditions available. Then there were losses through equipment, changes in flow and the electricity needed to move the fluids. I drew boxes and arrows on graph paper, wrote the units beside every number and rubbed out more than I kept. The waste bin acquired narrow grey curls from the eraser along with the pencil shavings.

I argued it with Jordan on night shift when the immediate work was caught up. He wanted to know why one more exchanger could not always take one more part of what remained. We used a clean piece of paper and followed the temperatures through. At some point the useful difference became small, while the equipment and pumping still had to be paid for. He shifted one of my assumed temperatures and changed the result. I checked his arithmetic. We could get more heat back in the calculation, but not all of it, and not without giving that recovered heat somewhere suitable to go.

At the cottage, Claire's brother-in-law had taken up the same argument. That had been during the long weekend, after I put the pressure switch back. He proposed using the recovered heat to run the equipment that recovered it. I worked through his suggestion with him, beginning with what temperature the heat would be when it reached each part. Claire came within hearing while we were still at it. She said she had only asked why the stack was hot. I told her that had turned out to be a fairly large question. She left us to finish the figures on the paper we had found.

Back at Cedar Street, I worked the mill's heat recovery on graph paper with what figures I could reasonably use. I kept measured values separate from estimates. Where I did not have a dependable flow, I ran the calculation at more than one value and left the range visible. I wanted to know whether the result survived a different assumption. Some pages ended in a line drawn through the working. Others stayed beneath the ledger until I needed them again. There was always some heat left at the end of the calculation, and I could finally explain to Claire why I had left it there.

The reading made the coursework for an engineering technologist designation seem like something I could fit into the hours I was already spending at the kitchen table. I got the requirements and read them through. There was coursework to complete, a written project report and an exam. Work experience did not take the place of setting things out properly. I put the dates on the calendar beside my shifts and made a list of what had to be submitted. The list was longer than I had expected, but each item was specific enough to put a mark beside when it was done.

Through 2001 and 2002, I studied after shifts. On day shifts that meant clearing a place after supper. After nights I slept first and used the table later, with the curtains open if there was daylight left. Claire's shifts did not always match mine. Sometimes she was in the house and sometimes a note on the counter told me when she expected to be back. I kept the coursework together so the table could be used for meals. A stack of graph paper, the calculator, pencils and the current set of notes went into one box when I cleared up.

The coursework required a kind of checking different from finding whether a device worked. A correct result needed the steps that made it correct. I wrote the units at the right of a calculation and followed them through before I trusted the number. If I had copied a figure badly, a neat page did not improve it. I went back to the source. There were evenings when the work amounted to a few pages and evenings when I corrected one page for most of the time available. Before bed I left a note to myself about where to begin next.

For the project report, I used the heat recovery work. I had enough pages of calculations to make the first version look substantial. The reviewer sent it back. The comments asked for a clearer statement of the problem, a better account of the information used and a distinction between what the figures showed and what I had assumed. I read the comments twice, then put the report on the kitchen table and began the first rewrite. I moved the background out of the calculations and made a separate place for the assumptions. Several pages I had spent time on no longer had a place in it.

The next returned copy had fewer marks, though the marks required more work. I had explained equipment I knew as if the reader had stood beside it with me. I rewrote those sections, identified the boundaries of the calculation and put the references where they could be followed. That was the second rewrite. Claire lifted the pile once to wipe beneath it and asked whether it had become lighter. I said it had fewer pages. She set it back down in the same direction, with my pencil marking the section I was doing. I checked the tables again before sending it away.

It came back once more. The remaining corrections concerned what I could claim from the work. I had carried a result farther than the quality of one estimate allowed. For the third rewrite I changed that passage, kept the range in the figures and made the limitation explicit in the report. I checked that the conclusion matched the tables rather than the version I remembered writing. Then I went through the whole thing, page by page, including the page numbers. The clean copy occupied less of the kitchen table than the drafts and returned copies stacked beside it.

The exam required its own preparation. I went back over the coursework instead of relying on the sections I used most often at the mill. I worked problems without looking at the examples, checked them and did the ones I had missed again on fresh paper. On the day, I took the permitted things with me and read the instructions before beginning. I worked through what I could complete, marked the questions that needed another look and used the remaining time to check figures and units. When the exam was finished, I put the calculator away and went home.

I got the engineering technologist designation in the fall of 2002. The notice arrived at Cedar Street with the rest of the mail. I read it at the kitchen table, then left it for Claire where she would see it. There was rain on the back step that evening, and I wiped my shoes before going out to the garage with the box of coursework. I kept the report and the useful notes together on a dry shelf. The ledger stayed in the kitchen. Before my next shift, I entered two receipts, checked the column totals and sharpened the pencil.

Chapter 21

In 2002 the mill shuffled its crews over the summer shutdown and moved Luc Gagnon onto my night shift. He was a millwright, a couple of years older than me, broad through the shoulders, with a moustache he kept trimmed with a pair of nail scissors he carried in his lunch box. I had known him to nod to since I came back to the mill in 1995, but millwrights and instrument people did not work together much on days. On nights there were fewer of us and everybody covered everything. If a pump seal let go at two in the morning, the millwright pulled the pump and I disconnected whatever transmitter or switch was hung off it, and we stood in the same puddle waiting for the same parts.

We were on twelve-hour shifts then, seven at night to seven in the morning, four nights on and four off. Thursday was the last night of the run. The first Thursday in September, Luc caught me at the time clock and asked if I wanted a beer. I said it was seven in the morning. He said it was seven at night for us, and that his kitchen didn't have a clock in it anyway, which was not true; it had one over the stove shaped like a frying pan.

He lived alone in a bungalow on the east side with a carport and a kitchen that faced west, so in the morning it was dim, which suited the hour. He had a case of Labatt Blue in the fridge, twenty-four bottles, and he put the whole case on the table rather than taking out two. We sat across from each other with our coveralls unzipped to the waist and the sleeves tied around us and talked about the shift: the felt that had wandered on number two machine, the hydraulic leak on the chip conveyor that had been dripping since June, the call for a millwright at three in the morning to look at a valve that turned out to be an instrument problem. Luc talked more than I did. He said the house was quiet enough the rest of the week that he liked a bit of noise in it on a Thursday.

By nine the case was mostly gone. Then he got up and took a bottle of Canadian Club down from the cupboard over the fridge, and two glasses that had come free with a fill-up at the Esso, and poured rye over ice. I had two. At ten I drove home, the four streets across town to Cedar Street, and went to bed. I slept until three in the afternoon. Claire was on days at the hospital that week and came home at four to find me making coffee.

"How was Luc's," she said.

"Fine," I said.

It was meant to be once. It was the next Thursday too, and the one after. Some weeks it was a case of Blue and some weeks it was Molson Export if that was what was on sale at the Beer Store, and the rye was always Canadian Club, which Luc bought in the 1.14-litre bottle because it worked out cheaper by the ounce. I worked that out for him on the back of a parts requisition one morning, eighty-one cents an ounce against ninety-four, and he said it was the most useful thing an instrument man had ever done for him. By Christmas it was every Thursday without fail. If a run ended on a stat holiday we went anyway. The one Thursday Luc booked off sick, he phoned me at the mill at half past six and told me to come over regardless, and I did. I did not miss one that year. It had become part of the rotation, like the four nights and the four off, and I went where the schedule put me.

That same fall the mill replaced the consistency transmitter on the stock line to number two machine. Consistency is the percentage of fibre in the pulp slurry, and the machine needs it held steady, around three and a half percent at that point in the line, or the sheet comes off uneven and the reel gets a soft spot. The old transmitter was a blade-type unit that had been there since before my time, with a mechanical linkage and a signal that jumped around like a pressure needle on a reciprocating pump. The new one was a microwave unit from a European outfit, about eleven thousand dollars installed, and the brochure said it had built-in smart filtering, adaptive damping and noise rejection. It was commissioned from a laptop in an afternoon, and when it was done the trend line on the control room screen was smooth.

That was what bothered me. When I bumped the dilution valve by hand to test the loop, the reading moved, but it didn't move the way I expected. Sometimes it seemed to arrive at the new value before the stock could have got there. Sometimes it lagged by twenty or thirty seconds. The manual gave three filter settings, low, medium and high, and said nothing about what the words meant. I phoned the distributor in Toronto, and the man there told me the algorithm was proprietary.

I had done some filtering at the college, enough to know what a first-order lag was and how a moving average worked, but no more than that. So I borrowed a textbook off the shelf in the engineering office, a signal processing book with a green cover and a cracked spine, about six hundred pages, most of it mathematics. I kept it in the cab of my truck. At lunch, which on nights came around one in the morning, I sat in the lot with the engine running for the heater and the dome light on and worked through it with a pencil and a Hilroy notebook, the kind with the blue cover. I bought a TI-83 calculator for a hundred and nineteen dollars at the Staples in Sudbury because the exercises needed one.

It took me from October to about March. I filled one notebook and started a second. I worked the problems in the order they came, and when I didn't understand a chapter I went back and did the one before it again. I learned what a transfer function was, and why every filter that smooths a signal has to delay it, since it can only work with readings that have already come in. Near the back of the book there was a chapter on estimators, the Kalman filter and its relations, which carry a model of how a process behaves, make a guess at what the next reading should be, and then correct the guess against the reading when it arrives. That was what I had been seeing on the screen. The transmitter wasn't only smoothing the signal. It had some idea of where the consistency was headed and was leaning that way.

I tried to explain this to Luc one Thursday in his kitchen, somewhere in the second half of the case.

"It's predicting," I said. "It's got a model of the line in there. It sees the reading going up and figures it'll keep going up, so it reports a bit ahead."

"So it's guessing," Luc said.

"It's estimating."

"What's the difference."

"An estimate's got the arithmetic written down."

"Ray, a filter's a filter. It takes the junk out." He poured. "It doesn't know where anything's going. Nothing knows where anything's going."

"This one does. For about two seconds."

"Two seconds," he said, as if that settled it in his favour, and maybe it did. We went around like that for most of an hour, him saying a filter could only clean up what it was handed and me saying it could also take a run at what was coming, and neither of us gave anything. Luc didn't care which of us was right. He liked the argument because I got going on it, and he kept it going by saying the same thing again whenever I slowed down. In the end I set the transmitter's filter to low and put a plain first-order lag in the control system instead, with a time constant I could see and change, and I wrote down why in the instrument file. The machine ran as well as it had before and nobody noticed the difference, which is the usual result of instrument work done properly.

Maya was born on the eleventh of February, 2003, at the district hospital, on the same floor where Claire worked, so most of the nurses who looked after her had eaten lunch with her mother at one time or another. She was six pounds fourteen ounces. It was minus thirty-one with the wind that night, and the truck took two tries to start in the hospital lot when I went home at four in the morning for Claire's bag.

Claire had read all the books. I had read one, the one the hospital handed out, and what I took from it was that a baby needed feeding every two to three hours, needed changing about as often, slept in between, and would be weighed by the doctor to see whether she was gaining. That I could keep track of. The day we brought her home I took a blue Hilroy notebook, the same kind I used in the truck, and ruled it with a pencil and a twelve-inch steel rule into columns: date, time, feed (left, right or bottle, and how many minutes or ounces), diaper (wet, dirty or both), sleep start, sleep end, and a narrow column on the right for remarks. One page did one day. I kept it on the counter by the kettle with the pencil tied to the spiral with a length of mason's line so it would stay with the book.

I also labelled the drawers of her change table. It was a pine one from the Sears catalogue that I had put together in December, three drawers under the pad. I used the label maker from my toolbox, the Brother with the yellow tape, and did them by size: NEWBORN, 0-3 MO, 3-6 MO. A week later I made a fourth label for the shelf underneath, DIAPERS / WIPES / CREAM, because the cream kept ending up in the drawers with the sleepers.

Claire found the notebook the second night. She was sitting up in the rocking chair feeding Maya at two in the morning, and I came in with it to write the feed down.

"What is that," she said.

I showed her. She turned the pages with her free hand. I had already filled in two days, both sides of the page.

"It's the maintenance log," she said. She said it straight, and then she laughed hard enough that Maya came off and had to be put back on.

If I was a good father in those years, it was by accident and by habit.

After the first week Claire filled it in too, though she never used the remarks column and I did. I kept it up for Maya's first six months, until the middle of August. By then there were six notebooks. From them I could tell you that she went from eight feeds a day to five, that her longest sleep in March was three hours and ten minutes and in July was seven hours and forty, and that a feed was followed within twenty minutes by a dirty diaper about two times in three. When the doctor asked at the four-month visit how she was sleeping, Claire handed him the notebook, and he read a page of it and handed it back without saying anything.

I stopped in August because by then Maya had a schedule and it was the same every day, and there is no point logging a reading that doesn't change. I kept the notebooks. They are in a banker's box in the basement, on the shelf above the tax returns.

Claire's mother came the day after we brought Maya home and stayed six weeks. She drove up from the Bouchards' place in her own car with two suitcases and a cooler of frozen tourtières, and she took the spare room, which until then had held my filing cabinet and an old drafting table I'd bought at the college when they replaced theirs.

She was good with Maya and good with Claire, and on my day-shift weeks she got up for the night feeds so I could sleep, and I have no complaint about any of that. She also rearranged the kitchen. Over the first week the coffee moved from the cupboard by the stove to the one by the sink, the cutlery drawer traded places with the tea towel drawer, and the can opener went somewhere I didn't find until April. The pots went under the counter nested by size, which I might have done myself if I'd thought of it.

The back hall, between the kitchen and the door to the garage, was where I kept the tools I used around the house: the Makita cordless drill in its case, a twenty-four-inch Stanley level, a claw hammer, a box of drywall anchors and a stud finder, on two shelves I'd put up beside the door. About ten days in I went to hang a hook for Maya's snowsuit and found a laundry basket on one shelf and boots on the other. I looked in the basement and in the front closet. I found the drill and the level in a cardboard box that had held a case of paper towel, out in the garage on the concrete beside the snow tires for Claire's car. The hammer and the anchors were in there too, and the stud finder on top with its battery door off.

I carried the box back in, found the battery door in the bottom of the box and put it back on, and put everything in the furnace room on the workbench, where nobody else had any use for the space. I put up the hook. I didn't say anything to Claire's mother about it, and I didn't say anything to Claire. It was a small house with a new baby and four people in it, and the drill wasn't in anyone's way in the furnace room. When Claire's mother went home at the end of March, she left the kitchen the way she'd made it, and we kept most of it that way, except the coffee, which I moved back.

In April of 2003 I signed up for the fourth-class power engineer's certificate by correspondence. The mill had a power boiler and a recovery boiler running steam at about six hundred pounds, and the stationary engineers who ran them held a ticket I didn't. A man with an instrument ticket and a power engineer's ticket could be useful in more places, and the hours I had already put in around the boiler house on the combustion controls counted toward the qualifying time. The course cost seven hundred and forty dollars with the books. It came in a cardboard box by courier: four binders of modules, each ending with a test you mailed in to be marked, and a sheet listing the exam topics, which were boiler construction, pumps, combustion, water treatment, refrigeration, basic electricity, safety and the regulations.

The difficulty was time. I was on rotation. Claire was on maternity leave and Maya was up every three hours. I found that the hour after the four o'clock feed was the only one in the day that belonged to nobody. Claire would feed her and go back to sleep, and I would take Maya for the burping and a few trips up and down the hall, and once she was down I'd sit at the kitchen table with a binder and a mug of coffee and work until half past five. On night-shift weeks I did the same thing at lunch in the truck, which by then had two textbooks in the cab instead of one.

The modules were not hard so much as there was a great deal of them. I knew the electrical and the controls already and went through those in a couple of weeks. The water treatment and the boiler construction were new to me: fire tube against water tube, the reasons for blowdown and how often, how to read a gauge glass and what you were supposed to do if you couldn't find the level in it. I made flash cards on index cards from the dollar store and kept them in a rubber band in my shirt pocket at work and went through them on the walk from the time clock to the shop. I mailed the module tests one at a time from the post office counter at the back of the drugstore on Main Street, in brown nine-by-twelve envelopes, and they came back four to six weeks later marked in red. The lowest mark I got was seventy-one, on refrigeration, which I had never touched before. The rest were in the eighties and nineties.

I wrote the exam in Sudbury in May of 2004, in a hotel conference room with about thirty other people at folding tables. It was three hours. I drove down in the morning and back in the afternoon and was home in time for Maya's supper. The letter came in July saying I had passed, with the certificate folded inside it. Claire put it on the fridge with a magnet from the pizza place until I took it down and put it in a frame from Canadian Tire and hung it in the furnace room, over the workbench, beside the drill.

In October of 2003 Claire's car started grinding when she stopped. It was a 1998 Pontiac Sunfire with about a hundred and forty thousand kilometres on it. She took it to a garage in town near the highway because they could see it the same day and I was on nights and asleep. They called her that afternoon and told her the front calipers were seized on both sides and the rotors were scored, and quoted her eighteen hundred dollars with tax for calipers, rotors, pads and a brake fluid flush.

Claire told me when I got up at three. I asked if the car pulled to one side when she braked. She said no. I asked whether the front wheels were hot after a drive. She didn't know. I said I'd look at it before she agreed to anything, and she phoned the garage and told them to leave it, and I went and got it after supper.

The next morning was my first day off. It was about four degrees and drizzling. I backed the car into the driveway, jacked the front end with my floor jack, set two jack stands under the pinch welds, and pulled the front wheels with the breaker bar and laid them flat on a sheet of cardboard. The inside pads were down to the backing plates on both sides, which was the grinding. The rotors had grooves in them, but when I put the micrometer on them they were inside the minimum thickness stamped on the hub, and they could be turned. Then I checked the calipers. I took each one off its bracket and pushed the piston back with a C-clamp and an old pad, and on both sides it went back smooth and even. The slide pins were dry but they moved. I pulled them, cleaned them with a rag and brake cleaner, and greased them with silicone grease. Nothing was seized. I hung the calipers back on, put the wheels on, lowered the car and drove it around the block twice. It didn't pull.

I phoned the garage to tell them where things stood. The man said the calipers were seized. I said I'd had them off that morning and they weren't. He said they might be sticking now and then. I said that wasn't seized, and he said all right, and that was the end of it.

I took the car to the shop on the industrial road that did the fleet work for the mill trucks. They put on new pads and turned the rotors for four hundred and twelve dollars. Claire drove the car for a long time after that. I kept both pieces of paper in the file folder for the car, the quote for eighteen hundred stapled behind the invoice for four hundred.

That same month Luc bought a new all-terrain vehicle, a Honda Rancher, red, four-wheel drive, a little over eight thousand dollars with the trailer. He phoned on a Saturday morning and asked if I wanted to see what it could do. Claire was home with Maya, so I went. He picked me up at ten with the machine on the trailer behind his truck.

The old ski hill sits above town on the north side, past the water tower. It had been closed for years. The lift towers were still up with no cable on them, and the runs had grown in with alders and poplar along the edges, but the main run was still open down the middle, a long grassy slope with rocks showing through. Luc backed the trailer up to the bottom where the old lodge had been, which was just a concrete pad by then, and unloaded the Rancher, and drove it up the service road that went back and forth across the hill to the top. I stood by his truck. It was overcast and about eight degrees, and the leaves were mostly down.
