He came straight down the main run. He didn't use the service road or cut across. He pointed it down the fall line and came, standing on the footrests, the front end bucking over the rocks, and at the bottom he slid it sideways on the wet grass and stopped about thirty feet from the truck. He was laughing and his face was red.

"Your turn," he said, and got off and held the handlebars out toward me.

"No," I said. "I'll stay down here."

"It's nothing. It's got a low range. You just let it come."

"I'll stay with the truck."

He looked at me for a second and then shrugged and got back on. He went up the service road and came down again, faster, and then a third time, and on the third run the back end came around near the bottom and he caught it and kept going. I stood by the tailgate with my hands in my jacket the whole time. When he'd finished he winched it back onto the trailer with the hand winch and I ran the ratchet straps for him, two at the front and two at the back, and checked them, and we drove back down into town. He dropped me at the house by noon. He didn't bring it up again, and the following Thursday in his kitchen he talked about the Rancher for an hour and never once mentioned that I hadn't driven it.

The house to the north of ours on Cedar Street belonged to a retired railway man who had worked forty years on the CN line, the last fifteen as a section foreman. His wife had died the winter before Maya was born. He was in his eighties and lived alone, and he walked with a cane once the ground froze. He had a long gravel laneway that ran from the street past the side of his house to a detached garage at the back, and the town plough left a ridge of packed snow across the end of it every time it went by.

The winter of 2003-04 was a heavy one. By the first week of December we'd had two good falls, and on the second one I looked out at seven in the morning, getting home off nights, and saw him at the end of his laneway with a grain shovel, working at the plough ridge a few inches at a time. I had a Toro snowblower I'd bought used two winters before for three hundred and fifty dollars, eight horsepower, twenty-four-inch cut. I got it out, did our driveway, and then went next door and did his, the whole length to the garage, and the ridge at the end, and a path to his side door.

After that I did it every morning after a snowfall through the rest of the winter. If I was on nights I did it when I got home at half past seven. If I was on days I got up at half past five and did it before I left. If I was off I did it when Maya went down after her first feed. I did ours first and then his. It took about twenty minutes for both. He came out the first few times and tried to give me a twenty-dollar bill, and then tried to give me a bottle of Crown Royal still in its bag, and I wouldn't take either, so he stopped coming out and instead stood at his side window and lifted his hand when I went by with the machine. I changed the shear pins once that winter, on his laneway, after the auger picked up a piece of the gravel the plough had thrown up.

On a Sunday in late January, at about nine in the morning, he knocked on our back door. It was minus twenty-six. His furnace had stopped sometime in the night and the house was down to twelve degrees. I went over with my multimeter and a flashlight. It was a mid-efficiency gas furnace, about ten years old, with a hot surface igniter. The inducer fan came on when the thermostat called, then the igniter should have glowed, and it didn't. I shut the gas and the power off, took out the igniter, and found a crack across the silicon carbide element. It read open on the meter.

I turned his oven on and left its door open, set up the electric heater from our basement in his living room, and drove out to the Canadian Tire on the highway, which was open on Sundays from ten. They had a universal hot surface igniter on the rack for forty-two dollars and change. I bought it, drove back, fitted it with the adapter plug that came in the package, turned the power and the gas back on, and watched it go through a full cycle: inducer, igniter glow, gas valve, flame, blower. I let it run through two more calls for heat to be sure. By noon the house was back up to twenty.

He asked what he owed me for the part. I said nothing. He said forty-two dollars wasn't nothing. I said it was a Sunday and the store was on my way home anyway, which it wasn't, and he let it go. I put the old igniter in a sandwich bag and left it on his kitchen counter so he could see what had failed, and he kept it there, next to the bread box, for the rest of the winter.

Claire's family held a corn roast every Labour Day at her parents' place out on the river road, on the far side of town. In 2004 there were about forty people in the yard: her aunts and uncles, her cousins and their children, people from her father's work, a couple of neighbours. They set up three folding tables end to end on the grass with paper tablecloths held down by thumbtacks, and her father boiled the corn in a galvanized washtub on a propane burner by the garage, three dozen cobs at a time, and the children ran between the tables. Maya was nineteen months old by then and had a nap at one o'clock every afternoon without fail.

We got there at noon. I parked on the road behind about fifteen other cars. I shook hands with her father and said hello to her mother and to the people Claire walked me over to, and held Maya, and then handed Maya to one aunt and then another, and ate a cob of corn standing up by the garage, and a hot dog. The yard was loud. Somebody had brought a horseshoe pit and set it up on the side lawn, and there was a group around it, and another around the washtub, and another at the tables, and each of the groups was larger by the time I'd been around once.

At about ten past one Maya started rubbing her eyes. I found Claire at the end of the third table with two of her cousins.

"She's due down," I said. "I'll take her home for her nap. You stay."

Claire looked at Maya and then at me. "You'll come back after?"

"I'll see how long she goes."

She said fine, and that one of her cousins could drive her home later. I put Maya in her car seat, folded the stroller into the trunk, and drove back across town to Cedar Street. She was asleep before we got to the bridge.

I carried her in and put her down in her crib, and she went on sleeping. I came downstairs. The house was quiet in the way it only was when she was asleep and Claire was out; the fridge ran, and the clock in the kitchen ticked, and next door the old man's storm door banged once when he went out to his garage. I made a pot of coffee. I sat at the kitchen table with it and the newspaper from the Saturday, which I hadn't got to. Then I went down to the furnace room and changed the filter, which was due, and wrote the date on the new one in marker. I came back up and wiped the counters down and sat at the table again.

Maya slept until twenty to four, which was long for her. I changed her and gave her a snack of crackers and cut-up peach in her high chair, and we sat in the kitchen together while she ate it. Claire's cousin dropped Claire off a little after six with a plastic bag of leftover cobs and half a sheet cake in a foil tray. She said her mother had asked where I'd gone. I said Maya had needed her nap. Claire said that was what she'd told her, and put the corn in the fridge.

Chapter 22

In 2006, the closure was announced on a Tuesday afternoon. I took the paper they handed out at the mill and folded it twice so it would fit in my jacket. It gave the shutdown dates and said information about severance would follow. The company name was at the top, with more space around it than the rest of the notice needed. I read it standing beside my locker. Then I put my lunch container in my bag and went home to Cedar Street. There was nothing on the paper that required me to stay.

That night I went out to the garage with a bottle and a glass. The garage still held the usual collection: winter tyres, lengths of lumber too good to throw away, a bench with a vice, and cardboard boxes that had softened at the bottom. I turned an empty compound bucket upside down and sat beside the bench. The concrete drew heat through my boots. Above the vice, a fluorescent tube had a dark patch at one end. I watched it while I poured.

I drank most of a bottle of rye in the garage the night the closure was announced, the start of the worst three years of my life.

I had no work laid out. A screwdriver sat beside a packet of washers, and I moved the packet away from the wet bottom of the glass. I read the closure notice again, flattening its folds with my palm. It did not improve with another reading. I put it beneath the screwdriver so it would stay flat. After a while I stopped using the glass and drank from the bottle. The cap rolled beneath the bench. I left it there.

Claire found me at two in the morning. She stood inside the door in her housecoat, pregnant with Theo, with one hand against the door frame. The light from the house reached across the step behind her. She looked at the bottle and then at the bucket. I told her I was coming in. She waited while I put the bottle on the bench and got to my feet. I switched off the fluorescent tube. The closure notice stayed under the screwdriver until the next day.

Kessler Paper had bought the mill in 2005. They held the first meeting in the cafeteria, where the tables had been pushed together and the chairs faced the serving counter. Two managers from head office stood at the front with folders. I took a seat near the back, keeping my work jacket on. Jordan sat beside me. Annie stood by the wall with her arms folded. There were coffee cups on the tables, but no one had cleared away the sugar packets or the rings beneath them.

The managers said the mill had a long future. They said new investment was coming. One spoke while the other turned pages, ready to supply a figure or repeat a phrase. They named capital projects: improvements to the controls, replacement equipment, work that would bring down operating costs. The descriptions were broad enough to fit on the sheet they gave us. There were no purchase order numbers, delivery dates or shutdown windows. I wrote the projects down on the back of the sheet anyway.

I had worked there since 1995. A new piece of equipment usually announced itself well before it arrived. There were drawings to check, power supplies to allow for, cable routes, fittings, mounting brackets and questions about whether something ordered in inches would have to connect to something measured in millimetres. Even a straightforward replacement could leave a trail of paper across the bench. The managers spoke as if all that work sat just beyond the cafeteria doors. I had seen none of it.

At the library I found the annual report and read it at a table. I kept my notebook beside it and copied figures rather than whole sentences. There were numbers for production, debt and costs, and there were paragraphs about making better use of what the company owned. I looked for the investment the managers had described. I turned back to the financial statements, checked the notes, then read the relevant pages again. The report gave me no reason to expect the equipment on my list.

Back at the mill, I checked what had actually been ordered. I started with the spare parts because those were easier to follow than a capital budget. Orders that should have been routine had stopped. A part used from stock left an empty place, and the empty place stayed. I checked the outstanding orders, then the requests waiting for approval. Some had dates old enough that I remembered writing them. They had not advanced. I put a mark beside each one in my notebook.

Then I went through the projects from the cafeteria meeting, one at a time. I checked for requisitions, approved orders and anything due at receiving. I checked whether the control work had produced drawings for us to review. I checked the replacement equipment for a supplier and a delivery date. None of it had been ordered. There was no crate coming by rail, no truck booked, no new unit waiting somewhere for us to make room. I crossed out nothing on the managers’ list. I wrote “not ordered” beside every item.

Jordan asked whether I thought the investment would happen. We were at the bench, with a pressure transmitter between us and its cover laid upside down to keep the screws together. I showed him the list. Annie read it over his shoulder. I said the parts orders had stopped as well. We finished checking the transmitter, put the cover back on and returned it to service. The list went into my jacket pocket. I kept it there through the rest of that year, folded behind the small notebook I used for dimensions and part numbers.

The Christmas party was at the Legion hall in December 2005. I went because Claire wanted to see her friends among the mill families. She had arranged the evening and asked me to put on a clean shirt. I drove. The roads had packed snow along the edges, and the parking area had been scraped down to a hard, uneven layer. I left enough space beside the car to open the door without putting it into the bank. Inside, wet boots had made a grey track across the entrance.

Kessler Paper had paid for the party. The tables were set close together, with paper coverings and folded napkins. Coats hung along the wall. Claire found the people she had come to see, and I stayed beside her while places filled around us. There were more chairs than gaps between them. Every time someone passed behind me, I had to pull my feet under the table. I had eaten and was holding an empty cup when the managers began collecting at the front of the hall.

I recognized the folders before they opened them. One manager checked the room while the other arranged papers. Chairs scraped as people turned to face them. Claire was talking with Annie, and I leaned close enough to tell her I was going home. She said she would stay. Annie said she would bring her back. I put my cup down and went along the wall to the coats. Mine had slipped behind another jacket. I worked one sleeve free, then the other, without pulling the rest onto the floor.

I left the company Christmas party before the speeches, gratefully, and drove home alone.

The car windows had frosted while we were inside. I scraped them, including the rear window and the small side panes, then put the scraper back on the floor. A ridge from the plough lay across the exit. I took it slowly and turned onto the road. At home I hung my shirt over the back of a chair and put the kettle on. Claire came in later with cold air in her coat. She said Annie had dropped her at the door. I asked whether she had enjoyed seeing everyone. She said she had.

In the weeks after the announcement, I went to Luc’s house in the afternoons. The first time, I left my jacket over a kitchen chair and sat down while he brought glasses. After that I knew where to put it. We used the same places at the table. A forty-ounce bottle stood between us, close enough that either of us could reach it without asking. Luc’s severance cheque was stuck to the fridge with a magnet. From my chair I could read his name, though not the amount.

The cheque had begun to curl at the bottom. Each time the fridge opened, its loose edge lifted and dropped. Luc had put it high enough to keep it clear of the handle. He said he would take it in. Then he poured. There were marks on the table where glasses had stood before ours, pale circles that showed when the surface caught the window light. We put our drinks on them. The bottle moved from the centre to his side, then back to the centre when he filled mine.

At first we talked about the mill in useful terms. We named equipment that could have kept running and repairs that had been held off. We went through the parts stores from memory. Luc would name a job, and I would tell him what had happened to the order. After several afternoons we covered the same ground without noticing where we had started. Sometimes we stopped in the middle of an account because the other one already knew it. The bottle gave us something to do with our hands between sentences.

Claire called my cellphone. I answered the first calls and told her where I was. Later I looked at the display and let it ring. I put the phone face down beside my glass. When it vibrated against the table, I moved it onto my folded gloves. That stopped the noise. I could still see a strip of light beneath it when another call came. Luc looked at it once, then at me. I reached for the bottle. We did not discuss the phone.

I stayed until the window above the sink showed the kitchen reflected back at us. The clock was behind my shoulder, and I had to turn to read it. I began leaving without checking. I put my glass in the sink, took my jacket from the chair and found my keys in the pocket. Outside, I cleared whatever had settled on the windshield. I drove home after dark. At the house I put the keys down carefully so they would not strike the counter. Claire could hear the door regardless.

There were afternoons when I intended to stay for one drink and left with no clear count of what I had taken. The measures had no marks. Luc poured to roughly the same height, but the glasses were different sizes. Once I noticed that and brought it up as if it were a fault we could correct. He exchanged my glass for another one. We kept drinking. The cheque remained on the fridge. Its white rectangle showed above his shoulder whenever he leaned forward to refill us.

At home, Claire asked whether I had heard the phone. I said I had. She asked why I had not answered. I took off my boots and set them on the mat with the toes against the wall. She waited for an answer that dealt with what she had asked. I said I had been at Luc’s. She already knew that. My jacket went on its hook, and the cellphone stayed in the pocket until the next morning. Its battery ran down there more than once.

My severance meeting took place at the mill, across a table from a personnel officer. The papers were laid out in the order they wanted them signed. I had brought my first pay stub from 1995 in my jacket pocket. I had kept it flat inside a folded sheet so the printing would not rub away. It gave the pay period, the hours and the deductions. The date was clear. I had checked it at home before leaving and knew exactly where it sat against the lining of my jacket.

The officer counted my service from the end of my probation. I watched the pen move down the calculation. There was my service date, there was the number of weeks, and beneath it was the gross amount. Counting from my hiring date would have added several weeks’ pay. I checked the dates again and did the subtraction on the edge of the paper they had given me. The difference did not depend on a rate or a complicated rule. It depended on which date they started with.

My hand went into my jacket pocket. I felt the folded sheet around the pay stub and kept it there while the officer explained the next page. I could have laid it beside their calculation. Instead I took my hand out empty. I asked which copies were mine. The officer separated them, tapped the bottom edges on the table and pointed to the first signature line. I signed where I was shown. The pen dragged slightly on the final page, and I went over the last part of my surname.

Losing the mill taught me that no paycheque is a promise.

I put my copies inside my jacket with the pay stub. The two sets of paper made a stiff patch over my ribs. Outside the office, I stopped to fasten the zipper because a corner had caught in it. I eased the paper clear rather than pulling harder. Then I went out to the car. Before starting it, I wrote the amount of the missing weeks in my notebook. I underlined the figure once and closed the book. The first pay stub went home without having left its folded sheet.

Jordan had been laid off with the rest of us. He came to the garage after he had taken a job in the Alberta oil sands. He had the job but not the money for the move. We stood beside the bench while he went through what he would need. He kept bringing the amount down by taking things out of his plans. There was a limit to that. The distance stayed the same, and he still had to get himself and his tools there before the work could pay him anything.

I had a spare rolling toolbox against the wall. One caster had a flat spot, but the drawers ran properly and the lock worked. We pulled it out and checked the bottom drawer, where a socket had lodged behind the runner. I tipped the drawer enough to free it and put the socket on the bench. Jordan said the box was worth something. I told him I knew that. I found the key in the shallow drawer where I kept loose keys and tried it twice before handing it over.

I gave him fifteen hundred dollars as well. I counted it on the clear end of the bench, keeping it away from the oil bottle and the filings beside the vice. He started to tell me when he could send it back. I told him not to pay it back. He said it again in a different way, with a first payday and an amount he could spare. I pushed the money towards him and repeated myself. Then we went back to the toolbox because he needed to know which caster stuck.

We rolled it over the joints in the concrete to see how it travelled. Empty, it was manageable. With tools in it, he would have to allow for the weight before moving it over a threshold. I showed him the latch that held the top drawer closed and gave him the spare key. He put both keys together. There was no more to settle about the money. When he left, a clean strip showed on the wall where the box had stood, and I moved a carton of fittings into the space.

After shutdown, the salvage contractor offered cash to mill hands willing to strip copper cable from the overhead trays. I was at the mill when Luc told me about it. He described the cable and where the runs went, though I already knew the route. The trays crossed above equipment and open stretches of floor. There was money in the copper, and the contractor wanted it brought down. The cash was for going up and getting it. There was no fall protection supplied with the offer.

I looked up at the trays from the floor. Cable ties still held the bundles where we had left them. In places, the copper ran past brackets and across gaps that could not be reached from a proper working surface. Removing it would mean cutting and pulling while keeping a footing on whatever happened to be underneath. I asked about fall protection so there could be no mistake about what was being offered. The answer did not change. I said I would not take the work.

Luc took it. He told me the cash would be useful. I said I would not go up the trays at any price. He looked along the run again and worked out where he would start. I stayed on the floor. There were cable ends to watch for even there, and I moved around them on my way out. At the door I wiped my boots on the mat out of habit, although the floor behind me no longer belonged to a place where I had a job.

I cleared my locker on a separate visit. I brought cartons and a marker, and I checked each piece of equipment before packing it. Some things on the bench belonged to the mill. I left those where they were. My own meters and testers had marks I recognized, receipts in the binders or serial numbers in my records. I matched them instead of relying on memory. Small leads and adapters went into bags so they would not settle beneath the cardboard flaps and disappear when I unpacked.

The locker held work gloves, old notes, a spare shirt and things that had become part of its corners. I took out the shelf liner and found a washer beneath it. A folded wiring sketch had slid behind the upright at the back. I checked both before deciding what to keep. The shirt went into a bag. The washer went into a carton with the small hardware. I wiped the shelf with the shirt’s sleeve, then remembered I was taking it home and folded the dirty part inward.

The calibration binders needed their own carton. They were heavier than their size suggested, with plastic sleeves, certificates, manuals and dividers that no longer lay straight. I carried that carton separately and set it flat in the car. The equipment followed in loads I could lift without shifting my grip. Before leaving, I opened the locker once more. The bare metal had pale rectangles where labels had been. I checked the upper shelf with my hand, closed the door and took the lock with me.

At home I cleared the garage in sections. The lumber went together along one wall, supported off the concrete. I moved the tyres and broke down the softened cartons. I kept a clear path from the door to the bench and measured the wall for shelving. The first measurements went on the back of an envelope. I transferred them to a sheet before cutting anything. Shelves full of test equipment would weigh more than the empty brackets suggested. I found the studs, marked their centres and checked them again.

I put up the shelving with the bottom shelf high enough to clean beneath. The screws went into sound wood. I set the heavier equipment low and kept the binders where I could read their spines without bending. Bins took the fittings, adapters, spare fuses and leads. I labelled the fronts before filling them, then changed two labels when I saw how much room the contents needed. Pressure fittings had one bin. Electrical connectors had another. Things waiting to be checked had a place of their own.

For the inventory I used ledger sheets with ruled columns. Every meter, gauge and tester received a line. I wrote the description, serial number and certificate date, turning each item under the light to read the plate. Some numbers had been stamped lightly. I used a magnifier for those and checked the certificate against the plate before writing. Where a certificate was missing or out of date, I entered that in full. A blank space could look like an oversight. I wanted the sheet to tell me what I actually had.

The pressure gauges took longer because I recorded the ranges as well. A gauge that went to twice the pressure I needed was not automatically suitable just because the needle moved. I checked the faces, threads and cases, then returned each one to its assigned shelf. Leads went beside the testers they fitted. I wound them loosely and kept the plugs from hanging over the edge. At the end, each instrument had a place and a line. I could stand at the bench with the ledger and find what the sheet described.

I began reading about metrology in the garage. I had used calibrated equipment for years and had filed the certificates that came with it. Now I read past the dates and the word “pass.” A measurement traced back through a chain of calibrations to a national standard. Each comparison depended on another comparison. I followed examples through the chain, writing the steps on graph paper until I could see what had been compared with what, and which part of the claim belonged to each instrument.

Every link added uncertainty. I worked through that with a pencil and a calculator, first using the figures in the examples and then the figures from my own certificates. The reference had an uncertainty. The instrument being checked had resolution and repeatability to account for. Temperature could matter. So could the method and the person making the reading. I made separate columns rather than putting everything into one allowance. When the units differed, I wrote the conversion out. A misplaced decimal could survive several lines if the numbers looked tidy enough.

I kept at the uncertainty budgets for weeks. The graph paper made it easy to line up the entries and just as easy to carry a wrong figure neatly down the page. I checked my sums from the bottom as well as the top. For repeated readings, I made room for all of them instead of entering only the average. I noted the garage temperature when I worked. On cold days I left equipment alone long enough to settle before taking readings. The time belonged on the sheet along with the result.

Annie came by and found the pages spread across the bench. She moved a bin so she could lean without covering the figures. I explained the chain to her, though she knew what a calibration certificate was. She asked what I planned to use as a reference, who would calibrate it and what I would do while it was away. I showed her the relevant certificates. She read the dates first. Then she asked how I would prove the equipment had stayed within its limits between one certificate and the next.

We argued about whether a one-man shop could be a link in that chain. Annie said a national standard did not make my garage a laboratory. I said I had not claimed it did. She pointed at the door, the concrete floor and the thermometer. I pointed at the temperature entries and the limits I had put on the work. She asked who would check my calculations. I said I could send a result out for comparison. She asked what that would cost. I had not put a figure beside that yet.

She made me show her the whole budget for one measurement. I took a clean sheet and copied the quantities, their units and the allowances I had used. Halfway through, she stopped me over a figure I had taken straight from a certificate. I went back to the certificate and read the statement beneath it. The figure needed treatment I had skipped on that page. I corrected it, recalculated the total and drew one line through the old answer so it could still be read. Annie stayed until the new total was written down.

Theo was born that year. When Claire brought him home, I cleared the kitchen table of the ledger and the calibration papers. I moved the whole stack instead of sorting it there. She put his things where she could reach them, took off her coat and sat with him. I stood beside the table until she told me to wash my hands. There was garage dust along the crease below my thumb. I used soap twice, cleaned beneath my nails and dried my hands on a clean towel.

She passed Theo to me with his head supported in the bend of her hand until I had him properly. His face was turned towards my shirt. One hand had worked free of the wrapping, with the fingers closing and opening beside his cheek. I sat because that made it easier to hold him without shifting him about. Claire adjusted the cloth at his shoulder. The papers I had moved were visible through the doorway, a square white stack on the garage bench. I left them there.

Later, while Claire attended to Theo, I carried the ledger fully out of the kitchen and put the certificates back in their binders. I had left a calculator beneath a folded cloth and found it by running my hand over the table. I checked the chairs and the floor for pencils. Theo’s things occupied surprisingly little space when they were folded and considerably more when they were in use. I cleared the counter beside the sink as well, then took the empty carton out to the garage.

The bottle from closure night was no longer on the bench, but a ring remained where I had set it. I wiped the surface and fitted a piece of clean board over the part where I worked with the meters. The bucket went beneath the lower shelf. I put the corrected uncertainty budget in a binder and wrote its date at the top. Inside the house, Claire called for a clean cloth. I washed my hands, took one from the folded pile and brought it to her.

Chapter 23

In 2006 I started a business in my garage on Cedar Street, because the mill had closed that spring and nobody else in Brule Falls had any use for an instrument technician. I was thirty-five. Theo had been born that winter, Maya was three, and Claire was home on her year of leave from the district hospital. My severance came to a little under thirty-eight thousand dollars once the government had taken its share. I put twenty-two thousand of it in a separate account at the credit union and told Claire that was the business and the rest was the house, and she said that was fine as long as the business knew it.

The garage was a single bay with a slab floor that sloped toward a drain the previous owner had never connected to anything. I built a bench along the back wall out of two-by-sixes and a sheet of three-quarter plywood, ran a new twenty-amp circuit off the panel, and hung two fluorescent fixtures from the joists. I registered the name Lacasse Calibration with the province for sixty dollars. The equipment came used, mostly from dealers in the south: a Fluke 744 documenting process calibrator, a pneumatic hand pump good to six hundred psi, a hydraulic hand pump good to ten thousand, and two digital reference gauges with certificates that still had seven months on them. All of it came to just over nine thousand dollars. I wrote each item in a hardcover ledger with the date, the seller, the serial number and the price, and put the receipts in an accordion file on the shelf above the bench.

The first real work came in July, a phone call from the maintenance superintendent at a sawmill an hour north. Their insurer wanted a documented calibration on every pressure gauge and transmitter in the plant before the policy renewed at the end of October: the boiler house, the steam headers on the dry kilns, the hydraulics on the carriage and the debarker, the compressed air, and whatever else was screwed into a pipe. He thought there were about a hundred and eighty gauges and fifty-odd transmitters. He asked me for a price over the phone, and I gave him one before I had hung up: twenty dollars a gauge, forty a transmitter, travel and certificates included, done by the twenty-seventh of October. It came to a little over six thousand dollars. He said yes so fast I should have known what I had done.

I said yes to the first calibration contract, foolishly underpriced, and worked every weekend to meet it.

The count was wrong to begin with. When I walked the plant with him the first Saturday, with a clipboard and a flashlight, there were two hundred and twelve gauges and sixty-one transmitters, and about a third of the gauges had never been off the pipe since they were installed. Some had glycerine gone brown behind the glass. Some had pointers stuck to the stop pin. A dozen on the kiln headers had been painted over along with the pipe, dial and all. I had priced twenty dollars to take a gauge off, test it, put it back and hand over a certificate. I had not priced the hour it took to free a fitting that had been sitting in steam since 1979, or the evenings typing certificates at the kitchen table. The superintendent did not mention any of that, and I did not ask him to change the number. I had given it.

The sawmill was weekend work because that was when their crew could shut down lines and lock them out for me. I asked for the lockouts in writing, a sheet for each line with their man's signature on it, and I didn't touch a fitting until I had the sheet in my hand. Their crew thought that was slow. I kept doing it.

So from the middle of July to the end of October, every Saturday and every Sunday, I worked on the sawmill. Saturdays I left Cedar Street at half past five in the Ranger with the pumps and the 744 in a plastic tote behind the seat and a thermos of coffee Claire had made the night before. It was an hour and ten minutes on the highway in good weather. I calibrated the transmitters in place with the hand pump and the 744 and pulled the worst of the gauges to bring home. Each gauge went into a milk crate with a strip of masking tape on its back, marked with the line, the location and the date. I wrote the same thing in the ledger before I left the parking lot. By the end of August I had six milk crates.

Sundays I worked in the garage. Claire had the children all week, and she needed a few hours on Sunday where nobody was hanging on her, so I took them out to the garage with me after breakfast. Maya sat on a piece of carpet I had laid on the slab, building things out of the offcuts from the bench: two-by-six ends, short pieces of plywood, the triangles left over from the shelf brackets. She would stack them as high as they would go, then knock them down and start again, and she told me about each one while she did it. Theo slept in his carrier on the floor by the electric heater, which I kept at the far end of the bench where she couldn't reach the cord. When he woke I gave him the bottle Claire had sent out with us, changed him on the bench on a towel, and put him back down. Some Sundays he slept two hours at a stretch. Some Sundays he did not sleep at all and I calibrated gauges one-handed with him on my shoulder, which takes longer than you would think.

The bench work was always the same. A gauge went on the hydraulic pump with an adapter, the reference gauge on the other port. I took it up through zero, a quarter, half, three-quarters and full scale, wrote down what it said against the reference, then brought it back down through the same points. If it read within tolerance it got a green sticker. If it was out and had an adjustable pointer, I reset the pointer and ran it again. If it couldn't be adjusted, it got a red sticker, and the sawmill bought a new gauge, which I tested before I put it on. Seventy-three gauges failed. That was seventy-three new gauges to test and seventy-three trips up a ladder or under a conveyor, none of which was in my price.

In August the garage was hot by ten in the morning and I kept the door up and a fan on the floor. In September it rained most weekends and the sawmill yard turned to mud the colour of coffee with milk in it. The first frost came the second week of October, and on Sundays I ran the heater from the time we came out until the time we went in. Maya wore her snowsuit on the carpet by then. She had moved on from towers to what she called roads, long rows of offcuts across the slab from the bench to the door.

I typed the certificates at the kitchen table on a used laptop with a template I had built myself. Two hundred and seventy-three of them. The inkjet in the spare room ran out of black cartridge twice. Claire sometimes sat across from me with tea and read the certificates back against the ledger, catching transposed numbers, because by eleven at night I read what I expected to see.

On Friday the twenty-seventh of October I drove the binders up, three of them, white, two-inch rings, with tabs for each area of the plant and a summary sheet at the front. The superintendent flipped through the first one standing at his desk and signed the delivery sheet. The invoice was six thousand two hundred and sixty dollars. The cheque came on the twenty-fourth of November.

That weekend I added up what the job had cost me in fuel, fittings, adapters, printer ink, a new set of flare-nut wrenches and lunches from the gas station on the highway, and then the hours from the ledger. It came to a little under seven dollars an hour. I showed Claire the page. She looked at it for a while and asked what I was going to charge the next one, and I told her, and she said that sounded more like it.

By then the garage was full. There were crates of instruments from a water treatment plant in the next township and from two small mines that had started sending me work, and the car had sat in the driveway since July. Claire wanted it back inside before the real cold. I wanted somewhere I could leave work on a bench overnight without a three-year-old building roads out of it.

The dairy had been closed since the eighties, a long cinder-block building on the gravel road at the edge of town, past the last streetlight. The front half was rented to a man who stored boats in it. The back half had been the cold room, the loading dock and a small office with a window onto the dock. The owner walked me through it on a grey Saturday at the beginning of November, the lights coming on one bank at a time. The cold room still had its insulated wall, about forty feet of it, faced with painted sheet steel, with a stainless rail along it at waist height where they had hung the milk cans. The floor had drains that went somewhere. The office had a steel desk he said I could keep.

He asked six hundred a month, heat and hydro included. I said that was fine, and we shook on it on the loading dock. That night at the kitchen table I wrote it in the ledger: rent, back half of dairy building, $600 per month including heat and hydro, agreed by handshake, Saturday November 4, 2006. I wrote it the same way I wrote everything else, because the ledger was where things went.

I took the first two weeks of December to fit out the workshop. The benches went along the old cold-room wall, because it was the straightest wall in the building and the insulated steel held the same temperature all day, which mattered more than anything else about the room. I built three of them, eight feet each, with laminated plywood tops on welded steel frames from a fabricator in town, and bolted them to the floor so they wouldn't walk. The milk-can rail stayed where it was. I hung hoses and test leads off it.

Under and beside the benches I put steel drawer cabinets, bought used from a closed machine shop, and labelled every drawer by instrument type with a label maker: pressure gauges, pressure transmitters, differential pressure, temperature transmitters, RTDs, thermocouples, switches, controllers, flow, analyzers, fittings by thread, adapters by thread. The NPT fittings had one drawer, the metric another, and the BSP that came on some of the European equipment a third, so nobody would ever force one into the other.

Every customer instrument got a numbered tag the moment it came through the door. I bought a thousand aluminium tags stamped from 0001, with steel wire loops, and hung the box on a nail inside the dock door. When an instrument came in, before I put it down anywhere, I wired a tag to it. Then it went on the bench marked incoming and I opened the ledger to the next line. It was a new ledger, ruled in columns I drew with a pencil and a steel rule: tag, date in, customer, description, manufacturer, model, serial, range, as-found, as-left, date out, certificate number, invoice number. One line per instrument. If I couldn't fill a column I wrote a dash so I would know I had looked. The first line was tag 0001, a two-hundred-psi gauge from the water treatment plant, in on the eleventh of December.

At the end of the month I bought a 2001 Chevrolet Express with two hundred and eight thousand kilometres on it and a little rust over the rear wheels, from a plumbing contractor in Sudbury, for eight thousand four hundred dollars. I spent three evenings building it out, with plywood drawers on full-extension slides along the driver's side and a shallow bench with a vise on the passenger side. The van drawers had the same labels as the workshop drawers, in the same order from front to back as the workshop ran from left to right. If I needed a half-inch to quarter-inch NPT adapter on a mine site, I went to the same drawer I would have gone to at the bench. I kept a box of tags behind the driver's seat and a thin ledger on the dash, and copied its lines into the main one when I got back.

The first rent was paid on the first of December, six hundred dollars by cheque. In the second month, on the eighth of January, the owner came by in the afternoon. It had been minus thirty-one that morning and was still minus twenty-four. He stood by the dock door with his hat on and said the heating was running higher than he had figured, with me in there every day and into the evenings, and the rent would have to be seven-fifty from February. He said it like it had been part of the deal and he had forgotten to mention it.

The ledger was open on the desk where I had been entering the morning's work. The line from November was two pages back, in my hand, with the six hundred and the date and the word handshake. I did not turn back to it. I told him seven-fifty was fine. He said good and went out to his truck. That evening I wrote underneath the November line: rent increased to $750 per month from February 1, 2007, owner's notice January 8. I paid the seven-fifty on the first of February and every month after, and never said anything to him about it.

I still needed a deadweight tester. The mines had hydraulic equipment running to five and ten thousand psi, and their engineers wanted those gauges checked against a piston and weights. In February I found a used one through a dealer in southern Ontario, a hydraulic tester with a full set of stainless weights in a fitted wooden case, for three thousand two hundred dollars. He emailed me photographs and a scan of the certificate, which was from a calibration lab in Toronto. He said on the phone it was from last spring and good for another year at least. I paid by wire and it came on a pallet two weeks later.

The tester looked right. The piston turned freely and the weights were stamped with serial numbers that matched the certificate. Before I used it for anything I phoned the lab, whose number was printed at the bottom of the certificate, and read the woman who answered the certificate number, the serial number of the piston and the date. She put me on hold for about four minutes. When she came back she said they had never issued a certificate with that number, that their numbers for that year started with a different prefix, and that they had no record of an instrument with that serial. I asked her to send that in writing.

The letter came by fax the next afternoon on the lab's letterhead. I phoned the dealer and read it to him. He said there must be some mix-up at the lab. I said that might be, that I was sending the tester back in its crate at his expense, and that I would expect the money within ten business days. It went out on a freight truck the next morning. The money came back on the eighth business day, all of it. I wrote the purchase and the return on two lines one under the other, with the fax stapled to the page. In April I bought a different tester from a different dealer for three thousand nine hundred, and before I paid I phoned the lab on its certificate and read them the number.

A bottle of rye lived in the bottom drawer of the workshop desk. I had brought it out from the house the week I moved in, about two-thirds full, and when it was empty I bought another at the liquor store on the way home and put it in the same drawer. There was a heavy short glass in there with it, and I washed it in the utility sink in the morning.

Most evenings when the work was done I sat at the desk and poured a drink. I would put the last instrument on the outgoing shelf, sweep the bench, put the tools back in their drawers, turn off the bench lights and leave the desk lamp on. Then I opened the bottom drawer. I entered the day's lines, checked the certificates against them, made up the invoices for anything going out in the morning, and wrote the next day's list and put it on the first bench. By then I had usually had three drinks, sometimes four, rye with a bit of tap water, no ice. I put the bottle back, locked the dock door, and drove the van home, four minutes on the gravel road and then three blocks.

Claire would be at the door, or near it, when I came in, sometimes holding Theo, sometimes getting Maya out of the bath. She smelled it on me before I had my boots off. Most nights she didn't say anything. A few times she said I was late, and once in March she said, "You had a few," not as a question. I said I had had a couple at the desk doing the books. She said all right, handed me Theo, and went to finish the dishes. The bottle stayed in the drawer.

The mines were where the money was. There were a dozen of them within three hours' drive, with thousands of instruments on calibration schedules. The two that were sending me work had each told me that their head offices were moving to accredited labs for anything that touched safety or environmental reporting, and that I would have to be accredited or they would have to stop. That meant ISO 17025, the international standard for calibration laboratories, assessed by the national body out of Ottawa. In January I decided the shop would be accredited, and I sent away for the application.

The application fee alone was eleven hundred dollars. I needed a quality manual, a written procedure for every type of calibration on my scope, control of documents so nobody could use an old version, records of the room conditions, traceability for every reference back to a national standard, an uncertainty budget for every measurement, and procedures for complaints and for work that went wrong. I wrote it all through February at the workshop desk and the kitchen table. The assessor came for two days in the middle of March.

He was a retired metrologist, careful and slow, who drove up from Ottawa in a rented car and took a room at the motel by the highway. He spent the first morning reading my quality manual at the desk and the afternoon in the ledger. On the second day he watched me do a gauge on the deadweight tester from start to finish, then asked me to show him, for each number on the certificate, where it came from. At four o'clock he sat at the desk and wrote out his findings by hand on carbonless forms. There were thirty-one of them.

Some were small. I had fixed two ledger entries with correction tape, where the standard wanted a single line through the error, initialled and dated, so the original could still be read. The room thermometer had no certificate of its own. Some were large. My uncertainty budgets used the manufacturer's specifications for my references and nothing else: no repeatability, no resolution, no allowance for the room. My procedures said what to do but not what to do when something went wrong. I had no record of having checked the tester's weights after shipping. He went through the list item by item and told me all of it would have to be closed before he could recommend me. He shook my hand at the dock door and drove back to Ottawa.

I spent the next eight months rewriting. The customer work went on in the daytime, because the customers paid for everything, and in the evenings, after the ledger and the invoices, I worked through the findings one at a time. I rewrote the pressure gauge procedure four times and the transmitter procedure three. I set up a document register, one sheet with every controlled document, its version, its date and my initials, and pulled every old copy out of the drawers and the van into a box marked superseded. I logged the room temperature and humidity at eight and four every working day.

For the uncertainty findings I redid the measurements. I ran the reference gauges against the deadweight tester ten times at each test point, on different days and at different times of day, so I would have a repeatability number that was mine and not the manufacturer's. I did the same with the 744 against reference resistors and a voltage source I sent away twice for calibration. I weighed the tester's weights on a balance at the assay lab of one of the mines, which they let me use on two Saturday mornings. Every measurement had its own sheet, and the sheets went in binders on the desk next to the ledger. By the end there were three binders.

I sent the evidence to Ottawa in three batches, in May, August and October. In November the assessor came back for one day, reread everything, watched me do a transmitter and a gauge, and asked me again where each number came from. I showed him the sheets. At three o'clock he signed off the last of the thirty-one findings and wrote his recommendation. The certificate came in the mail a month later, with Lacasse Calibration typed on it and my scope on the second page: pressure, temperature, electrical signal.

In hindsight, the business was the only place where I let myself be stubborn.

I framed the certificate and hung it above the desk, and faxed copies to the two mines, and they sent me their schedules for the following year.

That spring, while I was still working through the uncertainty findings, I had ordered the international guide to the expression of uncertainty in measurement, which everyone in the field called the GUM. It came as a thick paperback from Geneva and cost more than a hundred dollars with shipping. I read it cover to cover over about five weeks at the workshop desk, a few pages a night, working the examples on a pad beside it to see if I got the same answers. Some nights I read the same page four times.

The idea was simple once I had it. Every measurement had an error you could never know exactly, and the job was to put an honest number on how far off you might be and show how you got it. Some inputs came from measuring the same thing over and over and doing the statistics, which they called Type A. Some came from anything else you knew, a certificate, a specification, the resolution of a display, which they called Type B. You combined them by the root of the sum of the squares and multiplied by a coverage factor, usually two. The guide was careful to say that Type A was not better than Type B, only different.

In the back of a metrology newsletter I read how the volt and the ohm were kept now. At the college I had learned the volt by way of the ampere and the ampere by the force between two wires. That was still true on paper. But since 1990 the national labs had kept the volt by the Josephson effect, a superconducting junction that gave a voltage tied exactly to a frequency and two constants of nature, and the ohm by the quantum Hall effect, a resistance that came out the same in any lab that did it right. My voltage source, when I sent it away, was compared to something that was compared to a chip in a cryostat in Ottawa, counting microwave cycles. I printed the article and put it in the binder with the uncertainty sheets.

In June an engineer at one of the mines phoned about my certificates for a set of differential pressure transmitters on a scrubber stack. He wanted to know why my uncertainty was larger than the manufacturer's spec sheet. The sheet said plus or minus 0.075 percent of span. My certificates said 0.11 percent at a coverage factor of two. He said the manufacturer had tested those transmitters, the spec sheet was the measured uncertainty, and he wanted my number to match it so his reports would be consistent.

I said the spec sheet was the manufacturer's claim about the model, written before his transmitter had ever been on a pipe. It was a fair Type B input for what a new one should do, but it was not a measurement of his transmitter. My number came from his transmitters, on my bench, against my references, with my repeatability and my room in it. He said my references must be worse than theirs, then. I said they might be, and that was in the number too, and I read him the budget line by line. He said the guide allowed a manufacturer's specification. I said it allowed it as an input, to be combined with everything else you knew. We went around it for an hour. In the end he said he would put my number in his report with a note, and I said that was what the number was for.

I kept turning it over for days afterward. He was not entirely wrong. The manufacturer had tested thousands of those transmitters and knew how they drifted better than I did, and that was knowledge, the same as my repeatability was knowledge. The question was what it was knowledge of. I worked the budget three different ways at the desk in the evenings, once with the spec sheet as the only input, once without it, once with it as a rectangular distribution, which the guide recommended when all you had was a limit. The third way gave me a number very close to my first. I wrote a short note explaining it into the procedure and left the certificates as they were.

In April I had a call at the workshop from the widow of a millwright I had worked with at the mill. He had died in February, at sixty-two. He had kept forty years of tools in the basement and a shed behind the house, and she wanted them gone so she could sell and move closer to her sister. Some men had been around offering her a hundred dollars for the lot, and she didn't know if that was fair. He had always said I was honest about money.

I spent three weekends at her house. The first I went through everything and listed it in a notebook, the way I would have walked a plant. There were two Starrett toolboxes of precision tools: micrometers to six inches, inside mics, dial indicators with magnetic bases, a vernier height gauge, a machinist's level, and a set of gauge blocks in a wooden case. In the shed there were wrenches to two and a half inches, torque wrenches, pullers, a drill press, a bench grinder, and taps and dies put away oiled. Maya came with me one Saturday afternoon and sat at the widow's kitchen table drawing, and the widow gave her cookies and a glass of milk.

The second weekend I cleaned everything and tested what could be tested. I checked the micrometers against the gauge blocks, and the gauge blocks against my own at the workshop. Two mics were out, and I adjusted them and noted it. I checked the torque wrenches on my tester and put a card in each case with the readings. I ran the drill press and the grinder twenty minutes each to listen to the bearings. The third weekend I priced every item from the classifieds and the dealers' used prices, came down about a fifth for a quick sale, and typed up a list.

I ran an ad in the regional paper for two weeks, put the list on the board at the supplier's counter in town, and phoned the men I knew who would want the precision tools. Nothing went for less than the list price. The gauge blocks went to a machine shop in Sudbury for eight hundred dollars. The total was four thousand two hundred and sixty dollars, which I gave her as a cashier's cheque with the list of every item, its price and its buyer stapled to the envelope. She wanted me to take something for my time. I told her no. She sent a pie to the house the following week, and Claire cut it for dessert.

That summer the town got a business association. A few shop owners on Main Street had started it after the mill closed, and they sent a letter to every business with a licence, which was how they found me. They held a breakfast the first Tuesday of every month at the curling club, seven o'clock, ten dollars, with a speaker. In September I went.

The lounge had three long tables set end to end, with paper placemats and coffee in steel carafes, and about thirty people at them, most of whom I knew by sight from the bank or the post office. I sat at the end of the last table, nearest the door, next to a man who sold insurance. There were eggs, bacon, toast and hash browns. A man from the economic development office in Sudbury talked about grants for twenty minutes. Then the chair stood up and said they would go around the room and everyone would give their name, their business and one thing they hoped for this year. It started at the far end of the first table. I finished my coffee, left a ten and a toonie under my saucer, and went out to the van. I had a gauge on the tester by half past eight. They sent the letter again in October and November, and then they stopped. I didn't go back.

By the end of 2007 the ledger had reached tag 1388 and the van had two hundred and forty thousand kilometres on it. The certificate hung above the desk, the bottle was in the bottom drawer, and most evenings I sat between them with the lamp on and entered the day's lines before I drove home.

Chapter 24

In late 2008, two mines cancelled their contracts with Lacasse Calibration, and my work dropped by half. One cancellation arrived before I had finished putting away the equipment from the previous job. The other came while I was making up the next month's schedule. I drew a line through each booking, then rubbed out the travel days I had set aside on either side. There was enough room left on the calendar to write the grocery list.

At the workshop in the old dairy building, I had a bench, a desk, shelving and a cot. The rent did not change when the calendar cleared. Neither did the insurance or the cost of keeping the van on the road. I went through my accounts and separated what I could stop buying from what I had to keep paying. Calibration certificates, reference equipment and fuel stayed. I put off replacing a drawer cabinet whose runners had worn through. A strip of hardwood under the bottom drawer kept it level.

I made a list of businesses within driving distance that used pressure gauges, temperature controls or anything else that needed its reading checked against a known standard. Most had never heard from me. I wrote down the telephone numbers, called, and entered the answers beside them. A blank space meant I had not got through. A line meant there was no work. A date meant I had a reason to put fuel in the van.

I drove four hundred kilometres a week to customers I had never called on before. Some visits produced a single gauge. Some produced nothing I could invoice. I carried my certificates in a folder with clear sleeves, along with a sheet listing the work I could do. The folder rode on the passenger seat until a corner began to curl from the heater. After that I kept it flat under the seat.

The van collected road salt along the lower doors and a ridge of frozen slush behind each wheel. Before leaving a yard I knocked the ridges off with the handle of the snow brush. At the workshop I brought the reference equipment inside every night. I could leave an empty case in the van, but I could not leave a pressure reference out in that cold and expect to use it immediately the next morning. Bringing everything in took two trips. Taking it out took two more.

I added safety-valve testing to the services on my sheet. That meant more than adding a line with the typewriter. I worked through the procedures, checked what the reference equipment would cover, and made record sheets for the set pressure, the opening pressure and the result. A valve had to come off the equipment and be isolated before I would test it. I recorded the identification from its plate before doing anything else. I kept the completed sheets with the certificates, not loose in the van.

The driving continued through 2009. I billed the work, entered the mileage and went back when the next checks came due. A customer with three gauges could become a customer with six, but only after the first three had been done and the paperwork returned. By the end of that year the books were back where they had been before the cancellations. There were more names in the customer file and more kilometres on the van. The total at the bottom was about the same.

In January 2009, the widower next door showed me the final notice on his heating bill. We were outside our houses on Cedar Street. He held the paper by one corner while he worked a glove back onto his other hand. The notice had been folded into quarters, with the amount due visible on the outside. I read it standing beside the packed ridge left by the plough.

The amount was not a month's bill. There were arrears, a current charge and a date by which the whole balance had to be paid. I checked the date twice. The account number was printed above it. He folded the paper again and pushed it into his coat pocket. I asked to have it for the afternoon. He handed it over without opening it again.

I took it to the workshop and laid it beside my own unpaid bills. The utility used heavier paper than most of my suppliers. I flattened the folds with the side of my hand and copied the account number onto an envelope. Then I checked my bank balance in the ledger. There was money there for fuel, rent and the bills already entered. Paying his account would leave less between those amounts and the balance, but it would cover the notice.

I went to the utility office myself. I brought the notice and paid the full amount against the account printed on it. I waited for the receipt and checked the number before putting it in the envelope. There was no reason to leave my business name on anything, so I did not use a Lacasse Calibration cheque. Outside, I zipped my coat before taking my gloves from the pockets. The van's windshield had begun to frost along the bottom while it sat.

I returned the notice with the receipt folded inside it. I pointed out the line showing that the balance had been paid. That was the whole explanation. Back at the workshop I entered the withdrawal as personal money. It was not fuel, materials or a business expense, and I did not put it under one of those headings to make the column look better.

I paid a neighbour's heating bill that January without telling my wife, which was generosity and secrecy in equal measure.

At home, Claire had left her boots on a piece of newspaper near the door. I put mine beside them and hung my coat up with the empty envelope still in the pocket. At supper we discussed when I would need the van the following day. I gave her the times. The receipt stayed with my papers at the workshop, behind the bank statement for January.

That winter Luc Gagnon wanted me to go snowmobiling with the old mill crew. They were doing the lake trail runs every Saturday. He gave me the starting time as though the only thing left to settle was whether my machine would start. I had an old snowmobile sitting at home, covered and taking up space. I had not put fuel in it for the season.

Luc came to the workshop when I was sorting fittings into a shallow steel drawer. He stood by the desk and described the route. I kept the pipe threads separate from the machine threads while he talked. He said I could bring the old machine out and see how it went. I said I would sell it to him instead.

He asked what I wanted for it. I gave him a price. He named a lower one, and we settled between the two. I told him how long it had been sitting and what I had last done to it. There was no point including the cover in the price and then trying to sell that separately. I said he could take the cover too.

We dealt with it at the house. I pulled the cover back so he could look it over. The skis had left two narrow marks where the snow had packed beneath them. I brushed the loose snow off the seat and lifted the hood. Luc checked what he wanted to check. I stayed beside the machine and answered him. I did not take it onto the lake to demonstrate it.

He paid me, and I wrote out a receipt with the make, the identifying number, the amount and the date. I made a second copy for myself. The pen would not write properly against the cold surface of the seat, so I finished the receipt inside. I put the money in an envelope and gave him the papers that belonged with the machine.

He asked once more whether I wanted to come along on a Saturday. I said no. Selling him the snowmobile had not been a way of arranging to borrow it back.

After he took it away, I folded the empty cover straps that had been left on the ground and put them with the other loose hardware. The space beside the house was clear except for the marks under the skis. On Saturday I stayed home. There was no machine to uncover, no fuel can to fill and no starting time to meet. Luc had the receipt and the snowmobile. I had the money entered in my personal account.

My father was dying at the district hospital in the spring of 2009. I went there from the workshop with my hands washed and a clean shirt under my coat. There were places around my nails where the dirt stayed after soap and a brush. I cleaned those with the corner of a towel before leaving. At the hospital I folded my coat over the back of the chair instead of putting it on the bed.

Armand had become small inside the hospital bedding. The blanket lay over his feet without much shape beneath it. His hands were outside it. I had known those hands with a pencil behind one ear and a handful of change being counted into the other palm. On the bed they had nothing to carry. One rested near the edge of the sheet, with the fingers slightly bent.

I sat where he could see me without turning his head. There was water within reach, though he was not reaching for it. I moved the chair closer when I found myself leaning forward to hear him. We did not discuss the stock at the store or the state of my accounts. Those subjects had taken up a fair amount of our time when he was able to sit across a counter from me.

On the last night I was alone with him. I checked the time when I arrived and then left my watch under my sleeve. The chair had a hard front edge. After a while I shifted forward and planted both feet flat on the floor. A wheel on the bed was turned sideways. I looked at the rubber tread and the metal lever above it, then at his hand on the sheet.

His breathing left intervals in which I could hear the ventilation. I did not try to fill them with news from the workshop. The van was outside, the next day's papers were in a folder, and there was nothing from either place that needed to be brought into that room. I put my hand beside his. The sheet was tucked tightly enough that it did not move when I rested my wrist on it.

I said a Hail Mary in French, once, the way I had learned it as a boy. I spoke quietly and went through it from beginning to end. Then I remained in the chair. I did not start it again.

There was a little water on the table under the cup. I wiped it with a tissue and put the tissue aside. When his fingers moved, I moved my hand nearer. I could see the bones at his wrist where the sleeve had pulled back. The cuff was too wide for him. I straightened it without lifting his arm.

He died at the district hospital that spring. The last night stayed with me in separate pieces: the turned wheel, the water under the cup, his wrist, the edge of the chair against my legs. I had sat there until there was no further reason to keep the chair in that position. When I stood, I held its back for a moment while I got my coat off it. I checked the pockets for my keys before leaving the room.

After the funeral I went to Luc's house. His wife had moved out. The kitchen table had more empty space around it than I remembered, though there were still two chairs close enough to use without moving anything. Luc put a bottle of rye on the table. I had brought one as well. We each kept our own bottle beside our own glass.

I took off my coat and folded it over the chair. Luc opened his bottle first. I broke the seal on mine and set the cap beside it. We did not measure the drinks. When my glass was empty I put more in it. At first I put the cap back on the bottle each time. Later I left it where it was.

We spoke about the store. I said there would be stock to count. Luc said there was always stock in a hardware store that nobody remembered ordering. I told him my father had invoices. That led to the old mill stores and what could be found in a drawer after the label had worn off. We stayed with things that had part numbers and quantities for as long as the conversation held there.

The bottles stood between the glasses and the centre of the table. Luc turned his slowly by the neck when he talked. I kept mine near my right hand. There was a ring beneath the bottom of my glass. I moved it once and made another ring. Neither of us fetched anything to wipe the table.

We each went through a bottle of rye. By the time mine was empty, I had stopped putting it down carefully. The base struck the table each time I set it there. Luc's bottle was empty too. We left both standing. I found the cap under my sleeve and screwed it back on mine, though there was nothing left to keep in.

I did not drive. I left the van where it was and walked to the workshop. I had to hold my key still against the lock before I could get it in. Inside, I left my shoes near the cot and lay down in my clothes. The cot frame made its usual short scrape on the concrete when I put my weight on it.

I slept there that night and the next. The blanket was one I kept folded at the foot, and it did not cover both my shoulders and my feet unless I bent my knees. In the morning I drank water from the workshop tap and sat at the desk with the ledger closed. My coat lay over the back of the chair, with one sleeve turned inside out. I pulled the sleeve straight before putting it on. The second night I took my shoes off before sitting on the cot.

Later in 2009, a buyer from out of town offered to take Lacasse Hardware with its stock. He put the stock at forty thousand dollars at cost. I looked at the shelves behind the counter and then at the bins underneath it. Forty thousand was a round number for that many separate things. I asked what count he had used. There was nothing I could check against the invoices.

Denis and I spent a weekend counting. We started on Saturday morning at Main Street with pencils, pads and the supplier invoices. I brought a calculator and a scale for the loose nails. Denis cleared a space on the counter. We put completed sheets on one side and invoices still to be checked on the other. The first rule was that nothing went onto the completed pile until both the quantity and the cost had been entered.

The nail bins took longer than the shelves. Each size had its own compartment, and a few nails had crossed the partitions. We picked those out before weighing. I checked the empty weight of the container, counted a sample and used that to check the quantity by weight. Where the quantities were small, we counted them by hand. Denis read the figures back while I entered them. A bin label was not enough to establish what was inside it.

There were nails in opened cartons below the bins as well. We pulled those out and counted them with the stock above, then marked the cartons so they would not be counted again. The floor behind the counter had a line of dust where the boxes had stood. By noon there were pencil marks on the sides of my fingers and small bits of cardboard caught in the cuffs of my trousers.

We counted every box of hinges. Some boxes were full, some held two where the label said three pairs, and some contained loose screws that had to be matched to the right packet. I opened them rather than multiplying the number of boxes by the number printed on the end. Denis stacked the checked ones with their labels facing out. When we reached the end of a shelf, he ran a finger along it and checked that we had not left a small box flat behind a larger one.

Every tin of varnish went onto the sheets too. We checked size and quantity against the supplier invoices. A small tin and a large tin could have nearly the same label. I read the capacity from each one, and Denis found the line on the invoice. We counted what was there at cost. We did not use the retail price from the shelf ticket or assign a value from what either of us thought somebody might pay.

On Sunday we worked through the remaining shelves and checked the additions. Denis took my sheets and added them without looking at my totals. I did the same with his. Where the figures differed, we went back to the shelf or the invoice. One multiplication had been entered in the quantity column. Another total had missed the last line on the page. We corrected both in pencil and initialled the changes.

The stock came to sixty-seven thousand dollars. I wrote the total on the cover sheet and put the calculator tape beneath it. Twenty-seven thousand separated that count from the buyer's figure. We left the checked stock where it belonged and put the invoices back in order. Before locking up, I walked along the bins once more with the sheet in my hand. There was nothing further to add. I turned off the lights above the counter and carried the count home.

The buyer made a second offer. It was higher than the first and still well under the sixty-seven thousand we had counted. By then we were into the latter part of the year. At my mother's kitchen table, I put the count sheets in front of me with the cover page on top. Denis sat with the offer. Hélène had cleared the table far enough that none of the papers had to overlap.

I had brought copies of the invoice pages that accounted for the larger amounts. They were clipped behind the count. The nail sheets were separate because there were more of them. I squared the piles against the edge of the table while Denis read the terms again. A corner of the cover sheet had softened from being handled. I pressed it flat with my thumb.

Denis said they should accept it. Hélène said she wanted the store done with. She had the figure in front of her and did not ask me to explain the count again. Denis put his finger beside the amount on the offer. He said the buyer would take the stock as it stood. We would not have to empty the shelves and sell it a box at a time.

I thought we should wait for spring. The store could stay shut through the worst of the winter, and the stock would still be there when the roads cleared. There were costs to that, and I had those on a separate sheet. I could have put that sheet beside the offer. I could have shown what another few months would take out of any higher price. It remained under the count.

Hélène moved the document towards Denis. He checked where she had to sign and set the pen down pointing towards her. I watched the nib make a small dot when she rested it on the paper before starting. Her name took less room than the line provided. She moved her hand away and waited while Denis turned the page.

I said nothing. When they needed a witness, I drew the paper towards me, checked the space and signed. I printed my name underneath in the same small letters I used on calibration certificates. Then I put the date where it belonged and returned the pen.

The count sheets had not changed while we sat there. They still totalled sixty-seven thousand. I unclipped the copies of the invoices and put them back behind the cover page. Hélène collected the signed papers. Denis checked that none had been left under my stack. I took the count away with me in its folder. The sheet about waiting through winter was still underneath it, without a mark added at the kitchen table.

That autumn, a customer's data loggers kept drifting out of time. The readings themselves were usable, but the time stamps did not stay together. I set the units beside one another at the workshop and checked the clock settings before leaving them to run. When I came back, I compared the records. A sequence that should have lined up across the units had spread apart.

I noted the amount of drift and how long each unit had run. I checked the settings again, including the date, the sampling interval and the way each logger kept its time. There was no use correcting a temperature reading if I could not say when it had been taken. I put the results in a table and started reading about clock correction and synchronisation.

That brought me to satellite navigation clocks. I knew the receiver needed accurate time to calculate a position. I had not known that the clocks in orbit required correction because time ran at a different rate there. I read the explanation once, then went back to the beginning and checked what was being compared. Motion made one difference. Being higher in the gravitational field made another. The two did not cancel.

The combined difference was about thirty-eight microseconds a day. I wrote that down as a fraction of a second, then worked through what light travelled in that time. It was a large distance for such a small number. The correction was part of making the system work. It was not an adjustment for a poorly made clock.
