I turned it over for a week. Between jobs I went back to the pages I had printed and followed the arithmetic again. The loggers on my bench still had an ordinary clock problem. Nothing about their location in the workshop required a correction for orbit. I kept those notes separate from the customer's test sheets, but I continued reading after I had enough information for the work.

At supper I explained it to Maya, who was six. I said that a clock travelling high above the earth did not keep exactly the same time as one on the ground. She asked how far behind it got. I said that in the navigation satellites, after the effects were combined, it would get ahead without correction. I used the edge of my plate for the earth and held one finger above it. That established the positions, though it did nothing for the numbers.

Claire said it could not be true. A second was a second. I said the clocks could both work properly and still disagree after following different paths. She said then something had to be wrong with one of them. I said that was what I had first gone back to check. The corrections had to be included or the positions would be wrong.

We argued it across the table while Maya finished eating. I fetched the page with the daily difference on it and put it beside Claire's plate. She read the number and asked how anybody checked a fraction that small. I started explaining the comparison of clocks. The food on my plate cooled while I talked. Later, I folded the page along its old crease and took it back to the workshop with the rest of the notes.

The store sale closed near the end of 2009. That night I sat at the workshop desk with my own year-end ledger and my father's last one. I had brought his from the store with the papers that were being kept. The cover had worn smooth at the corners. There was a pale mark near the spine where a label had once been stuck. I set it to the left of mine and opened it to a completed month.

His columns were narrower than the ones I had been using. He had left just enough room for a date, an account and the amount. He ruled the divisions in pencil, pressing hard enough that the lines could be felt from the other side of the page. The account names were printed above them. He did not give a heading more room merely because it was a long word.

I took fresh ledger sheets and a ruler from the drawer. My pencil was blunt, so I sharpened it over the waste bin and brushed the loose graphite from the point with a scrap of paper. I measured the first page against his and marked the column widths. Then I ruled every account and labelled each one in pencil, using the columns he had used.

I redid my year-end ledger from the records, not from the final totals alone. Each receipt and payment went into the new columns. I checked the invoices against the deposits and the expenses against the bank statements. Fuel occupied more lines than it once had. There were the workshop payments, equipment costs, insurance and the small purchases that had come home in the same bag as an invoice. I kept the personal withdrawals apart.

The heating payment appeared where I had originally entered it, among the money I had taken personally. The snowmobile money was not business income. I checked both against the statements and moved on. A total could agree with the bank and still have the wrong things inside it. I worked down the pages with the ruler under the line I was copying, shifting it after each entry.

Outside, the van had cooled enough that the windshield was opaque at the corners. I could see it through the workshop window when I stood to stretch my legs. I stayed inside, put water on and returned to the desk. The cot was folded back against the wall. A box of test fittings sat beneath it where I had put them to clear the floor.

I added the new columns twice. The second time I worked from the bottom upwards so I would not repeat the first addition in my head. The figures agreed. After the two lost mine contracts, the new calls, the safety-valve work and the driving, the business had finished the year back where it had been. I wrote the final figure and ruled beneath it. Then I put the receipts in their envelopes and arranged the envelopes in order.

My father's last ledger had a few loose papers inside the back cover. I left them there. I closed it, closed mine and put them upright together on the shelf above the desk, his beside my own.

I see now that every ledger I kept was an argument I was having with my father.

I put the ruler in the drawer and laid the pencil beside it. There was graphite on the heel of my hand. I washed it off at the sink, dried my hands and checked that the tap had stopped dripping. Before leaving, I took the next day's folder from the desk and put it with my keys. The two ledgers stayed on the shelf.

Chapter 25

In 2010 Lacasse Calibration made money for twelve months running, which was the first time I could say that without leaning on one good quarter to carry a bad one. I had eleven regular customers in January and nineteen by December: two mines, the sawmill in the next town down the river, the municipal water treatment plant, a pair of contractors out of Sudbury who did shutdown work, and a scatter of small outfits with a pressure gauge or a thermometer they needed paper for. I billed a little over seventy-one thousand dollars that year and kept about thirty-eight after the rent on the workshop, the van payments, the insurance and the recertification on my reference standards. Claire was doing three twelve-hour shifts a week at the district hospital and picking up a fourth when they asked. Between the two of us the mortgage on Cedar Street went out on the first of every month, and in Brule Falls four years after the mill closed, that was more than a lot of people on our street could say.

The workshop was the back third of the old dairy building at the edge of town. The floor was poured concrete that sloped to two drains, from the days when they hosed it down every night, and when it rained hard the place still gave off a faint sour smell that no amount of bleach took out. I had built my benches from two-by-sixes and three-quarter plywood and covered the tops with hardboard I could replace when I burned or gouged it. Pressure on the left wall, temperature in the middle, electrical on the right, and a long table by the overhead door for intake and shipping. The landlord charged me six hundred and fifty a month and left me alone. I was in by seven and out by half past five. During the day I worked steadily, the work came out right, and the customers came back.

The nights were another matter.

By that spring I was going through a bottle of rye every two days. I kept it in the garage at home, in a grey plastic bin on the second shelf of the steel rack by the side door, and the bin had a strip of masking tape on the front that said FITTINGS in black marker in my own printing. There were fittings in it too, a layer of brass elbows and compression nuts on top, and the bottle lay underneath on its side. I bought Wiser's in the 750 millilitre bottle, around twenty-six dollars at the time. For a while I bought it at the liquor store in town, and then, when I noticed I was in there more often than the woman at the till could have missed, I started buying it at the one in the next town on my way back from the sawmill, two bottles at a time, and paying cash.

The routine did not vary much. Supper, dishes, the kids in the bath, Theo into his pyjamas, Maya's chapter of whatever book she was reading out loud to me, lights out by eight-thirty. Claire usually went to bed early on the nights before a shift. I put on my jacket, went out through the back porch and across to the garage, and pulled the string on the bulb over the bench. I had an old aluminum lawn chair with green webbing and a plastic milk crate beside it for a table. In the cold months I ran a small electric heater off the bench outlet and kept my boots on. I poured the first glass about two fingers deep in a heavy tumbler I had taken from the kitchen and never brought back, and I sat there.

Sometimes I had something on the bench to look at: a gauge from work, a radio-controlled car of Theo's with a stripped steering servo, the carburetor off the lawnmower. More often I just sat. The garage had one small window facing the lane, and the street light at the end of the lane came through it at an angle and lay across the floor. I could hear the furnace come on in the house, and the odd transport down on the highway. I know now that I was drinking to keep the noise of those years at a distance. The mill going to Kessler Paper and then going to nobody, my father's funeral, the bank manager across his desk the day I asked for the line of credit, the months in 2006 when I didn't know whether I would make the rent on the dairy building. All of it was still there in the evenings, and the rye pushed it back out to where I didn't have to look at it straight.

I drank until the glass and the evening were done, usually around midnight, sometimes one. Then I rinsed the tumbler under the outside tap, put it on the shelf behind the bin, switched off the heater and the bulb, and went in through the porch. I took my boots off on the mat and carried them in my hand. I knew which stairs creaked and stepped over them. I looked in on the kids, pulled Theo's blanket back up because he kicked it off every night, and got into bed on my side without turning on the lamp. In the morning I was up at six and at the workshop by seven, and as far as I could tell nobody at the workshop knew any different.

Maya turned seven on the twenty-second of April. Claire had planned a supper at home, just the four of us, because Maya had had her party with the girls from her class the Saturday before. Spaghetti and meatballs, which was what Maya asked for, and a chocolate cake from the bakery on Main Street with her name on it in pink icing. Claire had told me about it twice that week and written it on the calendar on the fridge. Supper at five-thirty.

That afternoon Luc Gagnon called the workshop around three. Luc had been a millwright at the mill for twenty-two years. Since the closing he had done some contract work at the mines and some snowplowing in the winters, and at the time he was between both of those. He said his furnace was short-cycling and would I come by and look at the thermostat on my way home. I said I would. Luc lived in half a duplex on the other side of the tracks, about six minutes from the workshop. I got there a little after five. The thermostat was an old mercury-bulb one that had been knocked off level when somebody screwed a coat hook into the wall beside it. I levelled it and set the heat anticipator, and that took ten minutes. Luc had a case of beer in the fridge and a bottle of rye on the counter, and he poured two.

I am not going to make it more than it was. I knew what day it was. I had a drink, and then I had another, and Luc talked about the mill and about the men we both knew who had gone out west to the oil, and at some point I looked at the clock over his stove and it was ten to eight. I drove home. I should not have been driving.

The house was quiet when I came in. The cake was on the counter with a knife beside it and a quarter of it gone, and the seven candles were laid out on a paper towel next to the box, burnt down and pulled. The dishes were done and the pot was upside down in the rack. Claire was sitting at the kitchen table with a cup of tea, and she did not get up.

She had it organized. She had been a nurse for fifteen years and she charted things, and she had charted me. She went month by month, starting in January. In January, the night Theo had the croup while she was on a night shift, and the neighbour girl who was supposed to be helping me called her at the hospital because I was asleep in the garage chair and hadn't heard Theo or the phone. In February, the recycling bin, which she had started counting, and the morning she found the tumbler on the porch rail with ice frozen in the bottom. In March, the parent-teacher interview I had said I would go to and didn't, and the Sunday I slept until one in the afternoon and Maya had sat on the stairs waiting for me to take her skating. In April, the Tuesday she had come out to the garage at midnight to ask me something about the insurance and I had answered her in a way she said she would not repeat in front of the kids' drawings on the fridge. And today. She did not raise her voice. She had her hands flat on the table on either side of the cup.

Then she told me what had to change. There would be no bottle in the house or the garage. I would be home at six every night unless I phoned first. I would go and see a doctor at the clinic, not anybody she worked with, somebody I picked, and tell him the truth about how much. And if I could not do those things, she said, then we would be having a different conversation, and she would not be the one starting it.

I stood by the sink the whole time with my back against the edge of the counter. I still had my jacket on. I looked past her at the window over the table, where the kitchen was reflected and the two of us in it, and I did not say anything. I did not have anything to say that would have been true and would also have helped. When she finished she waited, and I still didn't answer, and after a while she picked up her tea and drank some of it.

I went out to the back porch and put my boots on and crossed to the garage. I pulled the string on the bulb and sat down in the lawn chair. The bin was where it always was.

In May I was at the water treatment plant doing the annual work on their chlorine analyzers and level transmitters, and one of the operators there was also a volunteer with the fire department. We were standing on the walkway over the clarifier while I waited for a reading to settle, and he mentioned that their gas detectors had been gone for five weeks. I asked where. He said they sent them to a service company in the south, because that was who the supplier had told them to use, and it took four to seven weeks round trip by courier, and while they were away the trucks went out with whatever was left on the shelf. Some years that had been one working meter for the whole department. He said they had gone into a basement on Pine Street in March on a suspected gas leak with no meter at all, and had to stand in the driveway and wait for the gas company.

I asked what kind they were. He said four-gas, the little yellow ones that clipped on the coat. Oxygen, combustibles, carbon monoxide and hydrogen sulphide. I had done that kind of thing at the mines. On the way home I stopped at the fire hall on the highway and talked to the chief, who was in the bay with a hose and a brush, washing down the pumper. They had six detectors. Two were still in the south. Of the four on the shelf, one had a cracked display and two had sensors that were past their stated life and had been for over a year. The department's budget for equipment service was whatever the town council gave them, and the council in those years was not giving anybody anything.

I told the chief I would calibrate them. He asked what it would cost and I said it wouldn't. He said the council would want an invoice and I said I could send one with a zero on it if that helped. When the other two came back I took all six to the workshop on a Saturday. I had a cylinder of four-gas mix already for the mine work, but I bought a second one for the department so I could keep the lot numbers separate, about three hundred and twenty dollars, and a demand-flow regulator to suit. I ordered replacement sensors from the distributor in Mississauga, two oxygen and one hydrogen sulphide, about a hundred and seventy dollars each, and paid for them on my own card. I swapped the cracked display for a good one off a parts unit I had from a mine that had retired its old meters. I bump-tested each detector, did the full calibration, zeroed them in fresh air by the open overhead door, logged the as-found and as-left readings, and printed a certificate for each with my name and the date on it, the same as I would for any paying customer. I drove them back to the hall on the Monday at noon and put them on the shelf myself.

From that year on I did them every spring. I put the department in the ledger with a rate of zero and kept the sensor receipts in a separate brown envelope in the desk drawer, and when a sensor came up short I replaced it and added the receipt to the envelope.

The thing that changed the business most that year was a drawer of cards. Up to then I had run on memory and on the customers calling me. Most of them called. Some didn't, and in June the sawmill had an insurance audit and found that four of the pressure transmitters on their dry kilns were three months past due, and the maintenance manager phoned me in a poor temper wanting to know why I hadn't reminded him. I said it wasn't my job to remind him. He said the fellow from Sudbury who used to do them always had. I did the transmitters that week, and when I had shipped them back I sat at the intake table and thought about it, and he was right, or right enough to cost me the account the next time it happened.

I bought a two-drawer steel card cabinet from a used office furniture place in Sudbury for forty-five dollars, and a thousand four-by-six index cards from the stationer. I made a card for every instrument a customer had ever sent me, going back through the ledgers to 2006. Each card had the customer's name and phone number across the top, then the tag number, the make and model, the serial number, the range, the date of the last calibration, the interval, and the due date written large in the top right corner. I filed them in the top drawer behind twelve dividers, one for each month, by due date. I put a coloured tab on each card for the kind of instrument: red for pressure, blue for temperature, green for flow, yellow for gas detection, white for electrical. That way I could open the drawer and see at a glance that February was heavy in blue and March was nearly all red, and plan the reference standards and the bench space around it.

There were three hundred and eighty-some cards when I finished, which took most of two weeks of lunch hours and slow afternoons. Every Monday morning after that I came in at seven, made a pot of coffee in the percolator on the side bench, opened the drawer, pulled that month's cards and sat down at the intake table with the phone. I worked down them one at a time. I called each customer who had something coming due, told them what it was and when, and booked a date for pickup or drop-off. I wrote the booking on the card in pencil and stood it up in a wooden tray on the desk until the instrument came in. When the job was done I erased the pencil, wrote in the new dates in ink, and filed the card forward. Some Mondays it was six calls and some it was thirty. By the end of the summer the empty weeks I used to have in the work were mostly gone, and the sawmill manager never brought up the kilns again.

In July one of Claire's friends from the hospital, a nurse she had worked nights with for years, had the summer barbecue she put on every year at her house on the hill on the north side of town, where the managers from the mill used to live. It was a big lot with a deck across the back, a lawn running down to a cedar hedge, and a double garage off the side. Claire wanted to go and the kids wanted to go because there would be other kids and a wading pool. It was thirty degrees and humid, with a white haze over the sky that never turned into anything. Claire drove, as she did by then whenever we went anywhere in the evening.

There were forty people or more in the yard when we got there, nurses and their husbands and a few of the doctors, and kids running through the sprinkler. I got a beer from a cooler on the deck and stood near the barbecue for a while. The host's husband was at the grill and he said, to nobody in particular, that their sump pump had quit in the big storm in May and was sitting on his bench in the garage, and he had been meaning to buy a new one. I asked him if he minded me having a look. He said help yourself, and went back to his sausages.

The garage was ten degrees cooler than the yard. The pump was a one-third horsepower submersible with a vertical float on a rod, and it had been sitting there long enough to have dried mud caked on the screen. The float had slid down the rod and jammed against the switch housing, so the switch never closed. When I pulled the bottom plate off, there was a small stone and a good handful of grit wedged in the impeller. The cord had a nick in the jacket just above the strain relief, likely from being dragged over the edge of the pit. I found a socket set on the shelf that was missing its ten millimetre, so I used a nut driver from his drawer instead. I cleaned out the volute and the impeller, freed the float, reset the stop collar at the height the manual on the box called for, and wrapped the nick in the cord with electrical tape and wrote REPLACE CORD on a piece of masking tape and stuck it to the handle. Then I filled a five-gallon pail from the garden hose at the side of the garage, set the pump in it, and plugged it into the outlet by the door. It came on when the float rose and shut off when the water dropped, and I ran it through that six times, refilling the pail each time, and it did the same thing every time.

That took the best part of two hours. Claire came to the door once with a paper plate of potato salad and a burger and set it on the bench beside me without saying anything. Maya came in to show me her freezie and stayed a while watching the pail fill. A couple of the men drifted in, looked at the pump, asked what had been wrong with it, and went back out. When I came out at last it was nearly nine and the light was going, and the host's husband shook my hand and said I'd saved him two hundred bucks. We collected the kids and their wet towels and left.

Theo fell asleep in his booster seat before we were off the hill. Maya was asleep a few minutes after. Claire drove with both hands on the wheel and didn't say anything until we were going past the rink.

"Two hours, Ray," she said. "In a garage. With a pump nobody asked you to fix."

"He said it had quit."

"He said it to the air. He wasn't asking you." She stopped at the light by the church, although there was nobody coming. "You're antisocial. That's what it is. You're antisocial."

In truth I was still the life of every party, whatever my wife said. I looked out the side window at the closed-up storefronts on Main Street and did not argue it. She turned onto Cedar Street and parked in the driveway, and I carried Theo in and she carried Maya, and when they were both in bed I went out to the garage.

In October one of the shutdown contractors from Sudbury came back with a flow meter. He was not one of my regulars, and he had sent it in by courier in September: a two-inch turbine meter that I had run on the small gravimetric rig I'd put together the year before, a pump and a tank and a platform scale, good up to about that size. Now he drove up in his own pickup, carried it in, and set it down on the intake table. He turned it over and pointed at the outlet flange, where there was a crack running out from one of the bolt holes. He said it hadn't been there when it left his yard, it must have happened in my shop, and he wanted a new one. The list price on a new one was about twenty-three hundred dollars.

I said I would have a look at my records. Every instrument that came into the workshop went on the intake table first, before anything else was done to it. I photographed it on all sides with a small Canon digital camera, with a paper tag showing the job number in the frame, and I wrote a line in the ledger: the date, the customer, the description, the serial number, and the as-found condition, including any damage. I got the ledger down off the shelf over the desk and opened it to September. The line for the fourteenth read: 2 in. turbine meter, the serial number, and then, in my own printing, hairline crack at outlet flange approx 15 mm from bolt hole, customer advised by phone. Beside that I had written the time I called his office and that I had left the message with the woman who answered.

Then I opened the job folder on the computer at the end of the bench and brought up the intake photos. There were eight of them. The fourth one was the outlet flange, with the job tag clipped to the meter body at the edge of the frame, and the crack was in it, the same length, from the same bolt hole. I zoomed in on it and turned the screen toward him.

He looked at it for a while. He said nobody in his office had told him. I said I could print the photo and the ledger page if he wanted them for his file, and he said he would take them. I printed them both, put them in an envelope with the meter's certificate, and he picked up the meter and the envelope and left. His cheque for the calibration came at the end of the month.

In November the temperature bath gave out. I had bought it used in 2007, a stirred liquid bath from a lab in Sudbury that was closing, for thirty-four hundred dollars. The circulator motor's bearing seized, the motor overheated before the thermal cut-out tripped, and it took the controller board with it. I called the manufacturer. They no longer made the board and had no used ones. A new bath with the stability I needed was quoted at ninety-four hundred dollars plus freight and duty, and the line of credit had about three thousand left on it. Temperature work was a fifth of my billing, and the blue tabs in the card drawer were thick for January and February. I sent out what I had to send that month to a lab in the south, at a loss on every job, and started building.

The first one I put together in December. I bought a twenty-litre stainless stockpot from the restaurant supply in Sudbury for eighty-five dollars and set it in a plywood box packed with two inches of rigid foam all round. I put in a thousand-watt immersion heater, an off-the-shelf PID controller for a hundred and sixty dollars, and a solid-state relay to switch the heater. For the stirrer I used a small gear motor on a bracket with a propeller I cut and bent from a piece of aluminum plate. I filled it with a mix of water and propylene glycol, put my reference thermometer in it and let it run. It would not hold temperature. It swung up and down by two-tenths of a degree on a slow cycle about four minutes long, and the bottom of the pot ran three-tenths colder than the top. The heater was far too big for the volume, the stirrer was too slow to move the liquid where it needed to go, and the lid was losing heat as fast as the heater put it in. I spent three Saturdays retuning the controller and it got no better.

For the second one I drew up a rectangular tank in two sections, a stirred well and a working well with a baffle between them, which is how the commercial ones are laid out, and had a sheet metal shop in Sudbury fold it from sixteen-gauge stainless and weld it. That cost three hundred and ten dollars. I had them weld a half-inch drain fitting into one bottom corner so I could empty it without tipping it. I bought a small used chiller from a dental lab for four hundred dollars and ran a coil of copper tubing through the stirred well so the bath always had a little cooling pulling against it. I split the heating into two: a six-hundred-watt heater on a switch to bring it up to temperature, and a two-hundred-watt trim heater on the PID. That one held much better. Overnight it stayed within three-hundredths of a degree. On the Monday morning in late January I came in and there was a puddle under the box and the foam on that side was soaked through with glycol. The weld at the drain fitting had a pinhole in it, and it had wept all weekend. I drained it, pulled it out of the box, threw out the foam, and sat down at the bench with the graph paper.

The third tank had no holes in it below the fluid line at all. I had the shop make it again without the drain, and I emptied it with a siphon hose after that. I moved the control sensor up next to the outlet of the stirred well, where the liquid had just come past the heater and the coil, instead of in the working well where it had been lagging. I made a new lid from two layers of plywood with foam between them and cut it so it fit around the thermometer ports with a quarter inch to spare. I put a heavier motor on the stirrer and turned the propeller speed up. I retuned the controller from scratch, with less gain and a longer integral time than I had thought it would want. In March I set it at fifty degrees, put my reference platinum resistance thermometer in the working well, hooked the readout to the laptop to log a reading every minute, locked up and went home. When I came in the next morning I let it run through the day as well. Over twenty-four hours it held to a hundredth of a degree. I printed the graph and pinned it to the wall above the bench. All three tries together, counting the chiller and the parts I threw away, had cost me about sixteen hundred dollars.

That spring I was at one of the mines doing the transmitters on their grinding circuit in the concentrator, the density gauges and the flow transmitters on the cyclone feed. The plant had just put in a new advanced control system, and one of their process engineers, a young man two or three years out of school, showed me the screen in the control room while I waited for a permit to be signed. It had the mill, the pump box and the cyclones laid out with targets and limits and trend lines running forward into the next half hour. He said it was basically magic. He said it knew what the mill was going to do before the mill did it.

I didn't say anything to him then. I went home and ordered a textbook on model predictive control through the bookstore in Sudbury, a hundred and thirty-eight dollars, and it took two weeks to come. I read it at the bench at noon with my lunch and in the garage at night with the glass on the milk crate beside me, with a pad of graph paper on my knee. It took me several weeks to get through the parts that mattered, and some chapters I read twice.

In May I went back to the mine to finish the job, and the engineer was in the control room again. I told him I had been reading about his magic. What it had, as far as I could see, was a model of how the circuit responded, built from step tests they would have done when it was commissioned, so much change in density for so much change in water, over so many minutes. It used the model to predict where everything was going to go over the next stretch of time, worked out the set of moves that kept those predictions inside the limits, made the first move, and then took a fresh measurement and corrected the model's error before it did the whole thing again. It was a process model with feedback on top. A good model and a lot of arithmetic, done fast, but not magic. And if the model was wrong, or if one of the transmitters feeding it was reading wrong, it would make the wrong moves just as confidently as the right ones.

He said the vendor didn't describe it that way. I said I expected not. He said it was still doing better than the operators had done on manual, and I said that might well be true. He went back to his screen and I went back to the density gauges.

I kept turning it over afterward at the bench in the workshop. I sketched step responses on graph paper, first-order curves with dead time in front of them, and worked out by hand roughly what that controller would do if one of the density gauges I calibrated for them drifted two percent high over a month. As near as I could figure, it would quietly add water to the circuit to bring the false reading down, and keep adding it, and the grind would go off and nobody would see why on the screen. I wrote that down in my notebook and the date beside it, and I moved their density gauges from a twelve-month interval to six on the cards.

The fall fair was the second weekend of September in 2011, at the fairgrounds down by the river. Maya was eight that year and Theo was five. It was cool for September, about fourteen degrees, with the wind out of the northwest and grey cloud moving fast over the grandstand. There was the midway along one side, the cattle and poultry barns at the far end, the pie tables in the exhibition hall, and that year the fair board had brought in a company with a zip line. They had put up a scaffold tower at the west end beside the grandstand, and a cable ran from the top of it over the midway and the rides to a landing platform on a pole near the barns. It was twelve dollars a ride.

The kids saw it from the parking lot and that was the rest of the afternoon decided. There was a lineup at the bottom of the tower, mostly kids with their fathers, the fathers in harnesses with the straps hanging off them and the kids pulling at their hands. Theo measured himself against the painted post at the gate and came in about an inch over the line. Maya took my sleeve.

"Come with us, Dad," she said. "Everybody's dad is going."

"Come on, Dad," Theo said.

I went to the ticket table and bought three tickets, thirty-six dollars, and gave them to Claire. She looked at the tickets and then at me, and then she put them in her jacket pocket.

"Your mother's going to go with you," I said.

I took the coats. Maya's purple jacket, Theo's blue fleece with the zipper that stuck, and Claire's windbreaker once she had her harness on. They went up the scaffold stairs with the attendant, Claire behind the kids with a hand on each of them on the turns, and I went down along the edge of the midway and stood near the landing platform at the far end, by the barns, with the coats over my arm.

Maya came first. She came down the cable over the Ferris wheel and the ring toss with her legs straight out in front of her, and the attendant at the bottom caught her and unclipped her. Theo came next, sitting in the harness with both hands on the strap, shouting the whole way across. Claire came last. When they were all down and out of the harnesses, Maya came over and took her jacket from me and put it on.

"You should have come," she said.

"Somebody had to hold the coats," I said.

Theo wanted to go again. Claire said once was enough for twelve dollars. I handed Claire her windbreaker and got Theo's zipper up for him, and we walked back along the midway to get fries from the truck by the exhibition hall. Claire drove us home.

Chapter 26

In the fall of 2011 I drove to Luc Gagnon's house after closing the workshop. There had been rain in the morning, and the road was still wet along the shaded edge. I parked behind his truck and left enough room for him to get out. He was on the porch with a cooler beside his chair. Water from the lid had made a dark patch on the boards. I stood at the bottom step until he moved his boots from the other chair.

I told him I was done drinking. I had worked out the words in the van, though there were only six or seven of them, depending how I put it. On the porch I used the short version. Luc lifted the cooler lid and held out a beer by its neck. The label had gone soft in the water. I said no.

It is plain to me now that stopping drinking was the one hard thing I did entirely for myself.

He kept the bottle out for another moment, then rested it against his knee. He asked whether I meant that evening or altogether. I said altogether. There was a can of pop lying sideways between the bottles. I pointed to it, and he handed it over. I wiped the top with my sleeve before opening it. The can was cold enough that I put it on the porch rail between drinks.

Luc said I could still come over. I said that was why I had come over. He put the beer back in the cooler and took out another for himself. We talked about the rain and the work I had in. A pressure calibrator needed a seal, and I had been waiting for the right part instead of fitting one that was nearly right. Luc asked what a seal could cost. I told him the seal was cheap. Getting the specified one into my hand cost more.

The porch boards had started to soften around one of the posts. I could see the screw heads sitting below the surface where the wood had swollen. Luc shifted his chair clear of the damp patch. He drank his beer, and I finished the pop. I did not give him an account of what I had drunk that week or the week before. He did not ask for one. When he reached into the cooler again, I said I was heading home.

It was still light when I started the van. I put the empty can in the passenger footwell, where it would not roll under a pedal. Luc stood in the porch doorway with one hand against the frame. I backed out slowly because his truck cut off my view of the road. On the way home I stopped at the workshop to check that I had locked the side door. The handle held. I drove on to Cedar Street before the street lights came on.

One Sunday that fall, Claire's brother-in-law offered me a flight over the lake. He had bought a two-seat ultralight and had room for one passenger. We had the truck there, with Maya and Theo beside it. The ground was firm where we parked and soft farther over, where the tyres had left dark tracks. I kept the children on our side of the truck while he showed us the machine.

There was not much to look at between the seats and the ground. I could see tubing, cable ends, and fasteners with marks across them. He explained where I would put my feet and how the belt fastened. I listened, then said no thanks. He said we would only go over the lake and come back. I said I would stay with the kids. That settled my part in it.

Claire said she would go. She put what she had in her pockets into the truck and handed me the keys. Her brother-in-law helped her with the belt. Maya wanted to know whether they could talk once the motor was running. Theo wanted to know which wheel left the ground first. I told them to watch from where we stood and keep clear of the open ground. Theo put his hands against the truck's front tyre until I told him there was mud on it.

They moved away from us, turned, and took off. I kept one hand on Theo's shoulder while he leaned forward to see. Maya stood against the truck with her arms folded, following them until the machine had become small enough that she asked which bit was Claire. I said I could not tell from there. The wind lifted the loose corner of a blanket in the truck box. I tucked it under the rest of the blanket.

We waited by the truck. Theo asked whether I would go when they came back. I said no. Maya said that meant there would be no second turn to wait through, and asked whether we could have the things Claire had brought to eat. I got them out and divided them on the tailgate. We kept the wrappers in the bag. There was a patch of water under the truck from the ground, and Theo stepped around it each time he changed sides to look.

When the ultralight came back, we stayed where we were until the motor had stopped. Claire unfastened the belt and climbed out. Her hair had worked loose at the side. She said she had seen the lake almost end to end. Maya asked whether she could see our truck. Claire said she could, once she knew where to look. I gave her the keys and put the food bag behind the seat. We had to get the children home, and I had work laid out for Monday.

In the spring of 2012 Claire told me at the kitchen table that she wanted to separate. She had already seen a lawyer, Ms. Wynne. The table had been wiped after supper, but there was still a wet line beside the salt. Claire put a sheet of paper in front of her and laid both hands on it. I sat in my usual chair. The dishwasher had not been started, and I could hear water moving in the sink drain when she pushed her chair closer.

She said she had made the appointment before telling me because she wanted to know what had to be done. I asked whether Ms. Wynne had given her the sheet. She said some of it was from the appointment and some was what she had written herself. I left it on her side of the table. I did not argue with her or ask her to stay. She had several things to say, and I let her get through them.

She said she would keep the house. I said all right. She said the children would be with each of us on alternate weeks. I agreed to that too. She went through the points separately, leaving a space after each one for me to answer. I answered each one before she went to the next. I did not try to make the house conditional on the children, or the children conditional on anything else.

If the separation taught me anything, it is that you cannot calibrate another person.

We talked about where I could live and what the children would need there. A room would not do. I needed two bedrooms and somewhere for their coats and school things. Claire said they should not have to carry everything they owned back and forth. I said I could get beds and keep clothes at my place. She made another note. The paper caught under her hand, and she flattened it before she wrote.

The children were elsewhere in the house while we spoke. Once, Theo's feet crossed the floor above us and stopped. Claire waited, then continued. She said we would have to tell them together. I agreed. She asked whether I had anything to say about the arrangements she had set out. I asked how soon she wanted me to find a place. She said soon, once there was something suitable. I said I would look for something on Main Street if there was anything available.

She turned the paper towards me when she had finished. I read it from top to bottom. There were practical details still to settle, but the main parts were there: the house, the weeks, the move. I put the sheet back where she could reach it. She asked if I wanted a copy. I said yes. Then I got up and started the dishwasher. I had to move a bowl from the top rack before the door would shut. Claire remained at the table while the machine filled.

The apartment I took was over a shop on Main Street. It had two bedrooms, a kitchen, and one closet. The stairs came up at the side, with a turn halfway that made a long item awkward to carry. I measured the rooms before moving anything. The smaller bedroom could take two beds if I left the wall under the window clear. I wrote the measurements on the back of the sheet with the rent and deposit amounts.

I packed my half of the house into plastic bins. I bought the same size so the lids would fit whichever bin was open. On each one I put a number in black marker, large enough to read from the van door. Inside each lid I taped an inventory sheet. The tape went along all four edges so a loose corner would not catch when I lifted things out. I kept a separate list in a folder, with the bin numbers down the left side.

Kitchen things took longer than clothes. I had to decide which items belonged with me without emptying a drawer Claire would open the next morning. I took what we had agreed on and crossed it off the list. I wrapped drinking glasses in tea towels and put heavier things at the bottom. The bins with books were only half full. A full one would have been a poor load on the stairs. I filled the space above the books with folded clothes.

I carried the bins down to the van in number order. At the apartment I carried them up in the order I needed them. The turn in the stairs required me to set each bin on the landing, move my feet, and pick it up again. I did not stack two in my arms. By the last trip there was a grey rub on the wall from a lid. I wiped it with a damp cloth before putting the empty cleaning bucket under the sink.

The first night I built shelves in the closet. I had measured it twice because the back wall was not square with the sides. I cut the shelf boards to the smaller measurement and fitted cleats to the wall. Where I could find a stud, I used it. Elsewhere I used anchors suited to the wall. I checked each cleat with a level before driving the second screw. The drill, square, tape, and pencil all fitted on a piece of cardboard outside the closet door.

I labelled every shelf. Maya and Theo each had a shelf they could reach without standing on anything. Above those were towels, sheets, and household supplies. My work papers went higher, clear of the children's things. I wrote the labels on white tape and folded over a small end so I could remove them without scraping. The shelf for sheets was still empty when I finished. I vacuumed up the drilling dust, put the tools back in their case, and ate at the kitchen counter.

The children came on alternate weeks. Their beds took most of the smaller room, just as the measurements had shown. Maya tested the space between them by walking through with her arms at her sides. Theo wanted the bed nearest the door. I put their folded clothes on the labelled shelves and showed them where the spare towels were. On the inside of the apartment door I fixed a short list: keys, school bags, lunches. We used it before going down the stairs in the morning.

The accreditation reassessment fell in the same month I moved out. Lacasse Calibration still occupied the rented part of the old dairy building. I had procedures to review while bins stood against the apartment wall waiting to be emptied. At the workshop I cleared one end of the bench for the reassessment files and put ordinary work at the other. I could not afford to let the invoicing stop while I checked the paperwork that allowed me to keep doing the work.

I worked sixty-hour weeks that month. I wrote the hours down because I was dividing my time between paid jobs, check measurements, procedure review, and the move. Some evenings I stayed until the outside door had cooled enough to draw moisture on its inside edge. I kept the room thermometer where it belonged and recorded the conditions. Turning up the heat to make myself comfortable would not have shortened the settling time for the standards already on the bench.

I read every procedure from the first line to the last. I checked the revision number against the master list, the equipment numbers against the equipment, and the intervals against the records. A sentence that said to allow something to stabilise had to say how I established that it had stabilised. A result needed units. A correction needed its sign. I used a pencil on my working copies and made the final changes only after I had checked the references.

Then I redid every check measurement. I cleaned the connectors before using them and let the electrical standards settle after handling. On pressure work I brought the test point up in the prescribed direction, waited, and recorded what the reference and the item under test actually showed. If I overshot, I went back and approached it again. I did not write down the number I expected to see. The uncertainty calculations had their own sheets, with the sources carried through in the same units.

I worked through the temperature checks after the room had settled. Leads lay clear of the edge, and the probes went to the specified depth. I checked the records for the reference thermometers against the identification on their stems. There were enough nearly identical pieces in the workshop that relying on their appearance would have been foolish. I entered the check results, compared them with the limits, and signed each page. Finished pages went into the file immediately, rather than into a pile to sort later.

At the apartment I washed work shirts and made room for the children. At the workshop I answered the reassessment questions from the records. The assessment went through the procedures, the equipment history, the measurements, and the way I reported them. I supplied what was requested and kept a note of which file it came from. I passed with no findings. I put the result with the accreditation papers, cleared the review copies from the bench, and finished a job that was due out. The reassessment invoice went into the month's expenses beside the rent.

The bill for heating oil arrived with a separate calculation for my share. The dairy building's owner had divided the cost and given me a total to pay. I read it at the workshop bench with the envelope still under the page. The amount was more than I expected from the weather and from the hours I had kept the place heated. I put the bill aside until I could lay out the records properly, instead of trying to settle it by telephone from the total alone.

I had noted the tank gauge reading monthly. The gauge was not a precision measuring device, and I did not treat it as one. I had written down the date, the reading, and whether a delivery had occurred since the previous entry. The notes were in the same book as the room checks and building expenses. I opened it flat, weighted the left page with a ruler, and pulled the oil company's delivery slips from the expense folder.

Each slip had a delivery date and a quantity in litres. I arranged them by date and copied the quantities onto a sheet with columns. Alongside those I put my gauge readings. Where a delivery fell between two monthly readings, I left enough space to show both. I checked the additions with a calculator, then checked them again from the slips. The discrepancy did not come from a decimal point or from my copying a figure into the wrong month.

There were nine hundred litres in the charge that had not gone to my side of the building. The delivery slips and the gauge notes agreed on that. I could not account for every litre consumed between one monthly reading and the next, but I could account for the deliveries. One quantity had been included in my share without appearing in the supply to my side. I marked it on a copy of the calculation and kept the original clean.

I phoned the owner with the papers in front of me. I gave the delivery dates and quantities rather than saying the bill looked high. When I reached the nine hundred litres, I said it had not been delivered to my side and should not be in my charge. I made copies of the relevant slips and the pages of readings. The copies went with a short note showing the subtraction. I dated the note and kept another copy in the folder.

The workshop used oil whether I had much work in or little. I still had to keep standards, paperwork, and equipment within the conditions I claimed for them. I entered the amount I could support in the accounts and left the disputed part identified separately. Before putting the book away, I went to the tank and took the next reading. I stood square to the gauge so I could read it at the same angle as before, then wrote the number beside the date.

Luc was still drinking. He lost his licence and came very close to losing his house. By the time I drove him to treatment in Sudbury, there was no question of him taking his own truck. We arranged the trip for a week when the children were with Claire. I cleared the passenger footwell of catalogues and old delivery envelopes, checked the van's tyres, and filled the tank. There was no equipment in the back that needed to stay warm.

I picked him up at the house. He came down from the porch with a bag and went back once for something he had left inside. I waited in the van. When he returned, he tried the door handle before taking out his key, though he had just locked it. He put the bag behind his seat. I asked whether he had the papers he needed. He checked an envelope, said yes, and fastened his belt.

There had been rain, and the wipers needed the intermittent setting for most of the first part of the drive. Luc talked about the licence. He talked about his house as well, giving me amounts in pieces instead of one total. I did not add them aloud while he spoke. When he stopped talking, I asked which payment was due first. He took the envelope from his coat and looked through it. I kept my eyes on the road while he read.

The treatment centre had a place to pull in near the entrance. I parked, shut off the van, and helped him take his bag inside. There were forms to deal with. Luc held the envelope against his leg while he sat, and I waited until he had finished. I paid the first month's rent on his room. I kept the receipt in my wallet until I got home, then put it with my personal papers, away from the business expenses.

Before I left, he asked what he owed me for the trip. I said we could leave that alone. He asked whether I would come back. I said I would come on my weeks without the kids. That was an arrangement I could keep without changing their weeks or closing the workshop for a full day. I went back to the van, put his empty envelope where he had left it on the seat, and drove home with no one beside me.

On the visits I took the same van and allowed for the road conditions. I finished the work that had to go out before leaving and put a note beside the telephone with the time I expected back. Luc asked after Maya and Theo. I told him about their room at the apartment and the things they left on their shelves. We talked about his house when there was something definite to discuss. When visiting time was over, I drove back and opened the workshop if there was still work I could finish that evening.

In the apartment I read on sober evenings after the kitchen was put away. One subject held me for weeks: the proposal to redefine the kilogram using a fundamental constant. The kilogram was still tied to a metal cylinder kept in a vault. The proposal would fix the value of the Planck constant and make the definition available through measurements, rather than through agreement with that particular object. I read the same explanations several times, with paper beside me for the parts I could put into equations.

The watt balance took longer. It had two separate operations. In one, an electrical current through a coil produced a force that balanced the weight of a mass. In the other, the coil moved through the magnetic field and produced a voltage. The same combination of the magnetic field and the coil's effective wire length appeared in both. Combining the measurements allowed that part to cancel. What remained connected the weight and a measured velocity to voltage and current.

I wrote out the units because the phrase about weighing mass against electrical power could make the machine sound simpler than it was. A force multiplied by a velocity gave watts. Voltage multiplied by current gave watts too. The weighing and moving did not happen together as an ordinary transfer of power. I worked that through on several evenings, putting the paper away when I had to make lunches or clear the counter. The next time I took it out, I started again from the two operations.

Maya asked what the papers were for. I told her people were working on a way to define a kilogram without relying on a cylinder in a vault. She asked what was wrong with the cylinder. I said an object could pick up material or lose material, and the copies used elsewhere could differ from it. She asked how anyone knew which one had changed. I said that was part of the difficulty. She sat down across from me and pulled the page round so the writing faced her.

She said they could leave the cylinder where it was and stop touching it. I said people still needed to compare other masses with it. She asked why they could not make several and take the middle answer. I said they did make copies, but then they had to decide what the answer was tied to. A fixed constant would give them something they could reproduce through an experiment. She asked whether our bathroom scale would be any better. I said probably not in any way she would notice.

That brought her back to why anyone would bother. I explained about measurements made in different places, and about being able to check work without carrying everything back to one object. She said the balance sounded like more equipment to get wrong. I said there was a great deal to get right, including gravity at the place where it stood. She tapped the page and said that supported her point. I left her finger there and read the equation upside down. When she went to get ready for bed, I turned the paper back and checked the units once more.

Annie Duquette called the workshop to tell me the old mill crew was holding a reunion at the Legion. I had the receiver between my hand and my ear, with a pencil resting across the open job book. She gave me the evening and said there would be people I had not seen for a while. I looked at the date on the wall calendar. It fell in a week when the children were with Claire.

I told Annie I could not make it. She said I could come for an hour if that was all I had. I said I would not be there. She did not ask me to account for the evening. We spoke briefly about work. I told her the business was still going and that I was in the same part of the dairy building. Before we hung up, she repeated the date in case I changed my mind. I wrote nothing on the calendar.

That evening I stayed alone in the workshop and recalibrated my reference thermometers. I cleared the bench first, putting finished work on the outgoing shelf and covering what would wait until morning. I laid out the thermometer records in order of identification number. The clean cloths stayed at one end of the bench, away from the used ones. I checked the baths and the readout, then went through the method before starting the measurements.

The work required waiting at each point. I watched the readings settle and recorded enough observations to establish that they had. I kept the immersion depth consistent and checked that the leads were not pulling the probes against the side. Between points I examined the entries for a missing unit or a transposed digit. Outside, the light went off the windows. I turned on the other bench light so I could read the small figures without leaning over the bath.

I calculated the corrections and compared them with the previous results. Where a value needed another look, I repeated the measurement under the same conditions and kept both records. The telephone remained on its shelf. There was no need to call anyone. I had the apartment keys in my trouser pocket, the van keys beside the job book, and work for the following morning already laid out under its cover.

When I finished, I dated and signed the sheets and filed them with the reference thermometer records. I dried the probes, put them away, and wiped the bench. The wastebasket held strips of label backing and the paper towel I had used under a wet lead. I emptied it, checked the heat setting, and switched off the bench lights. At the side door I tried the handle after locking it. Then I went back to Main Street, climbed the stairs, and put my keys on the labelled shelf.

Chapter 27

In 2013 I moved Lacasse Calibration out of the old dairy at the edge of Brule Falls and into a steel-sided unit in the business park on Ardmore Road. I was forty-two. Claire and I had been separated about a year, and I was renting an apartment on Main Street above a shop that sold cellphones: three rooms, a kitchen with one counter, and a bunk bed in the second bedroom for Maya and Theo on the weeks they were with me. The divorce would not be final until the next year. Most of what I remember about those two years is the building, because the building was the thing I could do something about.

The dairy had done me for seven years, but it was a bad room for calibration. The walls were concrete block with no insulation, the roof over the east end leaked every spring thaw, and the temperature in my part of it could go from fourteen degrees at eight in the morning to twenty-seven by four in the afternoon in July. You cannot certify a pressure gauge to a tenth of a percent in a room like that and say so with a straight face, and more of the plants I worked for wanted their certificates to state the temperature the work was done at. The unit on Ardmore Road was twenty-four hundred square feet, with a roll-up door at the back, a front office with a window on the parking lot, and a three-phase panel nobody had used since a cabinetmaker went out of business in it. The rent was eighteen hundred and fifty a month plus my share of the snow removal. I signed a five-year lease in January and took the keys on the first of February.

Before I moved one box I drew the place. I bought a pad of graph paper at the drugstore, quarter-inch squares, and let each square stand for six inches. I measured the unit with a hundred-foot tape and a laser measure I bought for the purpose, and I put down every wall, column, door swing, window, outlet, the panel and the floor drain. Then I drew in the benches. There were to be eleven, numbered one through eleven in the order the work came through: receiving at the back door, cleaning, the pressure benches, temperature, electrical, mass and force, dimensional, and the last two in the front room where the certificates were printed and the instruments packed to go back out. The controlled room went in the northwest corner, away from the roll-up door and the afternoon sun, twelve feet by sixteen with its own door and a small vestibule.

Every drawer got a label. I sorted by instrument class, the way I had sorted screws in a shoebox when I was a boy, only with more categories. P for pressure, T for temperature, E for electrical, F for flow, M for mass and force, D for dimensional, then a number for the bench and a letter for the drawer. P-3-B was the second drawer on bench three and held the fitting adapters, NPT to BSP, quarter-inch and up. T-5-A held the thermocouple connectors. I typed the list up, eleven pages, and printed it twice, one copy for the clipboard and one to tape inside the front office door.

I did most of the drawing at the kitchen table in the apartment in the evenings. Maya was ten that winter, and she would stand at my shoulder and read the labels out loud. She wanted to know why the drawers didn't just say what was in them. I told her a drawer that said fittings would end up holding everything anybody thought might be a fitting. She asked what was in M-8-C, and I looked it up and told her: the small check weights, one gram to fifty. She said that was a lot of work for some weights. I said it was work you did once so you didn't do it every day after.

The move was the first weekend of March, a week the children were with Claire. Through February I packed the dairy at night, one bench at a time, and every box got a strip of masking tape with its code in black marker. I rented a twenty-foot truck for the Saturday, a hundred and forty dollars plus the kilometres, and hired two boys from the high school at fifteen dollars an hour to carry. I gave each of them a copy of the floor plan with the bench numbers circled in red, and I chalked the numbers on the concrete in the new unit where each bench would stand. The old benches went in first, onto their chalk numbers, and then the boxes went straight off the truck to the bench whose number was on the tape. Nothing went into a pile in the middle of the floor. The deadweight tester came over on the Sunday by itself, strapped to a pallet in the back of my own truck, because I did not want anybody moving it but me and a dolly. By six on Sunday the dairy was swept and the keys were in an envelope for the owner, and on Monday morning I calibrated six pressure transmitters for the water plant at bench three on Ardmore Road and shipped them by noon.

That was the easy part. The building needed real work before it was the shop I had drawn, and I had no money to pay anyone to do it. The arrangement with Claire then was alternate weeks, Sunday evening to Sunday evening. On my weeks with Maya and Theo I worked business hours and was home by five-thirty to make supper, check homework and sign permission slips. On the weeks without them I worked business hours, went to the apartment long enough to eat, came back to Ardmore Road and worked on the fit-out until eleven or midnight, and all day Saturday and most of Sunday.

The benches came first. The old ones were a mix of things I had built in my garage in 2006 and things I had bought at the mill auction, and no two were the same height. I built new ones to a standard I wrote at the top of the floor plan: thirty-six inches to the work surface, thirty inches deep, frames of two-by-four glued and screwed, three-quarter fir plywood tops with a sheet of hardboard over that so the surface could be replaced when it got chewed up. Every bench got a strip of outlets along the back and a ground bar, and the electrical benches got static mats. I built roughly one a week on my free weeks, and as each was finished I pulled the old bench off its chalk number, set the new one in its place and emptied the drawers into new drawers with the same labels. The lumber for eleven benches came to about twenty-three hundred dollars at the yard, and the hardware another four hundred.

The reference equipment needed its own circuit. My standards were the most expensive things I owned: the deadweight tester, a dry-block calibrator with a reference thermometer, a multifunction calibrator, and a six-and-a-half digit multimeter that had just come back from its own calibration in Toronto at six hundred dollars for the certificate. I did not want them sharing a circuit with the air compressor or the shop vac. I ran a dedicated twenty-amp circuit from the panel in EMT along the north wall to the controlled room and the two electrical benches, with an isolated ground back to the panel, and I put the reference gear on a double-conversion UPS, so that when the voltage on Ardmore Road dipped, which it did every time the welding shop three units down struck an arc on its big machine, my standards never saw it. I pulled the permit myself and had it inspected. I did every bit of that wiring with the main breaker off and locked out and the key in my pocket, which is how I was taught.

The controlled room took the longest. I framed it in steel studs, insulated walls and ceiling, and hung two layers of drywall inside with the seams staggered. I put in a door with a proper seal and sweep, and a second door into the vestibule so coming and going didn't dump the room's air into the shop. The heat pump was a ductless unit with the outdoor half on a pad behind the building, and I paid an HVAC contractor four hundred dollars to charge the lines, because that is a licensed job and I don't hold the licence. With that and a small dehumidifier the room held twenty degrees plus or minus one and forty-five percent humidity plus or minus ten, which was the target I had written down in February. I set a logging thermometer and hygrometer on a stand in the middle of the room at bench height and let it run three weeks before I trusted it. In the first week of October, with a cold rain blowing sideways across the parking lot, I moved the reference equipment in and did my first job there, a batch of RTDs for a dairy co-op in the next county. The fit-out had taken seven months and about eleven thousand dollars, not counting my time, which I didn't count.

In June of that year, in the middle of all that, I drove to the district town to sign the separation agreement. Ms. Wynne's office was on the second floor of a brick building on the main street there, over a travel agent, forty minutes down the highway. Claire had hired her the summer before. I had not hired anybody. I had told myself there was nothing to fight about, and that a lawyer of my own would cost three or four thousand dollars I was spending on lumber and conduit.

The office had a long table with a box of tissues on it and a window air conditioner that dripped into a margarine tub on the sill. Claire was already there, sitting beside Ms. Wynne on the far side of the table in her scrubs, because she was going on shift at the hospital at three. Ms. Wynne was a small woman in a grey suit with reading glasses on a cord. She shook my hand and slid the draft across to me, twenty-two pages, stapled, with yellow tabs where I was to sign.

"Take whatever time you need," she said. "If you'd like to review it with your own counsel first, that's your right, and I'd encourage it."

I said I would read it there.

I read every page. Support came first. Claire's income and mine were set out, mine from my last two tax returns, and the table amounts for two children were set off against each other on the shared schedule, which left me paying her nine hundred and forty dollars a month. On top of that I was to pay sixty percent of what they called special expenses: hockey, the orthodontist, school trips. The house on Cedar Street was to stay with Claire and the children. I had bought it in 1997, before I met her. My half of the equity was to be paid to me when the youngest turned eighteen or the house was sold, whichever came first, with no interest. The schedule was alternate weeks, the exchange Sundays at six at the house, Christmas Eve and Christmas Day alternating, and the first two weeks of July with Claire every year.

By the last page I had a dozen points in my head. My income figure was the gross from the year I sold off some of the mill auction equipment, which made it four or five thousand higher than I really made. The no-interest payout meant I would get 2013 dollars back sometime in the 2020s. Sixty percent of the special expenses was not the ratio of our incomes, even on their figure. Christmas went to her in odd years, and 2013 was odd. There was nothing about who bought winter boots. There was a clause that said I could not move the children more than fifty kilometres from Brule Falls and no clause saying the same of her. I could have gone through them one by one. I had them in order.

Claire was watching me. Ms. Wynne was writing on a legal pad. The air conditioner dripped into the tub.

"Any questions?" Ms. Wynne said.

I said no. I took the pen out of my shirt pocket and signed at every yellow tab and initialled the bottom of every page, all twenty-two, and Ms. Wynne took it back and checked the tabs one at a time. I let my wife's lawyer write every term of the agreement without a word of objection, which is the old habit at its worst. Claire said, "Thank you, Ray," and I think she meant it, and for a second she also looked at me as if she was waiting for the rest. There was no rest. I drove back up the highway and was at bench six by two-thirty, putting a rebuilt mass flow controller back together.

The business park held a barbecue for all the tenants on the last Friday in July. The property manager put a notice through every mail slot two weeks ahead, and on the day they set up two rented barbecues and a row of folding tables under a canopy in the parking lot between the sign shop and the welding shop, with coolers along the curb. By four o'clock there were thirty or forty people out there from the eleven units, with their families.

It was one of my weeks without the children, and I was in the controlled room putting the second layer of drywall on the ceiling with a rented drywall lift. I closed the front door and pulled the roll-up door down, which made the shop hot, and kept at it. Through the steel siding I could hear people talking and laughing and the scrape of the barbecue lids, and I drove screws.

A little after six somebody knocked at the front door. It was the man who ran the sign shop two units down, in an apron, holding a paper plate with another paper plate upside down over it and a can of beer in his other hand. "You missed the whole thing," he said. "Figured you might be hungry." Under the top plate were a cheeseburger, a scoop of potato salad and a butter tart. He held up the can and asked if I wanted one. I said no, thank you. I took the plate and thanked him for it and said I had a job to finish. He said there was plenty more out there if I changed my mind. I thanked him again and closed the door. I ate the burger standing at bench nine with drywall dust on my hands, and I finished the ceiling a little after ten, when the parking lot was empty and the canopy was folded up against the welding shop wall.

In August I had a call from the quality manager at a bottling plant two hours south. They had twelve magnetic flow meters on their filling and clean-in-place lines that were due for calibration before an audit by one of their big customers. He told me their own technician had already calibrated all twelve. What they needed was the signature of an outside shop on the certificates. He had the certificates made up already. He would email them, I would sign them and send them back, and he would pay my half-day rate for the trouble.

I said I didn't sign for anything I hadn't measured.

He said the meters had been checked, his technician knew his business, and it was a formality. I said if it was a formality then the meters would pass, and it would cost him the price of the testing, and if it wasn't, he would want to know before his auditor did. I said my name on a certificate meant I had put the instrument against a standard and written down what I saw, and I had never signed one any other way. He said he would have to think about it. He called back the next morning and said all right.

They pulled the meters at their weekend shutdown and couriered them up in four crates, half-inch to two-inch. I ran them on the water rig at bench seven, which is a weigh tank on a reference scale with a diverter valve and a timer, and I took each meter at three flow rates, ten, fifty and ninety percent of range, three runs at each. Nine of the twelve were inside their tolerance of half a percent of reading. Three were not. One of the two-inch meters on a filling line read 1.4 percent high across the range. A one-inch on the clean-in-place return read 0.9 percent low. The third, a half-inch, was fine at fifty and ninety percent and read 2.1 percent high at ten, and when I pulled the liner inspection report from the crate I found a note in pencil that the electrodes had been cleaned in March. They had a coating on them again by the look of the readings.

I wrote up all twelve with the as-found numbers, flagged the three in red on the summary page, and sent the certificates for the nine that passed and the data sheets for the three that didn't, with a note on each of what I would check. The invoice was twelve meters at a hundred and eighty-five dollars, twenty-two hundred and twenty plus HST, plus the courier back. He paid it in thirty days without a word about it. The next spring he sent me the meters again without calling first.

In November Annie Duquette phoned me at the shop. Annie had been an electrician at the mill when I was there, the best one they had. Her husband had had a stroke in September, and he had come home from the hospital at the end of October on oxygen. The concentrator ran on house power and had a battery that was good for a few hours, and they had backup cylinders from the supplier, but the line along her road came down in every ice storm and every big wind, and that fall it had already been out twice, once for nineteen hours. She wanted a standby generator with an automatic transfer switch. She could have wired it herself in her sleep. She couldn't leave him long enough to do it, and she said so.

I went out on a Tuesday evening and looked at her panel and her service, a two-hundred-amp overhead drop on a mast at the side of the house, and we priced it at the kitchen table while her husband slept in the front room with the concentrator humming beside him. A fourteen-kilowatt propane unit and a service-entrance-rated transfer switch came to fifty-six hundred dollars from the supplier, before the pad and the gas line. I told her I would pay half the generator. She said no. I said I had already decided, and that she could argue with me about it while I worked, and she let it go.

I did it over two weekends in late November, both of them weeks without the children. The first Saturday I poured a small pad of pre-cast slabs on compacted gravel on the north side of the house, three feet from the wall and clear of the windows, and set the generator on it with a rented dolly and a come-along. I ran the conduit for the control wiring and the feeders and mounted the transfer switch beside the meter base. The second weekend Hydro came on the Saturday morning, pulled the meter and left it pulled until I was finished, so the whole service was dead from the street in. I landed the feeders, wired the controls, and the gas fitter Annie had found ran the propane line from the tank and tested it. The inspector came the Wednesday after. On the Sunday afternoon, with the meter back in, I opened the main breaker to simulate an outage and timed it with my watch: eleven seconds for the generator to start and the switch to throw, and the concentrator never stopped, because it was on its battery for those eleven seconds anyway. Her husband watched from the window. I set the exerciser to run the generator twenty minutes every Thursday at noon.

Annie tried to give me an envelope with money in it for my time. I wouldn't take it. She put it in my coat pocket in the hall while I was tying my boots and I took it out and left it on the shelf by the door with her keys. She said I was the most stubborn man she had ever worked with, and that she had worked with the mill's entire maintenance department. I said I knew. In January the line on her road came down for thirty-one hours in an ice storm, and she phoned me the next day to tell me the generator had run the whole time.

The week before Christmas 2013 one of my customers, the maintenance foreman at a sawmill west of town, stopped at the shop on his way home with a gift bag. Inside was a bottle of good Canadian rye, the kind that costs sixty dollars, with a card that said thanks for the year. I had done his scale and his kiln temperature probes that fall on short notice when an inspector was coming. I thanked him, and we talked for a few minutes about the snow, which had come early and stayed, and about whether his scale would hold up through the cold, and then he went out to his van. I stood at the front window and watched him back out, and when the van had turned onto Ardmore Road and gone I took the bottle out of the bag, carried it to the stainless sink by the cleaning bench, cracked the seal and poured it down the drain, and ran the hot water after it for a minute. I poured the bottle a customer gave me down the shop sink, and I am still prouder of that than of any contract. I put the empty bottle in the recycling bin out back under the cardboard. I kept the card. I wrote him a thank-you note on shop letterhead the next morning and mailed it.

In May of 2014 the principal at the children's school phoned me at the shop and asked me to come in about Theo, who was eight. There had been a fight at recess. It was one of my weeks, so I went. Claire was on days and couldn't get away.

Theo had told me about it the night before at supper, without my asking. A boy in his class had taken his hat at recess and thrown it on the roof of the portable, and when Theo went to tell the yard teacher the boy shoved him from behind into the fence, and when Theo got up the boy shoved him again, and Theo shoved him back, and the boy fell and cut his lip on the asphalt. The yard teacher only turned around at the end of it, and what she saw was the other boy on the ground with a bloody mouth and Theo standing over him.
