The principal's office had a round table and four plastic chairs, and Theo sat on one beside me with his hands under his legs. The principal went through it his way. Theo had pushed another student to the ground and injured him. There had been words between them before that, which was not unusual for those two, and the school had a firm policy on physical contact. He said the other boy's parents had been in that morning. He said Theo would spend three days of recess in the office and write a letter of apology, and that he hoped this was the end of it. He asked if I had anything to add.

I knew what Theo had told me. I knew about the hat and the roof of the portable and the fence. I said no, I didn't, and that I would make sure the letter got written. The principal said he appreciated that, and shook my hand, and told Theo he could go back to class.

At three-thirty I picked him up out front in the truck. He got in and did up his belt and didn't say anything until we were past the arena. Then he said, "Why didn't you say anything? I told you what happened."

I understand now that silence was never the same thing as kindness. What I said to him in the truck was that the principal had already made up his mind. Theo said that wasn't a reason. I said no, it wasn't. He looked out the side window the rest of the way to Main Street, and that night he wrote the apology letter at the kitchen table in pencil, four lines, and I signed the bottom where it said Parent or Guardian. His hat was still on the roof of the portable when I picked him up on the Friday. I phoned the school the next week and asked the custodian if he would get it down, and he did.

The divorce was final in September of 2014, on the terms in the agreement I had signed in Ms. Wynne's office the summer before. The certificate came in the mail to the apartment in a long brown envelope with a copy of the order. I read it at the counter, put it in the filing box in my closet behind the tax returns, and went to work.

That October a feed mill I did work for called about a bin scale. It was a single load cell on a hanging hopper, and their complaint was that when they loaded the hopper at the end of the day and left it full overnight, the reading crept upward. By morning it would be three or four tenths of a percent higher than it had been at five o'clock, with nothing added. When they emptied it the reading came back to zero, or close. They had had the cell replaced the year before by somebody else and it had done the same thing with the new one.

I checked it on site first, with test weights, and it was linear and repeatable over a few minutes. So I hung the weights and stayed. Over two hours it crept 0.08 percent, and I came back the next morning and it was 0.31 percent up from where I had left it. I knew what creep was in a general way. I did not know it well enough to tell a customer why his scale was doing it, so I spent the next week reading about it in the evenings: the manufacturer's application notes, a textbook on strain gauge measurement I ordered from Toronto for ninety-four dollars, and some papers on the slow flow of metal under constant load.

I wrote it out on the whiteboard at the shop to get it straight. Stress is load over area. Strain is stress over the elastic modulus. The gauge changes resistance in proportion to strain, the change in resistance over resistance equal to the gauge factor times the strain, and in a full bridge the output voltage is the gauge factor times the strain times the excitation voltage. All of that assumes the strain is fixed once the load is fixed. It isn't, quite. Under a steady load the metal of the spring element keeps straining a little more over time, fastest at first and then slower, roughly with the logarithm of time. That adds a time term to the strain, and the gauge reads the extra strain as extra load. Against that, the gauge's own backing and the glue under it relax under steady strain and pull the reading the other way, and a manufacturer sizes the end loops of the gauge grid so the two cancel at the cell's rated load. Push a cell harder than it was matched for, or use one whose matching was poor, and the metal wins and the reading goes up.

Theo was with me that week, and after school he did his homework at bench eleven while I worked. He was eight and he read the whiteboard over my shoulder the way his sister had read drawer labels, and he wanted to know why. Why did metal stretch more if nothing was pushing it harder? I said the load was the same, but the metal wasn't quite solid the way he thought; the crystals in it had faults in them that slid a little at a time. Why did they slide? Because the load gave them a push and the heat in the metal gave them the rest. Why didn't they slide forever? Because they jammed up against each other, which was why it slowed down. Why did the glue go the other way? I had to go back to the textbook for that one, and we read the page together, and I drew the gauge grid on the board with its end loops twice the size so he could see what the manufacturer was adjusting. He made me do the logarithm part three times. The third time I did it with the numbers from the feed mill, 0.08 at two hours and 0.31 by morning, and he worked out with me that if it were a straight line it would have been far more than that by morning, and that the curve was what the book said it should be.

By the Thursday we both had it. The feed mill's cell was rated at five thousand kilograms and they were running the hopper at forty-six hundred most nights, past where its creep was matched, and the replacement had been the same cheap model. I sent them the readings with a two-page write-up and a quote for a ten-thousand-kilogram cell with a published creep specification, and they ordered it. I left the derivation on the whiteboard for a month. Theo erased the log curve one afternoon by leaning on it, and drew it back in himself before he went home.

Chapter 28

In 2015, Denis phoned me at the workshop on Ardmore Road and said he had left the lakes. He was in Valparaíso, Chile. He had found an apartment he wanted to buy and needed me to co-sign the loan. I had a pressure gauge upside down on the bench, with the case screws in a tobacco tin and the phone held between my shoulder and ear. I put the screwdriver down before I answered him. He gave me the price in the local currency, then a figure in Canadian dollars, then a different Canadian figure after he checked the exchange rate. None of those figures included the fees.

He said the place was sound. The building was sound, the paperwork was sound, the deal was sound. He had been through it. There was a balcony and enough room for what he owned. He wanted to settle there and was finished with working on the lakes. I asked him what the monthly payment would be. He told me what the first payment would be. I asked whether it stayed at that amount. He said I could read the papers myself if I wanted.

I gave him the workshop email address, although he already had it. He said there was a deadline. I asked for the whole contract, including anything referred to on the signature page. What arrived that evening was a scan with one corner cut off on two pages. I sent it back and asked him to lay the sheets flat. The second copy had all four corners. I printed it single-sided because I wanted the back of each page free for notes. The stack used most of what was left in the printer tray.

I had it translated and paid for that myself. I put the original and the translation beside one another on the kitchen table, with a ruler under each line as I went. I did not know enough Spanish to check the wording, but the paragraph numbers matched. I could follow the amounts, dates and references. I wrote the Canadian equivalents in pencil, using the same exchange rate throughout, and kept the fees separate. Denis had included a photograph of the front of the building. I left that in the envelope with the rest of the pages I did not need yet.

The clause was partway down a page of conditions. It allowed the seller to raise the price after the agreement had been signed. There were words around it about adjustment and completion, but there was no fixed amount I could put in the margin. I read the paragraph before it and the paragraph after it, then followed the references back through the contract. The loan figure Denis had given me was attached to a price that could change. I marked the clause on the original and the translation, scanned both pages and sent them to him.

He called while I was putting the gauge back together. He said that was standard down there. I asked him what stopped the seller from adding another ten per cent. He said he had been told it would not happen. I told him that was different from having it written down. He wanted to know whether I thought he could not manage his own affairs. I said I had been asked to put my name on a loan, so I had read the loan papers. I had not signed anything. The marked pages were still beside the printer when I locked up that night.

Later that year, I sat at my mother's kitchen table while Denis spoke from Chile through the speaker on her telephone. Hélène had decided to sell the house and move into a seniors' residence. She had a folder from the residence beside her place mat. It held a floor plan, a list of charges and a sheet describing what she could bring. She had put a tick beside the smaller room. Denis asked whether she had looked at the larger one. She said she had, and she did not need to pay for floor she would only have to walk across.

I had a folded sheet in my jacket pocket. The back steps needed work. There was a handrail I could fit properly, a sticking door I could plane, and a low cupboard I could alter so she would not have to reach into the bottom of it. I had priced the lumber and hardware. I thought those jobs would give her another year in the house. I had written them in the order I could do them, with the outside work first. She opened the residence folder to the page showing the room measurements. I left my sheet in my pocket.

She asked me to measure her dresser. I fetched a tape from the van and checked the dresser against the space on the plan. It would fit with enough room to open the drawers. Her kitchen table would not. Denis said she should take whatever she wanted and have the rest dealt with. She told him that was easy to say from Chile. I wrote the dresser dimensions on the floor plan, then measured the armchair. She had already decided which chair she wanted. She was asking me whether it would go through the door.

We packed over several afternoons. She put things on the table and I found boxes for them. I used small boxes for books and heavier kitchen things, with folded paper between the plates. She rejected a box whose bottom had softened. I cut the sound side out of it for dividers and used another one. Each box got its contents written on two sides. She wanted the labels large enough to read without turning the box towards the window. I used the broad marker from the workshop instead of the ballpoint I had brought in my shirt pocket.

The residence supplied bedding, but she wanted her own blanket. She wanted the dresser, the armchair and the lamp from beside it. We measured the lamp table twice because the floor plan showed less clearance there than it appeared to have. She kept a biscuit tin for papers and put the residence agreement in it herself. Denis phoned while we were closing the last of the kitchen boxes. I set the telephone where she could hear it and carried the packed boxes out of the way. He asked how it was going. She said I was using enough tape.

On the afternoon we packed her clothes, rain ran off the back eaves into the flattened grass. I had brought a new length of weather stripping for the back door and left it in the van. She stood at the open dresser and told me which drawer to start with. I laid the clothes flat in a clean carton. She corrected the way I folded a cardigan, then folded it herself and handed it back. The repair list stayed in my jacket until I took the jacket home. I put it in the drawer with my household receipts.

Jordan Kwan came back from Alberta in 2016. He had been laid off after oil prices collapsed and had no work lined up. He came into the workshop in a pair of work boots with the toes worn pale, carrying a folded résumé he did not give me straight away. I knew what he had done at the mill when he was my apprentice. He told me what he had done since. We stood at the receiving bench because I had a transmitter opened up on my desk and there was nowhere clear to sit.

He asked whether I knew of anything. I had three jobs booked that week and one waiting on approval. One of the booked jobs was small enough to finish before lunch. The figures in the ledger did not support a second wage. I pulled a chair out from under the bench, moved the transmitter tray off my desk and opened the calendar. I asked him when he could start. He said Monday. We went over the hourly rate, hours, overtime and pay day. I wrote those down, made a copy and gave it to him before he left.

He was my first employee. Up to then, every hour on a job had been my own, including the hours I had left off an invoice because I had quoted badly. I worked out what his wage would cost with the deductions and employer contributions. Then I worked out how many billable hours it would take to cover it. I made a separate column for work I could pass to him while I went out on calls. There were several blank lines at the bottom. I left them blank instead of filling them with possible jobs.

He had found a place in town but needed the first month's rent. I fronted it to him. We wrote the amount on a sheet of paper and both kept a copy. He asked how soon I wanted it back. I said we could settle the repayment in amounts that left him enough from his pay to live on. I put the advance in the ledger separately from wages. There was no use giving him a pay stub that said one thing while the cheque said another. He folded his copy into the same pocket as the paper with his rate on it.

Before Monday, I cleared the second bench. It had become a place for empty cases, lengths of tubing I might use, and jobs waiting for parts. I sorted the tubing by size and threw out pieces too short to be useful. I checked the bench sockets, changed the blade in the utility knife and set out a meter with sound leads. Jordan would have his own tools, but I wanted the bench ready. I also cleared a shelf for his lunch. The fridge had room once I took out two containers I had failed to bring home.

He arrived on Monday before I had finished unlocking the side door. We went through the jobs on the board and the equipment waiting to be returned. He asked where I wanted the incoming work. I showed him the receiving shelf and the tags, then had to explain which pile of papers went with which gauge. By ten o'clock he had found a missing fitting I had put in the wrong drawer. At lunch I added a note to my pad about making the receiving procedure clearer. There was enough work for both of us that day. Tuesday was thinner.

On his first Friday, Jordan carried a case of beer through the door just after we had finished putting away a set of pressure gauges. He put it on the cleared end of the receiving bench and said we ought to mark the occasion. His boots had left wet prints from the van to the door. It had rained through most of the afternoon, and the tyres on the road outside made a steady tearing sound. I had already switched off the bench supply. I moved the completed paperwork clear of the case.

He opened it and held a bottle out to me. I said, "No thanks. There's ginger ale in the fridge."

I took one out and brought over the two chairs. He opened a beer and sat with his heels under the chair rungs. I sat across from him with the ginger ale between my hands. The workshop was cooler once the work had stopped. I kept my fleece on. We talked about the week, mostly the equipment that had come in with incomplete information and the amount of time it took to get an answer before touching it.

Jordan said he had expected more work on site. I said there would be site work, but somebody still had to do the bench jobs and write the results down. He asked about a pressure controller on the shelf. I told him what it had cost and how long I had looked for a used one before buying it. He turned his bottle on the bench and read the serial number I had written on a nearby case. He said I even numbered the things that came with numbers. I said mine were easier to find in the register.

He had the one beer. I finished the ginger ale, rinsed the can and put it with the recycling. We checked that the side door was latched and that the heater clearance was open. He took the case when he left. I dried the ring under his bottle with the cloth from the sink, then put the chairs back beneath the bench. The week sheets were clipped together beside the telephone. I checked the total on his once more before putting it away for payroll.

The shop manual started as six pages. I wrote it at the desk in the workshop, with a mug on one corner to keep the sheets from lifting whenever the door opened. I described where every tool lived on the shadow boards. The outlines were already there, but some tools looked enough alike to be put on the wrong hooks. I numbered the spaces and made a list. The torque wrench went back in its case at its lowest marked setting. The measuring leads hung without tight coils. Damaged leads went in the marked tray.

I wrote the receiving procedure in the order a job came through the door. The instrument got a tag before its case or packing was put aside. The tag number went into the log with the customer's details, the serial number, the requested range and whatever work had been authorised. Accessories stayed with the job. Any damage was recorded before we started. If the paperwork did not say what the customer wanted, we obtained an answer. I put a blank example form behind those pages and filled in another one to show how I wanted corrections made.

The last part covered the bench at night. Pressure released, electrical supplies off, test leads put away, ports protected, unfinished work identified. There was a separate line for leaving a note about anything that had been taken apart. I did not want either of us coming in the next morning and having to work out why four screws sat beside a closed case. I added where the waste went and which cloths could be used on the benches. Jordan read it standing up, with one hand on the back of his chair.

He asked whether he needed to sign the manual out before borrowing a screwdriver. Then he turned to the page about leaving the bench and said I had missed a procedure for pushing in the chair.

I have come to think that a life is mostly the things you keep doing when nobody is asking.

I gave him a pencil and told him to mark anything that was wrong. He corrected the drawer number for the quarter-inch fittings. I had moved them while clearing his bench and had written down the old location. He kept the manual beside his bench, under a clear cover so it did not collect oil. When we changed a procedure, I replaced the page and put a date at the bottom. The first cover lasted less well than the pages. Its corner split where he kept lifting it with a thumb that usually had something on it.

I also set out to add safety-valve recertification to my accreditation. That meant building a rig I could demonstrate and document, with pressure measurement I could trace and a way to control the rise closely enough to record the valve's opening. I measured the available space before drawing anything. The bench could not block the receiving shelf or narrow the route to the door. I made a list of the valves I expected to handle, their connections and pressure ranges. Every increase in range added something to the cost of fittings, pipe or protection.

The first design looked economical on paper. I bought the parts against a list, recorded the ratings and kept the certificates in a binder. I laid the assembly out before it was put together and checked access for the gauges and controls. The welds were the part I could not assess by looking at the finished surface. I arranged the first pressure test with water, a controlled test area and the required precautions. No valve was being certified. The rig itself had to pass before it could be used for anybody's equipment.

It failed at the welds. Water showed where there should have been none. I stopped the test, brought the pressure down and drained it before going near the joints. I marked the failed locations and wrote the test pressure and observations in the build record. Jordan stood at the other side of the cleared area and read the gauge value back to me before I released it. Afterwards, with the assembly empty, we examined the joints and compared them with the drawing. I put a tag on the rig stating that it was not for service.

I could have spent a good deal of time altering that first assembly. Instead, I went back through the design, including the way the loads reached the joints and the access needed to make and inspect them properly. The second drawing had fewer awkward connections and more room around the parts that needed attention. It also cost more. I entered the unusable work from the first rig as a cost of the project. I did not spread it among customer invoices. The second build had its own parts list and its own record from the first purchase.

It took fourteen months to build the second rig. Ordinary work occupied most of the bench time, and I bought parts as the account allowed. A fitting could spend weeks in its labelled bag before I was ready for it. Jordan made brackets and checked dimensions against the drawing while I handled the pressure arrangements and the records. We stopped whenever a rating or a connection was not right and obtained the proper part. Some evenings I spent longer updating the drawing than I had spent on the assembly. I kept the old versions with the changes marked.

By the time it was ready, I knew the price of nearly every piece without looking. I still checked the invoices when I totalled them. The second rig passed its pressure test. We released the pressure, drained what needed draining and recorded the result. I dried the outside before checking the joints again. Jordan took the test sheet to the desk while I removed the temporary barriers. The passing result went into the binder with the drawings and component records. I put the binder on the shelf where I could reach it from the rig without stepping over anything.

While the second rig occupied part of the shop, Jordan and I spent weeks arguing about readings at the edge of tolerance. A result well inside a limit took very little discussion. A result on the line could stop us at the whiteboard after lunch. Jordan wanted to know what the line was for if a reading on it did not settle the matter. I wanted the measurement uncertainty accounted for in the decision. He asked how far inside the line I proposed to move the argument. I erased what I had written and started again.

I read up on decision rules and the probability behind them. I printed the material I needed, kept the source with each set of notes and worked through the examples with a calculator. At first I used numbers close to those on our jobs. That made it too easy to argue about the particular instrument instead of the rule, so I changed to simple figures. On the whiteboard I drew a tolerance band and marked a reading near its upper edge. Around that reading I put the interval representing the uncertainty. Part of it lay outside the band.

Jordan said the reading itself was still inside. I said the measurement did not give us an exact value without any spread. He said a customer wanted a certificate, not a lecture beside a whiteboard. I agreed about the certificate and left the example up. The next morning I added a second reading, farther inside, with the same uncertainty. We could call that one a pass under the rule I was proposing. He asked what happened if the uncertainty grew. I widened the interval and worked the numbers again. We used a fair amount of marker ink.

The discussion came back in small pieces. We would be tagging a job or waiting for a pressure to settle, and one of us would raise another example. I put possible rules beside the consequences of each: accepting an item that was actually outside tolerance, or rejecting one that was actually inside. We could reduce one kind of wrong decision by moving the acceptance limit, but that affected the other kind. I checked the probability calculations twice, then had Jordan work an example from the figures without looking at my answer. We obtained the same result.

I kept trying explanations that were too long. They included terms I then had to explain with more terms. I shortened the wording on a pad beside the telephone until I could say it to a customer in one sentence: "We only call it a pass when the reading, with its measurement uncertainty allowed for, stays inside the agreed tolerance."

That was the sentence I kept. The detailed rule still belonged in the paperwork, agreed before the work, with the limits and uncertainty stated. I added the procedure to the manual. Jordan read it, asked one question about the example, and put the new pages under his clear cover.

At a customer's tank farm, Jordan and I arrived with the test equipment in the van and the job details clipped to a board. The level transmitter was reached by a fixed ladder on the tank. The site tech pointed it out from the ground and said we could go straight up. There was no suitable harness ready for us and no proper tie-off point arranged. I asked about the fall protection. He said everyone went up unclipped. The ladder rungs were wet, and water had collected along the edge of the concrete below it.

I told him I would wait until the proper equipment and tie-off were available. He said the transmitter was only a short distance above the top of the ladder. I put the clipboard back in its cover. Jordan had taken one of the cases out of the van. I told him to put it back for the time being. The site tech said it would take a while to get what we wanted brought out. I said we would be in the van. I did not put a foot on the ladder.

We waited two hours. I noted the start of the delay on the job sheet and rang through the effect on the schedule. Jordan sat in the passenger seat with his coat still zipped. We had sandwiches and coffee from the flasks we had brought. The wind pushed fine rain against the windscreen, enough to gather but not enough to run off until I used the wipers. We checked the paperwork for the transmitter and the equipment serial numbers. There was a spelling error in the site description that I corrected on both copies.

Jordan asked whether I wanted him to unpack the test leads so they would be ready. I said to leave the cases closed until we had somewhere suitable to work. He folded his sandwich paper and put it in the bag between his boots. We went over the range and the points we intended to check. After that, I finished the notes from the previous bench job. He waited beside me. Neither of us went back to the ladder while the arrangements were being made.

The harness and the provision for a proper tie-off finally came out. I checked what had been supplied and confirmed the arrangement before we took the equipment from the van. The delay stayed on the job sheet as two hours. When we were back at the workshop, I entered the actual site time and the reason for it. Jordan put the cases on the receiving bench instead of leaving them by the door. We had used most of the working day by then. I checked the next day's bookings before shutting down the computer.

My mother died in 2017. After the service at the funeral home, the relatives gathered at the Legion hall. I went over and stayed twenty minutes. There were cups set out and chairs pulled away from the tables. I kept my coat over my arm. I answered what was asked of me, checked the time once, then put my coat on. My van was parked near the edge of the lot. I drove from there to the residence with the empty boxes I had brought folded flat behind the seat.

Her room still held the dresser and the chair we had measured at the house. The blanket was folded at the end of the bed. I shut the door behind me and stood the boxes against the wall where they would not interfere with the drawer openings. I had brought tape, a marker, clean paper and a utility knife. I opened the blade only far enough to cut the tape on the flattened cartons. Then I retracted it and put it on the dresser. I took off my coat and hung it over the chair.

I packed the clothes first, one drawer at a time. The cardigan she had refolded at the house was among them. I folded it along the same creases and laid it flat. Small things went into a separate carton so they would not work their way to the bottom of the others. I wrote the contents on two sides in the same broad lettering she had asked for when we packed the house. There was less room for spreading things out here. I finished and closed each box before starting the next.

The biscuit tin was in the dresser. It held the residence papers and the other papers she had kept there. I left them in their envelopes, put the tin in a box by itself and filled the spaces with folded paper so it would not slide around. Her lamp was still plugged in beside the chair. I unplugged it, wound the cord loosely and secured it with a strip of paper. I packed the shade separately. The room became easier to move through as I worked, though the closed boxes took up most of the floor along one wall.

I spent the afternoon there alone. Outside the window, the light shifted across the parked cars and then left them in shade. I drank water from the cup I had brought and went back to the dresser. I checked the back of each drawer and ran my hand along the shelf above the hanging space. The blanket needed a larger box than I had expected. I opened one of the remaining cartons, reinforced its bottom and folded the blanket to fit without forcing down the flaps.

Near the end, I found the residence floor plan among the papers in the tin. My measurements were still beside the dresser and the chair. I put it back where I had found it. I checked the room again, including the sockets and the space behind the door, then gathered the loose paper and the empty tape core into a bag. The knife and marker went into my coat pocket. I carried the boxes to the van in several trips, keeping the heavier ones low. When I returned for the last box, I switched off the lamp socket and took my coat from the chair.

Chapter 29

In November of 2018 I flew to Valparaíso to see my brother. Denis had been after me to come since he settled there in 2015, in phone calls that came on Sunday nights and ran long on his end, and for three years I had told him next year. After our mother died I ran out of ways to say it. I booked the flights in August at the travel agent's in the mall, Toronto to Santiago with a change in Houston, $1,684 return, and wrote it in the ledger under personal because there was no other column for it. Jordan said he could run the shop for ten days if I left him the van keys and a list. I left him three lists, one for jobs booked, one for jobs that might call, and one for what to do if the dry-well controller started throwing the fault code it had thrown twice that fall.

The trip down took twenty-two hours door to door. In Houston I sat at the gate for four of them with a paperback and a bottle of water that cost me five dollars American. The overnight leg to Santiago was full, and the man beside me slept with his elbow on the armrest the whole way, so I kept mine in my lap and watched the little map on the seatback screen draw the line down the coast of South America one centimetre an hour. We landed at a quarter to eight in the morning. It was fourteen degrees and the mountains behind the airport still had snow on them in what was, down there, the end of spring.

Denis was waiting outside customs in a windbreaker, with a beard I had only seen in photographs. It had gone grey at the chin. He was louder in person than he was on the phone, which I would not have thought possible, and he took my bag off me before I had a hand free to stop him. He had a silver Hyundai with a crack running across the bottom of the windshield, and he drove the hundred and twenty kilometres to the coast the way he had driven our father's truck in 1986, with one hand, talking the whole time, turning to look at me when he wanted to make a point. The highway went through two long tunnels and a valley of vineyards with rows of wire and posts, and then dropped down toward the water.

Valparaíso is a port built on a thin strip of flat land with the hills going straight up behind it, forty-odd of them, Denis said, each with its own name. The houses are stacked up the slopes on posts and retaining walls and on each other, with stairs between them instead of streets in a lot of places, and the streets that do exist switch back so sharply that the buses take them in first gear. Denis had an apartment on the third floor of a narrow building on Cerro Alegre, up a lane that was half stairs and half ramp. From the kitchen window you could see the container cranes at the port and the ships at anchor waiting their turn. Denis stood me at that window before he showed me where the bathroom was and named every ship by its flag. He had not changed in that respect since he was eleven and had maps of the oceans tacked over his bed in Brule Falls.

He gave me the spare room, which had a single bed, a chair, and a window onto the back of the next building, about two metres away. There was a wall-mounted gas heater that I looked over the first night. It had a pilot and a thermocouple and a flue that ran out through the wall, and the flue joint had been sealed with what looked like silicone. I told Denis about it. He said everybody's was like that. I left the window open a crack and the heater off, and slept under two blankets.

That first evening set the pattern for the rest of the visit. Denis cooked, or bought food already cooked from a place at the bottom of the lane, and put it on the table in the kitchen with a bottle of red wine and a bottle of pisco, and usually two or three friends of his came up the stairs about nine o'clock and sat down without being asked. They ate late there. Nobody sat down to supper before nine and nobody got up from it before midnight. Denis poured for everyone the minute a glass got low, and he poured for me the minute he had poured for himself.

I turned my glass over. I did it the first night without saying anything, and the second night, and every night after. Denis would come around with the bottle and find the bottom of the glass looking up at him, and he would say something in Spanish to his friends that made them laugh, and then he would set the bottle down and go to the fridge and bring me a bottle of mineral water. It was a green bottle with a blue label, gas or no gas; I took it with gas. By the fourth night he had stopped going to the fridge and had started putting the water by my plate before we sat down. He still came around with the wine every time. I think he did it the way he used to knock on my bedroom door when we were boys and he wanted me to come out to the river, knowing what the answer was going to be and asking anyway.

One of those nights, the friends had gone and it was after one in the morning, Denis sat with the pisco in front of him and told me about the freighter years, which I had heard about in pieces over the phone. He had gone onto the lakes boats in 1985 as a deckhand and come off them thirty years later as a mate, and somewhere in the middle he had started taking the long trips on salt water that he had always wanted, and one of those had put into Valparaíso and he had walked up these same stairs and decided. He said he had gone back to Canada for six months to settle things and he had known the whole six months that it was only to settle things. He asked me if I ever thought about leaving Brule Falls. I said I had left it once, for Sudbury, and come back. He said that was not leaving, that was going to college. I drank my mineral water and he drank his pisco and neither of us pushed it any further.

On the third morning I woke at six, which was my time at home, and Denis was still asleep with his door shut. I put on my jacket and went out. The fog had come in off the water overnight and was sitting on the lower part of the city so that the port and the cranes were gone and only the tops of the hills showed. I went up instead of down, because down was where the fog was. The lane turned into stairs and the stairs into a street and the street into more stairs, and I counted them for a while out of habit and lost the count somewhere past three hundred. There were dogs asleep on the landings. Nobody owned them, Denis said later; the whole street fed them. They lifted their heads when I went by and put them down again.

About half an hour up I passed a church. It was not a big one, a plain front with a bell in a small tower, the door propped open, and an old woman coming out with a scarf over her hair. I went in. There were maybe a dozen rows of wooden pews and nobody in them. At the side there was an iron rack of candles in little glass cups, most of them burnt out, and a metal box with a slot. I put in a five-hundred-peso coin, which was about a dollar, lit a candle from one of the others still going, and put it in the rack. Then I sat in the end of the nearest pew and said a Hail Mary for my mother, in French, the way she had said it with us at the kitchen table when we were small. It had been a year and a half. I said it once through and got up. I was back out on the stairs in ten minutes, and the old woman with the scarf was still standing at the bottom of the steps talking to a man with a cart of bread. I bought two rolls off him and walked back down to the apartment. Denis was up making coffee. He asked where I had gone and I said up the hill. I did not tell him about the church, then or later.

On the Saturday Denis and four of his friends drove out to a beach north of the city. We went in two cars. I rode with Denis and a man and a woman from his building who worked, as far as I could tell, at a shipping agency down by the port. It took forty minutes along the coast road. The beach Denis wanted was not the long sand one where the families were but a small cove past it, down a dirt path, with a shelf of black rock running out into the water and dropping off on the far side to deep green. The Pacific there is cold all year, Denis said, because of the current coming up from Antarctica. I put my hand in at the edge and believed him.

The idea was to jump off the end of the rock shelf. The drop was four or five metres to the water at the highest point, a bit less further along. There was a swell running, not big, but enough that the water at the foot of the rock rose and fell about a metre between sets. Denis went first. He took off his shirt and walked out to the end in his bare feet and stood a second and went off with his arms out and came up shouting. The friends went after him, one at a time, and then two of them went together holding hands, and they climbed back up a gully at the side where the rock was broken into steps and did it again.

I sat on a flat part of the rock back from the edge with the bags. There were five of them, plus the cooler, and somebody's camera, and the car keys, and I put the keys in my pocket and the camera in the cooler out of the sun. Denis came up the gully dripping and stood at the edge and waved at me and shouted for me to come in, the water was good. I shook my head. He shouted again. I shook my head again, and pointed at the bags, and he laughed and went off the rock backwards. They stayed in for most of an hour. When they came out, blue around the mouths, I handed out the towels in the order the bags had come out of the cars. The woman from Denis's building said something to him and he told me afterward she had said I was a good man to bring to a beach. I took it the way it was meant.

On the way back Denis stopped at a place by the road that sold empanadas out of a window, and we ate them on the hood of the Hyundai. He did not bring up the rocks again. He said that when we were boys I was the one who would stand on the trestle and tell everyone how many metres it was to the water and how many centimetres the bolts were rusted through, and he was the one who jumped. He said it was good to know some things had held.

On the second-last night of the visit Denis put on a barbecue on the roof of his building. He had been planning it since before I landed. The roof was flat, with a waist-high wall around it and a tank for the building's water at one end, and somebody had built a grill out of half an oil drum on legs. Denis had invited twenty people and most of them came, and some of them brought people he had not invited. He had bought beef and sausage and chicken at the market that morning and carried it up the stairs in four trips. He wanted to show off his brother from Canada, he said, and he did. He took me around the roof by the elbow and introduced me to each person in turn, in Spanish, and I caught my own name and the word Canadá and the word calibración, which he must have looked up, and each of them shook my hand or kissed me on the cheek, which is what they do there, men and women both.

I stood by the wall with a bottle of mineral water for about an hour. I answered what I could when somebody spoke English. A man who had worked on the docks asked me about the cold in northern Ontario and I told him about the winter of 1994 in Sudbury when it went to forty below and the instrument air lines froze at the mine I was contracted to. He had not heard of instrument air. I explained it. He went off to get more sausage. At about half past ten, when the music on someone's phone got louder and more people came up the stairs, I found Denis by the grill, told him I was going to bed, and went down. He said all right. He did not try to stop me, which I noticed. I let myself into the apartment with the key he had given me, washed my glass and my plate, and went to bed with the window cracked open. I could hear them on the roof until about three.

In the morning there were people asleep on Denis's couch and on his floor, and he was in the kitchen in yesterday's clothes making coffee for all of them. He gave me a cup and said, "You lasted an hour. I had money on forty-five minutes." I asked him who had taken the bet. He said he had made it with himself.

The thing about Valparaíso that I kept coming back to, the whole ten days, was the ground. The city sits in some of the worst earthquake country there is. The plate under the Pacific goes down under South America just off that coast, and Chile has had more big quakes than anywhere in the world, including the biggest ever recorded, the one in 1960 down south. Valparaíso itself was flattened in 1906 and badly damaged in 1985, and the quake of February 2010, the 8.8, shook it hard enough that Denis's landlord still talked about it. And here were all these buildings hung on the sides of hills on posts.

I asked Denis about it the first day and he shrugged and said Chileans build for it. That was not an answer I could do anything with, so I started reading. There was a public library branch near the bottom of the hill with two computers and decent internet, and I spent a couple of hours there over three afternoons while Denis was at work. Chile has had a seismic building code since the 1930s and has tightened it after every big quake. The newer towers in Viña del Mar and Santiago are built with reinforced concrete shear walls, a lot of them, more than a North American engineer would put in, and some of the newest buildings, hospitals mostly, sit on base isolators. Those are big bearings made of layers of rubber and steel plate, sometimes with a lead core, set between the foundation and the building, so that when the ground moves sideways the building does not have to move with it. The bearings take the displacement. The building rides on top and moves slowly back. I read that one of the hospitals built that way had come through the 2010 quake with its equipment still on the shelves while older buildings nearby had lost their walls.

The funicular lifts were the other thing. Valparaíso has a set of them, the ascensores, going up the faces of the hills from the flat part of the city, some of them more than a hundred years old. There was one near the bottom of Cerro Alegre and I went and stood at the lower station two mornings in a row and watched it run. Each lift is two wooden cars on parallel tracks, tied to one cable that goes over a big wheel at the top. When one car goes up the other comes down, and they pass in the middle where the track splits, and the weight of the one going down does most of the work of lifting the one going up. The motor only has to make up the difference, plus friction, plus whatever the people inside weigh against each other. The old ones were built to run on steam or water ballast before they were electrified. I stood there and worked out with a pencil on the back of the receipt from the empanadas what size motor you would need if the cars were each twelve hundred kilos empty and the load difference was never more than about six hundred, and it was not much. I asked the man at the ticket window, in my bad Spanish, what horsepower the motor was, and he did not know, and I did not blame him.

On the long flight home, Santiago to Houston overnight, I could not sleep, and I turned it over. The two cars on one cable and the building on its bearings were the same idea when you came down to it. You did not try to stop the load. You balanced it against something, or you let it move and gave it a way to come back. I filled four pages of the notebook I keep in my jacket with drawings of isolator bearings, out of what I could remember, and the forces on a counterweighted cable at an incline. Between Houston and Toronto I slept for two hours.

When I got home I kept at it for weeks. I ordered two books through the library in town, one of them a university text on seismic isolation that had to come from Ottawa on interlibrary loan and that I renewed twice. I read about friction pendulum bearings, where the building sits on a curved steel dish and slides on it, and how the curve sets the period of the swing. I read about the damping. None of it had anything to do with calibrating pressure transmitters for a paper mill two towns over, which was what I was doing that winter, and Jordan asked me one day in December why there was a book about earthquakes on the shop bench. I told him about the funiculars. He listened to the whole thing and then asked if I wanted the 0 to 300 psi gauge done before lunch or after.

The Ardmore Road workshop burned on a Tuesday night in late February of 2019. The call came to my apartment at twenty past two in the morning from the alarm company, and a few minutes after that from the fire department. By the time I got there in the van the roof over the unit next door was open and the flames were coming out of it, and they had spread along the common roof into mine. It was minus twenty-three. The water from the hoses was freezing on the hydrant and on the trucks and in the parking lot so that the firefighters were walking on ice. They would not let anyone within a hundred metres. I stood behind the tape with the van running for heat until about six, when they had it knocked down. The fire inspector's report, which came three weeks later, put the cause as an electrical fault in a panel in the unit next door. My side had taken the fire through the roof and the shared wall, and then the water, and then the cold on the water.

The records were what I thought about first, before the equipment, because the equipment could be replaced and the records could not. Every calibration certificate I had issued since 2006, every as-found and as-left reading, every traceability chain back to the national standards, was what my customers paid me for as much as the work itself. When an auditor came to one of the plants I served, my paperwork was what he asked to see. I had kept a duplicate of every record from the start, in a two-drawer fireproof filing cabinet bolted to the floor at the back of the shop, and a second copy, on paper and on two hard drives, in a steel cabinet in the spare room of the apartment. I had updated the apartment copy every Friday for thirteen years. When I came home that morning at seven, still in my coat, I opened the steel cabinet and checked that the last Friday was there. It was.

They let me into the building at about eleven, with the fire captain walking ahead. The roof over the bench area was gone and the ceiling joists were charcoal. There was ice on everything: on the benches, on the wall of test equipment, on the floor about five centimetres thick where the water had pooled and frozen. The fireproof cabinet was where it was supposed to be, black on the outside, and when I got the drawer open with a pry bar the folders inside were dry and the paper was a little brown at the edges and readable. I took them out to the van in two loads.

That afternoon I bought a new notebook at the pharmacy, a hardcover one with numbered pages, and started an inventory. I did not try to list what I had lost. I already had that list in the asset register, which was on the hard drive at the apartment. I listed what came out. Item one was the fireproof cabinet contents, thirty-one folders. Item two was the Druck pressure calibrator that had been in its hard case under the bench and came out with the case melted on one side and the unit inside wet but whole. Item three was a box of fittings. I numbered every piece in the order it came out of the burned shop, and wrote beside each one where it had been found, what condition it was in, and whether it powered up, if it had power to begin with. Jordan helped me carry things out over the next four days and read the serial numbers aloud while I wrote them down. By the end of the week the notebook ran to item two hundred and forty-seven, which was a fire extinguisher that had been hanging on the wall by the door the whole time, unused, with its gauge still in the green.

The insurance adjuster came out the second week of March. He was a reasonable man in a company parka who had done a lot of fires and not a lot of calibration labs, and he walked the shop with me and made notes on a tablet. His estimate came by email nine days later. The building was the landlord's problem and the landlord's insurance. Mine covered contents and business interruption. On the contents, the line that mattered was the reference equipment, the standards I calibrated everything else against, and his estimate put it at $61,400. He had taken my asset register, applied a straight-line depreciation over ten years to everything on it, and arrived at a number that would have bought about a third of what I needed to start again.

I went through it line by line. I spent most of four evenings at the kitchen table at the apartment with the estimate, the asset register, a shoebox of purchase receipts and invoices going back to 2006, and the calibration certificates for every reference standard I owned. I wrote it up in three columns: what he had, what it should be, and why.

The first problem was the depreciation itself. A reference standard is not a pickup truck. My deadweight tester, which I had bought used in 2009 and which was the best piece of equipment in the shop, did not lose value with age as long as it held its calibration, and I had the certificates from the accredited lab showing it had held, inside its tolerance, every two years for ten years. The same was true of the standard resistors and the platinum resistance thermometer. A used one with a clean calibration history sold for close to what a new one cost, sometimes more, because the history was the point. I found three listings from dealers for equivalent units and printed them.

The second problem was what he had left out entirely. My asset register, which he had worked from, listed the main units, and he had not gone past it. It did not list the set of masses for the deadweight tester separately, because I had bought them with it, and there were twenty-two of them, each with its own certificate, and a replacement set alone was over eleven thousand dollars. He had left out four pressure modules for the Fluke documenting calibrator, which I had bought one at a time over six years and which were in the receipts but had never made the register. He had left out the cost of the accredited recalibrations themselves. Every reference standard I replaced would need to be sent out and certified before I could use it on a customer's job, and those certifications ran from four hundred to two thousand dollars an item, and he had valued the instruments as if they could go straight from the box onto the bench. He had left out the cables and adapters and test leads, which nobody thinks about and which came to more than five thousand dollars. He had left out the temperature bath because it was on a lease-to-own I had paid off in 2016, and the paperwork for the payout was in a different folder.

When I added it up it came to eighty-three thousand dollars he had not counted, before any argument about depreciation. I put the whole thing in a binder with tabs, one tab per item, the receipt and the certificate and the dealer listing behind each one, and called his office and asked for an hour with him. He gave me forty minutes on a Thursday at his office in town. I went through the binder with him in order, and when we were done he closed it and said he would have to take it back to the company. He said that most people who came in to argue an estimate brought a number and no paper. He took the binder with him. The revised offer came in May and accepted almost all of the missing items and about half of my case on depreciation. It was not everything. It was enough.

The business interruption coverage on my policy paid a portion of lost income over a waiting period, and it did not pay wages the way I needed it to, and there was a gap of months before any of it came through. Jordan had been with me since 2016 and had a mortgage on a house he had bought in 2017. When I told him the week after the fire that I was going to keep paying him his full wage until we had a shop again, he asked out of what. I told him out of my savings. He said he could go on EI. I said I knew he could and that he was not going to.

I had about forty-one thousand dollars put away in a savings account at the credit union that I had been building since the divorce, for the kids' schooling and for whatever came. I moved it in chunks into the business account as it was needed. Jordan made twenty-seven dollars an hour, forty hours a week, $1,080 gross, and I paid him every second Friday the way I always had, by cheque, with the deductions taken off and remitted. There was work for him, not full-time, but enough. For the site jobs I rented an enclosed cargo trailer, sixteen feet, for $585 a month from an equipment rental place on the highway, and the two of us spent a weekend fitting it out with a plywood bench screwed to the side wall, a shelf with a lip, a vise, two LED shop lights, a small electric heater, and a cord with a twist-lock plug so we could run it off a customer's outlet. He towed it behind his own pickup to the jobs that needed a bench on site, and parked it in the customer's yard, and worked out of it. By April he had it organized better than I had ever kept the old shop.

Every second Friday I sat down at the kitchen table and wrote his wage into the ledger, gross and net and the remittances on their own lines, the same way my father wrote every sale at the hardware store into the big green book under the counter. The money was going out with nothing coming in to meet it, and I wrote it down in ink anyway, and I did the arithmetic on what was left in the account each time. At fifty I can say that the man who keeps the ledgers is finally at peace with the man who wants to give things away. By September the savings account had $6,200 left in it and I closed the ledger for the month and left it at that.

The customers were the other thing I had to hold onto. A calibration business lives on the schedule. If a plant has two hundred instruments on a yearly cycle and you are not there when the cycle comes due, they find someone who is, and they do not come back. I phoned every customer on the list the week of the fire, before they heard it from someone else, and told them what had happened and what I could still do. Most of what I did had always been on site anyway: pressure transmitters, temperature loops, flow meters, the kind of work you do with a documenting calibrator and a hand pump at the instrument where it sits. I could do that out of the van. The insurance advance on the equipment came through in April, and I bought replacements in the order the schedule needed them, and sent each one out for certification as it came in. Until then I borrowed. Two of my customers loaned me calibrators of their own for specific jobs.

The bench work was harder. Some things have to go on a bench in a controlled room: the reference standards themselves, the precision gauges, anything where the temperature in the room matters to the reading. One of my larger customers, a plant with its own instrument lab two hours east, let me use their bench two days a week from April into the fall. I drove out Tuesday and Thursday mornings, leaving at five-thirty, with whatever needed bench time boxed in the back of the van, and worked in a corner of their lab under their quality man's eye, and drove home at night. I did it about sixty times. I paid them in calibrations, their own instruments done at no charge on the days I was there, and they took it. By the end of the summer I had lost two customers out of thirty-eight, both small, and both had been talking about going elsewhere before the fire.

The landlord's insurance settled with him in July, and in August he told me he was rebuilding the whole row of units and asked whether I wanted mine back. I said I did, on the condition that I could lay out the inside myself and that the electrical panel next door would be inspected before I moved in. He agreed to both. I signed the contract for the rebuild of my unit, the fit-out part that was mine to pay for, in late September of 2019, with a general contractor the landlord used, for $68,500, most of which the contents settlement covered.

I drew the floor plan myself on quarter-inch graph paper at the kitchen table over two weekends. I had nine years in the old shop to go on and I knew where everything had been wrong in it. The new plan put the controlled-temperature room for the reference standards in the centre of the unit, with no exterior walls, so the outside weather would have to get through two walls to reach it. It put the electrical panel on the opposite side from the shared wall with the unit next door, with its own dedicated circuits for the benches and a separate one for the bath and the dry-wells. It put a fire-rated wall between the standards room and everything else, and a second fireproof cabinet, larger, next to the first. It moved the receiving bench to the door, so that instruments coming in went to the bench before they went anywhere else, and the outgoing shelf to the other side, so the two never crossed. It put the deadweight tester on its own concrete pad, isolated from the floor slab with a gap and a strip of neoprene, so the vibration from trucks on Ardmore Road would not reach it. I took that last idea from the books on base isolation, scaled down a long way.

The contractor looked at my drawing and said he had never had a client hand him a plan with the outlet heights dimensioned. I asked him if that was a problem. He said no, it would save him a day. They poured the new pad in October. Jordan came out with me on a Saturday to see the forms before the pour, and he walked the outline of the standards room in the gravel, heel to toe, counting, and told me it was a foot short of what I had drawn. I checked the graph paper against the forms with a tape. He was right. The contractor moved the forms that afternoon.

I started the new asset register that same week, in the back half of the notebook from the morning after the fire. Item one in the new list was the fireproof cabinet, the old one, which I had cleaned and had the lock replaced on and which was going back in where it had stood. Item two was the Druck calibrator, which had come back from the repair depot in September with a new case and a clean certificate.

Chapter 30

In 2020, the new workshop on Ardmore Road went up on the footprint of the one that had burned. The slab gave us the measurements. I kept the office in the same corner, the receiving bench inside the same door, and the test benches along the same wall. On the drawings, the rooms looked smaller than they had when I stood in them. I checked the dimensions twice and left them alone. I already knew how to move a crate through that building without shifting three other things first.

I rebuilt the shop in eleven months, stubbornly, mostly on weekends.

During the week I worked with the contractors, settling dimensions, checking deliveries against the order, and marking where I needed outlets and bench lights. I kept the drawings in a plastic sleeve because there was nowhere clean to put them down. The sleeve collected grit at the folded edge. By the end, I could open it to the electrical drawing without looking at the page numbers.

There were days when my contribution was a tape measure and a pencil. I measured the clear opening at the receiving door before they finished it, then measured the trolley that would go through. I stood at the proposed benches with a piece of cardboard cut to the depth of a worktop. A line on a drawing did not tell me whether a drawer would clear a chair. Where something needed changing, I marked it before it was covered.

I did the interior on weekends. I assembled cabinets on the floor, set them upright, and shimmed them before fastening them to the wall. I used a long level, a cordless drill, and a box of screws that stayed on the same piece of scrap plywood until the cabinets were finished. There was no point carrying the box from room to room. I kept a smaller handful in my apron and went back when I needed more.

Theo was fourteen that year. He came on Saturdays and swept up. At first he swept around the stacks of material, leaving clean rectangles between them. Once I had moved the stacks, he went back over the whole floor. He used a wide push broom on the open slab and a smaller one along the walls. He learned to pull staples and bent screws out of the sweepings before putting them into a bag.

I gave him work that could be done from the floor. He flattened cartons, carried empty pails, and sorted the unused brackets from the packaging. When something was too heavy, it stayed where it was until I had help or a trolley. We ate with the work stopped and the dust settled. Afterwards he put the rubbish near the door and swept the place where we had sat.

Maya kept the parts list. She ruled columns for what we needed, what had been ordered, and what had arrived. A box counted as arrived only after she had opened it and checked the contents. The packing slip went behind the list. She caught different lengths of drawer slide in cartons with almost identical labels, and a shortage of shelf clips that I would otherwise have discovered with a shelf in my hands.

On Saturdays she worked at a temporary table while Theo swept. I brought her packets of screws and hinges and gave her the quantities. She counted them herself. If I said there were about twenty, she wrote nothing until she knew whether there were eighteen or twenty-two. By then the list had pencil marks through pencil marks. She made a clean copy and kept the old one underneath it.

I fitted the worktops, put the cupboard doors on, and adjusted the catches until they closed without a shove. The walls and floor were new, but the bench heights were the ones I had used before. I wanted the reference equipment at a height where I could read it sitting down. The heavier cases went low enough to move onto a trolley without lifting them to my chest.

The place reopened in 2020 on the same floor plan. I removed the temporary table, put the packing slips in the office, and swept the route from the receiving door to the benches. The first work went through the building by the old route: incoming inspection, the bench, paperwork, then the shelf for collection. I could reach for the office light switch without turning towards it. I still checked that I had switched it off before locking up.

The roof had been settled before I finished the interior. The roofing contractor stood with me outside and explained the membrane, the joints, and the work around the drains. He had the price on a sheet clipped to a board. There was room beneath the total for a change. He tapped that space with his pencil and said I could save two thousand dollars if Jordan and I laid the membrane ourselves.

Jordan Kwan was beside me. We looked at the rolls that had been delivered and at the flat roof above them. The contractor said he would explain the method. I asked whether the saving included all the labour or whether there would be another charge for having someone finish the edges. He went through the figures again. The saving was two thousand dollars if we did the laying.

I said no. I told him to leave the roof in his price. Jordan went back inside to the work we already had, and I kept the sheet long enough to check the total against my copy. Two thousand dollars paid for a fair amount of cupboard hardware and wire. I put the figure into the building costs with the rest.

Looking back, I was still the reckless one, the first up any ladder and the first off any roof.

I stayed on the ground while the roofers worked. When the contractor wanted to show me a detail, I had him bring it down on the drawing. I could see the line of the roof from the yard, but I could not see the joints. Those were his work. I checked that the drains were clear from below and kept our materials away from where the roofers were handling theirs.

The day they finished, there were pieces of wrapping near the wall and a few offcuts in the yard. I picked up what was on the ground. The contractor went over the completed work with me at the office desk. I paid the roofers and filed their bill with the roof papers. Jordan and I had enough work at bench height.

While the rebuilding went on, I read everything I could find about how the fire had started. The cause had been put down to an electrical fault next door. More precisely, a loose connection had heated until it arced. I copied that wording onto a page in my notebook. I wanted to know what had happened between the connection coming loose and the building burning.

I read reports, diagrams, and technical explanations at the temporary table after the tools were put away. The paper collected a fine layer of dust unless I kept it in a folder. I marked passages about contact resistance, heat, and the changes at a connection as it deteriorated. Some explanations stopped at the word fault. That did not give me much to work with.

A loose connection could carry current and still produce heat where it should not. The current could remain below the point that would trip an ordinary breaker. I worked through the figures with a pencil, keeping the resistance at the bad connection separate from the resistance of the load. A small part of a circuit could become very hot without the whole circuit drawing an exceptional amount.

Then there was the arc itself. I turned over the physics for weeks: the gap, the voltage across it, the conducting path, and the way it could break and form again. I drew rough circuits on the backs of printed pages. When an explanation used a clean, steady waveform, I looked for the part that dealt with an intermittent connection. The fault next door had not been a classroom resistor.

I argued with the electrical inspector about the newer arc-detecting breakers. We stood in the unfinished shop with the drawings spread on a worktop. I asked whether one would have caught that fault. He distinguished between the kinds of arcing the devices were intended to recognise and everything that might happen at a bad connection. I asked him to apply that distinction to the connection next door.

He would not say that a newer breaker would certainly have stopped the fire. I would not accept a general description of protection as an answer to that particular question. We went back through what was known and what had only been inferred after the damage. Neither of us could put the original connection on the bench and reproduce its condition before it failed.

After he left, I added notes beside the passages I had underlined. I kept the inspection material separate from the invoices, in a folder thick enough that the fastening no longer lay flat. The question stayed open on paper. There was no further measurement I could make on the loose connection that had started it. I put the folder in the office cupboard and returned to fitting the drawers.

Emma Rourke came to the shop in the spring of 2021. Her apprenticeship had ended halfway through when the shop she was with closed. She had tried elsewhere in the district, and no one would take her on. We sat in the office with her record between us. She showed me what she had completed and where the work had stopped. There was still a considerable part of the apprenticeship to do.

I asked her about the work she had actually done. A completed section on a record could cover a lot of different experience. I wanted to know what she had connected, what she had tested, and which figures she had entered herself. She answered in terms of the jobs. Where she had watched rather than done the work, she said so. I turned the pages back to check the dates.

Afterwards I opened my books. Jordan's wage was already there, along with the building costs and the ordinary expenses that arrived whether the benches were full or not. A second wage took more than the amount on the pay statement. There was the time I would spend training her, when I could otherwise have been doing chargeable work. I put that time into the calculation as well.

I hired a second apprentice that spring, happily, though the books said I could not afford her.

I cleared a place at the bench and trained her myself. We began with the whole procedure, including the parts that were easy to leave off when a job looked familiar. She recorded the equipment as it arrived, checked its identification, and wrote down its condition before connecting anything. I had her find the required range and the applicable tolerance in the paperwork rather than take either from me.

For pressure work, we checked the fittings and the working limits before assembling the line. I showed her how I arranged a test so that I could isolate one section without disturbing the rest. She made the connections, and I checked them before we applied pressure. Afterwards she released it and verified that it was gone before taking anything apart. We put the caps back on as we went.

I made her keep the readings she got, including the inconvenient ones. If a point needed repeating, she marked the repeat and kept the first result legible. A row of tidy numbers was no use if I could not tell what had happened at the bench. We worked through the calculation together, with the units written at every stage. I checked her arithmetic after checking the setup.

Jordan carried on with his work while I stood beside Emma. Sometimes I had to stop my own job in the middle, cap the connections, and leave a note about where I had reached. Training took that sort of time. At the end of the day I entered the hours, put the work back in order, and worked out what we could finish the next morning. Emma's next task went on a sheet beside the bench.

The card drawer had survived as my way of tracking jobs. Each card had the customer, equipment identification, dates, and room for the work. I moved it from one section to another as the job went through. Cards waiting for parts sat together. Completed cards went behind the divider at the back until I filed them. I could open the drawer and put a finger on the job I wanted.

Emma started moving the tracking into software on a tablet. She brought the tablet to the desk and showed me the fields she had set up. The customer and equipment identification were there, along with the dates and the stage of the job. She could bring up everything waiting for parts without moving a divider. She could also bring up everything due for collection, provided we had entered it properly.

I thought the cards worked better. I could have said so. A card stayed where I put it, and its whole face was visible without opening another screen. I had used them long enough to recognise some jobs by the colour of the writing. Instead, I gave her room at the desk and let her go through the drawer. I answered when an abbreviation needed explaining.

She did the transfer with the cards beside her. If an entry was unclear, she brought it to me rather than guessing. We checked the jobs still in the building against the entries on the tablet. I walked to the shelves, read the equipment numbers, and called them back to her. When there was a difference, we found the paper or the equipment before carrying on.

Then she boxed up the cards. She kept their order, put the dividers in with them, and labelled the box. The drawer came out empty. I wiped the bottom and put it back. For a few days I opened it when I wanted to check a job. Then I shut it and went to the tablet. The box stayed available, but it was no longer on the desk.

I learned the software one operation at a time. I entered an incoming job, changed its status after inspection, and found it again by the equipment number. Emma had me do the steps myself. Watching her thumb move over the screen had not taught me where anything was. I wrote a short sequence on paper and kept it by the charger until I stopped needing it.

The screen sometimes went dark while I was reading a number from a tag. I learned to wake it and return to the entry without starting another one. I also learned to check which field I was in before typing. A serial number in the notes could still be read, but it would not be found in the serial-number search. I corrected my entries before putting the equipment on the shelf.

By the time we had the current work entered, I could find a job without Emma standing beside me. I still went to the shelf to check the actual item when the information mattered. The software told me it was ready. The case, label, and paperwork told me whether it was ready to leave. Emma kept entering the work, and I kept learning the parts of the program I used.

In 2022, the purchasing manager at my largest customer told me the renewal rate would be ten per cent lower. I sat across the desk with my folder closed in front of me. The proposed rate was on the renewal papers. He said it as a figure already settled, then turned the page to the part that required a signature.

I had my costs worked out on a sheet inside the folder. I had put labour, travel, supplies, and the time spent on the paperwork into separate lines. I had used the work we actually did, not a full week of billable hours that never quite occurred. The sheet showed what remained at the old rate and what would remain after the reduction. I had checked the arithmetic before leaving the shop.

The folder had a pocket on the right. The cost sheet was in it, folded once, with the totals facing out. I could have brought it onto the desk and gone through the lines. Instead I left the folder closed. I read the renewal rate again and checked the period it covered. There was no change in the ten per cent when I looked at it a second time.

I did not object. I took the pen and signed where the purchasing manager indicated. He separated the copies, and I put mine into the folder in front of the cost sheet. The visit took less time than I had allowed. Outside, I opened the truck door, set the folder on the seat, and checked that I had the keys before closing it again.

Back at the shop, I took out both sheets. I wrote the renewed rate beside the old one in the working figures and recalculated the affected work. The costs did not move down to meet it. I filed the signed copy and kept the calculations with the customer papers. When the next job from that account went onto the bench, it went through the same procedure as the one before.

A vendor sent a specification sheet for a new digital pressure reference. On the stated accuracy, it could have replaced two of my older instruments. I laid the sheet beside their records and compared the ranges. There was enough overlap to make the idea worth checking. One case instead of two would have freed shelf space, and one set of controls would have meant less changing equipment between jobs.

I read the qualifications beneath the large figures. Accuracy could mean different things depending on the range, the temperature, and the period since calibration. I wanted the claim in the form that applied to the work I would do. I marked the relevant lines and arranged to borrow a demonstration unit. I did not order one. I cleared a place for it beside my own reference.

When it arrived, I recorded its identification and the condition of the case. I read the instructions, checked the required warm-up, and set it up with my reference so that I could compare them under the same conditions. The hoses and fittings were ones I had checked. I made sure a leak in my arrangement would not be presented later as a fault in the unit.

I tested it every day for a month. I kept the same sheet format, with the date, conditions, test points, and readings. I worked up through the range and back down. At each point I allowed the pressure to settle before writing anything. If I disturbed a connection or changed the arrangement, I recorded that too. The demonstration unit stayed on its assigned piece of bench between checks.

The first set of results was outside what the specification had led me to expect. I checked the units and the calculation. Then I checked my reference and repeated the comparison. The result did not improve enough to explain the difference. I left the figures as they stood and tested again the next day. There was no benefit in adjusting my method until it produced the vendor's number.

Jordan could see the sheets accumulating beside the case. Emma used the rest of the bench and left the arrangement alone. I did the comparison before moving on to the day's work, so the checks did not depend on remembering at closing time. On days when other jobs filled the bench, I kept that space clear. The demonstration had been borrowed to answer one question, and I continued until I had a month's readings.
