At the end of the month, it was four times worse than claimed. I checked that conclusion against the specification again, including the conditions and the way the tolerance was expressed. The difference remained. I made a clean table of my results and kept the daily sheets behind it. The older instruments still had their places on the shelves.

I did not buy the new reference. I put the demonstration unit back in its case with the accessories it had arrived with and returned it. The specification sheet went into the folder with the comparison, rather than into the drawer of equipment manuals. I had used a month of bench space and a little time each day. I continued using the two older instruments.

In the rebuilt shop, every drawer had a printed label. I had started the labels while I was fitting the cabinets and finished them after the work settled into place. Handwritten slips had done during the rebuilding, but several had curled at the corners. The printed ones fitted the holders. I used the same size of type, large enough to read while standing at the bench.

The drawers were arranged by what went into them. Fittings had their compartments, and small electrical parts had theirs. I kept different thread types apart, even when the pieces looked much the same from above. A part went back into the proper compartment after use. If I could not identify it, it did not go into a drawer just because there was room.

Every instrument had a numbered shelf location. The number appeared in the record and at the shelf, so the next person could find it without asking who had used it last. Heavy cases stayed on the lower shelves. Leads and attachments stayed with the equipment they belonged to unless they had an assigned place of their own. The cases stood with their handles facing out.

I kept the incoming work separate from work ready to leave. There was enough room to set down a case and read its label without balancing another one on top. Empty packaging went out of the way after we had decided whether it was needed for return. The receiving bench was a place to check things, not a place to keep a month's cardboard.

Friday's last paperwork included printing the week's job ledger from Emma's software. I checked the dates before sending it to the printer. The first time I did it myself, I printed a wider period than I needed. I set those pages aside for scrap and printed the correct week. After that I checked the preview as well as the dates.

I filed the ledger in a binder by month. The current month had a divider and the most recent week at the back. At the change of month, I checked that the weeks followed without a gap. If the last page contained only a few lines, it still went in. I did not compress the record to save a sheet and then have to work out where a week had gone.

The binder sat in the office cupboard, within reach of the desk. The boxed cards were elsewhere. I could use the tablet to find a current job and open the binder to see a week's work on paper. Before closing on Friday, I returned the tablet to its charger, put the loose documents where they belonged, and checked the benches. A printed ledger did not clear a bench by itself.

Luc Gagnon stopped by one afternoon with a cooler in his truck. He was drinking again. I saw the cooler when he opened the door, and he came towards the shop carrying a beer. He held it out to me before we reached the office. The can was cold enough to leave water on his fingers.

I said no. He kept it in his hand while I took us into the office. I moved a folder off the second chair and made him a coffee. There were two clean mugs in the cupboard. I put his on the desk within reach and sat down opposite him. The cooler stayed in his truck.

He talked, and I listened. There was no work for him to help with, and I did not take him onto the shop floor. The office door stood open. From my chair I could see part of the receiving bench and the edge of the shelves. When something needed my attention, I dealt with it and came back to the desk. Luc stayed in the chair.

I drank my coffee while it was hot. His cooled, and I replaced it. The first mug left a ring on the paper I had put underneath it. I moved the paper and wiped the desk. He shifted in the chair, then settled again. I left the keys and the folder where they were and stayed with him.

Coffee did not make him fit to drive. Time had to pass. I did not send him out with a full mug and call the matter finished. We sat through the afternoon. He went over some of what he had already said, and I let him. When the light changed at the office window, I switched on the desk light.

I kept him there until he was fit to drive. By then the coffee things had been washed, and the work on the receiving bench had been put away. I went outside with him and stood clear while he got into the truck. The cooler was still there. I waited until he had left, then went back into the office and wiped the ring from the desk properly.

In 2023, I was fifty-two. The labels were still on the drawers, though the ones beside the busiest bench needed wiping more often than the rest. I replaced a curled corner rather than leave it to catch on a sleeve. The shelf numbers were legible from the aisle. I could stand at the office door and see whether a case had been put back with its handle facing the wall.

Jordan and Emma used the same locations I did. If something was out for work, its place stayed empty until it came back. I checked the identification before moving a case into a vacant space. Similar cases could hide different equipment, and a number on a shelf was only useful if the right thing stood above it.

On Friday, I brought up the ledger on the tablet and set the week's dates. I checked the preview, printed it, and took the pages to the desk. The holes went over the binder rings without tearing. I turned to the month's divider, put the pages behind the previous week's, and closed the rings. Then I returned the binder to the cupboard.

Before leaving, I emptied the small bin under the desk and carried the bag to the rubbish. I swept the clear floor, put the broom away, and checked that the pressure lines had been released. The cases were shut. The tablet was charging. I switched off the bench lights, then the office light, and locked the door from outside.
